[^1]: Collated from the Greek (*Ѡ Еллинскагѡ изслѣдованъ*): Demonstrates the systematic textual collation undertaken by the scholarly circle of Metropolitan Petro Mohyla and the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra brotherhood directly against Byzantine Greek Triodia (Venice printed editions and Byzantine manuscript witnesses). This collation was intended to rectify scribal errors, corruptions, and divergent redactions that had accumulated in East Slavic liturgical manuscripts, establishing a philologically robust pre-Nikonian Kyivan recension.
[^2]: Now printed the second time (*Второе Тѵпомъ издано*): The first Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra folio edition of the Lenten Triodion was published in 1627 under Archimandrite Zacharias Kopystensky and proto-printer Pamvo Berynda. This 1640 imprint represents the revised second Lavra edition produced under the direct archpastoral supervision of Metropolitan Petro Mohyla.
[^3]: Dating formula: The imprint records the date according to both the Byzantine Creation Era (*Anno Mundi* 7148) and the Christian Era of the Incarnation (*Anno Domini* 1640), on March 23, in the 8th Indiction (March 23, 1640 O.S.).
[^4]: Armorial initials (*Ѳ Т / П / Σ / П / Z / K*): The Greek and Cyrillic initials flanking the heraldic crest stand for: *Ѳеодор Проскура Сущанский, Писарь Земский Киевский* (Theodor Proskura-Sushchansky, Land Scribe [Pisarz Ziemski] of the Kyiv Voivodeship), a prominent Orthodox nobleman and magistrate of the Commonwealth, and major patron of the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra.
[^5]: The Sushchansky-Proskura coat of arms (*Клейнотъ*): An armorial variation belonging to the Polish-Ruthenian heraldic tradition (associated with the *Kozieł* / *Proskura* arms). The escutcheon bears an upward-pointing arrow crossed by a transverse bar resembling the Christian Cross, issuing from a bifurcated base; it is ensigned with a nobleman's crowned helmet and three ostrich feathers.
[^6]: Endowed its own Archer (*своего Стрѣлца даровало*): A learned Baroque conceit (*conceptus*) linking the family's martial symbol of the arrow with the celestial constellation Sagittarius (the Archer of the Zodiac), asserting that the heavens themselves reflect and honor the virtues of the House of Sushchansky.
[^7]: Exarch of the Holy Apostolic Throne of Constantinople: A canonical title borne by the Metropolitans of Kyiv, reflecting their direct canonical subordination and jurisdictional tie to the Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople, as well as the stauropegial dignity enjoyed by the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra.
[^8]: Land Scribe of the Kyiv Voivodeship (*Писарь Земский Киевский* / *Pisarz Ziemski Kijowski*): A high-ranking judicial and administrative magistrate elected by the nobility of the Kyiv Voivodeship to serve in the land court (*sąd ziemski*) and provincial assembly (*sejmik*), responsible for maintaining the official territorial registers and acts.
[^9]: Luke 4:4 (citing Deuteronomy 8:3): "And Jesus answered him, saying, It is written, That man shall not live by bread alone, but by every word of God."
[^10]: Luke 21:36: "Watch therefore, and pray always, that you may be accounted worthy to escape all these things that shall come to pass, and to stand before the Son of Man."
[^11]: St. Isidore of Seville (*Sententiarum libri tres*, Book III, chapter 50: *De oratione*): "Prayer and reading are the two ways by which we converse with God. If anyone wishes to remain always with God, let him pray and read without ceasing; for when we pray, we speak with God, and when we read, God speaks with us."
[^12]: 1 Thessalonians 5:17: "Pray without ceasing."
[^13]: St. John Chrysostom, *Homilies on the Epistle to the Ephesians*, Homily 13: St. John extols the omnipotent efficacy of pure prayer, asserting that its spiritual force transcends all physical laws and the powers of created nature.
[^14]: Scriptural paradigms of the power of prayer: Exodus 14 (the miraculous division of the Red Sea by Moses); Daniel 3 (the preservation of the Three Holy Children Ananias, Azarias, and Misael in the fiery furnace of Babylon); 3 Kings 18 [1 Kings 18 MT] (the Prophet Elijah shutting heaven and praying for rain to moisten the earth); and Matthew 7:7 ("Ask, and it shall be given you; seek, and you shall find; knock, and it shall be opened unto you").
[^15]: St. Augustine on prayer: The holy Father defines prayer as the spiritual breath and enlivenment of the soul, a joy to the holy Angels, a scourge to demons, a sacred offering unto God, and the indispensable foundation of every virtue.
[^16]: Incorrupt bodies of the Pechersk saints: Metropolitan Petro Mohyla directs the reader's attention to the tangible, empirical reality of the hundreds of incorrupt relics of the monastic saints resting in the Near (*St. Anthony's*) and Far (*St. Theodosius's*) Caves of the Kyiv Lavra as living proof of divine grace and the truth of the Orthodox faith.
[^17]: King Władysław IV Vasa (*Władysław IV Waza*, reigned 1632–1648): King of Poland and Grand Duke of Lithuania, who in the "Articles of Pacification" (1632) and royal diplomas formally legalized and re-established the Orthodox hierarchy of Kyiv, recognizing Petro Mohyla as legitimate Metropolitan of Kyiv, Halych, and all Rus'.
[^18]: The 1627 Kyiv Triodion edition: Archimandrite Zacharias Kopystensky produced the editio princeps of the Pechersk Lenten Triodion in 1627. Due to the high demand across Ruthenian parishes and monasteries, copies quickly became scarce (*na skupost'*), necessitating this comprehensive 1640 edition.
[^19]: The Memento Mori tradition: Refers to the well-documented classical and early Christian practice—cited by Herodotus (*Histories* II.78) and Christian moralists—of displaying skulls or coffins at banquets to admonish participants of the vanity of earthly delights and the certainty of divine judgment.
[^20]: Matthew 3:2: "Repent, for the kingdom of heaven is at hand."
[^21]: The three stones of repentance: Petro Mohyla allegorizes the three stones from David's sling (1 Kings 17:40 [1 Sam 17:40 MT]) into the three traditional sacramental components of genuine repentance: contrition of the heart (*skrusheniye* / *contritio cordis*), oral confession (*spovidaniye hrikhov* / *confessio oris*), and satisfaction/acts of penance (*dosyt' chynennya* / *satisfactio operis*).
[^22]: Archimandrite Zacharias Kopystensky (d. 1627): Renowned Kyivan churchman, polemicist (author of the *Palinodiya*), and Archimandrite of the Kyiv-Pechersk Caves Lavra from 1624 until his repose in 1627; spiritual predecessor (*antecessor*) of Petro Mohyla.
[^23]: Doubtful Mars (*vontplyvomu Marsowi*): A typical Baroque humanist mythological allusion to Mars (the god of war), evoking Teodor Proskura's youthful military expeditions defending the southeastern borders of the Commonwealth against Tatar and Ottoman incursions.
[^24]: Matthew 11:12: "And from the days of John the Baptist until now the kingdom of heaven suffers violence, and the violent take it by force."

[^25]: Physical asceticism with iron chains (*желѣзом тѣла ѡковываютъ*): An allusion to the traditional East Slavic and Athonite ascetical practice of wearing heavy iron chains or penitential crosses (*verigi* / *żelaza*), exemplified among the monastic fathers of the Kyiv-Pechersk Caves (such as St. Theodosius of the Caves and subsequent cave-dwelling recluses) to mortify bodily passions and prevent physical sleep during nocturnal vigilance.
[^26]: Dating formula of the Mohyla Foreword: The dedicatory preface concludes on May 25, 7148 AM (1640 AD), precisely two months after the imprint date recorded on the architectural master title page (March 23, 1640 O.S.), demonstrating the chronological window between typesetting and final archpastoral imprimatur.
[^27]: Genesis 1:26: "And God said, Let Us make man in Our image, after Our likeness."
[^28]: Genesis 2:8, 15: God placing man in the Garden of Eden to dress it and to keep it.
[^29]: Psalm 8:5–6 (LXX Ps 8:6): "Thou hast made him a little lower than the angels, and hast crowned him with glory and honor."
[^30]: 1 Peter 5:8: "Be sober, be vigilant; because your adversary the devil, as a roaring lion, walks about, seeking whom he may devour."
[^31]: Matthew 4:2; Luke 4:1–2: The forty-day fast of Christ our Savior in the wilderness, establishing the archetype and spiritual pattern for the Holy Great Forty Days.
[^32]: Venial and mortal sins (*повше́хныи* vs. *смерте́лныи* / *peccata venialia* et *mortalia*): Metropolitan Petro Mohyla employs the precise moral-theological distinction between venial and mortal sins formulated in the Latin and Eastern theological syntheses of the 17th-century Mohylan circle in Kyiv.
[^33]: Matthew 22:13; Matthew 25:30: The cast outer darkness where there shall be weeping and gnashing of teeth.
[^34]: John 3:16: "For God so loved the world, that He gave His only begotten Son, that whosoever believs in Him should not perish, but have everlasting life."
[^35]: The 8th Indiction of 1640: The Byzantine 15-year indiction cycle corresponds exactly: 7148 AM divided by 15 leaves a remainder of 8, confirming the technical accuracy of the Pechersk printing house chronogram.
[^36]: Full-page woodcut engraving of the Crucifixion: Produced by the printshop of the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra, this plate synthesizes Byzantine iconographic conventions (the solemn, standing postures of the Theotokos and St. John, the Golgotha cavern containing Adam's skull) with Western Baroque naturalism in the anatomical rendering and the urban panorama of Jerusalem.
[^37]: 1 Corinthians 2:2 (Slavonic Pericope 129): "For I determined not to know anything among you, save Jesus Christ, and Him crucified."
[^38]: Title formula of the Triodion: *Triodion, that is, the Three-Ode Canon* (*ТРІѠ́ДІѠНЪ си́есть: ТРІПѢ́СНЕЦЪ*). So named because the weekday canons of Great Lent contain only three odes (ordinarily Odes 8 and 9 plus one preceding ode), rather than the customary eight or nine odes found in festal and Sunday canons.
[^39]: Great Vespers hymnographic structure: The typikon combines the Sunday Resurrectional cycle of the Octoechos with the specialized penitential hymnography of the Triodion, signaling the entry into the Pre-Lenten liturgical period.
[^40]: Luke 18:14; Matthew 23:12: "For whosoever exalts himself shall be abased; and he that humbls himself shall be exalted."
[^41]: Scriptural paradigms of tears: King Hezekiah delivered from death (4 Kings 20:1–6 [2 Kings 20 MT]; Isaiah 38:1–8), and the repentant sinful woman who washed Christ's feet with her tears (Luke 7:36–50).
[^42]: Mark's Chapters (*Сѵ́главъ Ма́рка І҆еромона́ха*): Liturgical rubrics attributed to Mark, Hieromonk of the Monastery of Saint Sabas near Jerusalem, codified across medieval Byzantine and Slavic Typika to resolve calendar coincidences when movable and immovable feasts intersect.
[^43]: Chapter 42 of the Typikon: Governs the coincidence of the Feast of the Meeting of the Lord (February 2) and its associated Forefeast and Afterfeast with the Pre-Lenten Sundays (Publican and Pharisee, Prodigal Son, Meatfare, Cheesefare).
[^44]: Festal Matins rubrics: Prescribes the Polyeleos and the Sunday Resurrectional Evlogitaria (*The Angelic Council*) when the Great Feast of the Meeting intersects with the Sunday service, showing the fusion of festal and dominical hymnography.
[^45]: Psalm 97:2 (LXX Ps 97:2): Appointed as the festal Entrance Verse (Introit) for the Meeting of the Lord.
[^46]: Patristic Homilies from the *Tolkovoye Yevangeliye* (Commentary Gospel): Appoints the classical homilies of St. John Chrysostom and St. Cyril of Alexandria on the Parable of the Publican and the Pharisee to be read at Sunday Matins.
[^47]: The Triodion Penitential Sticheron (*Мно́жество съдѣ́ѧнныхъ мои́хъ ѕѡ́лъ*): The 1640 Kyivan edition preserves this ancient post-Gospel sticheron based on Davidic compunction, which in later Synodal editions is grouped with *Open unto me the doors of repentance, O Giver of Life*.
[^48]: St. George of Nicomedia (*Творе́нїе Геѡ́ргїа*): Metropolitan George of Nicomedia (9th century), celebrated hymnographer who composed the triodia and tetraodia of the Pre-Lenten and Lenten seasons in the Byzantine rite.
[^49]: Katavasia of Ode 1: *I shall open my mouth* (*Ѡ҆тве́рзꙋ ꙋ҆ста̀ моѧ̑*), theirmos of the Canon to the Most Holy Theotokos (Tone 4), standardly appointed during the Pre-Lenten period.
[^50]: Ode 3 Heirmos: *There is none holy as Thou, O Lord my God* (*Нѣ́сть ст҃ъ, ꙗ҆́коже Ты̀, Гдⷵи Бж҃е мо́й*), based upon the Third Biblical Canticle (1 Kings 2:2 [1 Sam 2:2 MT], the Song of Hannah).

[^51]: Podoben incipit: *Speedily anticipate* (*Ско́рѡ предварѝ* / Greek: *Тахὺ προκατάлаβε*). A standard Tone 4 prosomoion model hymn frequently appointed for sessional hymns, sedalia, and apolytikia.
[^52]: Ode 4 Heirmos: *Christ is my power* (*Хрⷭто́съ мнѣ̀ си́ла* / *Christos mou dynamis*). Based upon the Fourth Biblical Canticle, the Prayer of the Prophet Habakkuk (Hab. 3:2–19).
[^53]: Philippians 2:7: "But made Himself of no reputation, and took upon Him the form of a servant, and was made in the likeness of men."
[^54]: James 4:6; 1 Peter 5:5; Proverbs 3:34: "God resists the proud, but gives grace unto the humble."
[^55]: The washing of the disciples' feet: John 13:4–15, Christ's supreme manifestation of kenotic humility, setting a physical paradigm for Christian discipleship.
[^56]: Ode 5 Heirmos: *By Thy divine light* (*Бж҃їимъ свѣ́томъ* / *Tō theio phengei sou*). Based upon the Fifth Biblical Canticle, the Prayer of Isaiah (Isa. 26:9–20).
[^57]: Classical allusion: The Lydian chariot (*Лѵді́йскїѧ колесни́ца* / *Lydion harma*). An ancient proverbial Greek metaphor for swift racing and athletic velocity, adapted into Byzantine hymnography to illustrate that the humble Publican outstripped the fast-moving, virtuous Pharisee.
[^58]: Psalm 109:2 (LXX Ps 109:2 / MT Ps 110:2): "The Lord shall send the rod of Thy power out of Zion: rule Thou in the midst of Thine enemies."
[^59]: Ode 6 Heirmos: *The sea of life* (*Жите́йскагѡ мо́рѧ* / *Tou biou tēn thalassan*). Based upon the Sixth Biblical Canticle, the Prayer of Jonah from the belly of the sea monster (Jonah 2:3–10).
[^60]: Podoben incipit: *Seeking the highest* (*Вы́шнихъ и҆щѧ̀* / *Ta anō zētōn*). Tone 2 melodic model appointed for the Kontakion and Ikos.
[^61]: Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (c. 1256–1335): Eminent Byzantine church historian, poet, and hymnographer of Constantinople who composed the definitive verse and prose Synaxaria for the movable cycle of the Triodion and Pentecostarion. In this 1640 Kyiv edition, the Lavra press translated the Greek synaxaria into elevated Early Modern Ruthenian (*prosta mova*) prose for the instruction of clergy and faithful.
[^62]: St. Cosmas the Melodist, Bishop of Maiuma (d. c. 760): Renowned 8th-century Byzantine hymnographer, foster-brother of St. John of Damascus, who composed the original three-ode canons (triodia) for Great and Holy Week.
[^63]: St. Theodore the Studite (759–826) and his brother St. Joseph the Studite, Archbishop of Thessalonica (d. 825): Byzantine monastic reformers and hymnographers who systematically completed the hymnographic corpus of the Lenten Triodion at the Studios Monastery in Constantinople.
[^64]: Emperor Leo VI the Wise (866–912): Byzantine Emperor, scholar, and hymnographer who authored liturgical poetry, including the Four-Ode Canon for Great and Holy Saturday.
[^65]: Bishop Mark of Hydrus (Otranto, 8th–9th century): A monk of the Lavra of Saint Sabas near Jerusalem and subsequently Bishop of Hydrus (Otranto in Apulia, Southern Italy), who completed the full nine-ode canons of Holy Saturday and other services of the Triodion.
[^66]: Marginal gloss *Catachrestice* (*Катахрисикѡ̃с* / *catachresis*): A rhetorical figure denoting an extended or improper use of a term; here explaining that the title *Triodion* ("Three-Ode Book") is applied by extension to the entire volume, even though Sunday and festal services contain full eight- or nine-ode canons.
[^67]: Military pedagogy in Mohylan Ruthenian prose: Metropolitan Petro Mohyla and the Kyiv-Pechersk scholars employ a vivid homiletic comparison between Cossack hetmans briefing their regiments before military engagement and the Church Fathers preparing Christians with spiritual armor for the ascetic warfare of Great Lent.
[^68]: Isaiah 14:12: "How art thou fallen from heaven, O Lucifer, son of the morning! how art thou cut down to the ground, which didst weaken the nations!"
[^69]: James 4:6; 1 Peter 5:5; Proverbs 3:34.
[^70]: Luke 18:14: "I tell you, this man went down to his house justified rather than the other: for every one that exalts himself shall be abased; and he that humbls himself shall be exalted."
[^71]: Publican (*мытникъ*): A tax-farmer or collector of state customs and road tolls (*myto*), infamous in the ancient and early modern world for extortion and financial injustice.
[^72]: Acts 23:8: "For the Sadducees say that there is no resurrection, neither angel, nor spirit: but the Pharisees confess both." The Kyivan synaxarion notes the etymology of Pharisee from the Hebrew *perushim* ("separated / set apart") and Sadducee from the priest Zadok (*Tsedek*, "righteousness").
[^73]: Ode 7 Heirmos: *The God-fashioned furnace* (*Богозда́ннꙋю ѹ҆́бѡ пе́щь*). Based upon the Seventh Biblical Canticle, the Song of the Three Holy Children in Babylon (Dan. 3:26–56).
[^74]: Romans 12:16: "Mind not high things, but condescend to men of low estate. Be not wise in your own conceits."
[^75]: Genesis 28:12: The Vision of Jacob's Ladder ascending from earth to heaven, universally interpreted in Byzantine and Slavic hymnography as a type and figure of the Mother of God uniting heaven and earth through the Incarnation.
[^76]: Ode 8 Heirmos: *From the flame Thou didst pour out dew* (*И҆з̾ пла́мени ст҃ы̑мъ ро́сꙋ и҆сточи́лъ є҆сѝ* / *Ek phlogos tois hosiois drosos epēgasas*). Based upon the Eighth Biblical Canticle, the Praise of the Three Holy Children (Dan. 3:57–88).
[^77]: Ode 9 Heirmos: *It is not possible for men to see God* (*Бг҃а чл҃кѡмъ невозмо́жно ви́дѣти* / *Theon anthrōpois idein adynaton*). Based upon the Ninth Biblical Canticle, the Magnificat and Benedictus (Luke 1:46–55, 68–79).
[^78]: Matthew 5:3: "Blessed are the poor in spirit: for theirs is the kingdom of heaven."
[^79]: Podoben incipit: *Give ear, ye women* (*Же́ны ѹ҆слы́шите* / Greek: *Gynaikes akoutisthēte*). Tone 2 exapostilarion model melody.
[^80]: Psalm 9:33 (LXX Ps 9:33 / MT Ps 10:12): "Arise, O Lord God, let Thy hand be lifted up: forget not the poor." Appointed as a psalm verse for the Stichera at the Praises.
[^81]: Psalm 9:2 (LXX Ps 9:2): "I will confess Thee, O Lord, with my whole heart; I will recount all Thy marvelous works."
[^82]: Internal folio cross-reference: *Зрѝ лѝ, б҃* ("See fol. 2", physical leaf p16), referring back to the first two Lord, I have cried stichera (*Let us not pray like the Pharisee* and *A Pharisee, overcome by vainglory*), which are chanted again at the Praises without repetition.

[^83]: The morning Gospel Stichera (Eothina) are normally chanted at the Praises at Sunday Matins. In Studite and early Kyivan practice, during the Triodion and Pentecostarion seasons they are additionally appointed to be chanted during the Litiya in the narthex at Glory... Both now...
[^84]: Domestic dialect (*дїале́ктомъ дома́шнимъ*): Petro Mohyla explicitly prescribes reading the patristic catecheses in the spoken Early Modern Ruthenian vernacular (*prosta mova*), rather than Church Slavonic, so that the monastics and laity might comprehend the ascetical instructions with total clarity.
[^85]: St. Theodore the Studite (759–826), Abbot of the Studios Monastery in Constantinople. His Great and Small Catecheses (*Ѡглаше́нїа*) formed an indispensable part of the monastic Lenten disciplina.
[^86]: Common Troparion of a monastic Father and Teacher of the Church, Tone 8: *Правосла́вїѧ наста́вниче* ("Instructor of Orthodoxy, teacher of piety and purity, light of the universe, divinely-inspired ornament of monastics, all-wise Theodore: by thy teachings thou hast enlightened all, O lyre of the Spirit; entreat Christ God that our souls may be saved").
[^87]: Liturgy lections for the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee: Epistle: 2 Timothy 3:10–15 (Pericope 296); Gospel: Luke 18:10–14 (Pericope 89). Communion Hymn: Psalm 148:1 (LXX Ps. 148:1).
[^88]: Artzivourion (*А҆рцивꙋ́рїевъ*, Armenian *Arachavorats*): A traditional pre-Lenten fast of the Armenian Apostolic Church occurring ten weeks before Easter. Byzantine and Kyivan polemicists vigorously rejected this fast, interpreting it as heretical and instituting a complete dispensation from fasting (fast-free week) for Orthodox Christians in direct opposition to it.
[^89]: The monastic Typikon prescribes partaking of dairy and eggs (*сыръ и҆ ꙗ҆йца̀*) even on Wednesday and Friday at the ninth hour during this week, while the laity eat meat on all days.
[^90]: Meatfare Saturday (Saturday of the Souls / Ancestral Saturday) is dedicated to the universal commemoration of all departed Orthodox Christians. If the Feast of the Meeting of the Lord (February 2) falls on this day, the services must be combined or transferred.
[^91]: Cemetery chapel / church (*гробница*): Kyivan Lavra custom possessed dedicated cemetery sanctuaries where parastases and memorial liturgies could be chanted without displacing festal services in the main Katholikon.
[^92]: Internal folio cross-reference: *ꙗ҆́коже ли́стъ, а҃. ѹ҆каза́ся* ("as indicated on folio 1", physical leaf p15), referring to the order of the All-Night Vigil set forth at the opening of the Triodion.
[^93]: Internal folio cross-reference: *Ли́стъ, г҃.* ("folio 3", physical leaf p17), referring to the detailed rubrical table for the concurrence of the Meeting of the Lord with pre-Lenten Sundays and Saturdays.
[^94]: Commentary Gospel (*Толковое Евангеліе*): The Gospel text accompanied by the patristic commentaries of St. Theophylact of Ohrid or St. John Chrysostom, appointed for monastic refectory and liturgical lectern reading.
[^95]: Luke 15:11–32 (The Parable of the Prodigal Son).
[^96]: St. John Chrysostom, *Homily on the Prodigal Son* (*Слово о блудном сынѣ*), beginning: *Възлю́бленнїи послꙋ́шайте само́гѡ Хрⷭта́* ("Beloved, listen to Christ Himself").
[^97]: Polyeleos (*Мнѡмлⷭтиво*): Psalms 134 and 135 (*Praise ye the name of the Lord* and *Confess unto the Lord, for He is good, for His mercy endures forever*).
[^98]: Psalm 136 (LXX Ps. 136:1–9 / MT Ps. 137:1–9): *By the rivers of Babylon* (*На рѣ́цѣ Вавѷлѡ́нстѣй*), appointed as a third psalm of the Polyeleos on the three Pre-Lenten Sundays (Prodigal Son, Meatfare, Cheesefare) with an elaborate red-letter Alleluia (*съ А҆ллилꙋ́їа кра́сною*).
[^99]: St. Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886), prolific Byzantine hymnographer who composed the Matins canons of the Lenten Triodion for the pre-Lenten Sundays and weekdays.
[^100]: Ode 1 Heirmos: *Moses' song...* (*Мосѵсе́йскꙋю пѣ́снь*). Based upon the First Biblical Canticle, the Song of Moses at the Red Sea (Exod. 15:1–19).
[^101]: Exodus 15:1–2: "Then sang Moses and the children of Israel this song unto the Lord... The Lord is my strength and song, and He is become my salvation: He is my God, and I will prepare Him an habitation."
[^102]: Tone 4 Heirmos incipit: *I shall open my mouth* (*Ѿве́рзꙋ ѹ҆ста̀ моя̀* / Greek: *Anoixō to stoma mou*), the standard model melody for Ode 1 of the Canon to the Theotokos.
[^103]: Ode 3 Heirmos: *My barren mind...* (*Непло́днаго ми ѹ҆ма̀* / Greek: *Steirōthenta mou ton noun*). Based upon the Third Biblical Canticle, the Prayer of Hannah (1 Sam. [1 Kgs.] 2:1–10).
[^104]: Tone 1 podoben incipit: *The soldiers keeping watch* (*Гро́бъ тво́й, Сп҃се* / Greek: *Ton taphon sou, Sōtēr*).
[^105]: Luke 15:18, 21: "Father, I have sinned against heaven, and before thee, and am no more worthy to be called thy son."
[^106]: Ode 4 Heirmos: *Foreseeing Thy birth from the Virgin...* (*Ѿ Дв҃ы твоѐ рожество̀*). Based upon the Fourth Biblical Canticle, the Prayer of Habakkuk (Hab. 3:1–19).
[^107]: Habakkuk 3:2–3 (LXX Hab. 3:2–3): "O Lord, I have heard Thy report, and was afraid: O Lord, I considered Thy works, and was astonished... God shall come from Teman, and the Holy One from the mount of Paran [the mount overshadowed]."
[^108]: Ode 5 Heirmos: *The night having passed...* (*Но́щи преше́дшей* / Greek: *Tēs nyktos parelthousēs*). Based upon the Fifth Biblical Canticle, the Prayer of Isaiah (Isa. 26:9–20).
[^109]: Ode 6 Heirmos: *The depth of transgressions...* (*Глꙋбина̀ съгрѣше́нїй* / Greek: *Vythos hamartēmatōn*). Based upon the Sixth Biblical Canticle, the Prayer of Jonah (Jonah 2:1–10).
[^110]: Alternate Ode 6 Heirmos: *In the depth of sins...* (*Въ глꙋбинѣ̀ грѣхѡ́въ* / Greek: *En vythō ptaismatōn*). Also based upon the Sixth Biblical Canticle (Jonah 2:1–10).
[^111]: Tone 3 podoben incipit: *Today the Virgin* (*Дѣ́ва дне́сь* / Greek: *Hē Parthenos sēmeron*), the famous kontakion melody of St. Romanos the Melodist.
[^112]: Luke 15:18–19: "I will arise and go to my father, and will say unto him, Father, I have sinned against heaven, and before thee, and am no more worthy to be called thy son: make me as one of thy hired servants."
[^113]: The Synaxarion for the Sunday of the Prodigal Son in Petro Mohyla's 1640 Kyiv Triodion is printed in Early Modern Ruthenian (*prosta mova*), translating the Greek text of Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos with pastoral expansions on despair (*отчаяніе*) and the sacraments of Confession, Baptism, and the Holy Eucharist.
[^114]: Ruthenian syllabic verse (*вірші*) preceding the Synaxarion, employing an AABB / rhymed couplet structure (*будь / дверь*).
[^115]: Luke 10:19: "Behold, I give unto you power to tread on serpents and scorpions, and over all the power of the enemy: and nothing shall by any means hurt you."
[^116]: Ode 7 Heirmos: *Likening themselves unto the Cherubim...* (*Херꙋві́мѡмъ подо́бящеся*). Based upon the Seventh Biblical Canticle, the Prayer of Azariah and Song of the Three Holy Children (Dan. 3:26–56).
[^117]: Daniel 3:27–28: "For Thou art righteous in all the things that Thou hast done to us: yea, true are all Thy works, Thy ways are right, and all Thy judgments truth."
[^118]: Ode 8 Heirmos: *In the bush unto Moses...* (*Въ кꙋпи́нѣ Мосѵ́си*). Based upon the Eighth Biblical Canticle, the Praise of the Three Holy Children (Dan. 3:57–88).
[^119]: Exodus 3:2; Daniel 3:57: "Praise the Lord, sing and supremely exalt Him unto all ages."
[^120]: Ode 9 Heirmos: *Who among the earthborn hath heard...* (*Ѡ҆ земны́хъ кто́ слы́ша*). Based upon the Ninth Biblical Canticle, the Magnificat and Benedictus (Luke 1:46–55, 68–79).

[^121]: Tone 2 podoben incipit: *Give ear, ye women* (*Же́ны ѹ҆слы́шите* / Greek: *Gynaikes akoutisthēte*), the standard model melody for Lenten and Paschal exapostilaria composed by St. Theodore the Studite.
[^122]: Luke 15:18–19: "Father, I have sinned against heaven, and before thee, and am no more worthy to be called thy son: make me as one of thy hired servants."
[^123]: Tone 6 idiomelon at the Praises: St. John of Damascus / St. Joseph the Hymnographer, drawing upon the typology of Christ stretching forth His all-pure hands upon the Cross to ransom mankind from the power of the devil (the "cruel beast").
[^124]: Liturgy lections for the Sunday of the Prodigal Son: Epistle: 1 Corinthians 6:12–20 (Pericope 135); Gospel: Luke 15:11–32 (Pericope 79). Communion Hymns: Psalm 148:1 (LXX Ps. 148:1) and Psalm 115:4 (LXX Ps. 115:4 / MT Ps. 116:13).
[^125]: St. Macarius the Great (c. 300–391), *Homily on Fathers and Monastics* (*Слово святаго Макаріа о отцѣхъ и инцѣхъ* / *Homilia de patribus et monachis*), incipit: *Ча́дца моѧ̀, ра́дꙋется срⷣце моѐ васъ ра́ди* ("My little children, my heart rejoices for your sake"), appointed for monastic reading at Matins throughout Meatfare Week.
[^126]: Apodosis (*Отда́ніе*): The liturgical Apodosis of a feast. When the Feast of the Meeting of the Lord (February 2) concurs with pre-Lenten Sundays or weekdays, its post-festal period and apodosis are specially regulated to accommodate the ancestral memorial and fast-preparation services.
[^127]: Internal folio cross-references: *л҃, л҃в* ("folios 30 and 32", physical leaves p48 and p50), indicating the rubrics for Meatfare Saturday and Sunday when the Meeting of the Lord occurs.
[^128]: Narrative woodcut illustration on leaf p44: Commemoration of all departed Orthodox Christians. Depicts the diverse manners of human death: on the left, men falling from cliffs, attacked and devoured by wild beasts, and drowning in turbulent waters; in the center, violent death in war by the sword, falls from heights, and destruction by fire; on the right, peaceful Christian dormition, showing a pious man upon his deathbed attended by a priest vested in epitrachelion administering the Holy Mysteries, with a candle and burial rites. Inscription above the woodcut: *Ѡ҆ ѹ҆со́пшихъ въ бл҃гоче́стїи разли́чна ви́д...* ("Concerning those who have reposed in piety through diverse manners of death").
[^129]: Meatfare Saturday (Saturday of the Souls / Ancestral Saturday): Dedicated to the universal commemoration of all Orthodox Christians who have fallen asleep throughout all ages in piety, particularly those who met unexpected, violent, or unrecorded deaths without the customary funeral rites.
[^130]: Tone 8 podoben incipit: *In Eden of old* (*И҆́же въ є҆де́мѣ* / Greek: *Ho en Edem paradeisos*), the standard model melody for memorial stichera at Vespers.
[^131]: Funeral Idiomelon of St. John of Damascus, Tone 8 (*Пла́чꙋся и҆ рыда́ю* / Greek: *Thrēnō kai odyromai*): "I weep and lament when I contemplate death...", from the Order of the Burial of the Dead (*Погребе́ніе*), incorporated into Meatfare Saturday Vespers and Matins.
[^132]: The memorial Alleluia replaces the festal Prokeimenon at weekday and Saturday memorial services in the Byzantine rite, accompanied by Psalm 64:5 (LXX Ps. 64:5 / MT Ps. 65:4) and Psalm 24:13 (LXX Ps. 24:13 / MT Ps. 25:13).
[^133]: Funeral Idiomelon of St. John of Damascus, Tone 6: *Нача́токъ ми и҆ соста́въ* ("My beginning and my substance was Thy creating command..."), meditating upon Genesis 2:7 (the creation of man from dust and the divine inbreathing of the soul).
[^134]: Memorial Troparion, Tone 8: *Глꙋбино́ю мꙋ́дрости* ("O Thou Who by depth of wisdom dost order all things..."), the universal troparion for Saturday ancestral and memorial services.
[^135]: Cemetery church (*гробница*): Kyivan Lavra custom possessed dedicated cemetery sanctuaries where parastases and memorial liturgies could be chanted without displacing festal services in the main Katholikon.
[^136]: Internal folio cross-references: *л҃н, л҃* ("folios 38 and 30", physical leaves p56 and p48), regulating the concurrence of Meatfare Saturday with the Forefeast of the Meeting or the First and Second Finding of the Head of St. John the Baptist (February 24).
[^137]: Internal folio cross-references: *л҃, з҃і* ("folios 30 and 17", physical leaves p48 and p35), concerning the transfer of memorial services when the Meeting of the Lord coincides with Meatfare Saturday.
[^138]: Internal folio cross-references: *л҃н, к҃д* ("folios 38 and 24", physical leaves p56 and p42), referring to the rubric for the patronal feast of a temple on Meatfare Saturday.
[^139]: St. John Chrysostom, *Homily 41 on 1 Corinthians* (1 Cor. 15 on the resurrection of the dead and the consolation of the departed; physical leaf p72, folio 54).
[^140]: St. John Chrysostom, *Homily on the Departed* (*Слово о усопшихъ*), incipit: *Вѣ́сть мно́гажды сло́во ѹ҆тѣша́ти бо́лѣзнь* ("Word is often wont to console pain"; physical leaf p62, folio 44).
[^141]: St. Andrew of Crete (c. 660–740), homilies and discourses on human mortality and the final judgment (physical leaf p60, folio 42).
[^142]: Gregory the Hieromonk, *Discourse on the Commemoration of the Departed* (*Слово Григорія Іеромонаха о усопшихъ въ вѣрѣ*), incipit: *Ѡ҆ ѹ҆со́пшихъ въ вѣ́рѣ па́мять съверша́юще...*
[^143]: Psalm 118 (LXX Ps. 118 / MT Ps. 119), the 17th Kathisma (The *Amomos* / *Непоро́чны*), divided into two stases with memorial refrains, forming the heart of the Saturday ancestral Matins and the Byzantine funeral rite.
[^144]: Memorial synaxarion / diptychs (*помянники* / *синодики*): The diaconal petition encompasses all historical ranks of ecclesiastical and civil hierarchy, followed by the commemoration of local deceased monastics, founders, and benefactors inscribed in the monastery's necrology books.
[^145]: Evlogitaria of the Departed (*Непоро́чны тропарѝ*, Tone 5): A cycle of funeral troparia chanted with the refrain of Psalm 118:12 (*Blessed art Thou, O Lord, teach me Thy statutes!*), traditionally attributed to St. John of Damascus.
[^146]: Prayer for the Departed (*Моли́тва за ѹ҆со́пшихъ*): "O God of spirits and of all flesh, Who hast trampled down death and overthrown the devil, and given life unto Thy world...", recited by the priest at memorial services.
[^147]: Sessional Hymn for the Departed, Tone 5: *Поко́й Сп҃се на́шъ съ Пра́ведными* ("Give rest, O our Savior, unto Thy servants with the Righteous...").
[^148]: Internal folio cross-reference: *нд҃* ("folio 54", physical leaf p72), pointing to the patristic reading from St. John Chrysostom's Commentary on 1 Corinthians 15.
[^149]: Biblical Canticles (Odes): The scriptural songs appointed to accompany the Matins canon (Exodus 15, Deuteronomy 32, 1 Samuel 2, Habakkuk 3, Isaiah 26, Jonah 2, Daniel 3, and Luke 1). On Meatfare Saturday, the Second Biblical Canticle from Deuteronomy is chanted in the Triodion canon.
[^150]: St. Theodore the Studite (759–826), Abbot of the Studios Monastery in Constantinople, author of the meatfare ancestral canon.
[^151]: Acrostic of the Meatfare Canon: In the Triadica and Theotokia of the canon, the initial letters spell out in Greek / Slavonic: *Недосто́йнаго Ѳео́дѡра* ("Of unworthy Theodore").
[^152]: Ode 1 Heirmos: *Let us, O peoples, sing a song...* (*Пѣ́снь въспѣ́мъ лю́дїе* / Greek: *Asōmen pantes laoi*). Based upon the First Biblical Canticle, the Song of Moses at the Red Sea (Exod. 15:1–19).
[^153]: Internal folio cross-reference: *Л҃ г҃* ("folio 33", physical leaf p51), referring to the Katavasia of the Meatfare Canon.
[^154]: Ode 2 Heirmos: *See, see that I am your God...* (*Ви́дите ви́дите, ꙗ҆́кѡ а҆́зъ є҆́смъ* / Greek: *Idete idete, hoti egō eimi*). Based upon the Second Biblical Canticle, the Song of Moses in Deuteronomy (Deut. 32:1–43). The Second Ode is chanted only during Great Lent and on Meatfare Saturday in the Byzantine rite.
[^155]: Deuteronomy 32:39: "See now that I, even I, am He, and there is no god with Me: I kill, and I make alive; I wound, and I heal: neither is there any that can deliver out of My hand."
[^156]: Ode 3 Heirmos: *O Word of God, Who with Thine own hand didst establish the heavens...* (*Ѹ҆твержде́й рꙋко́ю свое́ю Сло́ве Бж҃їй небеса̀* / Greek: *Ouranias hapsidos* / *Ho stereōsas tē cheiri sou*). Based upon the Third Biblical Canticle, the Prayer of Hannah (1 Sam. [1 Kgs.] 2:1–10) and Psalm 101:26 (LXX Ps. 101:26 / MT Ps. 102:25).

[^157]: Tone 5 podoben incipit: *The Word co-unoriginate* (*Собезнача́льное Сло́во* / Greek: *Ton synanarchon Logon*). The sessional hymn commemorates Christ's death and victory over Hades, pleading for the departed.
[^158]: Patristic reading appointed after the Third Ode of the Canon at Saturday Matins, typically drawn from the funeral discourses of the Holy Fathers (such as St. John Chrysostom or St. Gregory the Theologian).
[^159]: Ode 4 Heirmos: Habakkuk 3:3 (Fourth Biblical Canticle): "God came from Teman, and the Holy One from mount Paran... His glory covered the heavens, and the earth was full of His praise." In Byzantine hymnography, the overshadowing mountain typifies the Mother of God.
[^160]: Ode 5 Heirmos: Isaiah 26:9 (Fifth Biblical Canticle): "With my soul have I desired Thee in the night; yea, with my spirit within me will I seek Thee early: for when Thy judgments are in the earth, the inhabitants of the world will learn righteousness."
[^161]: Ode 6 Heirmos: Jonah 2:1–9 (Sixth Biblical Canticle): Jonah's prayer from the belly of the sea-monster ("Out of the belly of hell cried I, and Thou heardest my voice"), typifying Christ's three-day burial and resurrection, and the soul's cry for deliverance from the pit of corruption.
[^162]: Isaiah 11:1: "And there shall come forth a rod out of the stem of Jesse, and a Branch shall grow out of his roots."
[^163]: The memorial Kontakion, Tone 8 (*Съ святы́ми ѹ҆поко́й* / Greek: *Meta tōn hagiōn anapauson*), chanted at all memorial services and the burial of the dead.
[^164]: The memorial Ikos (*Са́мъ є҆ди́нъ є҆сѝ безсме́ртный* / Greek: *Autos monos ei athanatos*), meditating on Genesis 3:19: "For dust thou art, and unto dust shalt thou return."
[^165]: Synaxarion for Meatfare Saturday: In the 1640 Kyiv edition, this synaxarion is printed in Early Modern Ruthenian (*prosta mova*), reflecting the Kyivan Renaissance and Petro Mohyla's pastoral program of theological vernacularization, translating and expanding the traditional Greek synaxarion attributed to Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (c. 1256–1335).
[^166]: Marginal reference on leaf p55: Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, *Ecclesiastical Hierarchy* (*De Ecclesiastica Hierarchia*), chapter 7, §3: On the mystery over those who have fallen asleep in piety, expounding how the prayers of the priest and the church assist those who lived holily.
[^167]: Marginal reference on leaf p55: St. John of Damascus, *Discourse on Those Who Have Fallen Asleep in the Faith* (*Слово о усопшихъ въ вѣрѣ* / *De iis qui in fide dormierunt*), §10, recounting the apophthegma of St. Macarius the Great and the skull of the pagan priest who testified that when prayers and oblations are offered for the departed, the souls in Hades experience some small refreshment.
[^168]: St. Gregory the Great (the Dialogist, Pope of Rome, c. 540–604) and the ancient tradition of his intercession for the pagan Emperor Trajan, cited by St. John of Damascus in *De iis qui in fide dormierunt*, §16.
[^169]: The intercession of Empress St. Theodora (c. 815–867) and the holy confessors for her iconoclast husband, Emperor Theophilus, commemorated in the Synodikon of Orthodoxy and recounted by St. John of Damascus, *De iis qui in fide dormierunt*, §17.
[^170]: St. Gregory the Theologian (Nazianzen), *Oration 7: Funeral Oration on his Brother Caesarius* (*In laudem Caesarii fratris oratio funebris*, §17), advocating prayers, commemorations, and distribution of goods for the departed.
[^171]: St. John Chrysostom, *Homily 3 on Philippians* (on Phil. 1:21–26; PG 62:203–204), the classical patristic locus on offering the Holy Mysteries and alms for the reposed.
[^172]: The motif of the scales of divine judgment and God's mercy outweighing human sins, derived from the synaxarion tradition and St. Cyril of Alexandria's *Homily on the Departure of the Soul* (*De exitu animi*).
[^173]: Luke 16:19–31: The Parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus, cited by St. John Chrysostom to demonstrate that souls in the intermediate state retain consciousness, memory, and personal recognition of one another across the chasm.
[^174]: St. Gregory the Theologian, *Oration 7 on St. Caesarius* (§21), describing visions of his departed brother in celestial glory.
[^175]: Pseudo-Athanasius, *Quaestiones ad Antiochum ducem* (PG 28:597–700) and the pseudo-Athanasian homily *De iis qui in Christo obierunt* on the state of departed souls.
[^176]: Hebrews 11:40: The patristic doctrine of the intermediate state prior to the General Resurrection and Final Judgment, expounded by the holy Fathers.
[^177]: Psalm 101:25 (LXX Ps. 101:25 / MT Ps. 102:24): "I said: O my God, take me not away in the midst of my days: Thy years are throughout all generations." Cited in the margin of leaf p58 (`Ѱа: ра҃`).
[^178]: Psalm 38:6 (LXX Ps. 38:6 / MT Ps. 39:5) and Psalm 138:16 (LXX Ps. 138:16 / MT Ps. 139:16: "Thine eyes did see my substance, yet being unperfect; and in Thy book all my members were written"). Cited in the margin of leaf p58 (`и҆ рл҃н`).
[^179]: St. Basil the Great, *Homily on the Words: "Attone to Thyself"* (*In illud: Attende tibi ipsi*) and *Homily on Thanksgiving* (PG 31:217–237), examining the span of human life and divine providence.
[^180]: 1 Corinthians 11:30: The Pauline warning concerning unworthy reception of the Holy Mysteries causing weakness, illness, and premature death.
[^181]: Ecclesiasticus (Sirach) 3:6; Ecclesiastes 7:17: "Be not over much wicked, neither be foolish: why shouldest thou die before thy time?"
[^182]: Job 42:7–8: The Lord's rebuke to Eliphaz the Temanite and his two friends, commanding them to offer sacrifice and seek the intercession of Job.
[^183]: St. Athanasius the Great (attributed), *Discourse on the Term of Life* (*Sermo de termino vitae* / PG 28:604–608), examining whether the duration of human life is fixed by an inviolable boundary.
[^184]: The theological doctrine concerning the state of unbaptized infants, derived from St. Gregory the Theologian (*Oration 40: On Holy Baptism*, §23), who teaches that they are neither glorified nor punished by the righteous Judge.
[^185]: The traditional Byzantine physiological and mystical parallelism between the forty-day embryonic formation of man and the forty-day post-mortem dissolution of the body, expounding the liturgical appointments of the 3rd, 9th, and 40th day memorial services (*tritina*, *devyatina*, *sorochyna*).
[^186]: Ode 7 Heirmos: Psalm 101:26 (LXX Ps. 101:26 / MT Ps. 102:25) and Daniel 3:26, 52 (Song of the Three Holy Children / Seventh Biblical Canticle: "Blessed art Thou, O Lord God of our fathers: and to be praised and glorified unto ages").
[^187]: Ode 8 Heirmos: Exodus 3:1–6 (The burning bush upon Mount Horeb) and Daniel 3:56–88 (The Song of the Three Holy Children / Eighth Biblical Canticle: "O all ye works of the Lord, bless ye the Lord: praise and exalt Him above all forever").
[^188]: 1 Corinthians 4:5: "Therefore judge nothing before the time, until the Lord come, who both will bring to light the hidden things of darkness, and will make manifest the counsels of the hearts."
[^189]: Song of Songs (Song of Solomon) 4:12: "A garden enclosed is my sister, my spouse; a spring shut up, a fountain sealed." The traditional Marian type of perpetual virginity and life-giving grace.

[^190]: Ode 9 Heirmos (*Проявленнаго на Горѣ Законодавцу* / *Proyavlyennago na Gorye Zakonodavtsu*). Greek incipit: *Τὸν ἐπὶ Σινᾶ τῷ Μωσεῖ νομοθετήσαντα* (or collated variant *Τὸν ἐν τῇ βάτῳ τῷ Μωσεῖ*). The heirmos links the theophany of the unburnt bush on Mount Sinai (Exod. 3:1–6) with the virginal childbearing of the Mother of God, through which salvation is granted unto the faithful (Luke 1:46–55).

[^191]: The extensive hymnography of Meatfare Saturday is renowned for its exhaustive enumeration of diverse manners of mortal end—lightning, thunderbolts, earthquakes, maritime shipwreck, venomous beasts, violent assault, falls from precipices, war, hanging, and sudden unprovided death—pleading that Christ the righteous Judge extend His merciful rest unto all who departed in Orthodox faith, regardless of circumstance.

[^192]: Exapostilarion (Photogogikon), melody *Небо звѣздами* (*Nebo zvozdami*, "Having adorned heaven with stars"). The Slavonic text reads: *Живыми и мертвыми обладаяй яко Богъ, упокой своя рабы въ селехъ избранныхъ ти...*

[^193]: Praises Podoben model: *Въ Едемѣ рай* (*V Edemye ray*, "In Eden of old paradise" / Greek: *Ἐν Ἐδὲμ Παράδεισος*). The stichera provide a profound memento mori on the frailty of human flesh, anatomical dissolution in the grave, and the vain pursuits of worldly vanity in contrast to eternal life in Christ.

[^194]: Theotokion citation: Ps. 64:5 [LXX 64:5 / MT 65:4]: *Святый храмъ твой дивный въ правду* ("Holy is Thy temple, marvelous in righteousness").

[^195]: Psalm stichos for the departed: Ps. 64:5 [LXX 64:5 / MT 65:4]: *Блажени яже избралъ еси и пріялъ Господи* ("Blessed are they whom Thou hast chosen and taken, O Lord").

[^196]: Psalm stichos for the departed: Ps. 24:13 [LXX 24:13 / MT 25:13]: *Душа ихъ во благихъ водворится* ("Their soul shall dwell among good things").

[^197]: Sticheron of St. John of Damascus (*Дамаскинова творенія*), reflecting upon the primordial catastrophe of the Fall of Adam through tasting the forbidden fruit, the venom of the serpent, the universal entrance of death, and its overthrow by the Incarnate Lord.

[^198]: Internal codex cross-reference: *Зри Листъ, л҃в* ("See fol. 32"), which corresponds to physical leaf p46 of the 1640 Triodion codex, where the troparion *Глубиною мудрости* (*Glubinoyu mudrosti*, "By the depth of wisdom") and theotokion *Тебе градъ* (*Tebe grad*, "Thee, a wall and a haven") are printed in full at Vespers.

[^199]: The liturgical lectionary readings for Meatfare Saturday: Epistle of the day: 1 Cor. 6:12–20 (Pericope 146); Epistle for the departed: 1 Thess. 4:13–17 (Pericope 270); Gospel of the day: Luke 21:8–9, 25–27, 33–36 (Pericope 105); Gospel for the departed: John 5:24–30 (Pericope 16).

[^200]: Rubrical invariant: The entire funeral and memorial order of Meatfare Saturday is repeated without alteration on Trinity Saturday (the Saturday preceding Holy Pentecost), constituting the two universal ancestral Saturdays of the Byzantine liturgical year.

[^201]: Full-page-width woodcut engraving of the Dread Judgment (*Страшный Судъ*), executed in the Kyivan Pechersk Lavra graphic atelier. It depicts the apocalyptic vision of the Second Coming of Christ (Matt. 25:31–46; Dan. 7:9–14; Rev. 20:11–15): Christ enthroned in glory within an almond-shaped mandorla flanked by the Mother of God, St. John the Baptist, and angels rolling up the heavens; the Etimasia with the open Book of Life and Adam and Eve prostrate; the Twelve Apostles seated as assessors; the weighing of souls in the balances; the fiery river streaming from the judgment-seat into the maw of Hades; the earth and sea resurrecting their dead at the angelic trumpets; and the welcoming of the righteous into Paradise.

[^202]: Melodic model: Tone 6, *Все возложивше* (*Vse vozlozhivshe*, "Having laid aside all earthly hope" / Greek: *Ὅλην ἀποθέμενοι*).

[^203]: Internal cross-reference: *Листъ г҃* ("See fol. 3"), which corresponds to physical leaf p17 of the codex, detailing the concurrence rubrics for the Forefeast and Afterfeast of the Meeting of the Lord (Candlemas, Feb 2).

[^204]: Glory sticheron Tone 8 (*Егда поставятся Престоли* / *Egda postavyatsya Prestoli*), directly invoking the prophetic throne-vision of the Ancient of Days and the court of judgment from Daniel 7:9–10.

[^205]: Litiya Idiomelon Tone 6, paraphrasing the six corporal works of mercy specified by Christ in the Eschatological Discourse (Matt. 25:35–40) as the sovereign criterion of judgment.

[^206]: Aposticha Glory Tone 8 (*Увы мрачная душе* / *Uvy mrachnaya dushe*), an impassioned ascetical lament addressed to the soul to shake off torpor and despondency before the inevitable arrival of physical death and divine accounting.

[^207]: Series of internal codex cross-references for rubrical concurrences: fol. 30 corresponds to physical leaf p44; fol. 3 corresponds to physical leaf p17; fol. 4 corresponds to physical leaf p18; and fol. 24 corresponds to physical leaf p38.

[^208]: Detailed rubrical chapter governing the concurrence when the First and Second Finding of the Precious Head of Saint John the Baptist (Feb 24) coincides with Meatfare Sunday or Cheesefare Sunday, harmonizing the Triodion hymnography with the Menaion feast of the Forerunner.

[^209]: Patristic homilies appointed for the Matins vigil: Saint Gregory the Theologian's *Oration 14: On Love for the Poor* (*De pauperum amore* / *Слово о нищихъ*), and Saint John Chrysostom's exhortations on the Second Coming, the Dread Judgment, and repentance.

[^210]: The Canon of Meatfare Sunday is a monumental work of Saint Theodore the Studite (759–826), structured upon the biblical Canticle of Moses (Exod. 15:1–19), meditating upon the terrors of the general judgment, the opening of the books of conscience, and the separation of the sheep and the goats.

[^211]: Ode 3 Heirmos (*Утверди Господи на камени недвижимом* / *Utverdi Hospodi na kameni nedvizhimom*). Greek incipit: *Στερέωσον Κύριε, ἐπὶ τὴν ἄσειστον πέτραν*. Structured upon the biblical Canticle of Hannah (1 Kings 2:2 [LXX 1 Reg. 2:2 / 1 Sam. 2:2]: "There is none holy as the Lord, and none righteous as our God").

[^212]: Sessional Hymn (Sessional Hymn) Tone 4, Melodic model: *Гробъ твой* (*Hrob tvoy*, "Thy tomb, O Savior" / Greek: *Τὸν τάφον σου Σωτήρ*). The hymn pleads for deliverance from the goats on the left hand and placement with the sheep on the right hand at the Dread Judgment (Matt. 25:32–33).

[^213]: Sessional Hymn Tone 6: *Помышляю день страшный* (*Pomyshlyayu den strashny*, "I reflect upon that dreadful day"). An impassioned ascetical meditation on mortal accountability before the Holy Trinity.

[^214]: Rubric for the patristic reading (*И чтенїе*) appointed after the Sessional hymns of Ode 3, drawn from the *Commentary Gospel* (*Учительное Евангелїе*) or the homilies of St. Ephrem the Syrian on the Dread Judgment and repentance.

[^215]: Ode 4 Heirmos (*Услыша Пророкъ пришествїе твое Господи* / *Uslysha Prorok prishestviye tvoye Hospodi*). Greek incipit: *Ἀκήκοεν ὁ Προφήτης*. Structured upon the biblical Canticle of Habakkuk (Hab. 3:2 [LXX]: "O Lord, I heard Thy report, and was afraid: O Lord, I considered Thy works, and was amazed").

[^216]: Triadikon of Ode 4: The marginal annotation *Зрѝ* ("Mark!") draws attention to the orthodox patristic formulation of the hypostatic properties: the Son begotten of the Father (*рожественнѣ, зане и рожденїе*), and the Holy Spirit proceeding from the Father (*происходнѣ, зане и схожденїе*), emphasizing the single monarchical source of the Godhead in the Father against the Western *Filioque* addition.

[^217]: Ode 5 Heirmos (*Из нощи утреннюща человеколюбче* / *Iz noshchi utrennyushcha chelovyekolyubche*). Greek incipit: *Ἐκ νυκτὸς ὀρθρίζοντα φιλάνθρωπε*. Structured upon the biblical Canticle of Isaiah (Isa. 26:9: "From night until morning my spirit seeks Thee, O God, for Thy light is upon the earth").

[^218]: Ode 6 Heirmos (*Возопихъ всѣмъ сердцемъ моимъ* / *Vozopikh vsim sertsem moyim*). Greek incipit: *Ἐβόησα ἐν ὅλῃ καρδίᾳ μου*. Structured upon the biblical Canticle of Jonah (Jonah 2:3, 7 [LXX]: "I cried by reason of mine affliction unto the Lord, and He heard me; out of the belly of hell cried I, and Thou heardest my voice").

[^219]: The Kontakion of Meatfare Sunday (*Егда прїидеши Боже на землю съ славою* / *Egda priydeshi Bozhe na zemlyu s slavoyu*), composed in Tone 1 by Saint Romanos the Melodist (fl. 6th c.), depicting the apocalyptic scene of the general resurrection, the river of fire, and the opening of the books of conscience (Dan. 7:9–10; Matt. 25:31–46).

[^220]: The Ikos of Meatfare Sunday (*Страшное судище твое помышляя* / *Strashnoye sudishche tvoye pomyshlyaya*), expanding upon the self-condemnation of the soul confronted by the incorruptible truth of Christ the Judge.

[^221]: Synaxarion on the Sunday of Meatfare, commemorating the Dread Second and Impartial Coming of our Lord Jesus Christ. In the 1640 Kyiv Triodion, Petro Mohyla's atelier printed the Synaxaria not in Church Slavonic, but in Early Modern Ruthenian (*prosta mova*), translating the Greek text of Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos (c. 1256–1335) into elevated theological prose.

[^222]: Marginal Scriptural citation: Mark 13:32 ("But of that day and that hour knows no man, no, not the angels which are in heaven, neither the Son, but the Father").

[^223]: Traditional Byzantine-Slavic cosmic chronography reckoning seven thousand years from the Creation of the World (Anno Mundi 5508 BC) according to the Septuagint chronology, viewing the seventh millennium (reached in AD 1492 / AM 7000) as the threshold of the eschatological age.

[^224]: Patristic reference to Saint Hippolytus of Rome (c. 170–235), *Treatise on Christ and Antichrist* (*De Christo et Antichristo*, ch. 14–19), linking the lineage of Antichrist to the tribe of Dan based upon Gen. 49:17 ("Dan shall be a serpent by the way, an adder in the path").

[^225]: Marginal Scriptural citation: 2 Thess. 2:3, 8–9 ("And then shall that Wicked be revealed, whom the Lord shall consume with the spirit of His mouth... even him, whose coming is after the working of Satan with all power and signs and lying wonders").

[^226]: Marginal Patristic citation: Saint John of Damascus, *An Exact Exposition of the Orthodox Faith* (*De Fide Orthodoxa*, Book IV, ch. 26: "Concerning the Antichrist"), explaining that not the devil himself becomes man, but a man receives all the energy of Satan.

[^227]: Marginal Scriptural citation: Daniel 12:11–12, collated with Revelation 11:3–7 regarding the two apocalyptic witnesses, identified by unanimous patristic tradition as Enoch and Elijah.

[^228]: Marginal Scriptural citation: Matthew 24:22 ("And except those days should be shortened, there should no flesh be saved: but for the elect's sake those days shall be shortened").

[^229]: Cluster of marginal eschatological citations in the 1640 edition: Matt. 24:27, 29–30; Mark 13:24–26; Luke 21:25–27; 1 Cor. 15:52; 1 Thess. 4:16; Rev. 20:10–15.

[^230]: Ancient theological-topographical tradition locating the general judgment in the Valley of Jehoshaphat at Jerusalem (Joel 3:2, 12), venerating Jerusalem as the mystical center of the earth (Ps. 73:12 [LXX 73:12 / MT 74:12]: "God is our King before the ages; He hath wrought salvation in the midst of the earth").

[^231]: Concluding theological synthesis of the Kyivan Synaxarion: The Kingdom of Heaven is defined as eternal co-dwelling (*сполмешканє*) and perpetual illumination with God, whereas hell is defined fundamentally as estrangement from God (*ѿ Бога ѿдаленєся*) and eternal remorse of conscience.

[^232]: Ode 7 Heirmos (*Согрѣшихомъ, и беззаконовахомъ* / *Sohryeshikhom, i bezzakonovakhom*). Greek incipit: *Ἡμάρτομεν, ἠνομήσαμεν*. Structured upon the Prayer of the Three Holy Youths in the Babylonian furnace (Dan. 3:29–30, 52 [LXX]: "We have sinned, we have transgressed... blessed art Thou, O Lord God of our fathers").

[^233]: Triadikon of Ode 7: Classical Cappadocian trinitarian hymn affirming one divine essence (*едино существомъ*), three Hypostases (*три в лицѣхъ*), and a single indivisible divine power, will, and operation (*едина сила, хотѣнїе, и дѣйство*).

[^234]: Ode 8 Heirmos (*Егоже воинства небесная славят* / *Yego zhe voinstva nebesnaya slavyat*). Greek incipit: *Ὃν στρατιαὶ οὐρανῶν δοξάζουσι*. Structured upon the biblical Canticle of the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian furnace (Dan. 3:57–88 [LXX]: "O all ye works of the Lord, bless ye the Lord: praise and exalt Him above all forever").

[^235]: Eschatological troparia of Ode 8, meditating upon the unquenchable fire (*негасимый огнь*), the gnawing worm (*червь скрежещущій*), and the separation of souls at the dread tribunal (Mark 9:44, 48; Matt. 25:31–46).

[^236]: Triadikon of Ode 8: Classical Cappadocian patristic synthesis professing the unity of the divine essence (*единаго Бога по существу чту*) while distinguishing the three Hypostases (*три составы пою опредѣлителнѣ*) as one single Godhead in three Persons (*бж҃тво едино въ трїехъ лицѣхъ*).

[^237]: Ode 9 Heirmos (*Безсѣменна зачатія рожество несказанное* / *Bezsemyenna zachatiya rozhestvo neskazannoye*). Greek incipit: *Ἀσπόρου συλλήψεως ὁ τόκος ἄφραστος*. A classical Theotokion-heirmos celebrating the virginal conception and birth of Christ as the cosmic renewal of human nature (Luke 1:48).

[^238]: Troparia of Ode 9 invoking Moses trembling when he beheld the back parts of God on Mount Sinai (Exod. 33:20–23; Heb. 12:21) and Daniel filled with dread before the fiery tribunal of the Ancient of Days (Dan. 7:9–15, 28).

[^239]: Triadikon of Ode 9: An emphatic monarchical confession of the Holy Trinity characteristic of the Kyivan Triodion, invoking the Father as Sole Begetter of the Only-begotten Son, the Son as Radiance of the Light, and the Holy Spirit as proceeding solely from the One God (*единый единѣ единаго Бога ст҃ый Дш҃е*), excluding the Western *Filioque* interpolation.

[^240]: Sunday Exapostilaria of Meatfare: Two sequential hymns set to the classic Resurrectional automela: the first to *Соученикомъ* (*Τοῖς μαθηταῖς συνέλθωμεν* / "Let us assemble with the disciples"), and the second to *Жены услышите* (*Γυναῖκες ἀκουτίσθητε* / "Give ear, ye women"), focusing the mind upon the dread separation of sheep and goats at the Second Coming (Matt. 25:31–46; Mal. 3:2; 4:1).

[^241]: Tone 6 Idiomelon Sticheron at the Praises (*Помышляю день оный и часъ* / Greek: *Ἐννοῶ τὴν ἡμέραν ἐκείνην*), depicting the general resurrection where all arise in a single age (*возрастомъ единемъ*), reflecting ancient patristic eschatology concerning the spiritual perfection of the resurrected body (cf. St. Augustine, *De Civitate Dei* XXII.14; Eph. 4:13).

[^242]: Tone 6 Idiomelon Sticheron at the Praises (*О каковый часъ тогда и день страшный* / Greek: *Ὢ ποία ὥρα τότε*), drawing upon the apocalyptic imagery of Daniel 7:9–10 and Revelation 20:11–15, where the books of conscience are opened and all human thoughts and intents are laid bare before the heavenly hosts.

[^243]: Tone 8 Sticheron at the Praises (*Даніилъ Пророкъ, мужъ желаній бывъ* / Greek: *Δανιὴλ ὁ Προφήτης, ἀνὴρ ἐπιθυμιῶν γενόμενος*), linking physical fasting directly with active fraternal charity and mercy, strictly warning that bodily abstinence without the cessation of judging one's neighbor is futile and leads to condemnation (Dan. 7:10; 9:23; 10:11; Rom. 14:10).

[^244]: Tone 1 Sticheron at the Praises (*Предочистимъ себе братіе, Царицею добродѣтелій* / Greek: *Προκαθάρωμεν ἑαυτοὺς ἀδελφοί, τῇ βασιλίδι τῶν ἀρετῶν*), hailing Fasting as the "queen of virtues" and urging the faithful to enter upon the pre-Lenten discipline with joy in expectation of the Paschal Resurrection.

[^245]: Typikon rubric regulating the concurrence of the Feast of the Meeting of the Lord (Hypapante / *Стрѣтеніе*, Feb. 2) with Meatfare or Cheesefare Sunday. The chronological note *якоже бысть лѣтъ, ҂а҃* ("as occurred in year 1000" / A.D. 1640) directly situates this edition within the pontificate of Metropolitan Petro Mohyla.

[^246]: Homiletic rubric prescribing patristic readings at the monastic table (Trapeza) from the festal collection, specifically the *Sermon of Palladius the Monk* (*Слово Палладія Монаха*, incipit: *Инѣ исповѣждься душе* / "Now make thy confession, O soul"), an ancient ascetical discourse on repentance and the Last Judgment.

[^247]: Vespers of Meatfare Sunday: The appointed Tone 8 Idiomelon of the Day (*Ласкосердствовавше, первую подяхомъ наготу* / Greek: *Τρυφῆς μετασχόντες*), contrasting the belly-indulgence that led to the expulsion of Adam from Paradise with the ascetical fasting that prepares the Christian soul to partake of the Paschal Lamb on the night of the Holy Resurrection (Gen. 3:6–7; Heb. 13:9).

[^248]: The rubric heading *Пѡлка Сѵрнагѡ Тѵпїка* (The Order/Chapters of the Cheesefare Typikon) introduces the liturgical regulations for Cheesefare Week (White Week / Butter Week), during which meat is prohibited but dairy products, eggs, and cheese are permitted as a step-down discipline prior to the Great Fast.

[^249]: Comprehensive Kyivan Typikon rubrics detailing the liturgical order when the Feast of the Meeting of the Lord (*Стрѣтеніе*, Feb. 2) or its Afterfeast falls on the weekdays of Cheesefare Week, governing the distribution of the Triodion, Menaion, and Festal hymnody across Small Vespers, Great Vespers, Compline, Matins, and the Divine Liturgy.

[^250]: Typikon rubrics regulating the celebration of the First and Second Finding of the Precious Head of Saint John the Baptist (*Обрѣтеніе честныя Главы Іоанна Предтечи*, Feb. 24) when it coincides with Monday, Tuesday, or Thursday of Cheesefare Week, detailing the hymns of the Octoechos, Triodion, and Menaion.

[^251]: Cross-reference in body rubric: `зри Листъ, к҃д` (see fol. 24), referencing folio 24 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p48), which sets forth the general Typikon statutes and fasting regulations for the Triodion period.

[^252]: Homiletic rubrics for Matins of Cheesefare Monday prescribing the reading of two ascetic works of Saint Basil the Great: *On Giving Heed to Thyself* (*In illud: Attende tibi ipsi*, PG 31:197–218; incipit: *Слова потребу даде намъ создавый насъ Богъ*) and *On the Renunciation of the World and on Spiritual Perfection* (*De renuntiatione saeculi*, PG 31:625–648; incipit: *Пріидѣте къ мнѣ вси труждающіися*).

[^253]: The hymnography of Cheesefare Monday at Matins comprises two Tri-odes: the first composed by Saint Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886) in Tone 2, and the second composed by Saint Theodore the Studite (759–826) in Tone 1, greeting Cheesefare Week as the bright vestibule and spiritual springtime of the Holy Forty Days.

[^254]: Typikon chapter 46 (*Ѡ Ѿда́нїи Стрѣ́тенїа. Глава̀, м҃ѕ* / On the Apodosis of the Meeting of the Lord, Chapter 46), regulating the Apodosis (*Ѡтданїе*) of the Feast of the Meeting of the Lord (Hypapante, Feb. 2) when the feast falls on Wednesday, Thursday, Friday, or Saturday of Meatfare Week, in which case its Apodosis is appointed for Cheesefare Tuesday, setting forth the complete order of Vespers, Matins, and Divine Liturgy.

[^255]: Marginal cross-reference in body rubric: `зри Ли́стъ, к҃д` (see fol. 24), referencing folio 24 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p48), which sets forth the general Typikon tables, liturgical order, and fasting regulations for the Triodion period.

[^256]: Internal cross-reference in body rubric: `Ѡ се́мъ ли́стъ, г҃. и҆ л҃` (see fol. 3 and fol. 30), referencing folio 3 (physical leaf p27) and folio 30 (physical leaf p54) of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion, regulating the transfer of commemorated daily saints' services from the Menaion to Compline during the Triodion period.

[^257]: Sessional Hymn model *Tone 8, Podoben: "Of Wisdom and the Word" (Премꙋ́дрость и҆ Сло́во)*, based on the classical Byzantine automelon *Τὴν Σοφίαν καὶ Λόγον* composed by Saint Theodore the Studite or Saint John of Damascus, celebrating the divine Logos Who created all things visible and invisible and guiding the soul into compunction for the radiant season of the Fast.

[^258]: Homiletic rubrics for Matins of Cheesefare Tuesday prescribing the reading of Saint Basil the Great's *Homily on Thanksgiving* (*De gratiarum actione / Ѡ єѵхаристїи*, PG 31:217–237) or *On Fasting* (*De jejunio*, PG 31:163–198; incipit: *Мл҃итеся моли́теся людїе мои́* / "Pray, pray, O my people"), referencing folio 84 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p108 / Cheesefare Wednesday).

[^259]: Internal cross-reference in body rubric: `ли́стъ, ѻ҃е` (see fol. 75), referencing folio 75 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p99), governing the chanting of the canons of the Octoechos and Menaion throughout the weekdays of Cheesefare Week.

[^260]: Internal cross-reference in body rubric: `Ли́стъ, ѻ҃г` (see fol. 73), referencing folio 73 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p97), directing that if the Meeting of the Lord falls on Cheesefare Tuesday, the Tuesday Tri-ode is transferred to Cheesefare Wednesday at Compline.

[^261]: The hymnography of Cheesefare Tuesday at Matins comprises two Tri-odes: the first composed by Saint Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886) in Tone 3, and the second composed by Saint Theodore the Studite (759–826) in Tone 2, greeting the portals of the Holy Fast and employing the biblical imagery of rain and dew upon the tender grass (Deut. 32:2) as figures of compunction and spiritual renewal.

[^262]: Internal cross-reference in body rubric: `Ли́стъ, ѻ҃в` (see fol. 72), referencing folio 72 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p96), containing the psalm verses appointed at the Aposticha of Cheesefare Monday (Ps. 122:1, 3 [LXX 122:1, 3 / MT 123:1, 3]: "Unto Thee have I lifted up mine eyes..." and "Have mercy on us, O Lord, have mercy on us...").

[^263]: The Stavrotheotokion at the Aposticha of Cheesefare Tuesday evening (*А҆́гница нескве́рнаѧ* / Greek: *Ἡ Ἀμνὰς ἡ ἄχραντος*), set to the Tone 1 prosomoion *Прехва́лныи мч҃ннци* (*Πανεύφημοι μάρτυρες* / "O all-lauded martyrs"), depicting the Virgin Mother at the foot of the Cross, beholding her Son and Lord lifted up upon the Tree of Crucifixion as one condemned (John 19:25).

[^264]: Technical liturgical rubric *Methimon* (*МЕѲИМѠ́НЪ*), derived from the Greek refrain of Great Compline *Μεθ' ἡμῶν ὁ Θεός* ("God is with us", Isa. 8:8–10), designating the full monastic office of Great Compline (*Повече́рїе вели́кое*) celebrated with deep prostrations (*покло́ны вели́кїи*) and the Prayer of Saint Ephrem the Syrian (*Го́споди и҆ Влады́ко живота̀ моегѡ̀*).

[^265]: Typikon chapter 44 (*Ѡ҆ Препра́зденствѣ Стрѣ́тенїа* / On the Forefeast of the Meeting of the Lord, Chapter 44, noted in the margin as `Гла: д҃: ан.` / Chapter 44), regulating the liturgical order when the Forefeast of the Meeting of the Lord (Hypapante, Feb. 1) coincides with Wednesday or Friday of Cheesefare Week, governing the distribution of the Triodion, Menaion, and Festal hymnody.

[^266]: Internal cross-reference in body rubric: `лн҃, ѻ҃г` (see fol. 73), referencing folio 73 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p97), which sets forth the complete Typikon order for the Feast of the Meeting of the Lord (Hypapante, Feb. 2) when it coincides with Cheesefare Friday.

[^267]: Marginal cross-reference in body rubric: `л҃ ѻ҃г` (see fol. 73), referencing folio 73 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p97), directing that the Afterfeast of the Meeting of the Lord falling on Cheesefare Wednesday or Friday follows the general festal order appointed for Cheesefare Monday.

[^268]: Marginal cross-reference in body rubric: `* ли, п҃д` (see fol. 84), referencing folio 84 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p108), regulating the appointment of three great prostrations (*по трѝ покло́ны вели́кихъ*) at the conclusion of all canonical hours and liturgical offices throughout Cheesefare Week.

[^269]: Typikon chapter 54 (*Ѡ Ѡ҆брѣ́тенїи Главы̀ Прⷮтчи. Глава̀, н҃д. ѡ҆ по́лꙋ* / On the Finding of the Head of the Forerunner, Chapter 54, in the middle [i.e. folio 54 verso]), regulating the First and Second Finding of the Honorable Head of the Holy Prophet, Forerunner, and Baptist John (Feb. 24) when the feast falls on Cheesefare Wednesday or Friday.

[^270]: Internal cross-reference in body rubric: `лн҃, ѻ҃д` (see fol. 74), referencing folio 74 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p98), detailing the complete order of Stichera, readings, and Liturgy for a major saint's feast coinciding with Cheesefare Friday.

[^271]: Marginal cross-reference in body rubric: `л҃ ѻ҃д` (see fol. 74), referencing folio 74 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p98), governing the omission of the Canon to the Mother of God and the precedence of the Forerunner's Canon combined with the Tri-ode.

[^272]: Internal cross-reference in body rubric: `лн҃, п҃и` (see fol. 78), referencing folio 78 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p102), regulating the mitigation of prostrations to three great metanias when celebrating a Saint with a Troparion or a Temple Patronal feast on Cheesefare Wednesday.

[^273]: Patristic homiletic reading appointed for Matins of Cheesefare Wednesday: Saint Basil the Great's *First Homily on Fasting* (*De jejunio homilia I*, PG 31:163–184) or *Second Homily on Fasting* (*De jejunio homilia II*, PG 31:185–198; incipit: *Оу҆тѣша́йтеся, ᲂу҆тѣша́йтеся* / "Comfort ye, comfort ye, My people", citing Isa. 40:1).

[^274]: The first troparion of Ode 1 in the Canon of Saint Andrew of Crete (*Пода́ся гла́дꙋющимъ* / Greek: *Προσῆλθε τῷ πειράζοντι*), recalling the forty-day fast of the Lord in the wilderness and His triumph over the devil's temptations to food, vainglory, and worldly dominion (Matt. 4:1–11; Luke 4:1–13) as a model of ascetical warfare for the faithful.

[^275]: Martyrikon Kontakion (*Конда́къ мꙋ́чениченъ*): *И҆́же и҆с сѣ́ти* (Greek: *Ὁ ἐκ δικτύου / Ὁ ἐκ παγίδος*), referencing the ancient hymnographic genre honoring the Holy Martyrs who delivered their souls from the snare of the enemy and inherited crowns of incorruption.

[^276]: Textual witness in the 1640 Kyiv Triodion: `къ ᲂу҆стремле́нїемъ бл҃говѣ́тны пребыва́й` in the Matins Aposticha Idiomelon (`Ѡ҆ бра́шенъ постѧ́щисѧ дш҃е моѧ̀...`). The Greek original reads `πρὸς τὰς ὁρμὰς τῶν παθῶν ἀνδρίζου` ("stand manfully against the impulses of passions"), which the Kyivan text renders with an elevated call to steadfast spiritual vigilance.

[^277]: The ascetic Catecheses (*Оглашения*) of Saint Theodore the Studite (*Ѡ҆глаше́нїа Стꙋди́това*, PG 99:503–688) appointed to be read during the First Hour on weekdays throughout Great Lent according to the ancient Studite and Sabbaite monastic Typika.

[^278]: Rubrical instruction for the Ecclesiarch (*Є҆кклисїа́рхъ*) and Canonarch (*Канаона́рхъ*) regulating the precise liturgical protocol for chanting the Sixth Hour Troparion and announcing the tone of the Old Testament Paroimia Prokimena without prefacing them with the standard liturgical titles.

[^279]: Ancient canonical and liturgical decree rooted in the Palestinian Sabbaite tradition, prohibiting the celebration of both the full Divine Liturgy and the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts on Wednesday and Friday of Cheesefare Week because full aliturgical fasting discipline begins on these days.

[^280]: Internal cross-reference in body rubric: `Ли́стъ, п҃ѕ. и҃ пн.` (see fol. 86 and 88), referencing folios 86 and 88 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaves p100 and p102), regulating the order of "Alleluia" and prostrations at Matins and Vespers.

[^281]: Marginal cross-reference in body rubric: `Зри, ли́ст, п҃ѕ.` (see fol. 86), referencing folio 86 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p100), directing that meals be taken only once in the day on Cheesefare Wednesday and Friday even when a feast coincides.

[^282]: Patristic conciliar decree authorizing monastics and faithful to eat cheese, milk, and eggs once a day after Vespers (at the 4th hour of the afternoon / 10th hour of the natural day) on Cheesefare Wednesday and Friday as an intentional liturgical protest against the dualistic and Encratite heresies of the Sabellians, Andusanites, and the Monophysite Armenian fast of Artzivur.

[^283]: Marginal cross-reference in body rubric: `Зри * (л҃ з҃і)` (see fol. 37), referencing folio 37 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p51), containing the extended historical-theological excursus refuting the Armenian fast of Artzivur.

[^284]: Canonical ruling of Saint Nicephorus the Confessor, Patriarch of Constantinople (PG 100:1064), commanding monastics to observe strict fast until Vespers on Cheesefare Wednesday and Friday and then consume cheese and eggs, explicitly overturning the Monophysite Jacobite decree and the Tetradite heresy which imposed total fasts denying the goodness of God's material creation.

[^285]: Marginal cross-reference in body rubric: `Зрѝ, ли́стъ, н҃е.` (see fol. 55), referencing folio 55 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p69), governing the combined order of the Resurrection, Triodion, and Hierarchs' canons when the Feast of the Three Holy Hierarchs coincides with Meatfare Sunday.

[^286]: Marginal cross-reference in body rubric: `лн, п҃.` (see fol. 80), referencing folio 80 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p94), detailing the Apodosis of the Meeting of the Lord (Hypapante, Feb. 2) when transferred to Cheesefare Thursday.

[^287]: Marginal cross-reference in body rubric: `ли́стъ, ѻ҃д.` (see fol. 74), referencing folio 74 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p88), directing the order of the Finding of the Precious Head of Saint John the Baptist when it coincides with Cheesefare Thursday.

[^288]: Internal cross-reference in body rubric: `Лн҃, ѻ҃е.` (see fol. 75), referencing folio 75 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p89), specifying the general weekday structure of Cheesefare Matins.

[^289]: Patristic reading from the Lenten Sobornik: the Homily of Saint Anastasius, Abbot of Mount Sinai (*In psalmum sextum*, PG 89:1077–1116) appointed for Matins of Cheesefare Thursday.

[^290]: Marginal cross-reference in body rubric: `л҃ п҃ѕ.` (see fol. 86), referencing folio 86 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p100), governing the rule of combining Octoechos, Menaion, and Triodion canons when the Tri-ode has two heirmoi.

[^291]: The second Heirmos of Ode 4 (`Прише́лъ є҆сѝ ѿ дв҃ы`, Greek: `Ἐλήλυθας ἐκ Παρθένου`) is appointed from the ancient Byzantine Triodion canon of Saint Joseph the Hymnographer for Cheesefare Thursday.

[^292]: The penitential prayer alludes to the Prayer of Manasseh, King of Judah (2 Chron. 33; Ode 12 of the liturgical Psalter), a central patristic paradigm of contrition appointed throughout the services of Great Lent.

[^293]: The second Heirmos of Ode 8 (`ᲊстраши́сѧ ѿроко́въ чⷭ҇тыхъ`, Greek: `Ἔπτηξε παίδων εὐαγῶν`) commemorates the Three Holy Youths in the Babylonian furnace (Dan. 3), whose bodily incorruption through ascetic abstinence typifies the spiritual victory of the Fast.

[^294]: Typological allusion to the murder of Abel by Cain (Gen. 4:3–8), contrasted in Lenten hymnography with the sincere and blameless spiritual offering of bodily mortification and prayer.

[^295]: Kyivan theological rendering: `по ᲂу҆поста́си` (hypostatically / according to hypostasis), articulating Chalcedonian and Damascene Christology regarding the substantial union of divine and human natures in the incarnate Word.

[^296]: The second Heirmos of Ode 9 (`Невъмѣсти́магѡ Бг҃а`, Greek: `Τὸν ἀχώρητον Θεόν`), celebrating the divine maternity of the Theotokos whose womb contained the uncontainable Word.

[^297]: In the 1640 Kyiv Triodion, the rubric reads: `Свѣти́ленъ, Гла́съ, И҆, Сла́ва въ вы́шнихъ:`, omitting the specific Tone number of the Exapostilarion before the Lesser Doxology (*Gloria in excelsis*).

[^298]: Internal cross-reference in body rubric: `ꙗ҆́коже и҆ вы́ше въ вто́ сы́рный ᲂу҆каза́сѧ` (see fol. 88), referencing folio 88 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p102), which governs the prostrations and liturgical structure of Cheesefare weekday Vespers.

[^299]: Internal cross-reference in body rubric: `И҆ про́: лн҃, п҃ѕ.` (see fol. 86), referencing folio 86 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p100), directing the completion of the service and the order of prostrations at the Dismissal.

[^300]: Internal cross-reference in body rubric: `И҆ тꙋ ᲂу҆ка́ Пн҃: в Срѐ: Сы́рну.` (see fol. 78), referencing folio 78 of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion (physical leaf p92), regulating the rubrics for when the Feast of the Meeting of the Lord or the Finding of the Head of Saint John the Baptist coincides with an aliturgical weekday of Cheesefare Week.

[^301]: Patristic readings appointed for Matins of Cheesefare Friday: Saint Basil the Great's *De Jejunio*, Homilia I (*In illud: Clama ne cesses... Canite in initio mensis tuba*, PG 31:163–184) and his ascetic work *Sermo asceticus* (*Quomodo monacho se gerere oporteat*, PG 31:619–626).

[^302]: Liturgical rule governing the synthesis of the Octoechos, Menaion, and Triodion canons: when the Triodion appoints a double-heirmos Tri-ode (such as those by Saint Joseph the Hymnographer), the corresponding Odes from the Octoechos and the Menaion are entirely omitted to accommodate the twofold heirmological structure.

[^303]: The Heirmos of Ode 5 (`Вскꙋ́ю мѧ ѿри́нꙋлъ є҆сѝ`, Greek: `Ἵνα τί με ἀπώσω`), drawn from Psalm 87:15 (LXX) and Isaiah 26:9, expressing the soul's alienation from God through sin and its longing for restoration through the light of divine commandments.

[^304]: Scriptural allusion to the forty-day fast of the Prophet Moses on Mount Sinai (Exod. 24:18, 34:28) as the biblical prototype of the contemplative vision of God (*theoria*) attained through ascetic purification.

[^305]: Strict Nicene-Constantinopolitan and Damascene Trinitarian formula: `сла́вимъ въ є҆ди́номъ є҆стствѣ̀... и҆ раздѣлѧ́юще ли́цы, несмѣ́снѡ непрело́жнѡ слꙋжа́ще` ("we glorify in one essence... and distinguishing the Persons, serving without confusion and without change"), refuting both Sabellian modalism and Arian polytheism.

[^306]: Matins of Cheesefare Friday appoints the Sessional Hymn for the Martyrs (*Martyrion*) from the Octoechos in Tone 6 following Ode 6 of the Canon.

[^307]: The ascetical fast of the Holy Prophet Elijah on Mount Horeb (3 Kgs. 19:8 [LXX] / 1 Kings 19:8) and his fiery ascent (4 Kgs. 2:11 [LXX] / 2 Kings 2:11) are invoked throughout the Lenten Triodion as the supreme biblical paradigm of ascetic purification granting divine vision.

[^308]: Typological inversion of the Babylonian furnace (Dan. 3) and the parable of Dives and Lazarus (Luke 16:19–31): the fire of penitential tears willingly endured during the Fast consumes carnal passions and delivers the faithful from the unquenchable fire of Gehenna.

[^309]: The Heirmos of the Tri-ode for Cheesefare Friday (`По зако́нꙋ ѻ҆́тчꙋ`, Greek: `Νόμοις πατρῴοις`) was composed by Saint Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886), one of the principal Byzantine melodists of the Triodion.

[^310]: Scriptural citation of Ephesians 2:14: `граде́жа средостѣ́нїе разорн́выи` ("having broken down the middle wall of partition between us"), uniting heaven and earth and inaugurating the spiritual peace of the Fast.

[^311]: Strict Cappadocian Trinitarian theology articulates the personal (hypostatic) incommunicability within the consubstantial Trinity: `ни Ѻ҆ц҃ꙋ̀ и҆зстꙋпи́вшꙋ въ Сн҃а, нижѐ Сн҃ꙋ премѣ́ншивсѧ въ и҆схожде́нїе` ("the Father not having passed over into the Son, nor the Son having changed into the procession [proper to the Holy Spirit]"), excluding both Sabellian modalism and subordinationism.

[^312]: The second Heirmos of Ode 9 (`Недоꙋмѣ́етъ всѧ́къ ꙗ҆зы́къ`, Greek: `Ἀπορεῖ πᾶσα γλῶσσα`), drawn from the Ninth Ode of the Theotokos Canon, acknowledging the inadequacy of all created human and angelic language to articulate the mystery of the Incarnation.

[^313]: Patristic typological parallelism: the wound in Christ's side opened by the soldier's spear upon the Cross (John 19:34) heals the primal defeat and mortality that issued from Adam's rib with the creation and transgression of Eve (Gen. 2:21–22, 3:6).

[^314]: On the aliturgical weekdays of Cheesefare Week (Wednesday and Friday), the Divine Liturgy is not celebrated; instead, the Lenten Hours (Third, Sixth, and Ninth) are chanted with the penitential prostrations and the Prayer of Saint Ephrem the Syrian, serving as a pedagogical bridge and vestibule into Great Lent.

[^315]: The Sixth Hour reading from Zechariah 8:7–17 prophesies the eschatological gathering and restoration of Jerusalem from the east and the west, demanding righteous judgment, truth, peace, and the forsaking of deceit in preparation for the spiritual fast.

[^316]: The Ninth Hour is joined immediately to the Sixth Hour and Typika on Cheesefare Friday, chanted with deep bows and the Prayer of Saint Ephrem the Syrian in accordance with the Studite-monastic rule codified in Metropolitan Petro Mohyla's 1640 edition.
