[^317]: Internal cross-reference in body rubric: `зри ли́стъ, л҃. и҆ р҃д.` (see fol. 30 and 104), referencing folio 30 (physical leaf p44) and folio 104 (physical leaf p118) of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion, which govern the rubrics for combining the services of the Triodion with commemorations of Saints from the Menaion during Cheesefare Week.

[^318]: Internal cross-reference in body rubric: `Ра́звѣ Срѣ́тенїѧ прⷣз: и҆ прⷭ҇тн. и҆ли С҃: В҃: є҆́же є҆ хрⷱ҇а, зрⷱ҇, л҃. і҃.` (see fol. 30 and 10), referencing folio 30 (physical leaf p44) and folio 10 (physical leaf p24) of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion, regulating the feast of the Meeting of the Lord (*Hypapante*, February 2) or the temple patronal feast when coinciding with Cheesefare Friday.

[^319]: The full-page horizontal woodcut illustration heading Cheesefare Saturday in the 1640 Kyiv Triodion depicts the founders of monasticism: Saint Paul of Thebes, Saint Anthony the Great, Saint Agathon, and Saint Pachomius the Great, alongside the local Ukrainian-Rus' monastic patrons: Saint Nicetas of the Caves, Saint Anthony of the Caves, and Saint Theodosius of the Caves holding the architectural model of the Kyiv-Pechersk Dormition Lavra.

[^320]: The universal Commemoration of all the Holy Ascetic Monks and Nuns (*Synaxis of All Venerable Fathers in Asceticism*) on Cheesefare Saturday was instituted to inspire the faithful by displaying the multitude of saints who conquered passions through fasting before entering the arena of Great Lent.

[^321]: At Friday Vespers of Cheesefare Saturday, the first Kathisma of the Psalter ("Blessed is the man", Ps. 1–8) is appointed, marking the festal transition into the Saturday commemoration.

[^322]: Commemoration of the primordial geographical centers of early Christian anchoritic and coenobitic monasticism in the 4th century: Egypt, Libya, and the Upper Egyptian desert of the Thebaid.

[^323]: The theological phrase `Є҆́же по ѡ҆́бразꙋ, съблю́дше невре́димо... въ є҆́же по подо́бїю` (Greek: `Τὸ κατ' εἰκόνα τηρήσαντες ἀλώβητον... εἰς τὸ καθ' ὁμοίωσιν`) embodies the patristic doctrine of Genesis 1:26: man receives the divine image (*imago Dei*) by creation as an ontological endowment, but achieves the divine likeness (*similitudo Dei*) through the free ascetic struggle of virtue and deification (*theosis*).

[^324]: The theological phrase `зерца́ломъ разоре́ннымъ` (Greek: `τῶν ἐσόπτρων λυθέντων`, cf. 1 Cor. 13:12: "For now we see in a mirror, obscurely; but then face to face") denotes the eschatological transition from the indirect reflection of divine realities in mirrors (*speculum*) to direct, immediate contemplative vision (*visio beatifica*) in heaven.

[^325]: The rubric `Сꙋбѡ́тѣ тихїи` directs the choir to chant the quiet Dogmatic Theotokion appointed for Saturday Vespers according to the current Tone of the Octoechos.

[^326]: Zechariah 8:19. The four fasts of the Old Covenant commemorative of national calamities (the breach of Jerusalem's walls, the burning of the Temple, the assassination of Gedaliah, and the beginning of the siege) are transformed eschatologically into joyous feasts. In the Triodion typikon, this Old Testament pericope is read on Friday evening of Cheesefare Week to sanctify the liturgical fasts of the Church as seasons of spiritual joy, truth, and peace.

[^327]: The invocation `Мт҃и ст҃ая неизрече́ннагѡ свѣ́та...` is a specific dismissal megalynarion from the ancient Studite-Jerusalem tradition linked with the monastic reading of Saint John Climacus' *Ladder of Divine Ascent* (*Lestvitsa*).

[^328]: Internal codex cross-reference: `Листъ, р҃д` refers to physical leaf 104 in the 1640 Kyiv Triodion.

[^329]: The 16 great prostrations accompanied by the Prayer of St. Ephrem the Syrian appointed at the conclusion of Cheesefare Friday Vespers, sealing the Lenten liturgical discipline before the Saturday Sabbath commemoration.

[^330]: Physical leaf 104 cross-reference regarding the fasting rule of Cheesefare Wednesday and Friday (monastic xerophagy / strict fasting without formal midday cooked table).

[^331]: Abba Dorotheus of Gaza (6th c.), *Discourse on the Holy Fast* (*Doctrina XV: De sancta quadragesima*), traditional monastic refectory reading at the threshold of Great Lent.

[^332]: `[въ Гро́бници]` / `в Гробницѣ`: in the monastic crypt or chapel of the dead, where Friday evening compline canons for departed brethren were chanted.

[^333]: Chapter 46 of the Markian rubrical apparatus governing the rare coincidence of the Great Feast of the Meeting of the Lord (Hypapante, February 2) with Cheesefare Saturday.

[^334]: Chapter 47 of the Triodion Typikon regulates the Apodosis of the Meeting of the Lord when February 2 falls on the preceding Tuesday or Wednesday of Cheesefare, requiring full harmonization with the Commemoration of the Holy Ascetic Fathers on Saturday.

[^335]: Chapter 48 coordinates the celebration of the First and Second Finding of the Honorable Head of the Holy Prophet, Forerunner, and Baptist John (February 24) when it falls on Cheesefare Saturday.

[^336]: The liturgical practice of reading the regular Lenten Saturday Epistle and Gospel at the Hours (or joined together at Liturgy) when eclipsed by a major festal commemoration.

[^337]: Canonical Podoben model incipit: `Повелѣ́нное та́йнѡ` (Greek: `Τὸ προσταχθὲν μυστικῶς`).

[^338]: Canonical Podoben model incipit: `Свирѣ́лей па́стырскихъ` (Greek: `Αὐλῶν ποιμενικῶν`).

[^339]: Saint Ephrem the Syrian, *Three Homilies on the Holy Ascetics and Departed Fathers*, patristic reading appointed at Matins of Cheesefare Saturday.

[^340]: Saint Anastasius of Sinai (7th c.), *Encomium on the Holy Fathers and Ascetics* (incipit: `Τί τοῦτο σήμερον ἀγαπητοὶ μετὰ σπουδῆς...`).

[^341]: The first Biblical Canticle of Moses (Exod. 15:1–19), whose incipit `Гдⷭ҇еви пои́мъ` ("Let us sing unto the Lord") forms the basis of Ode 1.

[^342]: St. Theodore the Studite (759–826), abbot of the Studion Monastery in Constantinople and principal hymnographer of the Lenten Triodion. This expansive canon forms an alphabetical acrostic across the desert fathers and mothers.

[^343]: The universal scope of 4th–7th century desert monasticism commemorated on Cheesefare Saturday, focusing on the Egyptian desert, Upper Egypt (Thebaid), Libya, and Palestine.

[^344]: Saint Anthony the Great (c. 251–356), the "Father of All Monks" and founder of Christian anchoritic monasticism.

[^345]: Saint Amoun of Nitria (c. 294–356), founder of the great monastic community in the Nitrian Desert.

[^346]: Saint Arsenius the Great (c. 350–445), Roman imperial tutor who retired to Scetis under the divine call to *fuge, tace, quiesce* ("flee, be silent, pray in hesychia").

[^347]: Saint Ammonas of Egypt (4th c.), disciple and successor of Anthony the Great in Pispir and spiritual bishop.

[^348]: Saint Agathon of Scetis (4th c.), renowned in the *Apophthegmata Patrum* for profound humility, meekness, and discernment.

[^349]: Saint Achillas of Scetis (4th c.), desert elder celebrated for severe fasting and non-judgment.

[^350]: Saint Amoes (Ammoi) of the Nitrian desert (4th c.), disciple of Abba Isaiah and companion of Achillas.

[^351]: Saint Anoub (Anoub the Confessor, 4th c.), brother of Abba Poemen in Scetis and exemplary silent ascetic.

[^352]: Saint Alonius of Scetis (5th c.), desert father famous for saying: "Unless a man say in his heart, 'I alone and God are in the world,' he will not find rest."

[^353]: Saint Amonatha (Ammonatha), hermit of the Egyptian Kellia.

[^354]: Saint Indes, Egyptian solitary and ascetic commemorated in early Byzantine menologies.

[^355]: Saint Ares (Arisius), ascetic of the Nitrian monastic communities.

[^356]: Saint Apollos of the Thebaid (4th c.), leader of over five hundred monks in Hermopolis.

[^357]: Saint Adras (Athre/Adre), renowned in the Desert Fathers for absolute, unquestioning obedience.

[^358]: Saint Acacius of Sinai (6th c.), commemorated by John Climacus in the *Ladder* (Step 4) for heroic enduring obedience under a severe elder.

[^359]: Saint Cyris (Kyris/Cyrus) of Alexandria or Scetis, famed anchorite.

[^360]: Saint Auxentius of Mount Oxia (5th c., d. 470), monastic confessor of the Council of Chalcedon.

[^361]: Saint Abraham the Great of Kidunja (d. c. 360), solitary and hermit in Mesopotamia.

[^362]: Saint Aphrodisius, early ascetic father in Egypt.

[^363]: Saint Athenodorus, disciple of St. Pachomius or hermit in Upper Egypt.

[^364]: Saint Ammonius the Tall (c. 345–403), one of the four famous "Tall Brothers" of Nitria, scholar and ascetic.

[^365]: Saint Aninas (Ananias) the Wonderworker of the Euphrates (6th c.).

[^366]: Saint Antiochus of Palestine (7th c.), monk of Mar Saba and author of the *Pandects of the Holy Scriptures*.

[^367]: Saint Agapitus the Wonderworker, bishop of Synnada (4th c.).

[^368]: Saint Athanasius the Athonite (c. 920–1000), founder of the Great Lavra on Mount Athos. His inclusion in this canon demonstrates the Kyivan Triodion's integration of the Athonite monastic typology.

[^369]: The Second Canticle of Moses (Deuteronomy 32:1–43, "Give ear, O heavens, and I will speak..."), appointed only during Great Lent on Tuesdays, and here preserved on Cheesefare Saturday in the ancient Studite hymnographic layout.

[^370]: Saint Bessarion of Egypt (4th–5th c.), famed in the *Sayings of the Desert Fathers* for living like a bird of the air without roof or possessions, wandering forty days and nights amid thorns in prayer.

[^371]: Saint Isidore of Pelusium (c. 360–c. 435) or Isidore the Priest of Scetis, celebrated as a "new Job" for enduring afflictions with unyielding patience.

[^372]: Saint Benjamin of the Nitrian desert (d. c. 392), who endured dropsy for eight months before his repose, continually giving thanks.

[^373]: Saint Vitalis of Gaza (d. c. 625), who secretly redeemed prostitutes in Alexandria by hiring them for the night to protect them from sin while he spent the entire night praying for their conversion.

[^374]: Saint Euthymius the Great (377–473), master of the Judean desert monasteries.

[^375]: Saint Babylas the Ascetic or the Hieromartyr of Antioch, venerated among ascetic patrons.

[^376]: Saint Benedict of Nursia (c. 480–547), patriarch of Western monasticism and author of the *Holy Rule*. His inclusion in the Byzantine-Kyivan Triodion underscores the pre-schism ecumenical communion of saints.

[^377]: Saint Julian the Shepherd (Julian Sabas, 4th c.), hermit of Mesopotamia and the Sinai wilderness.

[^378]: Saint Basil the Hesychast or Basil of Cilicia, ascetic commemorated for living in a tomb in strict mortification.

[^379]: Saint Gelasius of Nilopolis in Egypt (5th c.), abbot celebrated in the *Sayings of the Desert Fathers* for total detachment.

[^380]: Saint Gerasimus of the Jordan (d. 475), founder of the Jordan Lavra, famously accompanied by a wild lion that served him in obedience.

[^381]: Saint Germanus and Saint Gaius, early desert ascetics in Egypt and Syria.

[^382]: Saint David of Thessalonica (c. 450–c. 535), tree-dwelling ascetic (*dendrite*) who lived for three years in an almond tree outside the city walls. The Slavonic `Дѣ́дъ` is a scribal corruption or folk Ruthenian approximation for `Давї́дъ`.

[^383]: Saint Daniel the Stylite (409–493), who lived for thirty-three years on a pillar near Constantinople.

[^384]: Saint Daniel of Scetis (6th c.), desert father and witness of early monastic sanctity.

[^385]: Saint Dius the Wonderworker (5th c.) and Saint Dalmatius (4th–5th c.), monastic archimandrites of Constantinople and staunch defenders of Orthodoxy against Nestorianism at the Council of Ephesus.

[^386]: Saint Dometian, bishop of Melitene (d. 601), and Saint Dometius of Persia (4th c.), monk and martyr.

[^387]: Saint Bezmenitius (Besarion/Viminatius), early ascetic commemorated in Eastern menologies.

[^388]: The theological formulation `Дш҃е ѿ Ѻ҆ц҃а̀ и҆сходя́й, Сн҃ом же ꙗ҆вля́емый` (Greek: `ἐκ Πατρὸς ἐκπορευόμενον, καὶ δι' Υἱοῦ πεφηνός`) represents the classical Patristic and John Damascene expression of the eternal procession of the Holy Spirit from the Father alone, manifested or made known through the Son (*per Filium*).

[^389]: Saint Eudocius (Eudoxius), fourth-century ascetic; Saint Helladius the Hieromartyr or ascetic solitary; Saint Ephrem the Syrian (c. 306–373), deacon of Nisibis and "harp of the Holy Spirit"; and Saint Eulogius the Stone-cutter (or Eulogius the disciple of John Chrysostom), celebrated for hospitality and ascetical mortification.

[^390]: Saint Zosimas of Palestine (c. 460–c. 560), elder of the Jordan monastery who met Saint Mary of Egypt; Saint Zacharias of Scetis, son of Carion, renowned for profound inner silence; Saint Zenon of Gaza (fourth century), disciple of Silvanus; Saint Zoilus; Saint Isaiah of Scetis (d. 491), author of the *Ascetic Discourses*; and Saint Elijah the Solitary of Egypt.

[^391]: Saint Theodore of Pherme (fourth century), prominent desert father in the *Apophthegmata Patrum*; Saint Ennathas (Ennaton), monk of the famous monastic federation at the Ninth Mile outside Alexandria; Saint Theodulus and Saint Theonas of the Nitrian desert; and Saint Theodosius the Cenobiarch (c. 423–529), founder of organized communal monasticism in the Judean desert.

[^392]: Saint Theoctistus (d. 467), co-laborer with Euthymius the Great in the Judean wilderness; Saint Thalassius of Libya (seventh century), hesychast and author on love and self-control included in the *Philokalia*; and Saint Theodore the Sykeote (d. 613), bishop of Anastasiopolis, famed for extraordinary wonderworking and exorcisms.

[^393]: Saint John the Hut-dweller (Kalyvites, fifth century), who lived unrecognized in a wretched hut outside his parents' palace in Constantinople in ascetic concealment; and Saint John Climacus (c. 579–649), abbot of Mount Sinai and author of *The Ladder of Divine Ascent*, commemorated here alongside his principal monastic companions and commentators.

[^394]: The theological triadikon `Тр҃це є҆диносꙋ́щная... раздѣле́нїемъ съединенїе и҆ проти́въ ли́цы є҆стство̀ и҆мꙋ́щая` reflects the classical Cappadocian formulation of the divine monarchy: God is one in nature and essence (*ousia*), yet distinct in three Hypostases (*prosopa*), having unity in distinction and distinction in unity without division or confusion.

[^395]: Classical Sessional Hymn model *Tone 8, Podoben: "Of Wisdom and the Word" (Премꙋ́дрости)* (Greek: *Τὴν Σοφίαν καὶ Λόγον*), commemorating Saint Anthony the Great (c. 251–356), Saint Paul of Thebes (c. 227–341), Saint Arsenius the Great (c. 350–445), Saint Theoctistus, and Saint Euphrasia the Virgin of Tabennisi (c. 380–410).

[^396]: Saint Hilarion the Great (291–371), disciple of Anthony and father of Palestinian monasticism; Saint Justin the Philosopher and Martyr (c. 100–165) or Justin the Ascetic; Saint Erasmus of Antioch; Saint Hesychius of Jerusalem (or Hesychius of Gaza, disciple of Hilarion); and Saint Joseph the Solitary.

[^397]: Saint Peter the Athonite (d. c. 734), the first known anchorite on Mount Athos, who dwelled in strict isolation on the holy mountain for fifty-three years; and the Emperor John (or Leo the Wise / Justinian), famed for royal ascetic patronage.

[^398]: Saint Jeremiah the Hermit; Saint Ischyrion of Scetis; Saint Charis; Saint Coprius of Palestine (fourth century); Saint Clistor; and Saint John Cassian the Roman (c. 360–435), who brought the monastic wisdom of the Egyptian desert fathers to the West in his *Institutes* and *Conferences*.

[^399]: Saint Callistus, Patriarch of Constantinople and hesychast author; Saint Laurence of Egypt; Saint Longinus the Anchorite of Cilicia; and Saint Leontius the Hermit.

[^400]: Saint Leontius of Byzantium (or Leontius the Theologian); Saint Maximus the Confessor (c. 580–662), doctor of the Church and heroic confessor against Monothelitism; Saint Marcian the Priest of Constantinople (fifth century); and Saint Mark the Ascetic (fifth century), abbot in Ancyra and spiritual writer.

[^401]: Saint Macarius the Great of Egypt (c. 300–391) and Saint Macarius the City-dweller (of Alexandria, d. c. 395); Saint Mark the Zealous (disciple of Silvanus); and Saint Moses the Ethiopian (the Eunuch / Black, c. 330–405), bandit chieftain turned desert father and martyr in Scetis.

[^402]: Saint Martinian of Caesarea (fourth century), who endured severe trials in the desert to preserve chastity; Saint Malchus of Syria (fourth century), captive monk whose life was recorded by Saint Jerome; Saint Marcellus the Akimete (d. c. 485), abbot of the "Unsleeping" monastics in Constantinople; and Saint Miles (Milos), bishop and miracle-worker in Persia.

[^403]: Saint Nilus of Sinai (d. c. 430), disciple of Chrysostom and ascetical writer; Saint Nicon the "Metanoite" or Nicon the Steersman; Saint Nathanael of the Nitrian desert (fourth century); and Saint Nidersus (Nidertius), early Egyptian solitary.

[^404]: Saint Xenophon, his wife Maria, and their sons John and Arcadius (fifth century), Roman senatorial family who embraced monastic poverty in Jerusalem; Saint Orsisius (Horsiesius, c. 400–c. 480), disciple and successor of Saint Pachomius; Saint Onuphrius the Great (fourth century), hermit who lived sixty years in total solitude in the wilderness of the Thebaid; and Saint Poemen the Great (c. 340–450), chief shepherd and spiritual guide of Scetis.

[^405]: Saint Pambo of Nitria (c. 303–c. 374), revered for silence, ceaseless labor, and weeping over the fallen; Saint Publius of Syria (fourth century), founder of two monastic communities; and Saint Pinnufrius of Egypt (fourth century), who repeatedly fled his own abbacy to labor as an unknown novice in other monasteries.

[^406]: Saint Paphnutius the Great (Bishop of the Upper Thebaid, confessor of the First Council of Nicaea) or Paphnutius the Ascetic; Saint Pisora; Saint Patermuthius (fourth century), convert and ascetic miracle-worker; Saint Paul the Simple (fourth century), disciple of Anthony celebrated for extreme obedience; and Saint Pityrun (Pityrion) of the Thebaid, successor of Anthony at Pispir.

[^407]: Saint Pachomius the Great (c. 292–348), founder of Christian cenobitic monasticism in the Thebaid; Saint Palamon (d. c. 330), elder and teacher of Pachomius; Saint Petronius (d. 346), beloved disciple and short-lived successor of Pachomius; and Saint Polychronius (Plicyarison), early ascetic of Syria or Egypt.

[^408]: In the ancient Studite-Sabbaitic hymnographic structure preserved in the 1640 Kyiv Triodion, Ode 6 functions as a climactic gathering of monastic wonderworkers and charismatic healers prior to the Kontakion and Synaxarion.

[^409]: Saint Rabbula of Edessa (d. 435), ascetic bishop; Saint Rufus of the Nitrian desert; Saint Sioes (Sisoes) the Great (d. 429), desert father of Scetis and Pispir renowned for absolute humility; Saint Sarapion (Serapion the Sindonite, fourth century), itinerant monk who owned only a single linen garment; and Saint Silvanus of Gaza (fourth century), master of twelve disciples.

[^410]: The hymnographer commemorates the four prominent holy fathers named Simeon: the three great pillar-dwellers—Saint Simeon the Stylite (the Elder, c. 390–459), Saint Simeon the Stylite of the Marvelous Mountain (the Younger, 521–596), and Saint Simeon the Third Stylite (sixth century)—together with Saint Simeon the Holy Fool for Christ of Emesa (sixth century).

[^411]: Saint Sabbas the Sanctified (439–532), founder of the Great Lavra in the Judean desert and author of the archetypal Jerusalem monastic Typikon; Saint Serapion of Thmuis; and Saint Silvanus.

[^412]: Commemoration of desert elders: Saint Dalmatius; Saint Timothy; Saint Tithoes of the Thebaid; Saint Hyperechius, author of ascetic admonitions; Saint Pharmuthius; Saint Phocas the Solitary; Saint Chariton the Confessor (d. c. 350), founder of the Pharan, Douka, and Souka Lavras in Palestine; Saint Chaeremon of Nitria (fourth century); Saint Psoes; and Saint Hor (Or) of the Thebaid (fourth century).

[^413]: Canonical Kontakion model *Tone 8, Podoben: "As first-fruits of nature" (Ꙗ҆́кѡ нача́тки)* (Greek: *Ὡς ἀπαρχὰς τῆς φύσεως*), attributed to Romanos the Melodist, sealing the collective monastic commemoration of Cheesefare Saturday before the onset of Great Lent.

[^414]: The Cheesefare Saturday Synaxarion in the 1640 Kyiv Triodion is composed in Early Modern Ruthenian (*prosta mova*) under the direct editorial sponsorship of Metropolitan Petro Mohyla. Combining Byzantine synaxarion motifs with contemporary seventeenth-century Ruthenian literary devices, it presents the ascetic fathers and mothers as exemplars of holy warfare to prepare the faithful for the spiritual combat of Great Lent.

[^415]: A distinct Baroque Ruthenian military trope introduced into the Kyivan liturgical print: the hymnographic compiler compares the Holy Fathers to Cossack Hetmans (*Гетма́нове*) who inspect, array, and embolden their regiments with military orations and exemplars of past heroic commanders before leading them into battle.

[^416]: The Ruthenian loanword `конте́рфектъ` derives from the German-Polish Baroque artistic and rhetorical term *konterfekt* (from Latin *contrafactum*), signifying an exact, lifelike portrait, model, or moral mirror.

[^417]: A widespread medieval Byzantine liturgical etiology attributing the relaxation of Cheesefare Week (abstinence from meat while permitting dairy and eggs) to an imperial vow made by Emperor Heraclius (r. 610–641) during his six-year campaign against King Khosrow II and the Sasanian Persian Empire. The Kyivan editor balances this historical legend by affirming that the Holy Fathers pastoral-ascetically devised this gradual transition to train the human body for strict fasting.

[^418]: Ode 7 marks a systematic thematic transition in the Studite canon: having commemorated the male anchorites and cenobiarchs in Odes 1–6, the hymnography dedicates Ode 7 exclusively to holy ascetic women, desert mothers (*ammas*), and chaste monastics.

[^419]: Saint Bryene (Bryaena) of Nisibis (fourth century), abbess and aunt of Saint Febronia; Saint Febronia the Virgin-martyr of Nisibis (d. c. 304); Saint Theomais; Saint Hieria the Ascetic; Saint Platonida of Nisibis (fourth century); and Saint Melandrosia.

[^420]: Saint Euphrasia the Elder and Saint Euphrasia the Younger (d. 410) of Tabennisi; Saint Theodora of Alexandria (fifth century), who lived disguised as a monk in repentance; Saint Theodora of Thessalonica; Saint Anastasia the Patrician (sixth century), Byzantine court lady who fled to Scetis and lived as the eunuch "Anastasius"; and Saint Anastasia the Virgin-martyr.

[^421]: Saint Mary of Egypt (fifth–sixth century), premier penitent of the Jordan wilderness, whose full Life is appended to this 1640 Triodion (see fols. 270r–284v); Saint Marina (Marinus, fifth century), who dwelled disguised as a monk and patiently bore the slander of fathering a child; and Saint Euphrosyne of Alexandria (fifth century), who lived disguised as the monk Smaragdus.

[^422]: Saint Theodula of Anazarbus; Saint Theodota and Saint Julitta; and Saint Isidora the Simple of Tabennisi (fourth century), fool for Christ who served in the monastery kitchen with a dishcloth wrapped around her head, described in the *Lausiac History* of Palladius.

[^423]: Saint Marina the Great-martyr; Saint Matrona of Perge (fifth century), abbess of Constantinople; Saint Syncletica of Alexandria (fourth century), desert mother and teacher of discernment; Saint Sarah of the Desert (fifth century), who lived sixty years by the Nile battling demons of lust; and Saint Justina the Virgin-martyr of Antioch.

[^424]: Saint Pelagia of Antioch (Pelagia the Penitent, fifth century), former actress who converted and lived as a hermit on the Mount of Olives; and Saint Thais of Egypt (fourth century), courtesan converted by Saint Paphnutius who died in penitential reclusion.

[^425]: Ode 8 widens the scope of the universal commemoration from desert monastics to the pastoral doctors, hierarchs, and theological defenders of the Orthodox faith throughout the Ecumenical Councils.

[^426]: The three great Cappadocian and Alexandrian doctors: Saint Basil the Great (c. 330–379), archbishop of Caesarea in Cappadocia; Saint Athanasius the Great (c. 296–373), patriarch of Alexandria and champion of Nicaea; and Saint Gregory the Theologian (of Nazianzus, c. 329–390), patriarch of Constantinople.

[^427]: Saint John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), archbishop of Constantinople; Saint Cyril of Alexandria (c. 376–444) and Saint Cyril of Jerusalem (c. 313–386); Saint Hesychius of Jerusalem (fifth century), presbyter and exegete; and Saint Meletius, patriarch of Antioch (d. 381), president of the Second Ecumenical Council.

[^428]: Saint Gregory of Nyssa (c. 335–c. 395), younger brother of Basil the Great; the two wonderworking fathers (Saint Gregory Thaumaturgus of Neocaesarea and Saint Gregory of Agrigento); Saint Epiphanius, bishop of Salamis in Cyprus (c. 315–403); and Saint Amphilochius, bishop of Iconium (c. 340–c. 395).

[^429]: The illustrious succession of bishops of Constantinople: Saint Metrophanes (d. 326), Saint Nectarius (d. 397), Saint Atticus (d. 425), Saint Gennadius (d. 471), Saint Anatolius (d. 458), Saint Eusebius, and Saint Proclus (d. 446).

[^430]: Saint Nicholas the Wonderworker, archbishop of Myra in Lycia (c. 270–343); Saint Sophronius, patriarch of Jerusalem (c. 560–638), author of the *Life of Saint Mary of Egypt*; Saint Eulalius of Antioch; Saint Diadochus, bishop of Photice (fifth century), master of spiritual sobriety and hesychia; Saint Eustathius, patriarch of Antioch (d. c. 337); and Saint Juvenal, first patriarch of Jerusalem (d. 458).

[^431]: Saint Ambrose, bishop of Milan (c. 339–397), famed for fearless episcopal boldness before Emperor Theodosius; Saint Hierotheus, first bishop of Athens (first century); and the holy patriarchs named Alexander: Saint Alexander of Alexandria (d. 326), champion against Arius, and Saint Alexander of Constantinople (d. 337).

[^432]: Saint Phaedimus, bishop of Amasea (third century); Saint Spyridon the Wonderworker, bishop of Trimythous (c. 270–348); Saint Antipatros, bishop of Bostra (fifth century); Saint Pambo; Saint Palladius, bishop of Helenopolis (c. 363–c. 430), author of the *Lausiac History*; Saint Nonnus, bishop of Edessa; Saint Jerome of Stridon (c. 347–420), biblical translator and monk of Bethlehem; and Saint Germanus I, patriarch of Constantinople (c. 634–c. 733).

[^433]: Saint Dionysius the Areopagite, disciple of Paul and mystical theologian; Saint Clement, Pope of Rome (first century) or Saint Clement of Ancyra (d. 312); Saint Flavian, patriarch of Constantinople (d. 449), martyr of the Robber Synod of Ephesus; and Saint Paul the Confessor, patriarch of Constantinople (d. c. 350).

[^434]: The champions of Orthodox iconoduly against Byzantine Iconoclasm: Saint Michael the Confessor, bishop of Synnada (d. 826); Saint Tarasius, patriarch of Constantinople (d. 806), convener of the Seventh Ecumenical Council (Nicaea II); Saint Nicephorus the Confessor, patriarch of Constantinople (758–828); Saint Theodore the Studite (759–826); and Saint Theophanes the Confessor (c. 759–c. 818), chronicler and abbot of Mount Sigriane.

[^435]: Ode 9 continues the commemorations of the ancient fathers and martyrs: Saint Peter of Alexandria (d. 311), hieromartyr; Saint Ignatius of Antioch (the Theophoros, d. c. 107), disciple of the Apostles; Saint Polycarp of Smyrna (d. c. 155), disciple of Saint John the Theologian; and Saint Cyprian of Carthage (d. 258), bishop and martyr.

[^436]: Tone 2 Exapostilarion (Exaposteilarion) melodic model: *Give ear, ye women* (Slavonic: *Жены ᲂу҆слы́шите*; Greek: *Γυναῖκες ἀκουτίσθητε*).

[^437]: Tone 2 Exapostilarion melodic model: *Let us assemble with the disciples* (Slavonic: *Съ ᲂу҆чн҃ки взы́иди*; Greek: *Τοῖς μαθηταῖς συνέλθωμεν* / *Σὺν μαθηταῖς ἀνέλθωμεν*).

[^438]: Tone 8 Sticheron melodic model at the Praises: *In Eden of old* (Slavonic: *Въ є҆де́мѣ ра́и*; Greek: *Ἐν Ἐδὲμ παραδείσῳ*).

[^439]: Doxasticon at the Praises, Tone 6: the phrase "into all the earth went out the sound of your righteous deeds" (*во всю̀ зе́млю и҆зы́иде вѣща́ні́е и҆справле́ні́й ва́шихъ*) reflects Greek *εἰς πᾶσαν τὴν γῆν ἐξῆλθεν ὁ φθόγγος τῶν κατορθωμάτων ὑμῶν*, citing Psalm 18:5 (LXX) and Romans 10:18.

[^440]: Typikon rubric governing Saturday Liturgy readings: on all Saturdays from the Pre-Lenten Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee through Great Lent until the Sunday of All Saints, the Epistle and Gospel of the continuous cycle (*рядовый*) take precedence over the commemoration of the Saint of the day.

[^441]: Liturgy pericopes for Cheesefare Saturday: Epistle of the day: Romans 14:19–23, 16:25–27 (pericope 115); Epistle of the Venerable Fathers: Hebrews 13:17–21 (pericope 335); Gospel of the day: Matthew 6:1–13 (pericope 16); Gospel of the Venerable Fathers: Luke 6:17–23 (pericope 24).

[^442]: Tone 6 Sticheron melodic model at "Lord, I have cried": *Having laid aside all earthly hope* (Slavonic: *Всѐ ᲂу҆пова́ні́е возло́жше*; Greek: *Ὅλην ἀποθέμενοι*).

[^443]: The "garment woven by God" (*Бг҃отка́нныя*) refers to the primordial robe of uncreated light and glory bestowed upon Adam before the Fall, contrasted with the garments of skin (*ко́жными ри́зами*) given after the transgression (Genesis 3:21).

[^444]: The famous Doxasticon at "Lord, I have cried" (*Сѣ́де А҆да́мъ пря́мѡ Ра́я*, "Adam sat opposite Paradise") is one of the classic hymnographic masterpieces of the Lenten Triodion, traditionally attributed to Saint Romanos the Melodist or Saint John of Damascus.

[^445]: Internal cross-reference: fol. 104 (`Ли́ⷭ, р҃д`) corresponds to physical leaf p118 in this codex.

[^446]: Doxasticon at the Litiya, Tone 6: the cosmic mourning over the closure of Paradise draws upon Joel 2:10, 31 (LXX) and Matthew 27:45.

[^447]: Aposticha Doxasticon, Tone 6: the response of the Savior combines 1 Timothy 2:4 ("Who desires all men to be saved, and to come unto the knowledge of the truth") and John 6:37 ("him that comes to Me I will in no wise cast out").

[^448]: Internal cross-reference: fol. 20 (`Ли́, к҃`) corresponds to physical leaf p34 in this codex.

[^449]: Internal cross-reference: fol. 30 (`Ли́, л҃`) corresponds to physical leaf p44 in this codex.

[^450]: Internal cross-reference: fol. 3 (`Ли́стъ, г҃`) corresponds to physical leaf p17 in this codex.

[^451]: Internal cross-reference: fol. 3 (`Ли́, г҃`) corresponds to physical leaf p17 in this codex.

[^452]: Internal cross-reference: fol. 54 (`Ли, н҃д`) corresponds to physical leaf p68 in this codex.

[^453]: Internal cross-reference: fol. 85 (`Ли́, пе҃`) corresponds to physical leaf p99 in this codex.

[^454]: Internal cross-reference: fol. 20 (`Ли́стъ, к҃`) corresponds to physical leaf p34 in this codex.

[^455]: The celebrated Great Lenten penitential troparion: *Open unto me the doors of repentance, O Giver of life* (Slavonic: *Покая́ні́я ѡ҆тве́рзи́ ми две́ри Жизнода́вче*; Greek: *Τῆς μετανοίας ἄνοιξόν μοι πύλας, Ζωοδότα*), sung after Psalm 50 at Sunday Matins from the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee through the Fifth Sunday of Great Lent.

[^456]: Internal cross-reference: fol. 5 (`Ли́стъ, е҃`) corresponds to physical leaf p19 in this codex.

[^457]: The Canon of Cheesefare Sunday was composed by Christopher the Protoasekretis (first secretary or imperial chancellor of Constantinople, 9th/10th century).

[^458]: Tone 4 Sessional Hymn (Kathisma) melodic model: *Joseph was amazed* (Slavonic: *Ꙋди́вися І҆ѡ́сифъ*; Greek: *Κατεπλάγη Ἰωσήφ*).

[^459]: Tone 4 Sessional Hymn melodic model: *Thou Who wast lifted up upon the Cross* (Slavonic: *Възнесы́йся на Крⷭ҇тъ*; Greek: *Ὁ ὑψωθεὶς ἐν τῷ Σтаυρῷ*).

[^460]: Patristic reading at Matins: Saint Gregory the Theologian (of Nazianzus), *Oration 19: On the Calamity of the City and the Reconciliation of the Emperor's Tax-Collector* (also known as *On the Hail* or *On the Plague*).

[^461]: Kontakion of Cheesefare Sunday (Tone 6, Idiomelon / Samoglasen). Attributed to Saint Romanos the Melodist (6th century). In Byzantine and Slavonic hymnography, this serves as the prooimion to his celebrated kontakion on the Fall of Adam.

[^462]: Ikos of Cheesefare Sunday. The 1640 Kyivan edition preserves four full strophes of Romanos the Melodist's original kontakion on the Fall of Adam, whereas later Russian Synodal editions abridge the Ikos to the opening strophe alone. This unabridged four-strophe witness reflects the conservative archaism of the 17th-century Kyiv-Pechersk liturgical recension.

[^463]: The Synaxarion for Cheesefare Sunday (Commemoration of the Expulsion of Adam from Paradise) is presented here in the Early Modern Ruthenian (*prosta mova*) prose recension established under Metropolitan Petro Mohyla at the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra.

[^464]: Marginal Scriptural citation: Matt. 4:2 (`Мѳ҃: д҃. в҃.`), the forty-day fast of Christ in the wilderness, fulfilling and reversing the intemperance of Adam.

[^465]: Marginal Patristic and Jewish concordance: Philo of Alexandria (`Фїлѡ́нъ`), *Quaestiones et Solutiones in Genesim* I. The variant opinions regarding the duration of Adam's stay in Paradise (one hundred years according to Philo; seven hours, seven days, or seven years according to various early Christian and Jewish commentaries) reflect ancient rabbinic and patristic chronographical speculation.

[^466]: Marginal Gospel citations: Matt. 27:45 (`Мѳ҃ к҃ѕ. м҃ѕ.` [an epigraphic erratum for Matt. 27:45–46]); Mark 15:33 (`Мр҃к҃: є҃і. л҃г.`); Luke 23:44 (`Лꙋк҃: кг҃. м҃д.`); John 19:14 (`І҆ѡ҃: ѳ҃і. д҃і.`). The typological concordance links Adam stretching forth his hand to the forbidden tree at the sixth hour of Friday with Christ stretching forth His hands upon the Cross at the sixth hour on Great and Holy Friday.

[^467]: Saint Gregory of Nazianzus (the Theologian), *Oration 38: On the Theophany* (and *Oration 45: On Holy Pascha*, §8). St. Gregory allegorizes the trees of Paradise as divine thoughts and intellectual contemplation (*noesis* / *theoria*), and the tree of the knowledge of good and evil as contemplation beyond man's spiritual maturity.

[^468]: Saint John Chrysostom, *Homilies on Genesis* (Homilies 16 and 17). Chrysostom insists on preserving the literal reality of the sensible Paradise while expounding its spiritual significance, rejecting purely allegorical dismissals of the physical garden.

[^469]: Marginal Scriptural citation: Luke 1:26 (`Лꙋк҃: а҃. к҃ѕ.`), the Feast of the Annunciation of the Theotokos (March 25), which consistently falls within the period of Great Lent or Great Week.

[^470]: Marginal Scriptural citation: John 11 (`І҆ѡа́: а҃і.`), the raising of Lazarus commemorated on Lazarus Saturday.

[^471]: Marginal Scriptural citations: Matt. 21:1 (`Мѳ҃: к҃а. а҃.`); Luke 19:29 (`Лꙋк: ѳ҃і. к҃ѳ.`); John 12:13 (`І҆ѡ: в҃і: г҃і.`), Christ's Triumphal Entry into Jerusalem commemorated on Palm Sunday.

[^472]: The liturgical reading of the Acts of the Apostles begins at the Paschal Vigil / Matins and continues throughout the fifty days of the Pentecostarion (from Pascha to Pentecost), bearing witness to the resurrection of Christ through apostolic preaching and miracles.

[^473]: The classical patristic computation of Great Lent as the spiritual tithe (*decima*) of the year is expounded by Saint John Cassian (*Conferences* 21.24–25) and Saint Dorotheus of Gaza (*Instructions* 15). The 36.5 fasting days constitute approximately one-tenth of the 365 days of the solar year.

[^474]: Marginal Scriptural citation: Matt. 4:2 (`Мѳ҃: д҃. в҃.`), the Lord's forty-day fast in the wilderness.

[^475]: Marginal Scriptural citations: Exod. 24:18; 34:28 (`И҆сх: к҃д. и҃і. и҃: л҃д. к҃и.`); Deut. 9:9, 18 (`Девтеро: ѳ҃. ѳ҃. и҃і.`), the forty-day fasts of Moses on Mount Sinai before receiving the tablets of the Law.

[^476]: Marginal Scriptural citation: 3 Kings (1 Kings) 19:8 (`Г҃ Цр҃: ѳ҃і. и҃.`), the forty-day journey of the Prophet Elijah unto Mount Horeb on the strength of the miraculous cake.

[^477]: Marginal Scriptural citation: Dan. 10:2–3 (`Дан: і҃. г҃.`), the three-week fast and mourning of the Prophet Daniel.

[^478]: Tone 2 Exapostilarion melodic model: *Give ear, ye women* (Slavonic: *Же́ны внꙋ́шите* / *Же́ны оу҆слы́шите*; Greek: *Γυναῖκες ἀκουτίσθητε*).

[^479]: Tone 2 Exapostilarion melodic model: *Let us assemble with the disciples* (Slavonic: *Съ ᲂу҆чн҃ки и҆де́мъ* / *Съ ᲂу҆чени́ки взы́демъ*; Greek: *Τοῖς μαθηταῖς συνέλθωμεν*).

[^480]: The Praises (*Lauds* / *Chvalite*) at Sunday Matins combine the psalmody of Psalms 148, 149, and 150 with stichera: 4 Resurrectional stichera in the Tone of the week, 1 Anatolian sticheron, and 3 proper idiomelic stichera from the Triodion for Cheesefare Sunday.

[^481]: The third Triodion sticheron at the Praises synthesizes the overarching theological typology of Cheesefare Sunday: Adam's loss of Paradise through sensual indulgence is countered by the forty-day fast of Moses, which purifies the soul to behold the uncreated glory of God and join the angelic choirs.

[^482]: Liturgical rubric for Cheesefare Sunday: the regular Sunday Gospel Sticheron (the Eothinon hymn celebrating the Resurrection) is omitted and replaced by the solemn idiomelon of Great Lent in Tone 6 (*Приспѣ̀ вре́мя, дх҃о́вныхъ по́двигѡвъ нача́ло*), anticipating the penitential discipline of the Great Fast.

[^483]: Conclusion of Cheesefare Sunday Matins: following the Great Doxology and the dismissal of Matins, the brethren process into the narthex (*притво́ръ*) for the chanting of the Gospel Sticheron (Eothinon) and the reading of the ascetical instruction of St. Theodore the Studite (*Поꙋче́нїе Стꙋді́тово*), a classic feature of Studite monastic horology before the final dismissal.

[^484]: Liturgical order for the Divine Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom on Cheesefare Sunday (Forgiveness Sunday): the appointed Epistle is Romans 13:11–14:4 (pericope 121), urging believers to put on the armor of light and not to pass judgment regarding food; the Gospel is Matthew 6:14–21 (pericope 17), presenting Christ's injunction to forgive others and to fast in secret rather than before men.

[^485]: Monastic Typikon ordinance regarding the Polyeleos: from Cheesefare Sunday until the Exaltation of the Precious Cross (September 14), the Polyeleos (Psalms 134–135) is discontinued at regular Sunday Matins throughout the spring and summer periods, being reserved exclusively for Vigils of the Master's Feasts (Dominical Feasts) or major feast-day Saints.

[^486]: Rubric for Cheesefare Sunday Vespers (Forgiveness Vespers), marking the solemn liturgical threshold of Great Lent. Ten stichera are appointed at *Lord, I have cried*: four penitential stichera in the Tone of the week from the Oktoechos (drawing from the evening and morning Aposticha), three proper stichera from the Triodion, and three from the Menaion.

[^487]: Tone 2 prosomoia composed by Lord Joseph the Hymnographer (*Творе́нїе Гдⷭ҇на І҆ѡ́сифа*), sung to the melodic model *When from the Tree* (Greek: *Ὅτε ἐκ τοῦ ξύλου*; Slavonic: *Е҆гда̀ ѿ дре́ва*). The hymns exhort the faithful to embark upon the stadium of the Fast with humility and to emulate the repentance of the Ninevites (Jonah 3:5–10).

[^488]: Tone 2 prosomoion composed by St. Theodore the Studite (*Гдⷭ҇а Ѳео́дѡра Стꙋди́та*), sung to the melodic model *Surpassing understanding* (Slavonic abbreviation: *Па́че [ᲂу҆ма̀]*; Greek: *Ὑπὲρ νοῦν τῶν δωρεῶν*). This sticheron defines the joyous, luminous theology of Great Lent: entering the fast not with gloom, but with brightness, washing the soul, and delighting in the virtues of the Holy Spirit.

[^489]: Rubric for the Entrance at Forgiveness Vespers: the celebrant vests in dark/purple vestments (*въ Багрѧ́нѡ Фелѡ́ни*) for the Entrance with the censer and taper-bearer, visually establishing the transition from the festal Pre-Lenten period into the solemn, penitential purple of Great Lent.

[^490]: The Great Prokeimenon of Cheesefare Sunday Vespers: *Turn not away Thy face from Thy servant, for I am in trouble...* (Ps. 68:18–19, 30, 33 [LXX]), chanted five times in Tone 8 with its verses to a slow, mournful melody. This rubric also establishes that throughout the Forty Days, the evening prostrations and metanias commence at *Vouchsafe, O Lord*.

[^491]: The Aposticha of Cheesefare Sunday Vespers features the celebrated idiomelon *Thy grace has shone forth, O Lord* (*Возсїя̀ блгⷣть твоя̀ Гдⷭ҇и*), proclaiming the commencement of the "acceptable time" of salvation (2 Cor. 6:2) and calling believers to put on the armor of light (Rom. 13:12). The concluding Theotokion is sung to the Tone 4 model *As one valiant among martyrs* (Greek: *Ὡς γενναῖον ἐν μάρτυσιν*; Slavonic: *Ꙗ҆́кѡ до́бля въ мꙋ́ченицѣхъ*).

[^492]: The Great Lenten Penitential Troparia at the conclusion of Sunday Vespers: *O Mother of God and Virgin, rejoice...*, *O Baptist of Christ, remember us all...*, and *Intercede for us, holy Apostles...*, each accompanied by a full bodily prostration to the ground (*метание великое*). The ancient Marian prayer *Beneath thy tender compassion* (*Подъ твою̀ млⷭ҇ть*) is recited without prostration.

[^493]: The solemn state intercession *O Heavenly King, strengthen our faithful sovereigns...* (*Нб҃ный Цр҃ю, вѣ́рныя Цр҃я на́ша ᲂу҆крѣпѝ...*), preserving the 1640 Kyivan text praying for Orthodox rulers, peace in the world, and the preservation of the monastery, recited by the reader before the Prayer of St. Ephrem.

[^494]: The Prayer of St. Ephrem the Syrian (*Моли́тва Преподо́бнагѡ Є҆фре́ма Сѵ́рина*), the quintessential prayer of Great Lent. The Kyivan rubric specifies that the brethren first stand with hands uplifted in mental supplication, reciting the three parts inwardly, accompanied by three great prostrations.

[^495]: The monastic Rite of Mutual Forgiveness (*Чи́нъ проще́нїя*): following the completion of Vespers, the brethren venerate the Precious Cross and the sacred icons at their stalls, bowing to one another to ask mutual forgiveness before embarking upon the ascetic labors of Clean Week.

[^496]: Small Compline (*Па́вечерница Мала́я*) on Cheesefare Sunday night: ordered with Lenten metanias and prostrations with the Prayer of St. Ephrem, keeping the service concise to allow rest after the labors of the preceding Vigil.

[^497]: Codicological and Typikon legislation for funerals and memorial services (*панихида / парастас*) during Great Lent: because the weekdays of Lent are strictly non-liturgical, no third-day, ninth-day, or special memorial services are celebrated during the week; all such memorials are deferred to Friday evenings and Saturday Liturgies. Regular daily commemorations and prosphora oblations for the newly departed resume only on Thomas Sunday (*Но́вая Недѣ́ля*) for a period of forty consecutive days.

[^498]: Typikon chapter heading: *Concerning the Feast of the Meeting of the Lord, Chapter 49* (*Ѡ҆ Пра́здницѣ Стрѣ́тенїа, Гла́: мл҃* [recte 49 / *м҃ѳ*]). The numeral *мл҃* in the Kyivan print is a typographical inversion or local chapter reckoning for chapter 49 of the Typikon, governing the liturgical confluence of Candlemas (Feb. 2) with Clean Monday.

[^499]: Astronomical and Paschalian note: Monday of the First Week of Great Lent (Clean Monday) is the absolute latest possible date in the Julian calendar on which the Feast of the Meeting of the Lord (February 2) can ever fall. Under the perpetual Julian Paschalion, the earliest possible date of Pascha is March 22, placing Clean Monday precisely on February 2.

[^500]: Comprehensive rubrics for celebrating the Feast of the Meeting of the Lord when falling on Clean Monday: the Divine Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom is celebrated, but the ascetical discipline of Clean Week is preserved at the monastic table (dry food and wine are permitted, but fish is strictly forbidden). At Compline, the first section of the Great Canon of St. Andrew of Crete is chanted without prostrations.

[^501]: Typikon rubric for the Feast of the First and Second Finding of the Honorable Head of the Holy Glorious Prophet, Forerunner, and Baptist John (February 24): if this feast coincides with Clean Monday, the entire festal service is anticipated and sung on Cheesefare Sunday, unless the church or monastery is dedicated to the Forerunner, in which case it is celebrated on Clean Monday with the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts.

[^502]: Monastic horology of Clean Week: the paraklesiarches (bell-ringer / sacristan) strikes the simandron or church bell (*знамена́етъ к̾ свѣ́тꙋ*) well before dawn to summon the brotherhood to the Midnight Office and Matins of Clean Monday.

[^503]: The Midnight Office (*Полунощница*) on Clean Monday: celebrated without prostrations during the psalmody, concluding with three great prostrations accompanied by the Prayer of St. Ephrem. The Kyivan rubric notes that this relaxation of psalmody prostrations is unique to the first day of the Fast; on all subsequent weekdays of Great Lent, the Midnight Office includes its full complement of prostrations.

[^504]: The Royal Office and opening of Matins on Clean Monday: the Six Psalms are preceded by the Trisagion and the Royal Psalms (Psalms 19 and 20), chanted without metanias, while the priest censes the sanctuary and nave. The rubric specifies that neither the local bishop nor the igumen is named in the introductory litany.

[^505]: Physical and ascetical discipline during the Six Psalms (*Шестопсалмие*): the monastic rule demands absolute stillness, silence, and internal concentration, hands folded across the chest, heads bowed, and eyes cast down while directing the inner spiritual gaze toward the East, with a total prohibition on whispering, spitting, or throat-clearing, treating the psalmody as an invisible conversation with God.

[^506]: Chanting of the Lenten *Alleluia* at Matins: replacing *God is the Lord*, the appointed monk and choirs chant *Alleluia* three times after each of four Scriptural verses from Isaiah 26 (*From night my spirit seeks Thee early in the morning, O God...*; Isa. 26:9, 11, 15, 17), followed by the Triadica (Trinity Hymns) of the current Tone of the Oktoechos.

[^507]: Ascetical reading cycle of Great Lent: after the First Kathisma of Matins, the readings are drawn from the homilies and discourses of St. Ephrem the Syrian, a daily monastic instruction that continues throughout Great Lent until Holy and Great Tuesday of Passion Week (*до Вто́рника Цвѣ́тнагѡ* / Palm/Passion Tuesday).

[^508]: Tone 2 Sessional Hymn of Lord Joseph the Hymnographer after the 2nd Kathisma of Matins, sung to the melodic model *Being a wellspring of loving-kindness* (Greek: *Εὐσπλαγχνίας ὑπάρχουσα*; Slavonic: *Милосе́рдїя сꙋ́щи и҆сто́чникъ*), imploring Christ to accept the prayers and abstinence of the Fast as choice incense.

[^509]: Tone 2 Sessional Hymn of St. Theodore the Studite after the 3rd Kathisma of Matins, sung to the melodic model *We venerate Thine all-pure icon* (Greek: *Τὴν ἄχραντον εἰκόνα σου*; Slavonic: *Пречи́стомꙋ ти ѻ҆́бразꙋ покланя́емся*), extolling the virtues of fasting, prayer, chastity, and spiritual courage as radiant armor leading to the glory of the three-day Resurrection.

[^510]: Liturgical sequence preceding the Canons: after the 3rd Kathisma and a reading from the *Lausiac History* (*Лаѵсі́акъ*) of Palladius, Psalm 50 is recited, followed immediately by the First Biblical Ode from the Psalter (*Song of Moses*, Exod. 15:1–19), appointed to 14 verses beginning at Exodus 15:8 (*The waters grew thick as a wall*).

[^511]: Antiphonal performance practice of the Lenten Triodia: the troparia of the two canons (by Lord Joseph and St. Theodore the Studite) are sung alternately by the right and left choirs to the verses of the Biblical Odes. At the Trinity Troparion, both choirs unite in the center of the nave (*съвоку́пльшеся ѻ҆́ба ли́ка*) with the canonarch to sing with elevated voices.

[^512]: Typikon catalog of the Biblical Canticles (Odes) throughout the Holy Forty Days: detailing the assignment of the nine Biblical Odes across the weekdays of Lent, with the Canticle of the Theotokos (*Magnificat*, Luke 1:46–55) sung daily at Ode 9.

[^513]: Typikon ordinance governing the integration of the Menaion and Triodion canons: when the Triodion appoints odes (the Triodia), the daily canon of the Oktoechos is omitted to accommodate the two canons of the Menaion saints alongside the Lenten Triodia of Joseph and Theodore.

[^514]: Ode 1 of the Clean Monday Triodion by Lord Joseph the Hymnographer, Tone 2, Heirmos *Come, ye peoples* (Greek: *Δεῦτε λαοί*; Slavonic: *Грядѣ́те, лю́дїе*), reflecting the soul's deep lamentation and grief over prodigal living as the holy Fast begins.

[^515]: Ode 1 of the Clean Monday Triodion by St. Theodore the Studite, concluding with the ascetical typology of the Old Testament: the fiery ascent of the Prophet Elijah (4 Kings [2 Kings] 2:11) and the forty-day fast of Moses on Sinai (Exod. 34:28) as the biblical exemplars of spiritual warfare and divine vision.

[^516]: The abbreviation "На з҃ пѣ́сни, Мꙋ́: Сѣ́: въ Ѻ҆кт́: гла́сꙋ" indicates that after Ode 6 (the letter *zelo* ѕ҃ / *zemlya* з҃ here denoting the mid-canon position after Ode 6 in the weekday Lenten tri-ode arrangement), the Martyr's Sessional Hymn (Мучениченъ Сѣдаленъ) from the Oktoechos of the current Tone is sung, followed by the appointed Patristic reading from the Prologue.
[^517]: Heirmos of the second Canon (Lord Theodore the Studite), Tone 2: *Him Who of old dewed* (*Дре́вле ѡ҆роси́вшаго є҆вре́йскїѧ дѣ́ти въ пла́мени*).
[^518]: The Stichologia of the Magnificat: the Canticle of the Mother of God (*My soul doth magnify the Lord*, Luke 1:46–55) with the refrain *More honorable than the Cherubim* (*Честнѣйшꙋю*).
[^519]: Heirmos of the second Canon (Lord Theodore the Studite), Tone 2: *Surpassing nature* (*Па́че є҆стества̀*).
[^520]: On Lenten weekdays without a feast, the stichera at the Praises are omitted; instead, the Psalms of Praises (Pss. 148–150) are recited plain (*стїхоло́гїемъ*), followed by the small spoken doxology (*Тебѣ́ сла́ва подоба́етъ...* and *Сла́ва въ вы́шнихъ Бг҃ꙋ...*) in an even speaking voice without chant.
[^521]: The sixteen penitential bows of the Prayer of St. Ephrem the Syrian (*Гдⷭ҇и и҆ Вл҃ко живота̀ моегѡ̀*): three great prostrations (*покло́ны до землѧ̀*) accompanied by each verse of the prayer; followed by twelve small metanias (*поя́сные покло́ны*) silently reciting "O God, cleanse me a sinner"; concluded by one final great prostration reciting the prayer in its entirety.
[^522]: On all Mondays of Great Lent, no Kathisma from the Psalter is appointed for the First Hour, because the 4th, 5th, and 6th Kathismata of the weekly Psalter course are read at the Third, Sixth, and Ninth Hours respectively, the first three having been read at Matins.
[^523]: Chanted in Tone 6 with full prostrations by the alternating choirs at each verse, characteristic of the Lenten weekday Hours.
[^524]: Cross-reference to folio 188 (*Ли́стъ, р҃пи* / physical leaf 188), where the Kontakion/Troparion *Премꙋ́дрости наста́вниче* (O Instructor of wisdom...) is recorded.
[^525]: The concluding prayer of the First Hour, *O Christ, the true Light* (*Хрїстѐ свѣ́те и҆́стинный*), attributed in Byzantine euchological tradition to St. Basil the Great.
[^526]: Cross-reference to folio 17 (*Ли́стъ, з҃і*), where the overarching rubrics prescribe the reading of the Catechetical Instructions (*Ѡ҆глаше́нїѧ*) of St. Theodore the Studite at the Litiya in the narthex on every Wednesday and Friday of the Holy Forty Days.
[^527]: The strict Studite monastic rule of silence (*безмо́лвїе*) observed by the brethren upon departing the temple, returning to their cells or manual obediences without idle conversation along the way.
[^528]: The Troparion of the Third Hour (*O Lord, Who didst send down Thine all-holy Spirit at the third hour...*) chanted in Tone 6 with prostrations to the ground at each verse.
[^529]: The reading from the *Ladder of Divine Ascent* (*Ле́ствица*) of St. John Climacus, appointed daily at the Third and Sixth Hours during Great Lent.
[^530]: The Troparion of the Sixth Hour (*O Thou Who on the sixth day and hour didst nail to the Cross...*) chanted in Tone 2 with prostrations.
[^531]: The Troparion of the Prophecy (*Тропа́рь Проро́чества*) at the Sixth Hour, Tone 5, anticipating the Paroimia from the Book of Isaiah.
[^532]: The opening pericope from the Prophecy of Isaiah (Isa. 1:1–20), appointed as the first Sixth Hour Paroimia of Great Lent on Clean Monday, inaugurating the reading of the entire prophet throughout the fast.
[^533]: Slavonic *ꙗ҆́кѡ ѻ҆во́щное храни́лище въ вертогра́дѣ*, translating Septuagint (LXX) ὡς ὀπωροφυλάκιον ἐν σικυηράτῳ ("as a watch-lodge in a cucumber field / garden").
[^534]: Cross-reference to folio 174 (*Ли́стъ, р҃од* / physical leaf 188), where the Sessional Theotokion *Being a wellspring of loving-kindness* (*Милосе́рдїя сꙋ́щи и҆сто́чникъ*) is given.

[^535]: The striking of the semantron (Slavonic *клепало* / *било*) nine times for the Ninth Hour, convening the monastic brotherhood in the temple for the final liturgical Hour before Vespers on Clean Monday.
[^536]: The regular Kathisma appointed at the Ninth Hour on Clean Monday is Kathisma 6 (Pss. 37–45 LXX) of the weekly Psalter course.
[^537]: The continuous Lenten reading from the *Ladder of Divine Ascent* (*Лествица*) of St. John Climacus, appointed daily at the Third, Sixth, and Ninth Hours.
[^538]: The sixteen penitential bows of the Prayer of St. Ephrem the Syrian (*Lord and Master of my life*), concluding the Ninth Hour.
[^539]: The Lenten Typika (*Обѣдница* / *Изобразительны*) chanted in Tone 8 without the Divine Liturgy, featuring the colometric chanting of the Beatitudes with prostrations.
[^540]: The tripartite penitential acclamation of the Thief from Luke 23:42 (*Remember us, O Lord... O Master... O Holy One*), raised in three successive vocal gradations, each accompanied by a full prostration to the ground (*поклонъ до земли*).
[^541]: The Greek Troparion *Προσέλθετε πρὸς αὐτὸν καὶ φωτίσθητε* (Ps. 33:6 LXX) and the angelic hymn *Χορὸς ὁ ἐπουράνιος ὑμνεῖ σε* ("The heavenly choir hymns Thee"), ancient components of the Lenten Typika office.
[^542]: The Marian Kontakion *Steadfast protectress of Christians* (*Заступнице христїаномъ непостыдная*), Tone 6, the standard concluding Kontakion of the Typika throughout Great Lent.
[^543]: Kathisma 18 of the Psalter (Pss. 119–133 LXX), the Songs of Ascents (*Къ Господу внегда скорбѣти ми*), appointed daily at weekday Lenten Vespers.
[^544]: The Greek automelon ὑπερβέβηκα πάντας (Tone 2), rendered in Slavonic as *Всѣхъ прейдохъ* ("I have surpassed all in prodigality / sin"), composed by Lord Joseph the Hymnographer for Clean Monday evening.
[^545]: The Greek automelon Ὅσιε πάτερ (Tone 5), rendered in Slavonic as *Преподобне отче* ("O venerable father"), composed by Lord Theodore the Studite.
[^546]: The beginning of the continuous Old Testament lectio from the Book of Genesis (Gen. 1:1–3), inaugurated at Vespers on Clean Monday and read throughout the Holy Forty Days.
[^547]: The second evening Prokeimenon of Clean Monday, drawn from Psalm 4 (Ps. 4:4 LXX), preceding the reading from Proverbs.
[^548]: The inauguration of the continuous reading from the Proverbs of Solomon (Prov. 1:1–7), appointed as the daily second Old Testament reading at Lenten Vespers.
[^549]: The evening Aposticha of Clean Monday with the Great Idiomelon *Let us fast with an acceptable fast* (*Постимся постомъ прїятнымъ*), Tone 3, encapsulating the true spiritual definition of fasting according to the Byzantine Fathers.
[^550]: Cross-reference to folio 170 (*Ли́стъ, р҃о* / physical leaf 184), where the order of evening dismissal and the Prayer of St. Ephrem was set forth on Cheesefare Sunday evening.
[^551]: The evening prayer to the Holy Trinity (*All-holy Trinity, consubstantial dominion* / *Всесвятая Троице, единосущная державо*), an ancient euchological prayer recited at the conclusion of the Lenten monastic day.
[^552]: Psalm 144 (*Exaltabo te Deus meus Rex*), appointed in the monastic Typikon to be recited at the blessing of the Trapeza table during Great Lent.
[^553]: The solemn monastic egress into the narthex (*Притворъ* / *Лития*) following Vespers for the commemoration of departed brethren and the prayer of departure.
[^554]: The traditional severe fasting rubric of the First Week: total abstinence from cooked food and liturgical consumption until the Presanctified Liturgy on Wednesday, or for rigorous ascetics until Friday.
[^555]: The Athonite monastic rule of the Holy Mountain (*Уставъ Святыя Горы*): complete abstinence on Clean Monday, followed by a ration of one litra (approx. 320 grams) of bread with water and salt on Tuesday, Wednesday, and Thursday.
[^556]: Typikon rubrics for the First and Second Finding of the Precious Head of St. John the Baptist (February 24, cross-referenced to chapter 62 / *глава 62* of the Typikon/Menologion), detailing its celebration when coinciding with a Lenten weekday.
[^557]: The Great Midnight Office (*Месониктъ же болшїй*), chanted without prostrations when a Polyeleos feast falls on a Lenten weekday.
[^558]: Detailed rubrics for the celebration of the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts with festal Gospel and Epistle readings on the feast of the Forerunner during Great Lent.
[^559]: The rubrical extension of this festal Lenten order to the Holy Forty Martyrs of Sebaste (March 9) and any other major feast occurring during the Holy Forty Days.
[^560]: Monastic Typikon rule prohibiting the singing of two canons alongside the Forty Martyrs at Lenten weekday Matins, transferring the second canon to Compline on the preceding evening.
[^561]: Slavonic rubric *ѡ Меднисонѣ* / *ѡ Медонисонѣ*, transliterating the Byzantine Greek liturgical term μεσονύσιον / μεσονύκтион (referring to the night office / Compline).
[^562]: The historical distinction between the eremitic Sabbaite rule (Great Compline recited individually in cells) and the Palestinian coenobitic communal practice (Great Compline chanted corporately in the monastery church).
[^563]: The quadripartite division of the Great Penitential Canon of St. Andrew of Crete across the first four days of Clean Week (Monday to Thursday evenings), before its complete recapitulation on Thursday of the Fifth Week.
[^564]: St. Andrew of Crete, Archbishop of Gortyna (c. 660–740), designated *Іерлимлянинъ* (the Hierosolymite) because he entered monastic life at the Brotherhood of the Holy Sepulchre and the Lavra of St. Sabbas.
[^565]: Heirmos of Ode 1 (Tone 6): *He was unto me a helper and a protector* (*Помощникъ и покровитель*), based on the First Canticle of Moses (Exod. 15:1–2).
[^566]: The universal Lenten penitential refrain *Have mercy on me, O God, have mercy on me* (*Помилуй мя Боже, помилуй мя*), accompanied by three metanias at each troparion of the Great Canon.
[^567]: Heirmos of Ode 2 (Tone 6): *Attend, O heaven, and I will speak* (*Вонми небо, и возглаголю*), based on the Second Canticle of Moses (Deut. 32:1), chanted exclusively during Great Lent.

[^568]: The Second Canticle of Moses (Deut. 32:1–43) forms the scriptural basis for Ode 2 of the Great Canon. Unlike the other biblical canticles sung throughout the liturgical year, the Second Ode is sung exclusively during the weekdays of Great Lent because of its stern rebuke, grave penitential character, and reminder of the soul's apostasy from God.
[^569]: The patristic antithesis of the two vestments: the soul having stripped off the original garment of incorruption woven by the Creator before the Fall (Gen. 3:21), and putting on the torn tunic of shame counselled by the serpent (Gen. 3:7).
[^570]: Colometric citation of Psalm 128:3 LXX (*Ἐπὶ τὸν νῶτόν μου ἐτέκταινον οἱ ἁμαρτωλοί, ἐμάκρυναν τὴν ἀνομίαν αὐτῶν* / *Дѣлаша на хребтѣ моемъ вси началницы злыхъ*), applied here by St. Andrew of Crete to the relentless scourging of demonic passions and habitual transgression.
[^571]: The Heirmos of Ode 3 (Tone 6) is derived from the Canticle of Hannah (1 Kings / 1 Sam. 2:2 LXX: "There is none holy as the Lord, and none righteous as our God; establish Thy Church upon the rock of Thy commandments").
[^572]: The flight of Lot from the burning of Sodom unto the mountain and his refuge in the small city of Zoar (Slavonic *Сигоръ* / Gen. 19:15–22), interpreted in Byzantine ascetical literature as the soul's urgent flight from carnal conflagration into the haven of prayer.
[^573]: The Heirmos of Ode 4 (Tone 6) is drawn from the prophetic vision and Canticle of Habakkuk (Hab. 3:2 LXX: "O Lord, I have heard Thy report, and was afraid; O Lord, I considered Thy works, and was astounded").
[^574]: Jacob's ladder (Gen. 28:12) allegorized by St. Andrew of Crete as the ascetical ascent of active virtue (*дѣянїе* / *praxis*) leading unto spiritual contemplation and theological knowledge (*разумъ въ видѣнїи* / *theoria*), reflecting the ascetical theology of Evagrius Ponticus and St. Maximus the Confessor.
[^575]: Jacob's two wives (Gen. 29:16–30) interpreted allegorically: Leah representing active ascetical labor (*дѣянїе*), fruitful in virtues, and Rachel representing spiritual contemplation (*видѣнїе* / *theoria*), attained only through long and patient striving.
[^576]: The Heirmos of Ode 5 (Tone 6) is derived from the Canticle of the Prophet Isaiah (Isa. 26:9 LXX: "From night my spirit awaks early unto Thee, O God, for Thy commandments are a light upon the earth").
[^577]: The transgression of Reuben defiling the bed of his father Jacob (Gen. 35:22; 49:4), typologically contrasted with the moral purity of Joseph who fled the solicitation of Potiphar's wife in Egypt (Gen. 39:7–12).
[^578]: Joseph cast into the cistern or pit by his brethren (Gen. 37:24) as an Old Testament archetype of Christ's descent into Hades and His three-day Burial and Resurrection.
[^579]: The Heirmos of Ode 6 (Tone 6) is drawn from the Canticle of the Prophet Jonah crying out from the belly of the sea monster (Jonah 2:3–7 LXX: "Out of the belly of Hades I cried... and Thou broughtest up my life from corruption, O Lord my God").
[^580]: The rebellion and destruction of Dathan and Abiram, swallowed alive into the pit of the earth (Num. 16:31–33), cited as a terrifying warning against spiritual rebellion and hardhearted impenitence.
[^581]: The sign given unto Moses upon Mount Horeb, whose hand became leprous as snow when drawn from his bosom and was restored whole upon being returned (Exod. 4:6–7), demonstrating God's sovereign mercy in cleansing a sin-diseased soul.
[^582]: The penitential Sessional Hymn (*Сѣдаленъ покаяненъ*) chanted after Ode 6, Tone 6, modeled on the Greek automelon *Ἡ ἐλπὶς τοῦ κόσμου* / Slavonic *Упованіе*, invoking the miraculous rescue of the sinking Apostle Peter upon the Sea of Galilee (Matt. 14:30–31).
[^583]: The Heirmos of Ode 7 (Tone 6) is drawn from the Prayer of Azariah in the Babylonian furnace (Dan. 3:28–34 LXX: "We have sinned, we have transgressed, we have done unrighteously... yet deliver us not up utterly for Thy name's sake").
[^584]: The biblical exempla of Saul who sought his father's asses and received an earthly kingdom (1 Kings / 1 Sam. 9:1–27), and King David who fell into double transgression through adultery with Bathsheba and the murder of Uriah, yet offered two-fold lamentation in Psalm 50 LXX (2 Kings / 2 Sam. 11–12).
[^585]: David composing Psalm 50 LXX as an enduring iconographic self-rebuke (*списавъ якоже на иконѣ пѣснь*) to expose his transgression before all generations.
[^586]: The Heirmos of Ode 8 (Tone 6) is derived from the Canticle of the Three Holy Children in the furnace (Dan. 3:57–88 LXX: "Him Whom the heavenly hosts glorify... hymn, bless, and exalt supremely unto all ages").
[^587]: The fiery ascent of the Prophet Elijah into heaven in a chariot of fire (4 Kings / 2 Kings 2:11), and the double portion of the spirit bestowed upon Elisha through Elijah's sheepskin mantle (melote) dividing the waters of the Jordan (4 Kings / 2 Kings 2:8–14).
[^588]: The hospitality of the Shunammite woman who built an upper room to receive the Prophet Elisha (4 Kings / 2 Kings 4:8–10), contrasted with the inhospitable soul facing exclusion from the heavenly bridal chamber (Matt. 25:10–12).
[^589]: Gehazi, servant of Elisha, smitten with Naaman's leprosy unto all generations because of deceitful covetousness and lust for silver (4 Kings / 2 Kings 5:20–27).
[^590]: The Messianic name Emmanuel ("God with us"), foretold by the Prophet Isaiah (Isa. 7:14) and fulfilled in the virginal conception of Christ (Matt. 1:23).
[^591]: The classical patristic image of the Incarnation as the weaving of Christ's human flesh within the Virgin's womb like an imperial purple mantle (*багряница*) dyed with spiritual crimson (*червленица*), popularized in the *Protoevangelium of James* and the homilies of St. Proclus of Constantinople.
[^592]: The pedagogical structure of the Great Canon, moving methodically through the Old Testament historical books from the Creation of the world (*миробытїе* / Genesis) to the Prophets as an ethical mirror for the soul.
[^593]: The transition in Ode 9 from Old Testament typologies to New Testament exemplars of salvation: Christ welcoming the penitent thief upon the cross (Luke 23:40–43), the harlot anointing His feet with tears (Luke 7:37–50), and the publican justified through humility (Luke 18:13–14).
[^594]: The forty-day fast of Christ in the Judean wilderness (Matt. 4:1–11; Luke 4:1–13) establishing the spiritual archetype and ascetical weapons of fasting and prayer to overcome demonic assault.
[^595]: The daily concluding supplicatory troparion addressed directly to the author of the Great Canon, St. Andrew of Crete, Archbishop of Gortyna (c. 660–740).
[^596]: Sabbaite Typikon rubric for concluding the Great Canon at Great Compline: both choirs unite to chant the Heirmos of Ode 9 (*Безсѣменна зачатїя*) with a full prostration to the ground, resuming the ordinary course of Great Compline from Psalm 4 (*Внегда воззвахъ*) in the Horologion (*Часословъ*), concluding with the Canon to the Theotokos and the colometric chanting of *Lord of hosts, be with us* (*Господи силъ съ нами буди*).
[^597]: The foundational rubric of the 1640 Kyiv Triodion establishing the complete liturgical structure of Clean Monday as an unchanging model, demonstration, and spiritual rule (*образъ и Показанїе и Правило Душевное*) for all subsequent weekdays of the Holy Forty Days.
[^598]: Sessional Hymn at Matins on Tuesday of the First Week, Tone 2, modeled on the Podoben *Most blessed* (*Преблагословенна еси*), commemorating Moses on Mount Sinai receiving the Law (Exod. 24:18; 34:28) and the Three Youths in Babylon triumphing over the furnace (Dan. 1:12–15; 3:19–27).
[^599]: Second Sessional Hymn at Matins, Tone 2, modeled on the Podoben *The doors of compassion* (*Милосердїя двери*), summoning the soul to bring forth the fruits of abstinence and conversion.

[^600]: Sessional hymn model (*Podoben*): *Tone 5, Podoben: "O Lord, the cup of torment" (Гдⷭ҇и, ча́шу му́ки)*; Greek: *Κύριε, τὸ ποτήριον τοῦ πάθους*. In the 1640 Kyiv text, the Slavonic heading reads: `По́: Гнⷭ҇и ча́шу му́ки:` (with titlo over *Господи*).
[^601]: Theotokion model (*Podoben*): *Tone 5, Podoben: "The Word co-unoriginate" (Собезнача́лное Сло́во)*; Greek: *Τὸν συνάναρχον Λόγον*. The hymn addresses the Theotokos using Old Testament types: the Rod of Jesse that blossomed (Isa. 11:1), the Ark of the Covenant, the golden Seven-branched Lampstand, the golden Jar of Manna (Heb. 9:4), and the Table of Showbread.
[^602]: Typikon rubric governing weekday Matins hymnody during Great Lent. On ordinary weekdays without a Triode, the Octoechos canons are chanted on 10 and the Menaion on 4. On days when the Triodion prescribes a three-ode canon (Triode—on Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, and Friday), the Octoechos canon is suspended: the Menaion canon is sung on 6 (with its Heirmos), and the Triode of the day is sung on 8, totaling the canonical 14 troparia.
[^603]: Biblical Canticle 2: The Song of Moses in Deuteronomy (Deut. 32:1–43). Outside of Great Lent, the Second Canticle is omitted from the daily Matins cycle; in Great Lent, it is chanted only on Tuesdays, accompanied by the penitential Ode 2 of the Triodion.
[^604]: The "Mountain of excellent deeds" and "entering into the darkness of honorable visions" evokes Moses ascending Mount Sinai into the thick darkness (*gnophos*) where God was (Exod. 20:21), a classic patristic and Dionysian image of mystical contemplative ascent (*theoria*) through ascetic purification.
[^605]: The second poetic canon of the Tuesday Triode, composed in Tone 5 by St. Theodore the Studite (the first canon being by St. Joseph the Hymnographer in Tone 2). Both poets utilize the biblical canticle model (*Heirmos*) based on the Song of Moses in Deut. 32 ("See, see that I am God").
[^606]: Ode 8 of the first canon (Tone 2, St. Joseph). The Heirmos incipit refers to the Burning Bush on Mount Sinai: *Tone 2, Heirmos: "In the bush to Moses" (Въ купинѣ̀ Мѡѷсе́ю)*; Greek: *Ἐν τῇ βάτῳ Μωϋσῇ*.
[^607]: Ode 8 of the second canon (Tone 5, St. Theodore the Studite). The Heirmos incipit: *Tone 5, Heirmos: "The Creator of creation" (Творца̀ тва́ри)*; Greek: *Τὸν ποιητὴν τῆς κτίσεως*.
[^608]: Allusion to Christ's parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus (Luke 16:19–31), contrasting luxurious feasting with ascetic poverty and ultimate comfort in the bosom of Abraham.
[^609]: Ode 9 of the first canon (Tone 2, St. Joseph). The Heirmos incipit: *Tone 2, Heirmos: "Who of the earthborn hath heard?" (Земны́й кто̀ слы́ша)*; Greek: *Τίς ἤκουσε τοιοῦτον*.
[^610]: Ode 9 of the second canon (Tone 5, St. Theodore the Studite). The Heirmos incipit: *Tone 5, Heirmos: "Rejoice, O Isaiah" (И҆са́їа лику́й)*; Greek: *Ἡσαΐα χόρευε*. This Heirmos is famously also chanted during the Mystery of Crowning (Holy Matrimony) and Holy Orders.
[^611]: Photagogicon (*Свѣти́ленъ* / *Exapostilarion*): On weekdays of Great Lent, the Trinity Hymns (*Troika*) of the Tone from the Horologion or Octoechos are chanted in place of a festal exapostilarion.
[^612]: Matins Aposticha idiomelon in Tone 3. The hymn paraphrases Christ's instructions in the Sermon on the Mount concerning fasting and prayer without hypocrisy (Matt. 6:7, 16–18), commending charity (anointing the head with the oil of good deeds) and pure interior prayer ("Our Father").
[^613]: First penitential stichos for the weekday Matins Aposticha during Great Lent: *Ps. 89:14 (LXX)*: "We were filled in the morning with Thy mercy, O Lord, and we rejoiced and were glad in all our days."
[^614]: Troparion of the Prophecy at the Sixth Hour on Tuesday of the First Week of Lent. The text draws on David's prayer in *Ps. 38:13 (LXX)* ("For I am a sojourner with Thee, and a stranger, as all my fathers were") and 1 Chron. 29:15.
[^615]: Sixth Hour Prokeimenon taken from *Ps. 5:3 (LXX)*. The response verse is *Ps. 5:2*.
[^616]: Sixth Hour Old Testament reading: Isaiah 1:19–2:3 (continuous liturgical pericope appointed for Tuesday of the First Week of Great Lent). The reading spans folios 212r–213r (leaves p226–p227), rebuking the corruption of Jerusalem and prophesying the exaltation of the Mountain of the Lord in the latter days.
[^617]: Prokeimenon closing the Sixth Hour: *Ps. 6:2 (LXX)*. Stichos: *Ps. 6:3*.
[^618]: Melodic model (*Podoben*): *Tone 2, Podoben: "When Thou didst appear" (Ꙗ҆́кѡ ꙗ҆ви́сѧ)*; Greek: *Ὡς ἐφάνης*. The incipit refers to the appearance of Christ upon the Cross.
[^619]: Melodic model (*Podoben*): *Tone 2, Podoben: "For the deeds done by me" (Съдѣ́ланныхъ ми)*; Greek: *Τῶν πεπραγμένων μοι δейнῶν*. The hymn reflects on the prohibition of the fruit of the tree in Genesis 2:16–17 and connects ascetic obedience during Great Lent with the mystery of Christ's Cross.
[^620]: First Vespers Prokeimenon before the Old Testament readings: *Ps. 7:2 (LXX)*. Stichos: *Ps. 7:2*.
[^621]: First Old Testament reading at Vespers: Genesis 1:14–23, recounting the Fourth and Fifth Days of Creation (the creation of the sun, moon, stars, fish, and fowl). The pericope spans leaves p228–p229.
[^622]: Second Vespers Prokeimenon (between the Genesis and Proverbs readings): *Ps. 8:2 (LXX)*. Stichos: *Ps. 8:2*.
[^623]: Second Old Testament reading at Vespers: Proverbs 1:20–33. Spans leaves p229–p230. Personified Divine Wisdom calls the simple and rebukes the scorners who reject knowledge and the fear of the Lord.
[^624]: Vespers liturgical prayer: "Vouchsafe, O Lord, to keep us this evening without sin" (*Сподо́би, Го́споди*), recited immediately following the second Old Testament reading on Lenten weekdays when the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts is not celebrated.

[^625]: Stichologia verse from the Gradual Psalms (LXX Psalm 122:1–2), prescribed for the first stichos of the Aposticha at Lenten Vespers.
[^626]: Stichologia verse from LXX Psalm 122:3–4, prescribed for the second stichos of the Aposticha at Lenten Vespers.
[^627]: The Song of Symeon (Luke 2:29–32), followed by the Trisagion Prayers, the Troparia of the day with metanias, and the Prayer of St. Ephrem the Syrian, concluding Lenten Vespers.
[^628]: Rubric governing the order of Great Compline (Повечерие великое / Μεγάλόδειπνον), including the introductory prayers, Psalm 69 (LXX), and the chanting of the Great Penitential Canon of St. Andrew of Crete (divided into four parts across the first four days of Clean Week).
[^629]: Heirmos of Ode 2 of the Great Canon, citing the opening of the Second Canticle of Moses (Deut. 32:1: "Give heed, O heaven, and I will speak; and let the earth hear the words of my mouth").
[^630]: Heirmos of Ode 3 of the Great Canon, drawing upon the Canticle of Hannah (1 Kings [1 Sam.] 2:2: "There is none holy as the Lord, and there is none righteous as our God").
[^631]: Heirmos of Ode 4 of the Great Canon, referencing the Canticle of the Prophet Habakkuk (Hab. 3:2: "O Lord, I have heard Thy report, and was afraid; O Lord, I considered Thy works, and was astonished").
[^632]: The Slavonic text glosses the Hebrew name Esau/Edom (Gen. 25:30) as "Раждеже́нїе" (Inflammation / Burning passion), drawing upon patristic etymological allegories of carnal concupiscence.

[^636]: Heirmos of Ode 7 of the Great Canon, citing the Prayer of the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian Furnace (Dan. 9:5; Song of the Three Youths 1:6–7: "We have sinned, we have transgressed, and have done wickedly").
[^637]: Heirmos of Ode 8 of the Great Canon, drawing upon the Song of the Three Holy Children (Dan. 3:57–88: "Bless the Lord, all ye works of the Lord; sing and highly exalt Him unto all ages").
[^638]: Heirmos of Ode 9 of the Great Canon, invoking the Canticles of the New Testament (Magnificat, Luke 1:46–55; Benedictus, Luke 1:68–79) praising the Virginal Incarnation.

[^685]: Feast rubric: If the First and Second Finding of the Precious Head of the Forerunner (February 24) or the Feast of the Holy Forty Martyrs of Sebaste (March 9) occurs on this day, the appointed Epistle and Gospel are read after "Let my prayer be set forth." The Ruthenian phrase *кратъ обереты* represents the local calendar and canonical formula for an occurring or concurring feast.
[^686]: The Cherubic Hymn of the Presanctified Liturgy (*Ныне силы небесныя*, "Now the Powers of Heaven"), replacing the Cherubikon of the Liturgies of St. John Chrysostom and St. Basil the Great.
[^687]: Prostration at the Great Entrance: Unlike the standard Liturgy, the faithful and chanters fall flat upon their faces (*ницъ падше*) in godly adoration (*поклоненіе богоподобное* / *latreia*), because the Holy Gifts are already consecrated and presanctified.
[^688]: The term *Anaphora* (*Анафоро*) is used here as a liturgical gloss for the *Aer* (*Воздухъ*), explicitly noting Palestinian and Jerusalem provenance (*въ странахъ Іерлимскихъ*).
[^689]: Elevation without lifting (*не воздвизаетъ*): At the Presanctified Liturgy, the priest touches the Holy Bread beneath the veil without elevating It high, since the sacrificial offering took place when It was consecrated on Sunday.
[^690]: Post-communion ablution (*ukrop*, warm water) and the ancient Thanksgiving hymn (*Благодаримъ тя, Христе Боже нашъ...*), a traditional variant preserved in pre-Nikonian Kyivan and South-Slavic liturgical witnesses alongside *Да исполнятся уста наша*.
[^691]: Fasting rubric: Strict xerophagy (*сухояденіе*) is appointed on Wednesday evening after the Presanctified Liturgy, consisting of nuts, figs, dates or dried fruit (*аниды*), and olives, with warm water and honey (*укропъ с медомъ*). On Thursday, the brethren maintain an unbroken fast until Friday afternoon.

For the birth of God renews natures; therefore all generations magnify thee in orthodox manner as the Mother of God!" (cf. Luke 1:48).
