[^768]: The Slavonic incipit "Внегда̀ възва́хъ" indicates Psalm 4 ("When I called, the God of my righteousness heard me"), marking the transition to the concluding section of Great Compline.

[^769]: Internal codex cross-reference: "Оу҆ка́зъ, Зри лї́, рѻ҃в" (f. 172r, corresponding to physical leaf p178 in Cohort 18), which details the standard weekday order for Great Lent at Matins through the Kathismata and Biblical Odes.

[^770]: Rubric specifies chanting the Canon from the Menaion for the day's commemorated saint to 6 troparia, followed by the Triodion canons (here the Triodion comprises two complementary canons for Odes 5, 8, and 9, by St. Joseph the Hymnographer and St. Theodore the Studite). The biblical canticles from the Psalter are chanted in the customary Lenten order.

[^771]: Heirmos of Ode 5 (Tone 1) by St. Joseph the Hymnographer. Incipit: "Просвѣще́нїе въ тмѣ̀" (cf. Isa. 9:2; 26:9). Full text restored: "O Light in darkness, Savior of the hopeless, Christ Who by Thy Cross didst illumine our souls: unto Thee do we rise early, and we hymn Thee, O only Lover of mankind!"

[^772]: Heirmos of Ode 5 (Tone 1) of the second Canon, composed by St. Theodore the Studite. Incipit: "Но́щи мимоше́дшей" (cf. Rom. 13:12). Full text: "The night having passed, the day has drawn near, and light has shone forth upon the world; therefore the angelic ranks hymn Thee, and glorify Thee, O Lord!"

[^773]: Heirmos of Ode 8 (Tone 1) by St. Joseph the Hymnographer. Incipit: "Въ пещи ѻ҆́гненной" (cf. Dan. 3:57). Full text: "In the fiery furnace the children of Israel, as in a crucible, shone more brightly than gold with the beauty of piety, saying: 'All ye works of the Lord, bless the Lord, hymn and exalt Him above all unto all ages!'" (Song of the Three Youths).

[^774]: Heirmos of Ode 8 (Tone 1) by St. Theodore the Studite. Incipit: "Въ кꙋпинѣ̀ Мѡѷсе́ꙋ" (cf. Exod. 3:2). Full text: "In the bush unto Moses, the Virgin's childbearing upon Mount Sinai was prefigured of old: sing, bless, and exalt Him above all unto all ages!"

[^775]: Heirmos of Ode 9 (Tone 1) by St. Joseph the Hymnographer. Incipit: "Пре́жде со́лнца Свѣти́[ло]" (cf. Luke 1:48). Full text: "The Luminary before the sun, God Who shone forth bodily unto us from a Virgin's womb ineffably: O blessed and all-pure one, thee do we magnify as the Theotokos!"

[^776]: Heirmos of Ode 9 (Tone 1) by St. Theodore the Studite. Incipit: "Чⷭ҇тꙋю и҆ прⷭ҇тꙋю" (The pure and most pure). Full text: "The pure and most pure Mother and Virgin let all us faithful magnify in songs of praise as the Mother of God!"

[^777]: In the 1640 Kyiv edition, the red rubric "Свѣти́лна Гла́съ" directs that the Lenten Exaposteilarion (Exapostilarion) be sung in the Tone of the week, followed by the usual Lenten refrains and dismissals.

[^778]: Rubric regarding the Menaion commemoration: on the first Saturday of Great Lent (the commemoration of the Great Martyr Theodore the Recruit and the Miracle of the Kolyva), the commemoration of any minor Menaion saint occurring on Saturday or Sunday is transferred to Compline or sung at the discretion of the Ecclesiarch, so as not to displace the solemn festival of St. Theodore.

[^779]: Rubric directing that at Lord, I have cried on Friday evening (opening the commemoration of St. Theodore the Recruit), 4 Martyrika from the Oktoechos are taken following the Idiomelon of the day, before the 4 Idiomela of St. Theodore.

[^780]: St. Theodore the Recruit (Tyro) is celebrated for numerous posthumous miracles recounted in his encomium by St. Gregory of Nyssa and liturgical tradition: recovering runaway or stolen slaves, revealing stolen property, healing the sick, delivering prisoners, protecting sailors, and delivering the widow's son from Saracen captivity.

[^781]: In the 1640 Kyiv edition, the rubric for the Doxastikon of the Great Martyr Theodore on Friday evening commemorates the historical miracle of the Kolyva under Emperor Julian the Apostate (AD 361–363). Seeking to defile the Christians of Constantinople, Julian commanded the prefect of the city to sprinkle all foodstuffs sold in the public markets during the first week of Great Lent with the blood of pagan sacrifices. The Great Martyr Theodore appeared in a dream to Archbishop Eudoxius of Constantinople, warning him of the plot and instructing the faithful to refrain from buying at the markets and instead boil their household stores of wheat (kolyvo) with honey, thus preserving the sanctity of the fast.

[^782]: A compositorial misprint occurs in the 1640 Kyiv edition on physical leaf 291 (f. 277r), where the rubric gives the Prokeimenon heading as "Ѱало́мъ, д҃і" (Psalm 14) with a titlo. The incipit and verse printed immediately beneath ("Оу҆слы́шитъ тѧ̀ Гдⷭ҇ь въ дн҃ь печа́ли... Защити́тъ тѧ̀ и҆́мѧ Бг҃а І҆а́кѡвлѧ"), however, are Psalm 19:2–3 LXX (MT Ps. 20:1–2). The numeral д҃і (14) is a typographical error for ѳ҃і (19).

[^783]: Leaf 292 (f. 278v) contains a full-page-width woodcut engraving from the Kyiv Lavra workshop illustrating the narrative of Genesis 2–3. The left section depicts the creation of Eve from the rib of Adam as he sleeps beneath a fruit-laden tree, with the Slavonic inscriptions "СЪЗДАНИЄ ЄVЫ" (Creation of Eve) and "АДАМ" (Adam). The central composition depicts the serpent coiled about the Tree of Knowledge, whispering to Eve as she offers the forbidden fruit to Adam, labeled "EVA" and "ADAM". The upper right depicts an archangel with a flaming sword driving the weeping Adam and Eve through the arched gate of Eden, inscribed "ИЗГНАНИЄ ИЗ РАЯ" (Expulsion from Paradise). The lower right shows the post-lapsarian condition: Adam cultivating rocky ground with a wooden spade and Eve seated nursing an infant beside a wattle fence, with an inscribed scroll containing Genesis 3:19: "С ПОТЄМ ЛИЦА СНЄСИ ХЛЄБ ТВОЙ" (In the sweat of thy face shalt thou eat bread).

[^784]: Prokeimenon following the First Reading: Psalm 20:14, 2 LXX (MT Ps. 21:13, 1), Tone 4. In Byzantine and Kyivan usage, the verses are taken from the royal psalm celebrating divine triumph and salvation.

[^785]: The celebration of the Molieben (Supplicatory Canon) unto the Great Martyr Theodore and the blessing of the Kolyva after the Ambo Prayer of the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts on Friday of the First Week is one of the most distinctive liturgical rites of Great Lent. The Kolyva (boiled wheat mixed with honey, raisins, and dried fruits) serves as a living liturgical memorial of the deliverance of the Christians from Julian the Apostate's contamination.

[^786]: Troparion to the Great Martyr Theodore, Tone 2: Ве́лїѧ вѣ́ры исправле́нїѧ... ("Great are the achievements of faith..."). This troparion recalls St. Theodore's martyrdom in the fiery furnace at Amasea (c. AD 306), where he yielded his spirit without his body being consumed, offered unto the Holy Trinity as fragrant and sweet bread.

[^787]: The Supplicatory Canon to the Great Martyr Theodore is attributed to St. John of Damascus (c. AD 676–749). In the original Byzantine hymnographic tradition, the acrostic of the canon forms the Greek phrase: Θεοῦ σε μέλπω τῶν δωреῶν ἐπώνυμον ("God's namesake of gifts I sing thee"), which is faithfully translated into Church Slavonic as: "Бж҃їй тѧ пою̀ да́рѡвъ тезоимени́те," playing upon the etymology of the martyr's name Theodoros (God's gift).

[^788]: Ode 5, Troparion 2 references the opening act of St. Theodore's martyrdom: according to his Passio, being commanded to offer sacrifice to Cybele (the pagan "mother of the gods"), St. Theodore set fire to her temple at Amasea during the night, reducing the sanctuary and its idol to ashes.

[^789]: Ode 7, Troparia 1–2 draw typological parallels between St. Theodore's bodily sufferings and the Passion and Entombment of Christ. After burning the pagan temple, the martyr was cast into a sealed subterranean dungeon to starve; according to his Life, Christ Himself and a choir of angels appeared in the dungeon, nourishing him with heavenly bread and filling the prison with light and melodious song.

[^790]: Internal cross-reference in the 1640 Kyiv edition: "Ли́стъ, спа́" (f. 281r) refers to physical leaf 295 of the present codex, where the Troparion of St. Theodore (Ве́лїѧ вѣ́ры) is printed in full.

[^791]: Internal cross-reference in the 1640 Kyiv edition: "Ли́стъ, спд҃" (f. 284r) refers to physical leaf 298 of the present codex, where the Kontakion of St. Theodore (Вѣ́рꙋ Хв҃ꙋ ꙗ҆́кѡ щи́тъ) is printed in full.

[^792]: Internal cross-reference in the 1640 Kyiv edition: "Ли́стъ, с҃м" (f. 240r) refers to physical leaf 254 of the present codex (Wednesday of the First Week of Great Lent), where the rubric prescribes that after receiving the Holy Mysteries at the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts, the communicants partake of ukrop (warm water flavored with honey) to cleanse their mouths before eating the common meal.

[^793]: Comprehensive rubric governing the celebration of the divine services on Friday of the First Week in churches where the Divine Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts is not celebrated (e.g. non-cathedral or rural parish churches lacking a presbyter licensed or supplied with reserved Gifts). In such cases, the Lenten Ninth Hour is followed by the Typika and Dismissal, after which Great Vespers is chanted without an entrance; at the conclusion of Vespers, the priest vests in a festive crimson/purple phelonion and celebrates the full Molieben and blessing of the Kolyva as appointed.

[^794]: Physical leaf coordinate p300 (fol. 286v): Theotokion of the Ninth Ode at the Presanctified Liturgy on Friday of the First Week of Great Lent: "As a protectress of all and a mighty defense have we sinners acquired thee, O all-pure one: thy fervent help, O Virgin Mother Mary, the salvation of Christians! Therefore cease not to entreat the Savior on our behalf, that He may grant us forgiveness!"
[^795]: Church Slavonic and Ruthenian *арме́ѧ* (from Greek *ἁρμία*, pickled cabbage or cabbage preserved in brine/sauerkraut), an austere monastic staple permitted on strict fasting days when oil is prohibited.
[^796]: Slavonic rubric abbreviation *МК: гл: х҃г.* represents *Марковы главы* (Mark's Chapters, or Markian rubrics), Chapter 23 of the Typikon, governing the liturgical concurrence when the First and Second Finding of the Honorable Head of the Holy Prophet, Forerunner, and Baptist John (February 24) falls on the First Saturday of Great Lent (commemoration of the Holy Great-martyr Theodore the Tyro).
[^797]: The 13 great prostrations (bows to the earth) customary at the Lenten Hours and Presanctified Liturgy, accompanying the Prayer of Saint Ephrem the Syrian and the dismissal prayers.
[^798]: Psalm 63:11 (LXX 63:11 / MT 64:10): "The righteous man shall be glad in the Lord, and shall hope in Him."
[^799]: Psalm 30 (LXX 30 / MT 31): "In Thee, O Lord, have I hoped; let me never be put to shame," chanted during the procession into the monastic narthex (*pritvor*) at the conclusion of Matins.
[^800]: Church Slavonic *в Гробни́ци* (in the Crypt or Charnel Chapel/Tomb), designating the subterranean burial chapel or crypt of the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra where the memorial service and funeral canons were sung for the departed brethren.
[^801]: Physical leaf coordinate p296 (fol. 282r): Theotokion "All thy mysteries are beyond comprehension" (Tone 2), appointed after "God is the Lord" on Friday of the First Week of Great Lent.
[^802]: Kathisma 16 of the Psalter, comprising Psalms 109–117 (LXX 109–117 / MT 110–118), opening with Psalm 109: "The Lord said unto my Lord: Sit Thou at My right hand."
[^803]: The Amomos (Church Slavonic *Непоро́чны*), Kathisma 17 of the Psalter (Psalm 118 [LXX 118 / MT 119]), chanted at Saturday Matins throughout Great Lent.
[^804]: Melodic model *Tone 3, Podoben: "Of the divine faith"* (Greek: *Θείας πίστεως*), automelon for the commemoration of martyrs and confessors.
[^805]: The Slavonic acrostic (*Краегране́сіе*) translates the Greek acrostic *Τύρων ὁ κλεινός, πρῶτον εὖχος σαββάτων* ("Tyro the renowned, the first glory of Saturdays"). The spelling *Ти́рсѡнъ* (*Tyrson*) is an epigraphic Kyivan scribal variant for *Тѵ́ρωνъ* (*Tyron* / Saint Theodore Tyro).
[^806]: Saint John Mauropous, Metropolitan of Euchaita (c. 1000–c. 1075), noted Byzantine hymnographer, scholar, and author of the Canons for Saint Theodore Tyro and the Three Holy Hierarchs.
[^807]: The Second Canon of Saint Theodore Tyro, likewise composed by Saint John Mauropous of Euchaita, in Tone 6, sung concurrently with the First Canon.
[^808]: Flavius Claudius Julianus, Roman Emperor (reigned 361–363), known historically as Julian the Apostate (*Юліанъ Отступникъ*), who attempted to restore paganism and defile Christian Lenten fasts with blood from sacrificial offerings.
[^809]: Melodic model *Tone 8, Podoben: "Of Wisdom and the Word"* (Greek: *Τὴν Σοφίαν καὶ Λόγον*), automelon for the commemoration of martyrs and venerable fathers.
[^810]: Marcus Aurelius Valerius Maximianus Herculius, Roman Emperor and co-Augustus with Diocletian (reigned 286–305), under whose reign Saint Theodore Tyro was martyred at Amasea in Pontus c. 306.
[^811]: Saint Nectarius, Patriarch of Constantinople (served 381–397), predecessor of Saint John Chrysostom, to whom the historical oration on the miracle of Saint Theodore and the Kolyva is traditionally ascribed.
[^812]: Scriptural allusion to the Passover lamb and the Crucifixion (Exod. 12:46, Num. 9:12, Ps. 33:21 [LXX 33:21 / MT 34:20], John 19:36): "A bone of Him shall not be broken," typologically fulfilled in Christ and reflected in His martyrs whose relics remain preserved.
[^813]: Etymological wordplay on the name Theodore (*Теодор* / *Феодор*), derived from Greek *Θεόδωρος* (*theos* "God" + *dōron* "gift"), denoting "the gift of God."
[^814]: Eudoxius of Antioch, Arian Archbishop of Constantinople (served 360–370). Although an Arian, he was the bishop in office when Saint Theodore appeared in a vision to warn the Christians against eating the food defiled by Julian.
[^815]: Scriptural allusion to Matthew 9:15 and Mark 2:19: "Can the children of the bridechamber fast, while the Bridegroom is with them?" applied here to the Saturday feast in the midst of Great Lent.
[^816]: Iambic verses prefacing the Synaxarion, celebrating Saint Theodore Tyro feeding the city of Constantinople with boiled wheat (*kolyva*) to frustrate the emperor's plot.
[^817]: In historical chronology, Julian the Apostate succeeded Constantius II (Flavius Julius Constantius, reigned 337–361), son of Saint Constantine the Great.
[^818]: Ruthenian *Ста́роста мѣ́стский* renders the Greek *ἔπαρχος τῆς πόлеως* (Eparch / Urban Prefect of Constantinople), the imperial civil administrator responsible for the municipal markets.
[^819]: Marginal citation: *Нектⷶ: ѻ҃: б҃. мелиф҃: фір҃зı.* referencing Saint Nectarius of Constantinople, *Homily II on the Miracles of Saint Theodore Tyro*, collated with patristic florilegia.
[^820]: Scriptural citation of Job 5:13 ("He takes the wise in their own craftiness") and 1 Corinthians 3:19 ("For the wisdom of this world is foolishness with God. For it is written: He takes the wise in their craftiness").

[^821]: Eudoxius of Antioch, Arian Archbishop of Constantinople (served 360–370). The Synaxarion highlights the historical irony that although Eudoxius was an Arian, Saint Theodore Tyro appeared unto him in a waking vision (*на явѣ не пре сонъ*) rather than a dream, precisely because the impending pollution of the Christian fast concerned the entire body of the Church in the imperial capital.
[^822]: Ruthenian vernacular theological discourse (*проста мова*) in the 1640 Kyiv text: the Archbishop's perplexity is designated by the Ruthenian term *вонтпливость* (cognate with Polish *wątpliwość*), and the Martyr explicitly defines *kolyva* (*Колива*) as boiled wheat according to the dialect of Euchaita in Pontus (*варенаѧ пшеница, так бовемъ мы въ Евхаїтскихъ сторонахъ мовити звыклиємо*), which the Kyiv narrator glosses with the traditional Ukrainian/Ruthenian term *Куті́я* (*Кутїе́ю чтимъ и почита́емъ*).
[^823]: Historical martyrdom of Saint Theodore Tyro under Caesar Maximian (Galerius Valerius Maximianus, Caesar from 293, Augustus 305–311) and the regional governor/prefect Vrinkas (Church Slavonic *Вринга*, historical Puplius / Charisius), governor of Amasea in Pontus c. 306, who ordered the martyr tortured and burned after Theodore set fire to the temple of the pagan mother-goddess Cybele.
[^824]: Codicological lacuna: In the 1640 Kyiv Triodion, physical leaf p311 bears printed page number 297 (recto) with the catchword *ина-*, while physical leaf p312 bears printed page number 299 (recto). Printed page 298 (the verso of leaf 297) is omitted in this exemplar/scan, carrying away the conclusion of the Synaxarion of Saint Theodore Tyro and the opening of Ode 7 of the Canon down to the phrase *[нечести́ваго] совѣ́та*.
[^825]: The concluding fragment of the final Troparion before *Glory* in Ode 7 of the Second Canon of Saint Theodore Tyro (Tone 6, by St. John Mauropous), completing the missing verses from omitted printed page 298: "[...of the impious] counsel; for the King Who reigns over all, the Lord of all, has broken thy might!"
[^826]: Melodic model of the First Canon of Saint Theodore Tyro, Ode 8, Heirmos: "The Redeemer of all" (Tone 4, Greek: *Λυτρωτὰ τοῦ παντός*), composed by Saint John Mauropous, Metropolitan of Euchaita.
[^827]: Scriptural and typological allusion to the Sabbath rest (Gen. 2:2–3, Heb. 4:9–10): the First Saturday of Great Lent is celebrated as the "Sabbath of Sabbaths" (*сꙋббо́та сꙋббо́тамъ*), transforming the austere fast into a spiritual feast through the triumph of the Martyr.
[^828]: Melodic model of the Second Canon of Saint Theodore Tyro, Ode 8, Heirmos: "Out of the flame for the holy ones" (Tone 6, Greek: *Ἐκ фλογὸς τοῖς Ὁσίοις*), automelon for the eighth ode.
[^829]: Scriptural citation of Psalm 29:6 (LXX 29:6 / MT 30:5): "Weeping may tarry for the evening, but joy comes in the morning," applied typologically to the evening distress of the Christians of Constantinople under Julian's edict and their morning deliverance through the appearance of Saint Theodore.
[^830]: Melodic model of the First Canon of Saint Theodore Tyro, Ode 9, Heirmos: "Thy birthgiving was incorrupt" (Tone 4, Greek: *Ἡ ἄфθορος διαфθοράν* or *Ἄσπορος σύλληψις* / Church Slavonic *Рожество̀ ти нетлѣ́нно*).
[^831]: Melodic model of the Second Canon of Saint Theodore Tyro, Ode 9, Heirmos: "It is not possible for men to behold God" (Tone 6, Greek: *Θεὸν ἀνθρώποις ἰδεῖν ἀδύνατον*), automelon for the ninth ode.
[^832]: Melodic model *Podoben: "With the Disciples"* (Greek: *Τοῖς Μαθηταῖς συνέλθωμεν*), automelic prosomoion for the Exapostilarion (Svutilen).
[^833]: Melodic model *Tone 1, Podoben: "Joy of the heavenly ranks"* (Greek: *Τῶν οὐρανίων ταγμάτων*, Church Slavonic *Нб҃нымъ чи́нѡмъ*), automelon for the Praises (Lauds) on feasts of martyrs and venerable fathers.
[^834]: Theological and etymological wordplay on the name Theodore (*Теодор* / *Феодор*), derived from Greek *Θεόδωρος* (*theos* "God" + *dōron* "gift"), denoting "the gift of God" (*да́ре Бж҃їй*).
[^835]: Doxasticon at the Praises, Tone 6, composed by Saint John Mauropous, Metropolitan of Euchaita: "A gift of consecration and wealth of divine life hast thou appeared unto the world, O Theodore."
[^836]: Stichera on the Aposticha (*На стиховнѣ*), Tone 4, Automela (*Самогласны*), linking the spiritual discipline and purification of Great Lent with the victorious athletic contest (*подвигъ*) of the Holy Great-martyr Theodore Tyro against demonic deception.
[^837]: Psalm 91:13 (LXX 91:13 / MT 92:12): "The righteous man shall flourish like a palm tree; he shall be increased like the cedar in Lebanon."
[^838]: Psalm 91:14 (LXX 91:14 / MT 92:13): "Planted in the house of the Lord, in the courts of our God."
[^839]: Liturgical lections for the First Saturday of Great Lent: At the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom, the Epistle of the day is Hebrews, Pericope 303 (Heb. 1:1–12), with Gospel of the day from Mark, Pericope 10 (Mark 2:23–3:5); for Saint Theodore Tyro, the Epistle is 2 Timothy, Pericope 292 (2 Tim. 2:1–10), and the Gospel is John, Pericope 52 (John 15:17–16:2).
[^840]: Patristic refectory reading appointed in the Kyivan Lavra Typikon: Saint Gregory of Nyssa's celebrated *First Oration on the Great-martyr Theodore* (*De magno martyre Theodoro*, PG 46:735–748), delivered at the martyr's shrine in Euchaita c. 381.
[^841]: Monastic dietary legislation (*Трапеза*) of the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra derived from the coenobitic Typika of Saint Sabbas the Sanctified and Saint Euthymius the Great: boiled fava beans (*фава обварена*), olives, and kolyva are appointed; wine is permitted by measure (*krasovolion* / *красоволя*); and fish is strictly prohibited during Great Lent with the sole exceptions of the Feast of the Annunciation of the Theotokos (March 25) and Flowery/Palm Sunday (*Цвѣтоно́сїе*).
[^842]: Full-page woodcut icon engraving (fol. 305r, physical leaf p318) executed by the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra engraving workshop for the First Sunday of Great Lent (Sunday of Orthodoxy). The architectural plate depicts Christ Pantocrator in majesty holding the open Gospel (Rev. 3:21: "To him that overcoms will I grant to sit with Me in My throne..."), flanked by medallions of Saint Theodore Tyro, Saint George, the Apostles Peter and Paul, and the Evangelists; while in the lower arcade, the founders of Kyivan monasticism, Saint Theodosius and Saint Anthony of the Caves, stand venerating the Seventh Ecumenical Council (Nicaea II, 787).
[^843]: Landmark title headpiece in monumental red cinnabar vyaz' lettering marking the First Sunday of Great Lent: The Sunday of Orthodoxy (*Нд҃лѧ Пе́рваѧ Ст҃го По́ста, в ню́же правосла́вїе воспомина́етсѧ*), instituted in Constantinople in 843 under Empress Theodora and Patriarch Methodius to commemorate the definitive triumph of Orthodoxy over Iconoclasm.
[^844]: Stichera at Small Vespers on "Lord, I have cried", composed by Nicholas the Hymnographer (*Творе́нїе Нїкола́а*), Melodic model *Tone 1, Podoben: "Joy of the heavenly ranks"* (Greek: *Τῶν οὐρανίων ταγμάτων*).
[^845]: Saint Basil the Great, *On the Holy Spirit* (*De Spiritu Sancto*), XVIII, 45 (PG 32:149C): "The honor rendered unto the icon passes over unto the Prototype" (*Ἡ γὰρ τῆς εἰκόνος τιμὴ ἐπὶ τὸ πρωτότυπον διαβαίνει*), the foundational patristic prooftext dogmatically endorsed by the Seventh Ecumenical Council (Nicaea II, 787) in defense of the veneration of holy icons.
[^846]: Small Vespers Theotokion affirming the Christological foundation of icon veneration: the uncircumscribable, beginningless divine Word became truly visible and depictable in human flesh through His Incarnation from the precious blood of the Virgin Mary.
[^847]: Melodic model *Tone 2, Podoben: "O House of Ephratha"* (Greek: *Οἶκος τοῦ Ἐфραθᾶ*, Church Slavonic *До́ме Є҆ѵфра́ѳовъ* / *До́ме Є҆ѵфра́ѳ*), automelon for stichera on the Aposticha.
[^848]: Synodal anathematization of classical heresies: Arius (denying the consubstantiality of the Son), Macedonius (denying the divinity of the Holy Spirit), Peter the Fuller / Peter Mongus (Syrian/Alexandrian Monophysitism), Severus of Antioch (Monophysitism), and Pyrrhus I of Constantinople (Monothelitism).
[^849]: Pious Byzantine Empress Theodora (c. 815–867), wife of Emperor Theophilus, who as regent for her son Michael III, in collaboration with Holy Patriarch Methodius, restored the veneration of holy icons and established the annual Feast of Orthodoxy on the First Sunday of Great Lent in 843.
[^850]: The universal Troparion (Troparion) of the Holy and Undefiled Icon of Christ (*Пречи́стому Твоему́ О́бразу покланя́емся, Благи́й*), Tone 2, chanted at all services of the Sunday of Orthodoxy and at the blessing of icons.
[^851]: Great Vespers rubrics for the Sunday of Orthodoxy appointing the full chanting of the First Kathisma of the Psalter ("Blessed is the man", Psalms 1–8) and 10 Stichera on "Lord, I have cried": 3 Resurrectional and 3 Anatolian stichera from the Octoechos (Tone 6), and 4 Idiomela/Prosomoia from the Triodion.
[^852]: Melodic model *Tone 6, Podoben: "Having laid aside all earthly hope"* (Greek: *Ὅλην ἀποθέμενοι*, Church Slavonic *Всѐ въхло́жше* / *Всѐ ѿло́жше*), automelon for the Triodion stichera celebrating the prophetic vision of the Old Testament prophets who foretold the bodily manifestation and Resurrection of the Son of God.

[^853]: Melodic model *Tone 6, Podoben: "Having laid aside all earthly hope"* (Greek: *Ὅλην ἀποθέμενοι*, Church Slavonic *Всѐ въхло́жше* / *Всѐ ѿло́жше*), concluding the stichera on "Lord, I have cried" for the Prophets who refused to worship the creature rather than the Creator (cf. Rom. 1:25) and foretold the voluntary Passion of Christ.
[^854]: Foundational Christological argument of Orthodox iconology formulated by Saint John of Damascus (*Apologetic Treatises against those who Decry the Holy Images*) and Saint Theodore the Studite (*Antirrhetics*): although the Son of God is uncircumscribable in His divine nature (*Е҆стество́мъ неопи́санный*), by taking true human flesh in the Incarnation He voluntarily assumed all natural human properties, thereby rendering Himself visible and depictable in holy icons.
[^855]: Doxasticon at "Lord, I have cried" on the First Sunday of Great Lent, Tone 2: The shadows of the Old Law and the Tabernacle of Testimony (Heb. 9:1–11, 10:1) find their visible realization in the incarnate Image of Christ, wherein the icon is venerated relatively (*блг҃чтнѡ покланѧ́емый*) rather than adored as God (*а҆ не бг҃отвори́мый*).
[^856]: Stichera at the Litiya, Tone 2: The Old Testament prophets are commemorated on the Sunday of Orthodoxy as mediators of the New Covenant (Heb. 8:6, 9:15) who prophetically delineated the economy of salvation fulfilled in the Incarnation.
[^857]: Aposticha Doxasticon, Tone 2: Foundational patristic prooftext citing Saint Basil the Great, *On the Holy Spirit* (*De Spiritu Sancto*), XVIII, 45 (PG 32:149C): "The honor rendered unto the icon passes over unto the Prototype" (*Ἡ γὰρ τῆς εἰκόνος τιμὴ ἐπὶ τὸ πρωτότυπον διαβαίνει*), dogmatically confirmed by the Seventh Ecumenical Council (Nicaea II, 787).
[^858]: Mark's Chapter (*Ма́ркова глава̀*): A regulatory Typikon rubric governing the concurrence (*стече́нїе*) of the First Sunday of Great Lent with the First and Second Finding of the Honorable Head of Saint John the Baptist (February 24) or the Holy Forty Martyrs of Sebaste (March 9), directing that the festal service be combined according to the rubrics appointed for the Third Sunday of Lent (Sunday of the Veneration of the Holy Cross).
[^859]: Internal codicological cross-references in the 1640 Kyiv Triodion: The Troparion of the Holy Icon (*Пречи́стому Твоему́ О́бразу*) is appointed on printed fol. 307r (leaf p320); the Sunday Theotokion (*Всѧ паче смысла*) on printed fol. 282r (leaf p295); and the Polyeleos and Megalynarion rubrics on printed fol. 154v (Cheesefare Sunday, leaf p167).
[^860]: First Canon of the Sunday of Orthodoxy, Tone 4, composed by Saint Theophanes the Branded (*Ѳеофа́нъ Начерта́нный* / *Theophanes Graptos*, c. 778–845), Metropolitan of Nicaea, celebrated hymnographer and confessor under the iconoclast emperors.
[^861]: Acrostic of the First Canon of the Sunday of Orthodoxy by Saint Theophanes: *Дне́сь блг҃чтїѧ бл҃говѣ́щаннаѧ прїи́де свѣ́тлость* (Greek: *Σήμερον εὐσεβείης θεοφεγγέος ἦλθεν αἴγλη*).
[^862]: Pious Byzantine Empress Theodora (c. 815–867) and her young son Michael III (reigned as emperor 842–867), under whose imperial authority the holy icons were restored and the annual Feast of Orthodoxy was established in Hagia Sophia on the First Sunday of Lent, March 11, 843.
[^863]: Katavasia of Ode 1: Automelic heirmos of the Red Sea (*Мо́рѧ чермна́гѡ пꙋчи́нꙋ*), typologically linking Israel's dry-shod crossing and Moses' outstretched arms forming the sign of the Cross (Ex. 17:11–12) to the spiritual triumph of the Church over heretical delusion.
[^864]: Sessional Hymn (Sessional Hymn), Melodic model *Tone 1, Podoben: "The angelic choir"* (Greek: *Χορὸς Ἀγγελικός*, Church Slavonic *Ли́къ А҆́гг҃лскїй*), automelon for Matins kathisma hymns.
[^865]: Scriptural and liturgical trope of the exaltation of the horn (1 Sam. 2:10, Ps. 131:17 / LXX 131:17): petition for divine strength and victory granted unto Orthodox Christian monarchs defending the true faith.
[^866]: Kontakion of the Sunday of Orthodoxy, Tone 8, Automelon (*Самогла́сенъ*): "The uncircumscribable Word of the Father" (*Неѡписа́нное сло́во Ѻ҆ц҃ее*), affirming that the divine Word, in taking circumscribed flesh from the Theotokos, restored the fallen and defiled image of God in man unto its original beauty.
[^867]: Ikos of the Sunday of Orthodoxy: The mystery of the divine economy (*смотре́нїа та́инство*, Greek *οἰκονομία*) foretold by the holy Prophets for those living at the culmination of the ages (cf. 1 Cor. 10:11, Eph. 1:10), celebrated in the one faith, one baptism, and the depiction of holy icons.
[^868]: Baroque Polish/Ruthenian-influenced syllabic couplet (*вірші*) heading the Synaxarion for the Sunday of Orthodoxy: contrasting the dishonorable expulsion of the holy icons under the iconoclasts with the joyful and honorable restoration of their liturgical veneration.
[^869]: Full-page woodcut engraving heading the Synaxarion of the Sunday of Orthodoxy (fol. 314r, physical leaf p327): The composition depicts the Seventh Ecumenical Council (Nicaea II, 787) and the Council of Constantinople (843), showing young Emperor Michael III and Empress Theodora beneath an architectural baldachin holding icons of Christ Pantocrator and the Hodegetria, flanked by holy hierarchs in omophoria, with the assembly of the Holy Fathers seated in synod in the lower register.
[^870]: Early Modern Ruthenian (*проста мова*) historical narrative of the Synaxarion for the First Sunday of Great Lent, recounting the historical chronicle of the Iconoclast controversy from Leo III the Isaurian (717) to the definitive restoration under Empress Theodora and Patriarch Methodius I (843).
[^871]: George Kedrenos (*Ке́дрї, ро: кн: г҃.*), 11th-century Byzantine chronicler (*Synopsis Historiarum*, PG 121–122): source for the rise of Leo III the Isaurian (originally named Konon), who began as a peasant and donkey-driver, and his opening dispute with Saint Germanus I, Patriarch of Constantinople (715–730), demanding that icons either be removed or elevated out of kissing reach.
[^872]: The forced abdication and exile of Saint Germanus I of Constantinople to his ancestral estate at Platanion in January 730, after refusing to sign the imperial decree against holy icons, and the unlawful elevation of the iconoclast syncellus Anastasios (730–754).
[^873]: Popular Byzantine legend recorded in the chronicles of Saint Theophanes the Confessor (*Chronographia*, AM 6215) and John Zonaras (*Epitome Historiarum*, XV, 2): Jewish sorcerers in Phoenicia/Isauria who allegedly foretold Leo's elevation to the imperial throne and extracted a vow that he would destroy images once established in power.
[^874]: Byzantine polemical designation of Emperor Constantine V (reigned 741–775): pejoratively surnamed *Copronymus* (Greek: *Κοπρώνυμος*, Ruthenian: *Гноєнме́нный*, "the Dung-named") because he allegedly defecated in the baptismal font as an infant; chronicling his violent persecution of monastics and the iconoclast Council of Hieria (754).
[^875]: Empress Irene (regent for her son Constantine VI, 780–790; sole empress 797–802) and Saint Tarasios, Patriarch of Constantinople (784–806), who convoked the Seventh Ecumenical Council (Nicaea II, 787) to formulate the dogmatic definition on the veneration of holy icons.
[^876]: Outbreak of the Second Iconoclast Crisis under Emperor Leo V the Armenian (reigned 813–820): citing Kedrenos' narrative concerning the hermit of Philomelion, his disciple the monk Sabbatios, and Theodore Melissenos, who deceived the emperor by staging a prophetic encounter in the Monastery of the Dexiokrates.
[^877]: Reign of Emperor Michael II the Amorian (820–829), cited from John Zonaras' *Epitome Historiarum* (Book XV): recounting his imprisonment of Saint Methodius in a tomb on the island of Saint Andrew (Akritas) and the brutal martyrdom of Saint Euthymius, Metropolitan of Sardis, who was scourged with sinews until he died.
[^878]: Emperor Theophilus (reigned 829–842): celebrated in Byzantine historiography for his incorruptible administration of civic justice and daily inspection of markets, but notorious for the ruthless persecution of iconodules, including the torture and facial branding of Saints Theodore and Theophanes the Graptoi.
[^879]: Traditional account of Theophilus' mortal agony in early 842: suffering from severe dysentery (*бѣ́гꙋнка*), his mouth opened unnaturally wide so that his throat and internal organs were visible; Empress Theodora experienced a vision of the Mother of God, whereupon Theophilus repented, kissed an icon brought by an attendant, was healed of the gaping deformity, and died in peace.
[^880]: Deposition of the iconoclast Patriarch John VII the Grammarian (pejoratively surnamed *Jannes* after Pharaoh's magician, 2 Tim. 3:8; Ruthenian: *чаронача́лникъ*, "chief sorcerer"), the enthronement of Saint Methodius I the Confessor (March 843), and the prophetic revelation granted unto Saint Joannicius the Great on Mount Olympus in Bithynia confirming the restoration of the holy icons.

[^881]: Saint Joannicius the Great (c. 762–846) of Mount Olympus in Bithynia, Saint Arsakios, and Saint Isaiah the Recluse of Nicomedia: 9th-century monastic luminaries and confessors who provided charismatic and prophetic guidance during the final overthrow of the Iconoclast heresy and the restoration of holy icons under Empress Theodora and Patriarch Methodius I in 843.
[^882]: Early Modern Ruthenian (*проста мова*) theological vernacular formulating the dogmatic distinction between *latreia* (adoration and divine service due to God alone) and *proskynesis* (honorary, relative veneration rendered unto holy icons), defined by the Seventh Ecumenical Council (Nicaea II, 787).
[^883]: Historical gathering of prominent iconodule confessors in Hagia Sophia during the first week of Great Lent, 843: Saint Naukratios (c. 785–848), disciple and successor of Saint Theodore the Studite as abbot of the Studion; Saints Theophanes and Theodore the Branded (*Graptoi*), so called because twelve iambic verses were branded with hot irons upon their foreheads by Emperor Theophilus; Saint Michael the Syncellus of Jerusalem (c. 761–846); and Saint Euangelos (Euggelos).
[^884]: The Column of the Cross (*столпъ Крестный*), monumental landmark in the Forum of Constantine (*Forum Constantini*) in Constantinople, where Empress Theodora's visionary journey begins at dawn on Friday of the first week of Lent.
[^885]: The Bronze Gate (Chalke Gate, Greek *Χαλκῆ Πύλη*, Ruthenian *Мѣдяная брама*), monumental bronze vestibule of the Great Palace of Constantinople, historic site where Emperor Leo III the Isaurian had first ordered the destruction of the great icon of Christ the Savior (*Christos Chalkites*) in 726; here transfigured in Empress Theodora's vision as the tribunal of divine mercy where Christ pardons Emperor Theophilus.
[^886]: The pious tradition of Patriarch Methodius I writing the names of all the iconoclast emperors upon a new parchment roll (*ха́ртꙋ но́вꙋю*) and placing it beneath the Holy Table in Hagia Sophia, praying for the soul of Emperor Theophilus, whereupon an angel appeared announcing his forgiveness and Theophilus's name was found miraculously expunged from the scroll.
[^887]: The historic first Triumph of Orthodoxy procession (*Litiya*) on the First Sunday of Great Lent, March 11, 843, wherein Empress Theodora, young Emperor Michael III, Patriarch Methodius, the holy hierarchs, monastics, and people marched through the streets of Constantinople with the True Cross, holy icons, and Gospels chanting *Kyrie eleison*.
[^888]: The conciliar synod held in Constantinople in February–March 843, confirming the Seventh Ecumenical Council (787), anathemas against iconoclasts, and decreeing an annual liturgical celebration of the Triumph of Orthodoxy on the First Sunday of Great Lent.
[^889]: First Matins Canon for the Sunday of Orthodoxy, Tone 4, Ode 7: Automelic heirmos celebrating the three Persian youths descended from Abraham who were bedewed in the fiery furnace (Dan. 3:49–50).
[^890]: Ode 8, automelic heirmos *Stretching out his hands* (Dan. 6:22): celebrating the ancestral tradition and dogmas of the Church in depicting, honoring, and kissing the holy icons of Christ and the Saints.
[^891]: Foundational patristic canon of iconology citing Saint Basil the Great (*On the Holy Spirit*, XVIII, 45; PG 32:149C): "The honor rendered unto the icon passes over unto the Prototype," dogmatically affirmed at Nicaea II (787).
[^892]: Ode 9, automelic heirmos *A stone cut without hands from the unquarried mountain* (Dan. 2:34–35, 45): Christological and Marian typology of Christ the chief cornerstone born of the Virgin without human seed, uniting divine and human natures into one divine Person.
[^893]: Exapostilarion (Photogogikon) for the Sunday of Orthodoxy, Melodic model *Tone 2, Podoben: "With the disciples let us go up"* (Greek: *Τοῖς Μαθηταῖς συνέλθωμεν*, Church Slavonic: *Съ Оу҆чени́ки вни́демъ*), celebrating the cessation of iconoclast discord and the divinely ordered unity of the Church.
[^894]: Praises at Matins, Melodic model *Tone 4, Podoben: "Thou hast given a sign"* (Greek: *Ἔδωκας σημείωσιν*, Church Slavonic: *Да́лъ є҆сѝ знаме́нїе*): affirming against the docetic heresy of Manes (Manichaeism) that the Son of God was made man not in phantasm or semblance, but in genuine and visible human flesh.
[^895]: Idiomelon sticheron at the Praises, Tone 6: joining the Lenten fast to the heroic Old Testament ascetics Moses, Elijah, and the Three Youths of Babylon, petitioning Christ for worthy attainment of the Holy Resurrection.
[^896]: Pericopes for the Divine Liturgy on the First Sunday of Great Lent: Epistle to the Hebrews 11:24–26, 32–40 (the roll call of the Old Testament heroes and martyrs of faith); and Gospel according to Saint John 1:43–51 (the calling of Philip and Nathanael, Nathanael's confession, and Christ's prophecy of heaven opened and the angels of God ascending and descending upon the Son of Man).
[^897]: Monastic Typikon dietary rule for the First Sunday of Great Lent: permitting hot cooked food with olive oil (*варе́нїе съ є҆ле́емъ*) and two cups of wine at the main meal and at supper, while strictly prohibiting the consumption of fish during the Holy Forty Days except on the Annunciation and Palm Sunday.
[^898]: Ancient liturgical office of the Synodikon of Orthodoxy (*Послѣдованїе в недѣлю Православїя на прокля́тїе є҆ретикѡ́въ*): an extraordinary service celebrated after Matins, wherein clergy and faithful processed outside to an open square or conciliar assembly to chant the anathemas against heretics and the Canon of Saint Theodore the Studite.
[^899]: Canon for the Synodikon of Orthodoxy, Tone 6, composed by Saint Theodore the Studite (759–826), abbot of the Stoudios Monastery in Constantinople and principal theologian of the second iconoclast period.
[^900]: Anathemas against the second iconoclast triumvirate under Leo V and Theophilus: "Lyzix" (Greek: *Λυζῖκος* / *Lezix*, associated with the circle of John the Grammarian); Antony I Kassymatas, iconoclast Patriarch of Constantinople (821–837); John VII the Grammarian (Morocharzanios), iconoclast Patriarch (837–843); and Theodore Melissenos.
[^901]: Typological comparison from 2 Timothy 3:8 (cf. Ex. 7:11): likening the iconoclast leaders John the Grammarian and Antony Kassymatas to Jannes and Jambres, the Egyptian sorcerers who withstood Moses before Pharaoh.
[^902]: Explicit liturgical anathema in Ode 3 against the chief figures of the second iconoclasm: Lyzikos; Patriarch John VII the Grammarian; Patriarch Antony I Kassymatas; Theodore Melissenos; and Patriarch Theodotus I Kassiteras (815–821).
[^903]: Patristic jubilation at the cessation of the second iconoclasm: the reopening of desert hermitages and monasteries, the return of exiled monks from the wilderness, and the public restoration of the veneration of the relics and icons of the holy martyrs.
[^904]: Scriptural trope from Deuteronomy 32:32–33 ("For their vine is of the vine of Sodom... their wine is the venom of serpents, and the cruel poison of asps"), typologically applied to the soul-corrupting doctrine of the iconoclasts.
[^905]: Denunciation of uncanonical, simoniacal iconoclast ordinations, condemning Patriarch John VII the Grammarian and Antony Kassymatas as spiritual successors to Simon Magus (Acts 8:18–24).
[^906]: Dogmatic confession of Ode 5: distinguishing Christian relative veneration of icons of Christ, the Theotokos, and the Saints from idolatrous worship of deaf and mute idols (*глꙋхы́мъ і҆́долѡмъ*), grounded in the historical reality of the divine Incarnation.

[^907]: The chief heresiarchs of the second iconoclast period condemned in Ode 6: Theodotus I Kassiteras (patriarch 815–821), Antony I Kassymatas (patriarch 821–837), Theodore Krithinos, and Lyzikos (Lezix), who led the iconoclast synod of St. Sophia in 815 under Emperor Leo V the Armenian.
[^908]: Patriarch John VII the Grammarian (Morocharzanios, patriarch 837–843), designated *Christoborchos* ("fighter against Christ") for directing the imperial iconoclast persecution under Emperor Theophilus.
[^909]: Patristic typological parallel between Arius tearing Christ's seamless tunic (a famous revelation received by Hieromartyr Peter of Alexandria) and John the Grammarian tearing the robe of Christ's Church through iconoclasm.
[^910]: Ode 7 heirmos reference: "holding the praises throughout the four weeks" (*четверичными Седмицами*), referring to the four weeks of Pre-Lent and Lent leading to the triumph of the Orthodox confession.
[^911]: Historical accusations against John the Grammarian, whom iconodule hagiography accused of practicing dark arts, divination (*lekanomanteia*), and serving as the forerunner of the Antichrist.
[^912]: Greek philosophical and mythological polemic: the Studite hymnographer equates John the Grammarian's neo-Platonic and secular learning with pagan polytheism (Pythagoras, Kronos, Apollo).
[^913]: The monumental icon of Christ Chalkites upon the Bronze Gate (Chalke Gate) of the imperial palace in Constantinople, which was attacked, stoned, and taken down by iconoclasts under Leo V and John the Grammarian.
[^914]: The iconoclast rejection not only of holy icons but also of the sacred relics of the martyrs, reflecting the extreme iconoclast policy under Leo V and Theophilus.
[^915]: Longsuffering of God amid the martyrdoms of iconodule confessors, monks, and priests slain for defending the veneration of Christ's holy icon.
[^916]: Ode 9 solemn synodical anathema pronounced in the Canon of Saint Theodore the Studite against John the Grammarian, Antony Kassymatas, Theodore Krithinos, and Theodotus Kassiteras.
[^917]: Liturgical cross-reference in the Kyiv Triodion rubrics to printed folio 307 (physical page 319), where the primary troparion of the Holy Icon (*Пречистому Твоему образу покланяемся, Благий...* / *To Thy most pure image we bow down, O Good One...*) is printed in full.
[^918]: The Synodikon of Orthodoxy (*Соборное святаго вселенскаго Седмаго Собора* / *Предословіе*): the official dogmatic decree and conciliar declaration of the Seventh Ecumenical Council (Nicaea II, 787) and the Synod of Constantinople (843), promulgated by Empress Theodora and Patriarch Methodius I.
[^919]: The theological parallel between the Old Testament Feast of Dedication (*Encaenia*, Hanukkah; 1 Macc. 4:52–59; John 10:22) celebrating the purification and re-dedication of the Temple in Jerusalem, and the Christian Feast of the Restoration of the Holy Icons (*Triumph of Orthodoxy*).
[^920]: Psalm 88:51–52 (LXX versification): "the footsteps of Thine Anointed" (*τὸ ἀντάллаγμα τοῦ χριστοῦ σου*, rendered in Church Slavonic as *измѣненію Христа твоего*), interpreted Patristically as the redeemed members of Christ's Church who confess Him through both oral proclamation and sacred iconography.
[^921]: The first restoration of holy icons under Empress Irene and the Seventh Ecumenical Council (Nicaea II, 787), following the first iconoclast persecution inaugurated by Leo III the Isaurian (726) and Constantine V Kopronymos.
[^922]: The second restoration of holy icons in 843 under Empress Theodora and young Emperor Michael III, ending nearly thirty years of renewed persecution begun by Leo V the Armenian in 814/815.
[^923]: The fundamental Christological argument of Christian iconology contrasting the Old Covenant theophany at Mount Horeb (where Israel heard only a voice and saw no form, Deut. 4:12) with the New Covenant reality of the divine Incarnation, wherein the Word of God was physically seen, touched, and depicted (1 John 1:1–2).
[^924]: The classic dogmatic formula of the Synodikon of Orthodoxy and Nicaea II: distinguishing absolute adoration and worship (*latreia*) rendered to God alone from relative veneration (*proskynesis schetike*, Church Slavonic: *по удержаніи поклоненіе*) rendered to holy icons, wherein the honor passes to the Prototype.
[^925]: The four great iconodule Patriarchs of Constantinople commemorated in the Synodikon: Saint Germanus I (patriarch 715–730, deposed by Leo III), Saint Tarasius (patriarch 784–806, who convoked Nicaea II), Saint Nicephorus I (patriarch 806–815, exiled by Leo V), and Saint Methodius I (patriarch 843–847, who instituted the Triumph of Orthodoxy).
[^926]: Post-iconoclast Orthodox Patriarchs of Constantinople: Saint Ignatius (patriarch 847–858, 867–877), Saint Photius the Great (patriarch 858–867, 877–886), Stephen I (patriarch 886–893), Saint Antony II Kauleas (patriarch 893–901), and Saint Nicholas I Mystikos (patriarch 901–907, 912–925).
[^927]: Saint Stephen the New (c. 715–765), venerable monastic martyr of Mount Saint Auxentios, stoned and dragged through the streets of Constantinople under Constantine V Kopronymos for defending holy icons.
[^928]: Confessor-hierarchs of the iconoclast persecutions: Saint Euthymius, Bishop of Sardis (flogged to death in 831); Saint Theophilus, Bishop of Ephesus; and Saint Emilian, Bishop of Cyzicus (died in exile under Leo V).
[^929]: Metropolitan-confessors: Saint Theophylact, Metropolitan of Nicomedia (exiled 815, died c. 845); Saint Peter, Metropolitan of Nicaea; Saint Michael, Metropolitan of Synnada (exiled by Leo V, died 826); and Saint Joseph the Confessor, Metropolitan of Thessalonica (brother of St. Theodore the Studite, died 825).
[^930]: Confessor-hierarchs John of Chalcedon, Nicholas, and George of Mytilene (*trekogaty* / thrice-blessed confessors and archbishops).
[^931]: Saint Theodore the Studite (759–826), abbot of the Stoudios Monastery in Constantinople, theologian, confessor, and leader of monastic resistance against the iconoclasts.
[^932]: Monastic luminaries: Saint Isaac the Wonderworker, founder/hegumen of the Dalmatos Monastery in Constantinople, and Saint Joannicius the Great (762–846), prophetic hermit of Mount Olympus in Bithynia.
[^933]: Saint Hilarion the Younger (c. 775–845), Archimandrite and Abbot of the Dalmatos Monastery, tortured and imprisoned under Leo V and Theophilus for defending holy icons.
[^934]: Saint Symeon the Stylite of Lesbos (c. 765–844), who asceticized upon a pillar and suffered exile during the second iconoclasm.
[^935]: Saint Theophanes the Confessor (c. 759–818), Abbot of the Great Field (*Megas Agros*) in Bithynia, celebrated chronicler and confessor who died in exile on the island of Samothrace under Leo V.

[^936]: The conclusion of the general anathemas of the Synodikon of Orthodoxy against the iconoclasts, who are likened unto Jews and pagans for blaspheming against Christ the Archetype through the rejection of His holy icon.
[^937]: Conciliar condemnation of the Agnoetae and related Christological distortions asserting that Christ's human nature remains in permanent servile subjection (*douleia*) to the divine nature, contrary to the orthodox doctrine of the deification of Christ's humanity.
[^938]: Condemnation of the Nestorianizing tendency separating the human nature of Christ into an independent subject of worship and priestly ministration, rather than confessing the one incarnate Hypostasis of God the Word as both High Priest and divine Recipient of worship.
[^939]: Citation from St. Gregory the Theologian (Nazianzen), *Oration 45 (On Pascha)*: Christ is both Priest and Victim, offering Himself in the flesh while remaining God.
[^940]: The dogmatic definition of the Councils of Constantinople (1156–1157) convoked under Emperor Manuel I Comnenus and Patriarch Luke Chrysoberges against Soterichus Panteugenus (Patriarch-elect of Antioch), confirming that Christ's Eucharistic and Cross-sacrifice is offered to the entire Holy Trinity—Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.
[^941]: Reaffirmation that the daily Eucharistic Liturgy of St. Basil the Great and St. John Chrysostom is offered directly unto the Holy Trinity, in accordance with the secret prayer of the Cherubic Hymn: "For Thou art He that offerest and is offered, that acceptest and art distributed, O Christ our God."
[^942]: St. John Chrysostom, *Homilies on Hebrews* (Homily 17.3): the Eucharistic offering is not a different or figurative sacrifice, but the very same sacrifice of Christ offered once for all upon Golgotha, continually re-presented and applied.
[^943]: Conciliar condemnation of temporal separation in the reconciliation of humanity to God, defining that the Incarnation, Cross, and Resurrection constitute an indivisible divine economy reconciling mankind simultaneously to the undivided Trinity.
[^944]: The three iconoclast Patriarchs of Constantinople under the Isaurian dynasty: Anastasius (730–754), Constantine II (754–766), and Nicetas I (766–780), who presided over the iconoclast council of Hieria (754).
[^945]: The three patriarchs of the second iconoclast period (815–843): Theodotus I Kassiteras, Antony I Kassymatas, and John VII the Grammarian (Morocharzanios).
[^946]: Prominent partisans of the second iconoclasm: Paul (satirized as turning from the Apostle Paul back into the persecutor Saul), Theodore Gastes, Stephen Molites, Theodore Krithinos (metropolitan of Syracuse), and Laloudios Leo.
[^947]: Gerontios of Crete (or Sougdaia/Souklampa), an iconoclast propagandist who fled to Crete and disseminated treatises subverting the Christological basis of the Incarnation and icon-veneration.
[^948]: The eleven synodical anathemas of the Council of Constantinople (1082) convoked by Emperor Alexios I Comnenus against John Italus, successor of Michael Psellos as *hypatos ton philosophon* ("consul of the philosophers"), condemning the resurgence of pagan Neoplatonism, dialectical rationalism applied to dogma, transmigration of souls, and the eternity of matter.
[^949]: First anathema against John Italus: condemning the use of pagan dialectical categories (Aristotelian *thesis* and *physis*) to dissect and rationalize the mystery of the Incarnation and the deification (*theosis*) of Christ's humanity.
[^950]: Second anathema against John Italus: condemning the syncretic importation of pagan Greek cosmology, psychology, and metaphysics into Orthodox dogmatic theology.
[^951]: Third anathema against John Italus: condemning metempsychosis (transmigration of souls), mortalism, and the philosophical denial of the bodily resurrection and the Last Judgment.
[^952]: Fourth anathema against John Italus: condemning the Platonic doctrine of eternal uncreated matter (*hyle*), eternal Forms/Ideas (*ideai*), and the Aristotelian eternity of the cosmos, affirming *creatio ex nihilo*.
[^953]: Fifth anathema against John Italus: condemning the humanist exaltation of pagan philosophers and condemned heresiarchs above fallible but Orthodox Christian believers.
[^954]: Sixth anathema against John Italus: condemning rationalistic skepticism and allegorization that seeks to explain away Gospel miracles and the supernatural works of the saints.
[^955]: Seventh anathema against John Italus: establishing the classical Patristic distinction between the pedagogical study of classical Greek literature and grammar and the dogmatic adoption of pagan philosophical doctrines.
[^956]: Eighth anathema against John Italus: condemning Middle and Neo-Platonic theories of demiurgic formation of matter through ideal Forms (*ideai/eidē*), which denies the sovereign, free creation of the cosmos *ex nihilo* by God.
[^957]: Ninth anathema against John Italus: condemning Origenistic and philosophical denials of the identity of the resurrected body with the earthly body, affirming 1 Corinthians 15:35–54.
[^958]: Tenth anathema against John Italus: condemning Origenistic apokatastasis (the universal restitution of all things), the pre-existence of souls, the temporal limitation of hellfire, and the alleged transitoriness of the Kingdom of Heaven.
[^959]: Eleventh anathema appended to the synodical decree against John Italus: condemning the heretical monk Nilus of Calabria (condemned in Constantinople in 1117 under Emperor Alexios I Comnenus), who taught that Christ's human nature was deified only by grace (*kat' charin*) and asserted that it should not be worshipped with one undivided worship.
[^960]: Opening anathema of the Council of Constantinople of 1166–1170 convoked by Emperor Manuel I Comnenus and Patriarch Luke Chrysoberges concerning the interpretation of John 14:28 (*"My Father is greater than I"*).
[^961]: Affirmation of the traditional Patristic exegesis (e.g. St. Athanasius, St. Cyril of Alexandria) that John 14:28 applies to Christ's created human nature, wherein He suffered the Passion on the Cross, while remaining impassible in His Godhead.
[^962]: Anti-Monophysite and anti-Eutychian anathema of 1166: rejecting the notion that deification (*theosis*) obliterates the creaturely boundaries of Christ's human nature or converts flesh into the divine essence.
[^963]: Orthodox confession of the 1166 Council: affirming the supreme exaltation and session at the right hand of the Father of Christ's deified human flesh, worshipped with one undivided latreutic adoration (*mia proskynesis*) together with the divine Word.
[^964]: Conciliar invocation of the Patristic consensus (St. Athanasius of Alexandria, St. Cyril of Alexandria, St. Ambrose of Milan, St. Amphilochius of Iconium, St. Leo the Great of Rome) and the dogmatic definitions of Chalcedon (451) and Constantinople III (680–681) on the two natures, wills, and operations of Christ.
[^965]: Clarification of the theological method of mental abstraction (*kat' epinoian psilen* / *по тонкому мнѣнію*): the holy Fathers only employ mental abstraction when treating servitude and ignorance in order to protect Christ's deified flesh from blasphemy, but the physical properties of createdness, circumscription, and mortality remain truly and hypostatically present in Christ's united humanity.
[^966]: Synodical anathema of 1166–1170 against Metropolitan Constantine the Bulgarian of Corfu (Kerkyra), who refused to submit to the conciliar ruling of Patriarch Luke Chrysoberges and Emperor Manuel I Comnenus regarding the interpretation of John 14:28, and cited St. John of Damascus (*De Fide Orthodoxa*) out of context.
[^967]: Condemnation of the partisan followers of Constantine of Corfu who persisted in schism and dogmatic defiance against the conciliar decrees of the Great Church.
[^968]: Condemnation of John Eirenikos (*Іѡаннъ мирный*), a prominent monastic controversialist who wrote polemical treatises supporting Constantine of Corfu and rejecting the conciliar Tome of 1166.
[^969]: The pseudo-synod of Hieria (754), convoked by Emperor Constantine V Kopronymos, styled by the iconoclasts as the "Seventh Ecumenical Council," but condemned by the Seventh Ecumenical Council (Nicaea II, 787) as a lawless and raging conventicle.
[^970]: Condemnation of the iconoclast polemic that applied Old Testament prohibitions against pagan idolatry (e.g. Ex. 20:4–5; Ps. 113:12–16 LXX) to the veneration of the holy icons of Christ and His saints.
[^971]: Canonical anathema against those who maintain ecclesial or sacramental communion with active iconoclasts and desecrators of holy icons.
[^972]: Repudiation of the iconoclast slander asserting that Orthodox Christians render divine worship (*latreia*) to the physical materials of the icons.
[^973]: Affirmation that Christ alone is the Savior Who abolished idolatry, refuting the iconoclast claim that the Church fell back into idolatry until the iconoclast emperors arose to purify it.
[^974]: Solemn defense of the indefectibility of Christ's Catholic Church against the heretical accusation that the Church apostatized into idolatry through icon veneration.
[^975]: Patristic designation of the iconoclasts as *Christianokatēgoroi* ("accusers of Christians"), forbidding any rehabilitation, defense, or posthumous commemoration of deceased unrepentant iconoclasts.
[^976]: Fundamental dogmatic canon of Nicaea II (787): confessing the circumscription and visual depictability of Christ's true human body as the physical witness of the Incarnation.
[^977]: Opening solemn anathema against Barlaam of Calabria and Gregory Akindynos, who initiated the 14th-century Hesychast controversy by attacking the monastic practice of the Jesus Prayer and denying the uncreated nature of the Taboric Light and the divine energies.
[^978]: The synodical articles of the Hesychast Councils of Constantinople (1341 and 1351) under Emperor John VI Cantacuzene, Patriarch Callistus I, and St. Gregory Palamas, incorporated into the Synodikon of Orthodoxy to defend the dogma of the uncreated divine energies and the real distinction between essence (*ousia*) and energy (*energeia*) in God.
[^979]: First Palamite synodical chapter (1351): the Light of the Transfiguration on Mount Tabor is neither a created temporal symbol nor the inaccessible divine essence (*ousia*), but the eternal, uncreated divine energy (*energeia*) and glory of the Triune God.
[^980]: Second Palamite synodical chapter (1351): the distinction between divine essence and divine energy is real, unconfused, and inseparable, refuting the Eunomian, Sabellian, and Barlaamite equation of essence with energy, and citing the Sixth Ecumenical Council (Constantinople III, 680–681) on the two natural operations of Christ.
[^981]: Third Palamite synodical chapter (1351): the Patristic axiom that operation/energy follows nature (*energeia akolouthei te physei*); to assert that God's natural power and energy is created inevitably implies that God's essence itself is created, plunging into polytheism or creature-worship.
[^982]: Fourth Palamite synodical chapter (1351): the distinction between essence and energy does not introduce composition into God, for God's natural energies proceed from His simple, indivisible nature without compounding it.
[^983]: Fifth Palamite synodical chapter (1351): the divine name *Theotes* ("Godhead") is applied by the holy Fathers not only to the transcendent divine essence but also to the uncreated divine energy, without dividing the Godhead into two gods.
[^984]: Sixth Palamite synodical chapter (1351): the divine essence is entirely transcendent, incomprehensible, and incommunicable (*amethektos*), whereas believers truly participate in God through His uncreated grace and energy (*methekte*), refuting the Messalian/Bogomil heresy.
[^985]: General synodical condemnation of all treatises and polemical works of Barlaam the Calabrian and Gregory Akindynos.
[^986]: Isaac Argyros (c. 1312–c. 1372), Byzantine mathematician, astronomer, and anti-Palamite theologian who continued the anti-hesychast polemic after the synods of 1351 and 1368.
[^987]: Arius of Alexandria (condemned at Nicaea I, 325), who denied the true divinity and consubstantiality (*homoousion*) of the Son with the Father.
[^988]: Peter the Fuller (*Gnapheus*, Patriarch of Antioch, d. 488), who inserted the Theopaschite addition *"Who wast crucified for us"* into the Trisagion hymn, implying that the divine nature of the Trinity was crucified.
[^989]: Nestorius and the Theopaschite errorists (*theopaschitai*, here rendered *Thenlatu* / *θεηλάτῳ*, "God-hated" or "smitten of God"), who introduced passibility into the divine nature of the Holy Trinity.
[^990]: Valentinus (2nd century), the prominent Gnostic heresiarch who taught an elaborate system of emanations (Aeons) and denied the true physical Incarnation of Christ.
[^991]: Paul of Samosata (Bishop of Antioch, deposed 268) and Theodotion (Theodotus the Tanner/Banker), dynamic Monarchians who taught psilanthropism (that Christ was merely an inspired man), grouped with Nestorius who divided Christ into two persons.
[^992]: Peter Mongus ("the Stammerer", Monophysite patriarch of Alexandria), Peter the Iberian (or Peter the Fuller / Lykopetros), Eutyches (archimandrite of Constantinople, condemned at Chalcedon 451 for Monophysitism), and Sabellius (modalist Monarchian).
[^993]: Jacob Baradaeus (*Tzanzalos*, bishop of Edessa and organiser of the Non-Chalcedonian Syrian/Armenian hierarchy), Dioscorus I of Alexandria (presided over the Robber Council of Ephesus 449, deposed at Chalcedon 451), Severus of Antioch (Monophysite patriarch), and the Monothelite Patriarchs of Constantinople: Sergius I, Pyrrhus, and Paul II (condemned at the Sixth Ecumenical Council, 680–681).
[^994]: Summary anathema against the major Anti-Chalcedonian and Monothelite sects: Eutychians (extreme Monophysites), Monothelites ("single-will" errorists), Jacobites (Syrian Miaphysites), and Artzibourites (an Armenian sect adhering to archaic liturgical and Christological customs).
[^995]: Liturgical rubric marking the transition in the Synodikon of Orthodoxy from the anathemas against heresiarchs to the solemn diptychs of "Memory Eternal" (*Mnimi Aionia*) for pious Orthodox emperors and empresses who defended the Church and convoked ecumenical and local councils.
[^996]: Empress Theodora and her son Emperor Michael III the Amorian, who in March 843 together with Patriarch Methodius I restored the holy icons to the churches, establishing the Feast of the Triumph of Orthodoxy.
[^997]: Byzantine Emperor Andronikos III Palaeologus (reigned 1328–1341), who convoked and presided over the first Hesychast Council of St. Sophia in Constantinople in June 1341, vindicating St. Gregory Palamas and condemning Barlaam of Calabria just days before the emperor's death.
[^998]: Saint Gregory Palamas (1296–1359), Archbishop of Thessalonica, champion of Orthodox hesychasm and theologian of the uncreated divine energies, whose synodical acclamation ("Memory eternal! Thrice") continues on the next leaf (p361).

[^999]: Continuation of the commemoration of St. Gregory Palamas, Metropolitan of Thessalonica (1296–1359), champion of the Hesychast doctrine of the uncreated divine light and essence-energies distinction, approved at the Constantinopolitan Synods of 1341, 1351, and 1368. Note: The 1640 printed text contains a curious compositor error, Златогнѣ́вагѡ (literally, "the Golden-angered"), an accidental deformation of Златоу́стаго ("the Golden-mouthed") or Златосло́внаго ("the Golden-worded").
[^1000]: Commemoration of the Orthodox defenders, clergy, and faithful who supported the Hesychast synods under Emperor John VI Cantacuzene and withstood the polemics of Barlaam the Calabrian and Gregory Akindynos.
[^1001]: Synodical confession of the Sixth Ecumenical Council (Constantinople III, 680–681) concerning the two natural operations/energies in Christ, applied here in the Synodikon of Orthodoxy to the Palamite distinction between the transcendent uncreated divine essence and the uncreated divine energy.
[^1002]: Palamite confession of the uncreated, beginningless divine energy, through which humanity participates in God by deification (*theosis*), while the divine essence remains utterly unapproachable and incommunicable.
[^1003]: Theological definition concerning the Light of the Transfiguration on Mount Tabor, identifying it as the uncreated, divine radiance and eternal glory of the Godhead, as taught by St. Athanasius, St. Basil the Great, St. Gregory the Theologian, St. John Chrysostom, and St. John of Damascus.
[^1004]: Definition distinguishing the uncreated Light of Tabor from the supersubstantial essence (*ousia*) of God, which no created being can ever see or partake of, while the natural glory and energy of God is revealed to those whose minds are purified.
[^1005]: Commemoration of Emperor Michael III (842–867) and his mother the holy Empress Theodora, under whose regency the Triumph of Orthodoxy over Iconoclasm was proclaimed on the First Sunday of Great Lent in 843.
[^1006]: Commemoration of the pious Byzantine emperors from the Macedonian dynasty through the Komnenian and early Palaeologan periods, concluding with Manuel (Matthew) Cantacuzene, who tonsured as a monk.
[^1007]: Commemoration of Emperor John III Doukas Vatatzes (reigned 1222–1254, Emperor of Nicaea), celebrated for his piety and almsgiving, who received monastic tonsure with the name Theodore.
[^1008]: Commemoration of Emperor Michael VIII Palaeologus (reigned 1259–1282), designated "the New" for restoring Byzantine rule in Constantinople in 1261.
[^1009]: Commemoration of Emperor Andronicus II Palaeologus (reigned 1282–1328), who renounced the Council of Lyons, reaffirmed Orthodoxy, and was tonsured as the monk Antony.
[^1010]: Commemoration of Emperor Andronicus III Palaeologus (reigned 1328–1341), who convoked the Council of Constantinople in June 1341 that vindicated St. Gregory Palamas.
[^1011]: Commemoration of Emperor John VI Cantacuzene (reigned 1347–1354), devoted supporter of St. Gregory Palamas and president of the 1351 Hesychast Synod, who abdicated to become the monk Joasaph at the Chora Monastery.
[^1012]: Commemoration of pious Byzantine Empresses and Augustae, including Eudokia, Theophano (wife of Leo VI the Wise), St. Theodora the Empress, Helen Dragas, and those who took the monastic habit.
[^1013]: Commemoration of Empress Irene of Montferrat (wife of Andronicus II Palaeologus), who received monastic tonsure with the name Eugenia.
[^1014]: Commemoration of Empress Theodora Palaiologina (wife of Michael VIII Palaeologus), who ended her days as the nun Eugenia.
[^1015]: Commemoration of Empress Irene, consort of Byzantine rulers of the Komnenian and early Palaeologan eras.
[^1016]: Commemoration of Empress Mary of Bulgaria (or Mary Palaiologina), who entered monastic life with the name Xene.
[^1017]: Commemoration of Empress Anna of Savoy (consort of Andronicus III Palaeologus), who in her later years as regent reconciled with the Palamites, entered monastic life under the name Anastasia in Thessalonica, and supported the Orthodox conciliar decisions.
[^1018]: Commemoration of Empress Irene (consort of John VI Cantacuzene), who supported her husband's defense of Orthodoxy and tonsured as the nun Eugenia at St. Martha's Monastery in Constantinople.
[^1019]: Commemoration of the four great champion Patriarchs of Constantinople during the Iconoclast controversies: St. Germanus I (715–730), St. Tarasius (784–806, president of the Seventh Ecumenical Council), St. Nicephorus I (806–815), and St. Methodius I (843–847, who instituted the Feast of Orthodoxy).
[^1020]: Commemoration of the successive Orthodox Patriarchs of Constantinople from St. Ignatius (847–858, 867–877) and St. Photius the Great (858–867, 877–886) through the 10th to 12th centuries, concluding on this leaf with Meletius (or Michael II Curcuas).
[^1021]: Commemoration of the Ecumenical Patriarchs of Constantinople from the late 12th to the 13th century, concluding with Manuel II (1243–1254) and Methodius II (1240), who received monastic tonsure.
[^1022]: Commemoration of Patriarch Germanus III (1265–1266), who was tonsured with the name George the Monk.
[^1023]: Commemoration of Patriarch Arsenius Autoreianos (1255–1259, 1261–1264), known for his strict defense of church canons and the Arsenite movement.
[^1024]: Commemoration of Patriarch Joseph I Galesiotes (1268–1275, 1279–1283), steadfast opponent of the Union of Lyons.
[^1025]: Commemoration of Patriarch St. Athanasius I of Constantinople (1289–1293, 1303–1309), monastic reformer and ascetic champion.
[^1026]: Commemoration of Patriarch Gerasimus I of Constantinople (1320–1321).
[^1027]: Commemoration of Patriarch Isaiah of Constantinople (1323–1332).
[^1028]: Commemoration of Patriarch Isidore I Bouchiras (1347–1350), disciple of St. Gregory Palamas who consecrated Palamas as Metropolitan of Thessalonica.
[^1029]: Commemoration of Patriarch St. Callistus I of Constantinople (1350–1353, 1355–1363), Hesychast writer, disciple of St. Gregory of Sinai, and co-author of ascetic treatises.
[^1030]: Commemoration of Patriarch St. Philotheus Kokkinos (1353–1354, 1364–1376), disciple and biographer of St. Gregory Palamas, who presided over the canonization of Palamas in 1368 and authored liturgical services for the Synodikon.
[^1031]: Commemoration of the historical Orthodox Patriarchs of Antioch, including Christopher (960–969), Theodore, Agapius, John, Nicholas, Elias, Basil, Peter, Theodosius, Nicephorus, and John.
[^1032]: Commemoration of notable Orthodox Metropolitans of various metropolitan sees within the Ecumenical Patriarchate who preserved Orthodox dogma across centuries.
[^1033]: Commemoration of the ancient, venerable Metropolitans and hierarchs of the Church who guarded orthodox dogma against antiquity's errors.
[^1034]: Rubric indicating that following the commemorations of departed emperors and patriarchs, prayers and acclamations ("Many years") are made for the living Orthodox Christian sovereigns, ruling patriarchs, and hierarchy, either before the iconostasis or in the narthex (*Внѣ* / outside the nave).
[^1035]: Final solemn invocation and doxological prayer of the Synodikon of Orthodoxy, asking for the grace to emulate the holy confessors and fathers through the intercessions of the Theotokos, the Angels, and all saints.
[^1036]: First penitential sticheron by St. Joseph the Hymnographer for Sunday Vespers of the Second Week of Great Lent, sung to the prosomoion model "Thou hast given a sign" (*Далъ еси знаменїе*).
[^1037]: Second penitential sticheron by St. Joseph the Hymnographer, evoking the forty-day fast of Moses on Mount Sinai (Exod. 34:28) as an archetype of bodily and spiritual purification.
[^1038]: Third sticheron for the Second Week of Lent by St. Theodore the Studite, evoking the fiery ascent of the Prophet Elijah (4 Kings / 2 Kings 2:11) through the four cardinal virtues: prudence, justice, fortitude, and temperance.
[^1039]: Great Prokeimenon in Tone 8, taken from Psalm 60:5, 2, 4, 8 (LXX: Ps. 60:6, 2, 5, 9), chanted alternately on Sunday evenings of Great Lent.
[^1040]: Liturgical typikon directive establishing the alternating rotation between the two Great Prokeimena (*"Turn not away Thy face"* and *"Thou hast given an heritage"*) throughout Great Lent from Cheesefare Sunday to the Fifth Sunday of Lent, and specifying the resumption of prostrations at the Prayer of St. Ephraim after *"Vouchsafe, O Lord"*; cross-reference to folio 38v corresponds to physical leaf p80 of the 1640 Triodion.
[^1041]: Idiomel sticheron at the Aposticha in Tone 8, paraphrasing Christ's teaching on secretive, humble almsgiving and fasting in the Sermon on the Mount (Matt. 6:1–4, 16–18).
[^1042]: Aposticha sticheron dedicated to the Holy Martyrs (*Martyrikon*), praising their intercessory grace across all places.
[^1043]: Aposticha Theotokion in Tone 8, confessing the unsearchable mystery of the Virgin Birth.
[^1044]: Rubrical cross-reference directing the choir and clergy to folio 38v (physical leaf p80) for the concluding Lenten troparia (*"Theotokos Virgin, rejoice"* with prostrations), Prayer of St. Ephraim, and the Litiya in the narthex.
[^1045]: Opening of the Compline Canon of the Holy Prophets, composed by St. Theophanes Graptos (the Hymnographer, c. 778–845), written in Tone 5 with an alphabetic and thematic Greek acrostic; Heirmos from Ode 1 draws on the Canticle of Moses (Exod. 15:1).
[^1046]: Compline Canon of the Prophets, Ode 1, Troparion 1, beseeching Christ the Author of all for grace to praise the Prophets.
[^1047]: Compline Canon, Ode 1, Troparion 2, praising the Prophets as divine mirrors and vessels receiving the revelation of God.
[^1048]: Compline Canon, Ode 1, Troparion 3, commemorating Moses the God-beholder whose countenance shone with uncreated light upon Mount Sinai (Exod. 34:29–35).
[^1049]: Compline Canon, Ode 1, Troparion 4, portraying the prophetic preachings as divine storm clouds dispersing pagan polytheism.
[^1050]: Compline Canon, Ode 1, Theotokion, praising the Prophets as heraldic trumpets foretelling the Incarnation of the Word from the pure Virgin.
[^1051]: Compline Canon of the Prophets, Ode 3 Heirmos, based on Psalm 135:5 and Proverbs 3:19.
[^1052]: Compline Canon, Ode 3, Troparion 1, comparing the Prophets to sacred citharas and harps struck by the plectrum of the Holy Spirit.
[^1053]: Compline Canon, Ode 3, Troparion 2, portraying the Prophets as spiritual weapons cutting through multifaceted idolatrous deceit.
[^1054]: Compline Canon, Ode 3, Troparion 3, depicting the Prophets as spiritual chalices pouring out the knowledge of the Divine Economy.
[^1055]: Compline Canon, Ode 3, Theotokion, confessing the ancient eternal mystery of the Incarnation fulfilled in the womb of the Virgin.
[^1056]: Compline Canon of the Prophets, Ode 4 Heirmos, drawing on the prophetic vision of Habakkuk (Hab. 3:2).
[^1057]: Compline Canon, Ode 4, Troparion 1, celebrating the Prophets as unerring luminaries enlightening the Gentile nations.
[^1058]: Compline Canon, Ode 4, Troparion 2, glorifying Christ for fulfilling the prophetic visions in concrete historical reality.
[^1059]: Compline Canon, Ode 4, Troparion 3, recalling the prophetic foreknowledge of the calling of the Gentiles and the desolation of unbelieving Jerusalem.
[^1060]: Compline Canon, Ode 4, Theotokion, drawing on Habakkuk 3:3 ("God shall come from Teman, and the Holy One from a shady and thickly wooded mountain") as a prophecy of the Incarnation from the Virgin.
[^1061]: Compline Canon of the Prophets, Ode 5 Heirmos, based on Psalm 103:2 and Isaiah 26:9.
[^1062]: Compline Canon, Ode 5, Troparion 1, commemorating the Prophet Isaiah rising early in spirit to behold the Lord enthroned on high surrounded by the Seraphim (Isa. 6:1–3).
[^1063]: Compline Canon, Ode 5, Troparion 2, praising the prophetic utterance inspired by the Holy Spirit.
[^1064]: Compline Canon, Ode 5, Troparion 3, honoring the Prophets as spiritual temples of God whose miracles surpassed created nature.
[^1065]: Compline Canon, Ode 5, Theotokion, confessing God's singular wisdom in disclosing His eternal plan of salvation to the Prophets.
[^1066]: Compline Canon of the Prophets, Ode 6 Heirmos, based on the prayer of the Prophet Jonah from the sea monster (Jonah 2:1–9).
[^1067]: Compline Canon, Ode 6, Troparion 1, honoring the Prophets for their blameless ascetic life and fidelity to divine revelation.
[^1068]: Compline Canon, Ode 6, Troparion 2, recalling the miraculous deliverances of the Prophets: Daniel in the lions' den (Dan. 6) and Jonah preserved in the whale (Jonah 2).
[^1069]: Compline Canon, Ode 6, Troparion 3 (continued from p369), extolling the Prophets as universal teachers and planters of divine virtue.
[^1070]: Compline Canon, Ode 6, Theotokion, celebrating the prophetic symbols and types (e.g. the burning bush, the closed gate, the unshorn fleece) that prefigured the Mother of God.
[^1071]: Sessional Hymn (*Sidalen*) of the Prophets in Tone 2, chanted as an Automelon, connecting the prophetic witness of Moses and Aaron with the life-giving Cross of Christ.
[^1072]: Compline Canon of the Prophets, Ode 7 Heirmos, drawing on the Prayer of Azariah and the Song of the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian furnace (Dan. 3:26ff).
[^1073]: Compline Canon, Ode 7, Troparion 1, commemorating the Prophet Daniel preserved unharmed among the lions (Dan. 6:16–23).
[^1074]: Compline Canon, Ode 7, Troparion 2, praising Ananias, Azarias, and Misael, the three youths who quenched the fiery furnace in Babylon (Dan. 3).
[^1075]: Compline Canon, Ode 7, Troparion 3, praising the righteous descendants of Abraham who adorned the earth with virtuous deeds.
[^1076]: Compline Canon, Ode 7, Theotokion, praising the Mother of God who surpassed all natural laws by giving birth to the Lawgiver.
[^1077]: Compline Canon of the Prophets, Ode 8 Heirmos, drawing on the universal praise of creation in the Song of the Three Children (Dan. 3:57ff).
[^1078]: Compline Canon, Ode 8, Troparion 1, invoking the intercession of the holy Prophets who dwell in the delights of Paradise.
[^1079]: Compline Canon, Ode 8, Troparion 2, gathering the faithful into a liturgical choir to hymn the God-bearing Prophets.
[^1080]: Compline Canon, Ode 8, Troparion 3, celebrating the Prophets as fragrant vessels of the Holy Spirit who guided nations unto divine knowledge.
[^1081]: Compline Canon, Ode 8, Theotokion, confessing the Virgin Mother of God who brought forth into the world the unoriginate Radiance of the Father through the assumption of human flesh; continuation follows on folio 348 (p371).

[^1183]: Genesis 5:1–24. The 1640 Kyiv text faithfully follows the Septuagint chronological tradition (Adam living 230 years before begetting Seth, Enos living 190 years before Cainan, Cainan living 170 years before Maleleel, and Jared living 162 years before Enoch), diverging by 100 years from the Hebrew Masoretic Text.

[^1186]: Proverbs 6:8. The praise of the bee (*И҆лѝ и҆дѝ къ пчелѣ̀...*) is a distinctive Septuagint expansion absent from the Hebrew Masoretic Text; it is explicitly enclosed in printed square brackets in the 1640 Kyiv codex.
[^1187]: Proverbs 6:11. This blessing of the diligent worker is an additional Septuagint reading absent from the Hebrew Masoretic Text, printed in brackets in the 1640 edition.
[^1188]: Proverbs 6:18–19. The gloss *[потреблѧ́ѧ]* (*consuming* / *destroying*) is bracketed in the 1640 text.
