[^1200]: Rubric cross-reference pointing to folio 273 (f. 273r), containing the standard conclusion of Matins, the First Hour, and the Order of the Presanctified Liturgy.

[^1201]: In the 1640 Kyiv Triodion, the rubric refers back to Wednesday of the Second Week of Great Lent (fol. 244), where the chanting of the 3rd, 6th, and 9th Hours together is prescribed.
[^1202]: The rubric refers to fol. 288 for the detailed rubrical directions on the omission of prostrations at Lenten Vespers entering into the weekend.
[^1203]: Reading from Isaiah 7:1–4a. In the left margin of the 1640 print, the rubricator notes 'єѵглиста Матѳеа' ('of the Evangelist Matthew'), referencing the prophetic fulfillment of Isaiah 7:14 recorded in Matthew 1:23.
[^1204]: The liturgical pericope concludes abruptly at the opening colon of Isaiah 7:14 ('Therefore the Lord Himself shall give you a sign:'), leaving the messianic announcement ('Behold, a virgin shall conceive...') for the feast of the Annunciation and the Nativity.
[^1205]: The rubric refers to fol. 234 for the complete order of Lenten Vespers and the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts.
[^1206]: The rubric refers to fol. 236 for the rubrics of the Little Entrance with the censer and candle during the Presanctified Liturgy.
[^1207]: Reading from Genesis 5:32; 6:1–7a. Septuagint versification.
[^1208]: Reading from Proverbs 6:20–31a.
[^1209]: The distich in brackets ('My son, honor the Lord, and thou shalt be strong; and beside Him fear none other') is an ancient Septuagint addition to Proverbs 7:2 preserved in the Church Slavonic text, absent from the Hebrew Masoretic Text.
[^1210]: The rubric refers to fol. 234 for the continuation of the Presanctified Liturgy.
[^1211]: The rubric refers to fol. 40 for the Kontakion of the departed ('With the Saints give rest').
[^1212]: The rubric refers to fol. 38 for the Theotokion of the departed in the Oktoechos.
[^1213]: The rubric refers to Meatfare Saturday (fol. 33) for the Evlogetaria of the departed ('The choir of the saints...').
[^1214]: The rubric refers to fol. 34 for the Sessional hymn for the departed ('Rest, O our Savior').
[^1215]: St. Theodore the Studite's poetic formulation articulates Orthodox Trinitarian dogma against both Sabellian modalism (which conflates the Three Persons) and Arian subordinationism (which divides the divine essence).
[^1216]: The rubric refers to fol. 40 for the Kontakion and Oikos of the departed.

[^1217]: The rubric refers to fol. 213 for the Troparion of the departed ('Remember, O Lord, since Thou art good').
[^1218]: The rubric refers to fol. 40 for the Kontakion of the departed ('With the saints give rest').
[^1219]: The rubric refers to fol. 32 for the Marian Kontakion ('We have thee as a rampart and haven').
[^1220]: Patriarch Philotheus Kokkinos of Constantinople (c. 1300–1379), a close disciple and biographer of St. Gregory Palamas, composed the liturgical office and synaxarion for St. Gregory following his canonization at the Synod of Constantinople in 1368.
[^1221]: The rubric refers to fol. 150 for the general order of Saturday Great Vespers during the Great Fast.
[^1222]: The canonical Tone 2 prosomoion model 'With what crowns of praise' (Greek: Ποίοις εὐφημιῶν στέμμασιν / Slavonic: Ко́ими похва́льными пѣ́сньми), originally composed for the Feast of the Holy Apostles Peter and Paul (June 29), is applied here to the laudation of St. Gregory Palamas.
[^1223]: The rubric refers to fol. 85 for the dismissal Theotokion 'O Unwedded Virgin' (Безневѣ́стная Дѣ́во).
[^1224]: The rubric refers to fol. 154 for the complete order of Sunday Lenten Matins preceding the Canons.
[^1225]: An allusion to St. Gregory the Theologian (Nazianzen), whose name and theological mantle St. Gregory Palamas inherited, defending the distinction between the unknowable divine essence and the uncreated divine energies.
[^1226]: The hymnographic text articulates the core Palamite doctrine of theosis (deification, Slavonic: ѡ҆боже́нїе), whereby humanity participates in the uncreated divine energies made accessible through the Incarnation of the Word.
[^1227]: The canonical Tone 4 prosomoion model 'Speedily anticipate' (Greek: Ταχὺ προκατάлаβε / Slavonic: Ско́рѡ предварѝ).
[^1228]: Barlaam of Calabria (c. 1290–1348), the primary philosophical opponent of St. Gregory Palamas and the Hesychasts, whose nominalist theology denying real human communion with the uncreated Light of Tabor was condemned at the Councils of Constantinople (1341, 1351).
[^1229]: St. Gregory Palamas is lauded as 'a second Theologian' (вторы́й Богосло́въ), ranking alongside St. Gregory the Theologian for his definitive exposition of the uncreated light and energies of God.

[^1230]: St. Gregory Palamas and the Hesychasts were vindicated against Barlaam of Calabria at the Council of Constantinople held in June 1341 under the presidency of Emperor Andronicus III Palaeologus (1328–1341), whose imperial edict confirmed the synodical decree.
[^1231]: Barlaam of Calabria asserted that the divine light of Mount Tabor was a sensible, created symbol (ktiston) that passed away, whereas St. Gregory Palamas demonstrated from the Fathers that it is the eternal, uncreated light and energy (aktiston) of the Holy Trinity.
[^1232]: St. Gregory Palamas formulated the definitive patristic distinction between the totally unknowable, unapproachable divine essence (ousia) and the eternal, uncreated, communicable divine energies (energeiai), by which mankind is deified (theosis).
[^1233]: The synaxarion verses for the Second Sunday of Great Lent, celebrating St. Gregory Palamas as the theologian of the divine and uncreated Light.
[^1234]: St. Gregory Palamas was born in Constantinople in 1296 of noble lineage; his father Constantine was an imperial senator and tutor to the future Emperor Andronicus III Palaeologus.
[^1235]: Mount Athos (the Holy Mountain), where St. Gregory entered monastic life around 1316 at approximately twenty years of age, accompanied by his brothers Macarius and Theodosius.
[^1236]: St. Nicodemus of Vatopedi (commemorated July 11), a holy hesychast elder on Mount Athos who guided St. Gregory in ascetic vigilance, fasting, and unceasing prayer of the heart.
[^1237]: The Great Lavra of St. Athanasius on Mount Athos, where St. Gregory spent three years as a monk, later withdrawing into the solitary skete of Glossia for deeper hesychastic contemplation.
[^1238]: In 1326, frequent Turkish pirate raids forced St. Gregory and his fellow monks to leave Mount Athos for Thessalonica, where he was ordained a priest at age thirty, after which he retreated to a hermitage near Veroia (Berrhoea).
[^1239]: The Hesychast Controversy extended from approximately 1337 (Barlaam's initial attacks on the monks) until the definitive Council of Blachernae and Tomos of 1351, spanning over two decades of fierce theological conflict.
[^1240]: Barlaam of Seminara (c. 1290–1348), a Greek-Calabrian monk and philosopher whose nominalist Aristotelianism led him to deny the possibility of direct experiential communion with God and to mock the hesychasts' prayer of the heart.
[^1241]: Barlaam and his partisans accused the hesychasts of polytheism and ditheism for distinguishing between the essence and uncreated energies of God, a charge the Orthodox fathers refuted by comparing it to Sabellianism and Arianism.
[^1242]: In the 1640 Kyivan text, Emperor Andronicus III Palaeologus (reigned 1328–1341) is designated as 'the fourth' (четве́ртый), counting all imperial sovereigns of that name across the Byzantine imperial registers.
[^1243]: The Council of Constantinople convened in the Church of the Holy Wisdom (Hagia Sophia) on June 10, 1341, where Barlaam was compelled to recant his writings and teachings against the hesychasts.
[^1244]: Following his condemnation at the Council of 1341, Barlaam left Constantinople for Italy, converted to the Roman Catholic faith, and was appointed Bishop of Gerace in Calabria by Pope Clement VI in 1342.
[^1245]: Gregory Akindynos (c. 1300–1348), originally a disciple of Palamas who later adopted Barlaam's anti-hesychast positions. The text plays on the Greek etymology: Akindynos ('without danger') versus Polykindynos ('full of many dangers').
[^1246]: St. Gregory Palamas was consecrated Archbishop of Thessalonica in 1347 by Patriarch Isidore I, although civil strife caused by the Zealot uprising prevented him from entering the city until 1350.
[^1247]: The three Byzantine Emperors were Andronicus III Palaeologus, John V Palaeologus, and John VI Kantakouzenos; the successive Patriarchs during the controversy included John XIV Kalekas, Isidore I, and Callistus I.
[^1248]: In 1354, while journeying by ship from Thessalonica to Constantinople to mediate peace between John V Palaeologus and John VI Kantakouzenos, St. Gregory was captured by Ottoman Turks near Gallipoli and held hostage for a year.
[^1249]: During his Ottoman captivity (1354–1355), St. Gregory engaged in famous theological disputations with Muslims and Christian renegades, defending the Incarnation, the veneration of the Cross, and holy icons, as recorded in his pastoral epistle to his flock.
[^1250]: St. Gregory Palamas served as Archbishop of Thessalonica for twelve to thirteen years (1347–1359) until his holy repose on November 14, 1359, at the age of sixty-three.
[^1251]: A reference to the comprehensive Life and Encomium of St. Gregory Palamas written by Patriarch Philotheus Kokkinos, which documents numerous post-mortem miracles and healings performed at his tomb in Thessalonica.
[^1252]: The hymnographic text articulates the core Palamite doctrine that the divine grace (charis) and divine energy (energeia) are uncreated (aktistos), flowing eternally from the divine essence and making man a partaker of the divine nature (2 Pet. 1:4).

[^1253]: The gathering of Polykindynos (Поликиндин) — The hymnographic wordplay on Gregory Akindynos (c. 1300–1348), originally a disciple of Palamas who later adopted Barlaam's anti-hesychast positions. Akindynos in Greek signifies "without danger" (ἀ- + κίνδυνος), inverted here by the hymnographer into "Polykindynos" ("full of many dangers", πολυκίνδυνος) because his nominalist doctrine threatened the Orthodox understanding of real communion with God.
[^1254]: The pious Emperor — Refers to Byzantine Emperor Andronicus III Palaeologus (1328–1341) and co-emperor John VI Kantakouzenos (1347–1354), who presided over synods in Constantinople vindicating the Palamite theology of the divine and uncreated energies.
[^1255]: Barlaam of Seminara (c. 1290–1348) — The Greek-Calabrian philosopher and monk who opposed the hesychasts and attacked their experiential vision of the uncreated light of Mount Tabor, condemned at the Council of Constantinople in June 1341.
[^1256]: Having become a god (Богъ бывъ) — An allusion to the patristic doctrine of deification (theosis), whereby through divine grace and uncreated energy man is made a partaker of the divine nature (2 Pet. 1:4) without confusion of created and uncreated essences.
[^1257]: Habitation of hesychia (безмолвія селеніе) — The inner silence, stillness, and spiritual watchfulness (nepsis) cultivated by the Athonite hesychast monks in the practice of the Jesus Prayer, dogmatically vindicated by St. Gregory Palamas.
[^1258]: Sovereign Lady of all (Владычице всѣхъ) — The incipit of the Sunday Exapostilarion Theotokion in Tone 2, addressed to the Mother of God as Queen and Mediatrix.
[^1259]: fol. 323 (Листъ тк҃г) — Cross-reference pointing back to the order of Sunday Matins and the Great Doxology on physical leaf 336 (printed fol. 323).
[^1260]: Common service for a single Hierarch (якоже Стятелю единому обще) — Directs that the remaining Liturgy variables follow the Common of a Hierarch for St. Gregory Palamas, conjoined with the Sunday Resurrectional variables.
[^1261]: fol. 323 (Листъ тк҃г) — Cross-reference directing to the opening psalmody and Penitential Stichera for Sunday evening at the start of Lent on physical leaf 336 (printed fol. 323).
[^1262]: Tone 8 Podoben: "Innumerable" (Неисчетенъ) — The prosomoion melodic model derived from the Penitential Stichera of the Octoechos, characterized by themes of compunction and the dread of future judgment.
[^1263]: fol. 129 and 194 (Листъ рх҃ѳ и рц҃д) — Directs the reader to the Great Prokeimenon "Turn not away Thy face" (Ps. 68:18 LXX [69:17]) and its accompanying psalm verses, printed on physical leaf 142 (printed fol. 129) and physical leaf 207 (printed fol. 194).
[^1264]: Sunday of Meatfare, at the Aposticha, fol. 72 (Листъ о҃в) — Points to the Sunday of the Last Judgment on physical leaf 85 (printed fol. 72), where the Theotokion "The heavenly choirs sing" (Небесная поютъ) is printed in full.
[^1265]: fol. 354 (Листъ тнд҃) — Points to the continuous order of the Lenten Aposticha and closing prayers on physical leaf 367 (printed fol. 354).
[^1266]: fol. 124 and 109 (Листъ рх҃д и р҃ѳ) — Directs to the rubrical order for Sunday evening Lenten Vespers and the Prayer of St. Ephrem the Syrian on physical leaves 122 (printed fol. 109) and 137 (printed fol. 124).
[^1267]: Compassionate love (соузъ страстное) — A Slavonic calque of the Greek συμпаθές (sympathetic, compassionate love), expressing God's infinite condescension and mercy toward fallen mankind.
[^1268]: Three days of Israel (Exod. 19:10–11) — The typology of Israel's three days of purification before the giving of the Law on Mount Sinai, prefiguring the three weeks of fasting leading up to the veneration of the Precious and Life-Giving Cross on the Third Sunday of Great Lent.
[^1269]: Gideon's three hundred (Judg. 7:7, 19–22) — Patristically interpreted as an image of spiritual warfare: the earthen pitchers broken to display the blazing torches prefigure ascetic self-denial and fasting that break the passions of the flesh to reveal the light of the virtues.
[^1270]: Elisha feeding the prophets (4 Kings 4:38–44 LXX [2 Kings 4]) — The prophetic miracle during the famine at Gilgal, where the Prophet Elisha rendered the bitter pottage wholesome and fed a hundred men with twenty barley loaves, prefiguring the spiritual nourishment of the Fast.
[^1271]: Troparion of the Prophecy (Тропарь пророчества) — The ancient troparion preceding the Old Testament prophecy reading at the Sixth Hour during Great Lent, preserving the archaic Palestinian cathedral structure of the mid-day Lenten office.

[^1272]: Isa. 8:13–14 LXX — Sanctify the Lord of Sabaoth Himself (Κύριον τῶν δυνάμεων αὐτὸν ἁγιάσατε). The Kyivan Triodion preserves the Septuagint text against the Masoretic recension, with a marginal gloss indicating the epigraphic Greek reading for the stumbling-stone (*въ язвите[л]...*, alluding to Greek προσκόμματι / πτώματι).
[^1273]: Isa. 8:19 LXX — Ventriloquists and belly-speakers (чревогадателя... чревобѣснующихъ) translates Greek ἐγγαστριμύθους (those who speak from the belly, necromancers/soothsayers), warning Israel against pagan divination and consulting the dead concerning the living.
[^1274]: Isa. 9:1 LXX — "Drink this first quickly!" (Сіе прежде скоро пій) translates the cryptic Septuagint opening of chapter 9: Τοῦτο πρῶτον πίε, ταχὺ ποίει, which contrasts the bitter cup of affliction with the sudden dawn of the Messianic light in Galilee of the Gentiles.
[^1275]: Isa. 9:6 LXX — "Angel of Great Counsel" (великагѡ совѣта агглъ) translates Greek Μεγάλης βουλῆς ἄγγελος, the classic Septuagint messianic title applied by the Church Fathers to the pre-incarnate Logos and Messiah, enclosed in editorial parentheses in the 1640 Kyivan print.
[^1276]: Tone 3 Podoben: "Come, all ye" (Пріидите вси) — Corresponds to the Byzantine automelon Δεῦτε ἅπαντες, sung during Great Lent to evoke joyful ascetic preparation for the veneration of the Precious Cross in Mid-Lent.
[^1277]: Gen. 6:14 LXX — Ark of square timbers (древъ негниющихъ [или кедровыхъ четвероуглныхъ]). The Septuagint reads ἐκ ξύλων τετραγώνων ("of squared timbers"), corresponding to the Hebrew *'atsei-gofer* (gopher wood). The Kyivan editor provides both the traditional Slavic rendering ("uncorruptible wood") and the Greek-collated gloss in brackets ("or squared cedar timbers").
[^1278]: Prov. 8:11 LXX — The bracketed clause (*преизбери́те же чꙋ́вствіа зла́тꙋ чи́стꙋ*) represents Greek καὶ προσλάβεσθε γνῶσιν ὑπὲρ χρυσίον κεκαθαρμένον ("and receive knowledge rather than pure gold"), a Septuagint variant placed in brackets by Mohyla's philological editors to mark collation against Greek manuscripts.
[^1279]: Tone 1 Podoben: "When the stone had been sealed" (Камени знаменану) — Byzantine automelon Τοῦ λίθου σφραγισθέντος, the primary Tone 1 Resurrectional Troparion melody, applied here to the Tuesday Matins Sessional Hymn on repentance and almsgiving.
[^1280]: Tone 3 Podoben: "Let the heavens rejoice" (Да веселя́тся) — Byzantine automelon Εὐφραινέσθω τὰ οὐράνια, the Resurrectional Troparion of Tone 3, adapted here to an ascetic sessional hymn anticipating the mid-Lenten veneration of the Holy Cross.
[^1281]: Deut. 32:39 LXX — Heirmos: "See, see that I am God" (Видите видите, яко Богъ азъ есмь). The second ode of the Great Canon and Lenten canticles takes its scriptural theme from the Song of Moses in Deuteronomy 32, emphasizing divine majesty, righteous judgment, and repentance.
[^1282]: Tone 3 Ode 8 Heirmos: "Him at Whom the angels tremble" (Егоже ужасаются) — Greek Ὃν φρίττουσιν ἄγγελοι, celebrating the awe of the angelic hosts before the divine throne, introducing the theme of eschatological judgment.
[^1283]: Tone 3 Ode 8 Heirmos: "Unto the children in the flame" (Во пламени к дѣтем) — Greek Ἐν τῇ φλογὶ τοῖς παισὶν Ἑβραίων συγκαταβάντα, referring to the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian furnace (Dan. 3) and typifying Christ's saving descent.
[^1284]: Tone 4 Ode 9 Heirmos: "The light-bearing cloud" (Свѣтоносный облакъ) — Greek Τὴν φωτοφόρον νεφέλην, a Marian hymnographic model depicting the Mother of God as the radiant cloud upon whom Christ, the Sun of Righteousness, descended to enlighten the world.
[^1285]: Tone 3 Ode 9 Heirmos: "On Mount Sinai" (На сїнаетстѣй горѣ) — Greek Ἐν Σιναίῳ τῷ ὄρει, invoking Moses' ascent of Mount Sinai (Ex. 19; 33–34) where fasting prepared the prophet to see the hinder parts of God in type, fulfilled spiritually in Christ.

[^1286]: Num. 25:3; Ps. 105:28 LXX [106:28] / Ex. 32:6; 1 Cor. 10:7 — The apostasy of Israel at Baal-peor (Веелфегоръ), where the people ate sacrifices offered to the dead and rose up to wanton play, is contrasted in the Tuesday Ode 9 Triodion canticle with the holy fast and ascent of Moses on Sinai.
[^1287]: Luke 15:18, 21 — The confession of the Prodigal Son ("I have sinned against heaven and before Thee, and am no more worthy to be called Thy son") provides the structural text for the Tuesday Aposticha Sticheron in Tone 7.
[^1288]: Tone 7 Aposticha Sticheron — "And as for the handwriting of my hands wherewith I am clothed" (къ грамотѣ же рукы имиже одѣянъ есмъ) alludes both to the handwritten bond of debt canceled by Christ on the Cross (Greek χειρόγραφον, Col. 2:14) and to patristic imagery depicting one's recorded sins as a garment of shame.
[^1289]: Isa. 9:9–14 LXX — Sixth Hour Old Testament reading for Tuesday of the Third Week. The 1640 Kyivan text translates the Septuagint reading against the Masoretic, noting Ephraim and Samaria's defiance and the prophetic judgment upon Israel.
[^1290]: Tone 3 Podoben: "They weighed thirty pieces of silver" (Поставиша) — Byzantine automelon Ἔστησαν τὰ τριάκοντα ἀργύρια (Zech. 11:12; Matt. 26:15), adapted here to the Tuesday Vespers Stichera of St. Joseph the Hymnographer focusing on repentance and the Holy Cross.
[^1291]: Tone 2 Podoben: "Let Him be crucified" (Да распнется) — Byzantine automelon Σταυρωθήτω (Matt. 27:22), sung during Great Lent to evoke joyful ascetic preparation for the veneration of the Precious Cross in Mid-Lent.
[^1292]: Gen. 7:1–5 LXX — Tuesday Vespers Old Testament reading. The preservation of Noah and every living pair within the ark during the forty-day deluge serves in Byzantine patristic typology as a figure of baptism and the forty-day fast of Great Lent.
[^1293]: Prov. 9:6 LXX — The bracketed clause (*въ вѣки царствуйте, и взыщите премдрость да поживете*) translates the Septuagint expansion ἵνα εἰς τὸν αἰῶνα βασιλεύσητε, καὶ ζητήσατε φρόνησιν ἵνα βιώσητε ("that ye may reign for ever, and seek wisdom that ye may live"), preserved in brackets by Mohyla's editors to record collation against Greek manuscripts.
[^1294]: Prov. 9:7 LXX — The bracketed clause (*ѡбличеніа же нечестивому, мозоліе емꙋ*) represents Greek LXX ἔλεγχοι δὲ τῷ ἀσεβεῖ μώλωπες αὐτῷ ("and reproofs to the ungodly are wounds to him"), absent in the Hebrew Masoretic recension but retained in the Greek liturgical text.
[^1295]: Prov. 9:10 LXX — The bracketed gloss (*разꙋмѣти же законъ помысла естъ блгагѡ*) translates Greek LXX τὸ δὲ γνῶναι νόμον διανοίας ἐστὶν ἀγαθῆς ("and to know the law is the sign of a good mind"), documented as a Septuagint witness in the Kyivan print.
[^1296]: Tone 2 Podoben: "When from the Tree" (Егда от древа) — Byzantine automelon Ὅτε ἐκ τοῦ ξύλου σε νεκρόν, sung at the Aposticha of Tuesday Vespers as a Stavrotheotokion anticipating the mid-Lenten veneration of the Holy Cross.
[^1297]: Tone 3 Podoben: "Beholding the beauty of thy virginity" (Красотѣ дѣтва) — Byzantine automelon Τὴν ὡραιότητα τῆς παρθενίας σου, the festive sessional melody adapted here to an ascetic sessional hymn on fasting and the Cross.
[^1298]: Tone 2 Podoben: "Having risen from the tomb" (Воскресъ от гроба) — Byzantine automelon Ἀναστὰς ἐκ τοῦ μνήματος, the Tone 2 Resurrectional sessional hymn adapted to mid-Lenten ascetic contemplation.
[^1299]: Tone 2 Podoben: "Being the wellspring of loving-kindness" (Милосердія) — Byzantine automelon Τῆς εὐσπλαγχνίας ὑπάρχουσα πηγή, sung as a Stavrotheotokion.
[^1300]: Tone 3 Ode 3 Heirmos: "The confirmation" (Утвержденіе) — Greek Τὸ στερέωμα τῶν ἐπὶ σοὶ πεποιθότων, calling upon Christ as the bedrock of believers.
[^1301]: Tone 3 Ode 8 Heirmos: "Unto Him Who walked upon the unstable waters" (Непостоянному) — Greek Τῷ ἐν ἀстаσίαις..., evoking Christ's divine power over creation.
[^1302]: Tone 2 Ode 8 Heirmos: "In the bush unto Moses" (Въ купинѣ Мѡѵсеи) — Greek Ἐν βάτῳ Μωσεῖ, typifying the virgin birth and divine revelation.
[^1303]: Tone 3 Ode 9 Heirmos: "In the shadow of the law" (Въ законѣ сѣни) — Greek Ἐν νόμου σκιᾷ καὶ γράμματι τύπον κατίδωμεν οἱ πιστοί, typologically interpreting Old Testament types (the bronze serpent, Num. 21) as fulfilled on the Cross.

[^1304]: Ode 9 Heirmos incipit: *Another Heirmos: "Thee, the noetic" (Тебе мысленную)*: Greek: Τὴν νοητὴν καὶ ἔμψυχον κλίμακα (Tone 2, Ode 9 Heirmos by St. Theodore the Studite).
[^1305]: Podoben model: *Tone 6, Podoben: "Having laid aside all hope" (Все отложше)*: Greek: Ὅλην ἀποθέμενοι (Stichera Prosomoia of Kyr Joseph).
[^1306]: Podoben model: *Tone 6, Podoben: "On the right hand of the Savior" (Ѡдесную Спаса)*: Greek: Ἐκ δεξιῶν τοῦ Σωτῆρος (Prosomoion of Kyr Theodore).
[^1307]: Prov. 9:12a–d LXX: The Septuagint and Church Slavonic witness contains four additional verses (Prov. 9:12a–d) absent from the Hebrew Masoretic Text, expanding the contrast between the wisdom of instruction and the deceit of falsehood.
[^1308]: Prov. 9:18a–c LXX: The Septuagint and Church Slavonic witness contains an extended concluding exhortation (Prov. 9:18a–c) warning against crossing strange waters and drinking from foreign fountains, absent from the Masoretic Text.
[^1309]: *Markian Chapter 4: On the Annunciation of the Most Holy Theotokos*: In the Typikon tradition, Markian Chapters (*Марковы главы*, named after the Byzantine canonist and hymnodist Mark, monk of Saint Sabbas or of Mount Saint Auxentios) provide detailed ordinal rubrics governing the coincidence of fixed feasts—specifically the Annunciation of the Theotokos (March 25)—with moveable fast and festal days of the Triodion.
[^1310]: Paschalion Key 2 (`Клю́чъ Па́схалїи, то, в҃, сі́и т҃е: Ма́ртїа, ке҃`): The Paschalian Key (*Kliuch Paschalii*) represents the cyclic Paschal letter/number in the Great Indiction (Kyivan *Kliuch granits*), designating the calendrical coordination where March 25 falls on Wednesday of the Third Week of the Great Fast.
[^1311]: Podoben model: *Tone 6, Podoben: "Having placed all their hope" (Все упованїе)*: Greek: Ὅλην ἀποθέμενοι (Sessional Hymn of Kyr Joseph after the 2nd Stichologia).
[^1312]: Podoben model: *Tone 6, Podoben: "The Angelic Powers" (Аггельскїя силы)*: Greek: Αἱ Ἀγγελικαὶ δυνάμεις (Sessional Hymn of Kyr Theodore after the 3rd Stichologia).
[^1313]: Ode 4 Heirmos: *Ode 4, Tone 6. Heirmos: "Christ is my power" (Христос моя сила)*: Greek: Χριστός μου δύναμις (Triode of Kyr Joseph).
[^1314]: Ode 4 Heirmos: *Another Heirmos, of Kyr Theodore. Tone 6, Heirmos: "The Prophet heard" (Услыша пророк)*: Greek: Ἀκήκοεν ὁ Προφήτης (Hab. 3:2; Triode of Kyr Theodore).

[^1315]: Ode 4 Heirmos incipit and text: *Ode 4 Heirmos: "The Prophet heard" (Услыша пророк)*: Greek: Ἀκήκοεν ὁ Προφήτης (Hab. 3:2 LXX; Tone 6 Triode of Kyr Theodore).
[^1316]: Ode 8 Heirmos: *Ode 8, Tone 6. Heirmos: "From the flame for the saints" (Из пламене святым)*: Greek: Ἐκ φλογὸς τοῖς Ὁσίοις (Tone 6, Ode 8 Heirmos by St. Joseph the Hymnographer).
[^1317]: Ode 8 Heirmos: *Another Heirmos, of Kyr Theodore. The same Tone [Tone 6], Heirmos: "Thy singing children" (Пѣнии твои отроци)*: Greek: Οἱ ὑμνῳδοί σου παῖδες ἐν τῇ καμίνῳ (Tone 6, Ode 8 Heirmos by St. Theodore the Studite).
[^1318]: Ode 9 Heirmos: *Ode 9, Tone 6. Heirmos: "God cannot be seen of men" (Бога человѣком)*: Greek: Θεὸν ἀνθρώποις ἰδεῖν ἀδύνατον (Tone 6, Ode 9 Heirmos by St. Joseph the Hymnographer).
[^1319]: Ode 9 Heirmos: *Another Heirmos, of Kyr Theodore. The same Tone [Tone 6], Heirmos: "Conception without seed" (Безсѣменна зачатіа)*: Greek: Ἀσπόρου συλλήψεως (Tone 6, Ode 9 Heirmos by St. Theodore the Studite).
[^1320]: Podoben model: *Tone 5, Podoben: "Rejoice, O container of ascetics" (Радуйся постником)*: Greek: Χαίροις ἀσκητικῶν (Stichera Prosomoia of Kyr Joseph).
[^1321]: Podoben model: *Tone 1, Podoben: "A cloud of light" (Ѡ́блакъ тя)*: Greek: Νεφέλην σε φωτὸς (Prosomoion of St. Theodore the Studite).
[^1322]: Woodcut engraving: Noah's Ark on the waters of the Deluge with Cyrillic inscription: *Пото́пъ го́рькагѡ мі́ра Непослу́шный ѡ҆сꙋжда́етъ* ("The Deluge condemns the disobedience of the bitter world"), depicting the Ark with animals and rain falling from clouds onto drowning mankind.
[^1323]: Gen. 7:11 LXX: The Septuagint and Church Slavonic witness records the onset of the Deluge "in the six hundred and first year of the life of Noah, in the second month, on the twenty-seventh day of the month" (LXX: ἐν τῷ ἑξακοσιοστῷ καὶ πρώτῳ ἔτει ἐν τῇ ζωῇ τοῦ Νῶε, τοῦ δευτέρου μηνός, ἑβδόμῃ καὶ εἰκάδι τοῦ μηνός), diverging from the Hebrew Masoretic Text ("in the six hundredth year... in the second month, the seventeenth day of the month").
[^1324]: Prov. 10:5a LXX: The Septuagint and Church Slavonic tradition contains an additional proverb (Prov. 10:5a) absent from the Hebrew Masoretic Text: "A man of understanding is saved from scorching heat, but a lawless son is smitten by the wind in harvest" (διεσώθη ἀπὸ καύματος ἀνὴρ νοήμων, ἀνεμοφόρητος δὲ γίνεται ἐν ἀμήτῳ υἱὸς παράνομος).
[^1325]: Prov. 10:10 LXX: The second hemistich in the Septuagint and Church Slavonic reads "but he that reprovs with boldness makes peace" (ὁ δὲ ἐλέγχων μετὰ παρρησίας εἰρηνοποιεῖ), contrasting with the Masoretic Text's repetition of "a prating fool shall fall".
[^1326]: Podoben model: *Tone 6, Podoben: "Having risen on the third day" (Третій день)*: Greek: Τριήμερος ἀνέστης (Stavrotheotokion).
[^1327]: Rubrical cross-reference: "(see fol. 474)[^1327]": Physical leaf p487 (printed book page 474) containing the fourth Markian Chapter rubric on the concurrence of the Feast of the Annunciation with Wednesday and Thursday of the Third Week of the Great Fast.

[^1328]: Concurrence rubric: If the Apodosis of the Annunciation (March 26) coincides with a Lenten Saturday (the Fourth or Fifth Saturday of the Great Fast), the Four-Ode Canon (Четверопѣ́снецъ) appointed for Friday Compline is anticipated and sung on Thursday evening at Compline.
[^1329]: Podoben model: *Tone 5, Podoben: "The Word co-unoriginate" (Собезнача́лное)*: Greek: Τὸν συνάναρχον Λόγον (Automelon of Tone 5). Hymnographer: St. Joseph the Hymnographer.
[^1330]: Podoben model: *Tone 4, Podoben: "Thy tomb, O Savior" (Гро́бъ тво́й)*: Greek: Τὸν τάφον σου Σωτήρ (ordinarily Tone 1 in Byzantine usage, but specifically designated Tone 4 in this 1640 Kyivan witness). Hymnographer: St. Theodore the Studite.
[^1331]: Triode of Kyr Joseph: The weekday Lenten three-ode canons (Triodia) composed by St. Joseph the Hymnographer (d. 886), paired with those of St. Theodore the Studite (d. 826).
[^1332]: Ode 5 Heirmos: *Ode 5, Tone 5. Heirmos: "Thou Who coverest Thyself with light" (Ѡдѣ́ѧйсѧ свѣ́томъ)*: Greek: Ὁ ἀναβαλλόμενος φῶς ὡς ἱμάτιον (Ps. 103:2 LXX; Tone 5, Ode 5 Heirmos by St. Joseph the Hymnographer).
[^1333]: Ode 5 Heirmos (Theodore): *Another Heirmos, of Kyr Theodore. Tone 4, Heirmos: "Rising early from the night" (Ѿ но́щи ѹ҆́треннююще)*: Greek: Ἐκ νυκτὸς ὀρθρίζοντες (Isa. 26:9 LXX; Tone 4, Ode 5 Heirmos by St. Theodore the Studite). The marginal letters "Зонъ" in the Slavonic text represent part of the acrostic designation for Theodore's kanonion.
[^1334]: Ode 8 Heirmos: *Ode 8, Tone 5. Heirmos: "Thee, the Creator of all" (Тебѐ вседѣ́телю)*: Greek: Σὲ τὸν Παντουργόν (Tone 5, Ode 8 Heirmos by St. Joseph the Hymnographer).
[^1335]: Ode 8 Heirmos (Theodore): *Another Heirmos. Tone 4, Heirmos: "The hymn-singers in the furnace" (Пѣсносло́вца въ пе́щи)*: Greek: Τοὺς ἐν καμίνῳ ὑμνολόγους (Dan. 3:51–90; Tone 4, Ode 8 Heirmos by St. Theodore the Studite).
[^1336]: Ode 9 Heirmos: *Ode 9, Tone 5. Heirmos: "O Isaiah, dance!" (И҆са́їа ликꙋ́й)*: Greek: Ἡσαΐα χόρευε (cf. Isa. 7:14; Tone 5, Ode 9 Heirmos by St. Joseph the Hymnographer).
[^1337]: Ode 9 Heirmos (Theodore): *Another Heirmos. Tone 4, Heirmos: "Thee Whom Moses beheld" (Тѧ̀ ю́же ви́дѣ)*: Greek: Τὴν φανεῖσαν τῷ Μωϋσῇ (Tone 4, Ode 9 Heirmos by St. Theodore the Studite).
[^1338]: Exaposteilarion rubric: Tone 4 Exaposteilarion of the day, sung from the general Triodion or Octoechos according to the Tone of the week.
[^1339]: Rubrical cross-reference: "(see fol. 74)[^1339]": Physical leaf 87 (printed book folio 74, Листъ о҃д), which contains the Aposticha verses (Ps. 122:1, 3 LXX) and weekday Martyrika.
[^1340]: Podoben model: *Tone 6, Podoben: "Having risen on the third day" (Тре́тїй дн҃ь)*: Greek: Τριήμερος ἀνέστης (Stavrotheotokion).
[^1341]: Isa. 13:3 LXX: The Septuagint and Church Slavonic witness records: "they are sanctified, and giants come to execute Mine anger, rejoicing together and reviling" (LXX: ἡγιασμένοι εἰσί, καὶ ἐγὼ ἄγω αὐτούς· γίγαντες ἔρχονται πληρῶσαι τὸν θυμόν μου, χαίροντες ἅμα καὶ ὑβρίζοντες), where the Hebrew Masoretic Text reads: "Mine anger, even my proudly exulting ones".
[^1342]: Isa. 13:8 LXX: The Septuagint and Church Slavonic tradition reads: "The elders shall be troubled... and their countenance shall they change like flame" (LXX: ταραχθήσονται οἱ πρέσβεις, καὶ ὠδῖνες αὐτοὺς ἕξουσιν ὡς γυναικὸς τικτούσης... καὶ μεταβαλοῦσι τὸ πρόσωπον αὐτῶν ὡς φλόγα), diverging from the Hebrew Masoretic Text ("They shall be dismayed: pangs and sorrows shall take hold of them...").
[^1343]: Isa. 13:10 LXX: The Septuagint explicitly specifies "Orion" (ὁ Ὠρίων; Church Slavonic: Ѡ҆рїѡ́нъ), representing the Hebrew constellation Kesil (כְּסִיל).
[^1344]: Isa. 13:12 LXX: The Church Slavonic reads "more honored than a stone from Sapphire" (па́че не́жели ка́мень и҆́же ѿ Сапфи́ра), reflecting the Greek reading λίθον τὸν ἐν Σουφίρ (Ophir / Souphir), vocalized as or associated with sapphire.
[^1345]: Isa. 13:13 LXX: The Septuagint and Church Slavonic close the clause with "in the day whereinsoever His wrath shall come" (LXX: ἐν τῇ ἡμέρᾳ, ᾗ ἂν ἐπέλθῃ ὁ θυμὸς αὐτοῦ).
[^1346]: Rubrical cross-reference: "(see fol. 312)[^1346]": Physical leaf 325 (printed book folio 312, Листъ т҃в́i), containing the complete order of Vespers stichera distribution and dismissals.
[^1347]: Gen. 8:4 LXX: The Septuagint and Church Slavonic text records the ark coming to rest "in the seventh month, on the twenty-seventh day of the month" (LXX: ἑβδόμῃ καὶ εἰκάδι τοῦ μηνός), contrasting with the Hebrew Masoretic Text ("seventeenth day").
[^1348]: Gen. 8:19b LXX: The phrase "on the first day of the third month" (LXX: ἐν τῷ μηνὶ τῷ τρίτῳ, πρώτῃ τοῦ μηνός; Church Slavonic: въ пе́рвый Мц҃а тре́тїягѡ) is a distinct Septuagint chronological specification absent from the Hebrew Masoretic Text.
[^1349]: Prov. 11:4b LXX: The second half of verse 4 in the Septuagint and Church Slavonic contains an additional sententia absent from the Hebrew Masoretic Text: "when a righteous man dies, he leaves repentance, but the destruction of the ungodly is sudden and derisive" (LXX: τελευτήσας δίκαιος ἔλιпе μετάμελον, πρόχειρος δὲ γίνεται καὶ ἐπίχαρτος ἀσεβῶν ἀπώλεια).
[^1350]: Prov. 11:7 LXX: The Septuagint and Church Slavonic reading affirms that "when a righteous man dies, his hope does not perish" (LXX: τελευτήσαντος ἀνδρὸς δικαίου οὐκ ὄλλυται ἐλπίς), contrasting with the Hebrew Masoretic Text which speaks only of the expectation of the wicked perishing.

[^1388]: Katavasia for Ode 4 of the Cross: "Seeing Thee upon the Cross, O Mighty One" (*На Крестѣ́ тя си́льне, свѣти́ло вели́кое ви́дя*), Habakkuk the Prophet's canticle type.

[^1511]: Heirmos incipit: Tone 1, Ode 8: *Се́й ᲂу҆́бѡ нарече́нный* ("This chosen and holy day", referring to the Paschal Heirmos model from Ode 8 of the Paschal Canon: Αὕτη ἡ κλητὴ καὶ ἁγία ἡμέρα).
[^1512]: Slavonic: *трича́стный и҆ вели́кїй* (tripartite and great). In Patristic and Kyivan liturgical symbolism, the tripartite Cross refers to the traditional Byzantine three-bar Cross (titulus, main crossbeam, and suppedaneum) composed mystically of three types of wood (cypress, pine, and cedar; cf. Isa. 60:13 LXX).
[^1513]: Scriptural citation: Jeremiah 11:19 LXX: "Come, and let us put wood into his bread, and let us utterly cut him off from the land of the living" (δεῦτε καὶ ἐμβάλωμεν ξύλον εἰς τὸν ἄρτον αὐτοῦ / приидите и вложимъ древо въ хлѣбъ его). The Church Fathers (e.g. Tertullian, St. Justin Martyr, St. Cyril of Jerusalem) interpret this as a prophetic type of Christ the Living Bread nailed to the Wood of the Cross by the Jews.
[^1514]: Scriptural reference: Daniel 6:16–24 / Daniel 14:31–42 LXX (Bel and the Dragon). Daniel stretching his arms crosswise in prayer within the lions' den.
[^1515]: Tone 4, Ode 9 Heirmos: *Вся́къ земноро́дный* (Let every earthborn mortal leap for joy in spirit). Byzantine Greek model: Ἅπας γηγενής.
[^1516]: Scriptural allusion: Joel 3:18 / Amos 9:13 LXX ("the mountains shall drop sweetness, and the hills shall flow with milk").
[^1517]: Scriptural citation: 4 Kings 6:5–7 LXX (2 Kings 6:5–7). Elisha causing the iron axe-head to float to the surface of the Jordan by casting in a piece of wood, a classic Patristic type of the Cross rescuing human nature from the abyss of sin.
[^1518]: Scriptural citation: Isaiah 53:2 LXX ("He hath no form nor comeliness; and when we saw Him, He had no form nor beauty").
[^1519]: Scriptural citation: Psalm 67:16–17 LXX ("A mountain of God, a rich mountain, a mountain of curds, a fat mountain: therefore look ye askance, ye curdled mountains?").
[^1520]: Exapostilarion melodic model: Slavonic *По́: Дх҃омъ* (Podoben: "In the Spirit", referencing the Byzantine Exapostilarion model *Τοῖς μαθηταῖς συνέλθωμεν* / *Дꙋ́хомъ во свѧти́лищи*).
[^1521]: Luke 1:26–38 (The Annunciation). The Cross-Theotokion poignantly contrasts the Archangel Gabriel's joyful greeting at Nazareth with the sorrow of Golgotha.
[^1522]: Scriptural citation: John 8:56 ("Your father Abraham rejoiced to see My day: and he saw it, and was glad") and Genesis 22 (the binding and sparing of Isaac on Mount Moriah as a type of the Resurrection).
[^1523]: Liturgical cross-reference: In the 1640 printed edition, the Sunday rubrics and idiomela for the Veneration of the Cross are found on fol. 524 (physical leaf p536). Codicological coordinate: Physical Leaf p536.
[^1524]: Codicological Note: In the digital exemplar / physical binding, leaves p575–p580 constitute a duplicate photographic sequence of printed pages 552–557 (which previously appeared on physical leaves p565–p570). In strict adherence to the Closed Mathematical Leaf Conservation Law, the text of each physical leaf is transcribed and translated unabridged as witnessed by the physical scan.
[^1525]: Tone 4, Ode 3 Heirmos: *Твоѧ̀ пѣвцы̀* (Thy hymnographers, O Theotokos; Byzantine Greek: Τοὺς σοὺς ὑμνολόγους Θεοτόκε).
[^1526]: Scriptural citation: Exodus 17:8–13. Moses holding his hands extended crosswise upon the mountain, securing victory over Amalek.

[^1529]: Tone 1, Ode 3 Another Heirmos: *Оу҆твердѝ Гдⷭ҇и цр҃ковь* (Establish, O Lord, Thy Church).
[^1530]: Scriptural citation: Psalm 131:7 LXX ("We will worship at the place where His feet stood").
[^1531]: Tone 4, Ode 4 Heirmos: *Сѣдѧ́й въ сла́вѣ* (He Who sits in glory; Byzantine Greek: Ὁ καθήμενος ἐν δόξῃ).
[^1532]: Scriptural citation: Genesis 48:13–19. The Patriarch Jacob crossing his hands when blessing Ephraim and Manasseh, forming the sign of the Cross.
[^1533]: Tone 4, Ode 5 Heirmos: *Оу҆диви́шася всѧ́ческая* (All things were amazed; Byzantine Greek: Ἐξέστη τὰ σύμπαντα).
[^1534]: Scriptural citation: Colossians 2:14 ("Blotting out the handwriting of ordinances that was against us, which was contrary to us, and took it out of the way, nailing it to His Cross").

[^1547]: Codicological Note: In the digital exemplar / physical binding, leaves p581–p582 conclude the duplicate photographic sequence of quires (printed pages 558–559, which previously appeared on physical leaves p571–p572). In strict adherence to the Closed Mathematical Leaf Conservation Law, the text of each physical leaf is transcribed and translated unabridged as witnessed by the physical scan.
[^1548]: Tone 1, Ode 8 Heirmos incipit: *Се́й ᲂу҆́бѡ нарече́нный* ("This chosen and holy day", referring to the Paschal Heirmos model from Ode 8 of the Paschal Canon: Αὕτη ἡ κλητὴ καὶ ἁγία ἡμέρα).
[^1549]: Slavonic: *трича́стный и҆ вели́кїй* (tripartite and great). In Patristic and Kyivan liturgical symbolism, the tripartite Cross refers to the traditional Byzantine three-bar Cross (titulus, main crossbeam, and suppedaneum) composed mystically of three types of wood (cypress, pine, and cedar; cf. Isa. 60:13 LXX).
[^1550]: Scriptural citation: Jeremiah 11:19 LXX: "Come, and let us put wood into his bread, and let us utterly cut him off from the land of the living" (δεῦτε καὶ ἐμβάλωμεν ξύλον εἰς τὸν ἄρτον αὐτοῦ / приидите и вложимъ древо въ хлѣбъ его). The Church Fathers (e.g. Tertullian, St. Justin Martyr, St. Cyril of Jerusalem) interpret this as a prophetic type of Christ the Living Bread nailed to the Wood of the Cross by the Jews.
[^1551]: Scriptural reference: Daniel 6:16–24 / Daniel 14:31–42 LXX (Bel and the Dragon). Daniel stretching his arms crosswise in prayer within the lions' den.
[^1552]: Tone 4, Ode 9 Heirmos: *Вся́къ земноро́дный* (Let every earthborn mortal leap for joy in spirit). Byzantine Greek model: Ἅπας γηγενής.
[^1553]: Scriptural allusion: Joel 3:18 / Amos 9:13 LXX ("the mountains shall drop sweetness, and the hills shall flow with milk").
[^1554]: Scriptural citation: 4 Kings 6:5–7 LXX (2 Kings 6:5–7). Elisha causing the iron axe-head to float to the surface of the Jordan by casting in a piece of wood, a classic Patristic type of the Cross rescuing human nature from the abyss of sin.
[^1555]: Codicological Note: Physical leaf p583 (printed page 560) begins Quire з҃з, where the unique liturgical sequence of the codex resumes with Thursday Matins Ode 8. The opening portion of Thursday Matins (prior to the final phrase of this troparion) was omitted in the quire imposition and assembly of this historical exemplar.

[^1659]: Liturgical Scripture pericopes for Saturday of the 4th Week of Lent: Epistle to the Hebrews, pericope 313 (Heb. 6:9–12); Epistle for the reposed, 1 Cor. pericope 163 (1 Cor. 15:47–57); Gospel according to Mark, pericope 31 (Mark 7:31–37).
[^1660]: An impressive full-page woodcut engraving (fol. 601r) depicting the Ladder of Divine Ascent (Κλῖμαξ / Лествица), signed at bottom left by the master Lavra engraver Monk Ilia ("ІЛЇА АЗ") and dated in Cyrillic numerals at bottom right "҂АХМ" (AM 7148 / AD 1640). The iconography illustrates the ascetical treatise of St. John Climacus, Abbot of Mount Sinai: thirty rungs ascending from earth to heaven, where Christ reaches down from celestial glory to crown ascending monks, while winged demons ensnare the negligent and drag them into the flaming jaws of Hades.
[^1661]: Rubrical witness noting the historical variation in typika: ancient Studite and early Jerusalem ordo kept the 4th Sunday focused primarily on the Parable of the Good Samaritan (as reflected in the Triodion canon), whereas later Athonite and neo-Sabaitic editions assigned the feast of St. John Climacus to this Sunday.
[^1662]: Tone 8 Prosomoion model: "O most marvelous wonder" (Greek: Ὢ τοῦ παραδόξου θαύματος; Slavonic: Ѡ҆ пресла́вное чꙋ́до / Ѡ҆ пресла́внагѡ чꙋдесѐ).
[^1663]: Allusion to Ps. 91:13–14 LXX (Ps. 92:12–13 MT): "The righteous shall flourish like a palm tree; he shall be increased like a cedar in Lebanon. They that are planted in the house of the Lord shall flourish in the courts of our God."
[^1664]: Internal cross-reference to leaf 304 (f. 304r) for the text of the Theotokion "O new wonder" (Ѡ҆ чꙋдесѝ но́вомꙋ).
[^1665]: Mark Chapter (Маркова глава) providing rubrical regulations for resolving liturgical concurrences when the commemoration of a Saint with Polyeleos or Doxology (a Martyr or other notable Saint) coincides with the Fourth or Fifth Sunday of Great Lent.
[^1666]: Rubrical variant providing an alternative allotment of the canonic odes at Matins when combining the Sunday Resurrection canon with the Theotokion and the two canons for the Martyrs.
[^1667]: Internal cross-reference to leaf 421 (f. 421r), where the common order for the 5th Sunday of Lent is treated.
[^1668]: Mark Chapter 84 (Маркова глава п҃д): Extensive typikon statute regulating the complex liturgical coincidence when the Great Feast of the Annunciation of the Most Holy Theotokos (March 25) falls on the Fourth or Fifth Sunday of Great Lent.
[^1669]: Tone 4 Prosomoion model: "Being called from on high" (Greek: Ὁ κληθεὶς ἄνωθεν; Slavonic: Зва́ный свы́шше).
[^1670]: The three traditional Old Testament Prophecies (Paremias) appointed for the Great Feast of the Annunciation: Ex. 3:1–8 (The Theophany in the Burning Bush on Mount Horeb); Prov. 8:22–30 (The uncreated Wisdom of God existing before all ages); and Ezek. 43:27–44:4 (The Prophet Ezekiel's vision of the closed East Gate through which none but the Lord God of Israel may enter).
[^1671]: Slavonic: кле́плетъ, referring to the sounding of the klepalo (the traditional wooden semantron or sounding board) to summon the brethren for the solemn liturgical procession (Litiya) outside the monastery walls.
[^1672]: Mark Chapter regulating the coincidence of the Synaxis of the Archangel Gabriel (March 26, the Afterfeast of the Annunciation) with the Fourth or Fifth Sunday of Great Lent.
[^1673]: Internal cross-reference to leaf 423 (f. 423r), where the initial order of Sunday Matins in Lent is detailed.
[^1674]: Heirmos of Ode 1, Canon of the Triodion, Tone 5: "Unto God our Savior" (Greek: Τῷ σωτῆρι Θεῷ; Slavonic: Сп҃телю Бг҃ꙋ). The hymnography of this canon is based upon the Parable of the Good Samaritan (Luke 10:25–37), allegorically representing humanity fallen among thieves (demons and sins) and healed by Christ the Good Samaritan.
[^1675]: Heirmos of Ode 1, Canon of St. John Climacus, Tone 8: "Having traversed the water" (Greek: Ὑγρὰν διοδεύσας / Slavonic: Во́дꙋ проше́дъ).
[^1676]: Katavasia: Heirmos of Ode 1 from the Canon of the Theotokos, Tone 4: "I shall open my mouth" (Greek: Ἀνοίξω τὸ στόμα μου; Slavonic: Ѿве́рзꙋ ᲂу҆ста̀ моѧ̑).
[^1677]: Heirmos of Ode 3, Canon of the Triodion, Tone 5: "Establish" (Greek: Στερέωσον; Slavonic: ᲊ҆твердѝ).
[^1678]: Heirmos of Ode 3, Canon of St. John Climacus, Tone 8: "Thou art the establishment" (Greek: Σὺ εἶ τὸ στερέωμα; Slavonic: Ты є҆сѝ ᲂу҆твержде́нїе).
[^1679]: Katavasia: Heirmos of Ode 3 from the Canon of the Theotokos, Tone 4: "O Mother of God, thy hymnographers" (Greek: Τοὺς σοὺς ὑμνολόγους, Θεοτόκε; Slavonic: Твоѧ̑ пѣ́сносло́вцы, Бцⷣе).

[^1680]: Matins Sessional Hymn of the Precious Cross, Tone 5, Podoben: "Strange wonder" (Стра́нное чꙋ́до / Τὸ ξένον θαῦμα), preserving the Mid-Lenten Cross veneration devotion on the Fourth Sunday of Great Lent.
[^1681]: Sessional Hymn to the Venerable John Climacus, Tone 4, Podoben: "Thou Who wast lifted up upon the Cross" (Вознесы́йся на Крⷭ҇тъ во́лею / Ὁ ὑψωθεὶς ἐν τῷ Σταυρῷ).
[^1682]: Heirmos of Ode 4, Canon of the Triodion, Tone 5: "I have heard, O Lord" (ᲊ҆слы́шахъ Гдⷭ҇и / Εἰσήκουσα Κύριε). The hymnography continues the allegorical contemplation of the Parable of the Good Samaritan (Luke 10:25–37), representing the soul wounded by passions and neglected by the Old Covenant Law and Priesthood (the Priest and Levite), awaiting Christ the Good Samaritan.
[^1683]: Heirmos of Ode 4, Canon to St. John Climacus, Tone 8: "I have heard, O Lord" (ᲊ҆слы́шахъ Гдⷭ҇и / Εἰσήκουσα Κύριε).
[^1684]: Rubrical instruction indicating the Katavasia of Ode 4 from the Canon of the Most Holy Theotokos (Tone 4): "He Who sits in glory upon the throne of the Godhead" (Сѣдѧ́й въ сла́вѣ на прⷭ҇тлѣ бж҃тва / Ὁ καθήμενος ἐν δόξῃ).
[^1685]: Heirmos of Ode 5, Canon of the Triodion, Tone 5: "Rising at dawn out of the night" (Ѡ҆ но́щи ᲂу҆́треннююще / Ὀρθρίζοντες ἐκ νυκτός), based on Isa. 26:9.
[^1686]: Heirmos of Ode 5, Canon to St. John Climacus, Tone 8: "Why hast Thou cast me off" (Въскꙋ́ю мѧ̀ ѿри́нꙋлъ є҆сѝ / Ἵνα τί με ἀπώσω), based on Ps. 43:24 and Ps. 87:15 LXX.
[^1687]: Katavasia: Heirmos of Ode 5 from the Canon of the Most Holy Theotokos (Tone 4): "All things were amazed at thy divine glory" (ᲊ҆диви́шасѧ всѧ́чєскаѧ ѡ҆ бж҃твеннѣй сла́вѣ твое́й / Ἐξέστη τὰ σύμπανта).
[^1688]: The historical Life of St. John Climacus (Vita Sancti Joannis Scholastici / Sinaitae), Abbot of Mount Sinai and author of the *Ladder of Divine Ascent*, composed in the 7th century by Daniel, monk of the nearby monastery of Raithu (Раїѳꙋ̀). In this 1640 Kyivan edition of Petro Mohyla, the Life is translated into Early Modern Ruthenian theological prose (*prosta mova*), designed for direct edification during monastic refectory or Matins reading.
[^1689]: Marginal scriptural reference to Heb. 12:22–23: "But ye are come unto mount Sion, and unto the city of the living God, the heavenly Jerusalem, and to an innumerable company of angels, to the general assembly and church of the firstborn, which are written in heaven."
[^1690]: Scriptural allusion to Ps. 25:12 LXX (Ps. 26:12 MT): "My foot hath stood in uprightness; in the congregations will I bless Thee, O Lord."
[^1691]: Marginal note: "Having dedicated his youthful years unto God in monastic life at sixteen years of age" (ро́кꙋ лѣⷮ свои б҃гꙋ поста́вѧ и҆ноко-).
[^1692]: Marginal note: "In very great obedience" (в ба́рзо великои є҆го послꙋша́нїе). John submitted himself as an obedient novice under the renowned Sinai elder Abba Martyrius.
[^1693]: Greek: σταδίους δὲ πέντε τῆς ἐκκλησίας ἀπελθών (five stadia, approx. two-thirds of a mile from the central basilica of the Monastery of the Transfiguration / St. Catherine).
[^1694]: Tholas (Ѳѡ́ла / Θωλᾶ), the valley and hermitage roughly two miles south of Mount Sinai where St. John spent forty years in solitary ascetical contemplation.
[^1695]: Marginal scriptural citation to Eph. 5:5: "For this ye know, that no whoremonger, nor unclean person, nor covetous man, who is an idolater, hath any inheritance in the kingdom of Christ and of God."
[^1696]: The eight principal vices (gluttony, fornication, avarice, anger, sadness, acedia/sloth, vainglory, pride) cataloged by Evagrius Ponticus, St. John Cassian, and analyzed extensively by St. John Climacus in Steps 14–30 of the *Ladder*.
[^1697]: Typological comparison to Bezalel (Веселеи́лъ / Βεσελεήλ, Ex. 31:2–5), whom God endowed with divine wisdom and craftsmanship to build the Tabernacle; St. John Climacus is hailed as the new spiritual architect of the tabernacle of virtues.
[^1698]: Moses, the solitary ascetic of Sinai who became Climacus' disciple and was later ordained priest, here preserved through Climacus' intercession.
[^1699]: The intense midday heat of August at Mount Sinai, where temperatures in the rocky desert canyons regularly exceed 40°C (104°F).
[^1700]: Marginal scriptural citation to Isa. 65:24: "And it shall come to pass, that before they call, I will answer; and while they are yet speaking, I will hear."
[^1701]: Marginal citation combining 2 Cor. 12:9 ("My grace is sufficient for thee, for My strength is made perfect in weakness") with Phil. 4:13 ("I can do all things through Christ Who strengthens me").
[^1702]: Scriptural imagery alluding to Moses ascending Mount Sinai into the divine cloud (Ex. 24:18), Ps. 44:2 LXX ("My heart hath poured forth a good word"), and Matt. 12:35 ("A good man out of the good treasure of the heart brings forth good things").
[^1703]: An allusion to a holy contemporary witness or elder named David, renowned among the Sinai or Raithu fathers.
[^1704]: John, Abbot of Raithu (Їѡа́ннъ и҆гꙋ́менъ Раїѳскій), who wrote the formal epistolary request to John Climacus entreating him to compose the *Ladder of Divine Ascent* for the guidance of monastics.
[^1705]: St. John Climacus reposed peacefully ca. AD 649 at an advanced age, having governed the Monastery of Mount Sinai for several years before retiring once again to Tholas.
[^1706]: Heirmos of Ode 7, Canon of the Triodion, Tone 5: "The highly exalted" (Превозноси́мый / Ὁ ὑπερυψούμενος).
[^1707]: Heirmos of Ode 7, Canon to St. John Climacus, Tone 8: "The children that went down from Judea" (Ѿ І҆ꙋде́а доше́дшїи / Οἱ ἐκ τῆς Ἰουδαίας καταντήсанτες), based on the Song of the Three Holy Youths in Babylon (Dan. 3).

[^1708]: Katavasia of Ode 7: Heirmos from the Canon of the Most Holy Theotokos (Tone 4): "The godly-minded youths worshipped not the creation rather than the Creator" (Не послꙋжи́ша тва́ри бг҃омꙋ́дрїи / Οὐκ ἐλάτреυσαν τῇ κτίσει οἱ θεόφρονες), based on Dan. 3 and Rom. 1:25.
[^1709]: Heirmos of Ode 8, Canon of the Triodion, Tone 5: "Unto Thee, the Creator of all" (Тебѣ̀ вседѣ́телю въ пещѝ ѻ҆́троцы / Σοὶ τῷ Παντουργῷ ἐν τῇ καμίνῳ παῖδες). The troparia continue the allegorical meditation on the Parable of the Good Samaritan (Luke 10:25–37), representing the soul wounded by sins and redeemed by Christ.
[^1710]: Heirmos of Ode 8, Canon to St. John Climacus, Tone 8: "The King of heaven" (Цр҃ѧ̀ нб҃наго, є҆го́же пою́тъ вѡ́и а҆́гг҃льстїи / Τὸν Βασιλέα τῶν οὐρανῶν).
[^1711]: Katavasia of Ode 8: Heirmos from the Canon of the Most Holy Theotokos (Tone 4): "The pious youths in the furnace" (Ѻ҆́троки бл҃гочести́выѧ въ пещѝ / Παῖδας εὐαγεῖς ἐν τῇ καμίνῳ).
[^1712]: Heirmos of Ode 9, Canon of the Triodion, Tone 5: "Rejoice, O Isaiah" (И҆са́їа ликꙋ́й, Дв҃а и҆мѣ̀ во чре́вѣ / Ἡσαΐα χόρευε), celebrating the fulfillment of the Messianic prophecy of the Virgin birth in Isa. 7:14.
[^1713]: Patristic allegorical interpretation of the two denarii given by the Good Samaritan to the innkeeper (Luke 10:35), signifying the redemption of both soul and body, or the two Testaments of Holy Scripture entrusted to the Church.
[^1714]: Heirmos of Ode 9, Canon to St. John Climacus, Tone 8: "Her who is more honorable and glorious" (Чтⷭ҇нꙋ́ю и҆ сла́внꙋю / Τὴν τιμιωτέραν καὶ ἐνδοξοτέραν), based on the Magnificat (Luke 1:48–49).
[^1715]: Katavasia of Ode 9: Heirmos from the Canon of the Most Holy Theotokos (Tone 4): "Let every earthborn mortal leap for joy" (Всѧ́къ земноро́дный да взыгра́етсѧ / Ἅπας γηγενὴς σκιρτάτω τῷ πνεύματι).
[^1716]: Exapostilarion to the Venerable John Climacus, Tone 2, Podoben: "Give ear, ye women" (Же́ны ᲂу҆слы́шите / Γυναῖκες ἀκουτίσθητε), celebrating his ascetical triumph over worldly desires and renewal of the soul's strength.
[^1717]: Idiomelon at the Praises, Tone 1, drawing typologically upon the Parable of the Laborers in the Vineyard (Matt. 20:1–16), where spiritual fasting and prayer yield the divine denarius of redemption from sins.
[^1718]: Codicological cross-reference to fol. 167 (p181) for the ordinary Sunday Matins conclusion, Great Doxology, and Trisagion prayers.
[^1719]: Cross-reference to fol. 440 (p454) for the Beatitudes and festive troparia of the Divine Liturgy.
[^1720]: Gospel pericope 40 for the Fourth Sunday of Great Lent, appointed from St. Mark 9:17–31 (the healing of the lunatic child and the power of prayer and fasting), cross-referenced with St. Matthew 17:14–23 and pericope 10 (Matt. 4:25–5:12, the Beatitudes) for St. John Climacus.
[^1721]: Cross-reference to fol. 167 (p181) for the general order of Great Lenten Sunday evening Vespers.
[^1722]: Sticheron of Lord Joseph the Hymnographer, Tone 3, Podoben: "Great is the power of Thy Cross, O Lord" (Ве́лїѧ крⷭ҇та твоегѡ̀ Гдⷭ҇и си́ла / Μεγάλη τοῦ Σταυροῦ σου Κύριε ἡ δύναμις).
[^1723]: Sticheron of Lord Theodore the Studite, Tone 7, Podoben: "Thy protection" (Покро́въ тво́й Хрїстѐ Сп҃се / Ἡ σκέπη σου Χριστὲ Σωτήρ), meditating upon the midpoint of the Lenten course.
[^1724]: Cross-reference to fol. 168 (p182) for the continuation of Great Lenten Sunday evening Vespers.
[^1725]: Idiomelon at the Aposticha, Tone 7, contemplating the spiritual meaning of the Good Samaritan: Christ came not from Samaria, but took flesh from the Holy Virgin to heal Adam and humanity stripped and wounded by passions.
[^1726]: Liturgical rubric governing the transfer of the Menaion commemoration from Thursday of the Great Canon (the Fifth Week), where the massive hymnography of St. Andrew of Crete displaces the Menaion, to Compline on Sunday evening of the Fourth Week.
[^1727]: Special Markian chapter (*Маркова глава*) regulating the liturgical coincidence when the Apodosis (*Apodosis*) of the Feast of the Annunciation of the Theotokos (March 26) falls on Friday of the Fourth Week of Great Lent.
[^1728]: Primary service headpiece inscribed: `В ПѦТО́КЪ Е҃ НЛ҃И СТ҃ГѠ ПОСТА̀` (On Friday of the Fifth Week of the Holy Fast). The running headline on fol. 624 (p637) reads `Ѕ҃ НЛ҃И` (Sixth Week), reflecting the dual orientation of this office: it concludes the weekday cycle of the Fifth Week before Akathist Saturday, while simultaneously anticipating the Sixth Week (Palm Week) and the raising of Lazarus.
[^1729]: Cross-reference to fol. 528 (p542) for the ordinary weekday Matins order.
[^1730]: Sessional Hymn, Tone 3, Podoben: "Beholding the beauty of thy virginity" (Красотѣ̀ дѣ́вства твоегѡ̀ / Τὴν ὡραιότητα τῆς παρθενίας σου).
[^1731]: Sessional Hymn, Tone 7, Podoben: "Tell us" (Рцы̀ на́мъ / Рцы̀ мы є҆мы̀ / Εἰπέ ἡμῖν), explicitly beseeching Christ to grant the faithful to traverse the upcoming holy week and reach the Resurrection of Lazarus.
[^1732]: Heirmos of Ode 5, Triodion Canon of Lord Joseph, Tone 3: "Thou Who of old by divine beck" (Во́ды дре́вле ма́нїемъ бж҃твеннымъ / Ὁ τὰ ὕдаτα πάλαι).

[^1735]: Heirmos of Ode 8, Canon of Lord Joseph, Tone 3: "The Babylonian furnace" (Вавѷлѡ́нскаѧ пе́щь / Ἡ Βαβυλωνία κάμινος).

[^1738]: Scriptural allusion to the barren fig tree cursed by Christ in Matt. 21:19 and Mark 11:13–14, an archetype of the soul lacking the fruits of repentance.

[^1740]: Heirmos of Ode 9, Canon of Lord Joseph, Tone 3: "A new wonder" (Но́вое чꙋ́до / Καινοπреπὲς θαῦμα).

[^1824]: Systematic typesetter erratum in the Kathisma numbering at the Lenten Little Hours on Wednesday of the Fifth Week: The printed text sets "Ка́: г҃і" (13), "д҃і" (14), and "е҃і" (15) for Kathismata 3, 4, and 5 respectively, inadvertently appending an iota (і = 10) to the single-digit numeral; the accompanying psalm incipits correspond strictly to Kathisma 3 at the First Hour (Ps. 17 LXX), Kathisma 4 at the Third Hour (Ps. 24 LXX), and Kathisma 5 at the Sixth Hour (Ps. 32 LXX).

[^1837]: Sticheron 2 of the Apostles by St. Joseph the Hymnographer, Tone 8.

[^1839]: The 24 Alphabetical Stichera of the Great Canon by St. Andrew of Crete, Tone 4, Podoben: "With tears I desired" (Хотѣ́хъ слеза́ми / Ἤθελον δάκρυσιν ἐξαλεῖψαι). Sticheron 1 corresponds to letter Alpha (Α): "Всѐ житїѐ моѐ..." (Ἅπαντα τὸν βίον μου...).
[^1840]: Conclusion of Sticheron 1 (Alpha) of the Great Canon from fol. 653v (physical leaf p666).
[^1841]: Rubric prescribing three metanias / prostrations at each of the 24 stichoi of the Great Canon at Vespers.
[^1842]: Sticheron 2 (Beta) of the Great Canon: "Тѧ́жестїю небреже́нїѧ..." (Βάρει ἀμελείας...).
[^1843]: Sticheron 3 (Gamma) of the Great Canon: "Бы́хъ преткнове́нїе чл҃вкѡмъ..." (Γέγονα πρόσκομμα ἀνθρώποις...).
[^1844]: Sticheron 4 (Delta) of the Great Canon: "Наро́денъ бы́хъ дꙋше́и мое́и..." (Δήμιος γέγονα τῇ ψυχῇ μου...). The Church Slavonic translates the Greek δήμιος (executioner/tormentor) etymologically from δῆμος (people) as "наро́денъ" (public/common/tormenting).
[^1845]: Sticheron 5 (Epsilon) of the Great Canon: "Во́лею съгрѣши́хъ..." (Ἑκουσίως ἥμαρτον...).
[^1846]: Sticheron 6 (Zeta) of the Great Canon: "Жи́ти бꙋ́детъ, гла̀ Прⷬ҇ро́къ..." (Ζήσεται, εἶπεν ὁ προφήτης, citing Ps. 118:175 LXX).
[^1847]: Conclusion of Sticheron 6 (Zeta) of the Great Canon from fol. 654v (physical leaf p667).
[^1848]: Sticheron 7 (Eta) of the Great Canon: "Съгрѣши́хъ съгрѣши́хъ..." (Ἥμαρτον, ἥμαρτον...).
[^1849]: Sticheron 8 (Theta) of the Great Canon: "Звѣ́рїе мѧ̀ ди́вїи ѡ҆держа́ша..." (Θῆρές με ἄγριοι περιέσχον...).
[^1850]: Sticheron 9 (Iota) of the Great Canon: "Цѣлба̀ бꙋ́ди ми..." (Ἴασίς μοι γενοῦ...).
[^1851]: Sticheron 10 (Kappa) of the Great Canon: "Сокры́хъ тала́нтъ..." (Κατέκρυψα τὸ τάλαντον...).
