[^1854]: Sticheron 12 (Mu) of the Great Canon: "И҆́маши сѣдѣ́ти на прⷭ҇тлѣ..." (Μέλλεις καθίσαι ἐπὶ θρόνου...).
[^1855]: Sticheron 13 (Nu) of the Great Canon: "При́зри ѻ҆́комъ..." (Νεῦσον ὄμματι...).
[^1856]: Sticheron 14 (Xi) of the Great Canon: "Ме́чъ ꙋ҆гото́ва Велїа́ръ..." (Ξίφος ἡτοίмасе Βελίαρ...).
[^1857]: Sticheron 15 (Omicron) of the Great Canon: "Ве́сь страсте́мъ порабо́тихсѧ..." (Ὅλος τοῖς πάθεσιν ἐδουλώθην...).
[^1858]: Sticheron 16 (Pi) of the Great Canon from fol. 656v (physical leaf p669): "Блꙋ́дница слеза́ми ѡ҆кроплѧ́етъ..." (Πόρνη τοῖς δάκρυσι...).
[^1859]: Rubric directing that the remaining alphabetical stichera of the Great Canon use the refrain "Glory to Thee, our God, glory to Thee."
[^1860]: Sticheron 17 (Rho) of the Great Canon: "Скве́рнꙋ ѡ҆чстити..." (Ῥύπον ἐκκάθαρον...).
[^1861]: Sticheron 18 (Sigma) of the Great Canon: "У҆твердѝ Вл҃ко..." (Στήριξον Δέσποτα...).
[^1862]: Sticheron 19 (Tau) of the Great Canon: "Стѣ́на нерꙋши́маѧ..." (Τεῖχος ἀῤῥαγές...).
[^1863]: Sticheron 20 (Upsilon) of the Great Canon: "Сы́нъ блꙋ́дный бы́хъ..." (Υἱὸς ἄσωτος γέγονα...).

[^1864]: Sticheron 21 [Greek Phi]: In the Byzantine liturgical tradition, these 24 penitential stichera by St. Andrew of Crete (sung at *Lord, I have cried* on Wednesday evening of the Fifth Week of Great Lent) form a continuous alphabetic acrostic following the 24 letters of the classical Greek alphabet from Alpha (Α) to Omega (Ω). The present sticheron corresponds to the twenty-first letter, Phi (Φ), based on the Greek opening *Φθόνῳ κατέβалεν* (*Phthonō katebalen*, "By envy cast down..."), faithfully reflected in the Church Slavonic incipit *За́вистію низложи́*.
[^1865]: Sticheron 23 [Greek Psi]: The twenty-third sticheron corresponds to the Greek letter Psi (Ψ), opening in the Greek archetype with *Ψυχὴν πρόσδεξαι* (*Psychēn prosdexai*, "Receive my soul..."). The 1640 Kyiv Triodion renders the verbal opening *По́има пріими́ мѧ*, preserving the prayer of whole-person dedication (*"soul and body entirely I place into Thy hands"*).
[^1866]: Rubric for the Theotokion: The rubric specifies chanting in a higher musical pitch (*высшимъ гласомъ*) by both choirs united in the center (*ѡ҆́ба ли́ка в̾кꙋ́пѣ*), celebrating the completion of the 24 alphabetic stichera of the Great Canon at Vespers.
[^1867]: Rubric: If the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts is not celebrated on Wednesday evening (e.g., if superseded or omitted in non-parochial monastic practice), this rubric establishes the plain Vespers order, directing that the 3 Podobni and the 24 Stichera of the Great Canon be sung at *Lord, I have cried*, followed by the idiomelon stichera of the day at the Aposticha.
[^1868]: Prokeimenon, Psalm 93:1-2 LXX: The Slavonic phrase *и҆ не ѡ҆би́ноуласѧ е҆́стъ* directly translates the Septuagint Greek *παρρησιάσατο* (*parrēsiasato*, "He acted boldly / spoke openly", from *παρρησία*), contrasting with the later Synodal text (*явися*).
[^1869]: Genesis 17:1-9: The prescribed Old Testament reading for Wednesday of the Fifth Week at Vespers, recounting God's covenant with Abram, the solemn promise of progeny, and the renaming of Abram to Abraham (*"father of a multitude of nations"*).
[^1870]: Prokeimenon, Psalm 95:1 LXX: The evening prokeimenon concluding the Genesis reading and preceding the second paremia from Proverbs.
[^1871]: Proverbs 15:20b - 16:2 LXX: The second Old Testament paremia follows the Septuagint versification, which exhibits distinct moral and sapiential maxims and structural variations from the Masoretic Hebrew text.
[^1872]: Proverbs 16:3-10 LXX: The continuation of the paremia on fol. 661. In the Septuagint, verses 3-10 emphasize the contrast between the ways of the humble and the destruction reserved for the haughty.
[^1873]: Fasting Rubric at the Trapeza: Monastic typika for Wednesday of the Fifth Week prescribe strict fasting without oil (*на масло не разрѣшаемъ*) and without wine (*вина не пиваемъ*), because of the bodily labor required for the all-night vigil of the Great Canon (*труда ради бдѣннаго, хотящаго быти*).
[^1874]: Small Compline in Cells: Small Compline is read in individual monastic cells without prostrations, incorporating the 24 stichera of St. Andrew's Canon after the Trisagion prayers, while the Menaion service is deferred to Compline.
[^1875]: Typikon Transfer Rubric: If a feast of the Menaion having a vigil (such as a major saint) coincides with Thursday of the Great Canon, its service is transferred in advance to Tuesday of the same fifth week, safeguarding the integral chanting of the Great Canon without curtailment.
[^1876]: Annunciation Transfer Rubric: In the rare liturgical occurrence of the Feast of the Annunciation (March 25) falling on Thursday of the Great Canon, the Great Canon is transferred backward to Tuesday of the fifth week, accompanied by Tuesday's triodion, while the triodion proper to the Great Canon is combined on Thursday with the Annunciation office.
[^1877]: Matins Rubric: The Matins of the Great Canon begins exceptionally early in the night ("in the fourth hour of the night", corresponding to approximately 10:00 PM or 11:00 PM). The Psalter reading is restricted to Kathisma 8 (Psalms 55-63 LXX), followed by the first section of the Life of St. Mary of Egypt by St. Sophronius of Jerusalem, read in the church prior to Psalm 50.
[^1878]: Great Canon Structure and Metanias: The rubric enumerates 250 troparia in the complete Great Canon (*Канѡнъ, .с҃: съ .н҃.*) and 750 prostrations (*а пок: .ѱ҃н.*), calculated at precisely 3 full metanias or prostrations per troparion, alternating between the right and left choirs to maintain liturgical solemnity and order.
[^1879]: Ode 1 Heirmos: Composed by St. Andrew of Crete in Tone 6, drawing its biblical theme and incipit from the first Canticle of Moses at the Red Sea (Exodus 15:1-2: *"He was a helper and protector unto me for salvation; He is my God, and I will glorify Him"*).
[^1880]: Ode 1, Troparia 4-5: St. Andrew develops the ascetic distinction between the sensory Eve of Genesis (*Еѵ́ва чꙋ́вственнаѧ*) and the inward noetic Eve (*мы́сленаѧ Еѵ́а*)—the impassioned thought in the flesh that entices the soul to illicit spiritual consumption.
[^1881]: Ode 1, Troparion 10: Evoking the creation of man from the dust of the earth (Gen. 2:7), the hymnographer addresses Christ simultaneously as the Potter/Fashioner (*зда́тель*), Creator (*Тво́рецъ*), Deliverer (*И҆зба́витель*), and Judge (*Сꙋдїѧ*).
[^1882]: Ode 1, Troparia 14-15: The Parable of the Good Samaritan (Luke 10:30-37) is allegorically interiorized: the traveler stripped and wounded by robbers is the soul overcome by sinful thoughts; the Priest and Levite signify the Old Testament Law unable to effect healing; and Christ, born of the Virgin Mary, is the compassionate Samaritan Who binds up the soul's wounds.
[^1883]: Ode 1, Troparion 20 Catchword: At the foot of fol. 664v (p. 677), the printed catchword is *Бога́-*, which corresponds to the opening of the traditional troparion of the Prodigal Son in Ode 1 (*"Богатство мое, Спасе, изнурив в блуде..."*).
[^1884]: Fol. 665r Compositor Omission: A significant codicological discrepancy occurs between fol. 664v (p. 677) and fol. 665r (p. 678). While the catchword at the foot of fol. 664v signals the troparion *Бога́-тство*, fol. 665r opens abruptly mid-phrase with *...бє́зчестныѧ, и҆ цр҃твїѧ нн҃ѣ Хв҃а ѡ҆́бщники...*, which constitutes the second half of the troparion to St. Andrew of Crete (*"Молитвами твоими нас, Андрее, избави страстей безчестных, и Царствия ныне Христова общники..."*). The 1640 Kyiv printer evidently omitted the intervening troparion of the Prodigal Son and the troparion to St. Mary of Egypt (*"Ты ми даждь светозарную благодать..."*) during quire imposition.
[^1885]: Ode 2 Heirmos 1: The First Heirmos of Ode 2 is based on the Second Biblical Canticle of Moses in Deuteronomy 32:1 (*"Give ear, O heaven, and I will speak; and let the earth hear the words of my mouth"*).
[^1886]: Ode 2, Troparion 10: Direct scriptural citation of Psalm 128:3 LXX: *"The plowers plowed upon my back; they made long their furrows"* (Slavonic: *На хребтѣ̀ мое́мъ дѣ́лаша грѣ́шницы, продолжи́ша беззако́нїе своѐ*), applied here to demonic and passionate impulses furrowing the soul.
[^1887]: Ode 2, Troparia 12-14: The coats of skin (*ко́жныѧ ри́зы*) from Genesis 3:21 are interpreted patristically as the mortal, corruptible, and passible condition of fallen human flesh, contrasting with the radiant "God-woven garment" (*Бг҃отка́ннаѧ ѡ҆де́жда*) of original innocence.
[^1888]: Ode 2, Troparion 24: Allusion to the Publican's prayer in Luke 18:13 (*"God be merciful to me a sinner"*), held up by St. Andrew as the quintessential archetype of penitent prayer.
[^1889]: Ode 2 Heirmos 2: Unlike other odes which possess a single heirmos, Ode 2 features two distinct heirmoi by St. Andrew of Crete. The second heirmos (*Ви́дите, ви́дите...*) draws from Deuteronomy 32:39 (*"See now that I, even I, am He, and there is no god beside Me"*).
[^1890]: Ode 2, Troparion 3 (to Second Heirmos): St. Andrew surveys the righteous figures of the antediluvian era—Seth (Gen. 4:26), Enos (Gen. 4:26), Enoch who walked with God and was translated (Gen. 5:24; Heb. 11:5), and Noah the preacher of righteousness (Gen. 6:9)—contrasting their piety with the soul's negligence.

[^1891]: Genesis 7:11 (LXX): "all the fountains of the great deep were broken up, and the cataracts of heaven were opened."
[^1892]: Genesis 4:23: "And Lamech said unto his wives, Adah and Zillah, Hear my voice... for I have slain a man to my wounding, and a young man to my hurt."
[^1893]: Genesis 11:4: The Tower of Babel ("Let us build us a city and a tower, whose top may reach unto heaven").
[^1894]: Genesis 19:24: "Then the Lord rained upon Sodom and upon Gomorrah brimstone and fire from the Lord out of heaven."
[^1895]: Matthew 14:31: St. Peter sinking beneath the waves of the Sea of Galilee. St. Mary of Egypt turned in tears toward the icon of the Mother of God, seeking repentance before entering the church in Jerusalem.
[^1896]: St. Andrew of Crete, author of the Great Canon, commemorated throughout the odes in dedicated troparia of intercession.
[^1897]: Ode 3 Heirmos of the Great Canon (Tone 6).
[^1898]: Genesis 19:20-22: Lot fled from the burning of Sodom to the mountain town of Zoar (Segor).
[^1899]: 1 Samuel (1 Kings LXX) 2:2: "There is none holy as the Lord, and there is none righteous as our God."
[^1900]: Genesis 6-7: The impious generation submerged in the great Deluge.
[^1901]: Genesis 9:22-23: Ham mocked the nakedness of his father Noah, whereas Shem and Japhs walked backward with a garment to cover him.
[^1902]: Genesis 12:1-4: Abraham departed from Haran at the divine calling unto the land of promise.
[^1903]: Genesis 18:1-10; 21:1-2: Abraham welcomed the Three Angels beneath the Oak of Mamre, and in his old age Isaac was born according to the promise.
[^1904]: Genesis 22:1-14: The binding and mystical whole-burnt offering of Isaac upon Mount Moriah.
[^1905]: Genesis 21:9-14: Ishmael, son of Hagar the Egyptian bondwoman, was cast out into the wilderness of Beersheba.
[^1906]: Genesis 28:12: Jacob's ladder extending from earth unto heaven, with the Angels of God ascending and descending on it.
[^1907]: Genesis 14:18; Hebrews 7:1-3: Melchizedek, king of Salem and priest of the Most High God, a biblical type of the eternal priesthood of Christ.
[^1908]: Genesis 19:26: Lot's wife looked back from behind him, and became a pillar of salt.
[^1909]: Church Slavonic: `предсѣда́телю` (*proedros* / presiding hierarch), referring to St. Andrew's position as Archbishop of Gortyna in Crete.
[^1910]: Sessional Hymn composed by Kyr Joseph the Hymnographer (*Кѵ́р І҆ѡ́сифа*).
[^1911]: Tone 8, *Podoben: "Thou didst arise from the tomb"* (*Воскреслъ еси ѿ гроба*).
[^1912]: Tone 8, *Podoben: "That which was commanded mystically"* (*Повеленное таинственно*).
[^1913]: Liturgical rubric for the Great Canon vigil of Thursday of the Fifth Week: The Life of St. Mary of Egypt by St. Sophronius of Jerusalem is read in two parts (Part 1 after Kathisma 8 / Ode 3, and Part 2 after Ode 6). The triodion of the Apostles is chanted without metanias.
[^1914]: Habakkuk 3:2 (LXX): "O Lord, I have heard Thy report, and was afraid; O Lord, I considered Thy works, and was amazed."
[^1915]: The choir of the twelve Apostles is figured as an *organon* (a twelve-stringed musical instrument) sounding forth the melodious gospel of salvation.
[^1916]: The second triodion canon of the Apostles, traditionally composed by St. Theodore the Studite.
[^1917]: In the 1640 Kyiv print edition, the printed foliation leaps from fol. 672 (`х҃ов`, p686) directly to fol. 675 (`х҃ое`, p687), omitting folios 673 and 674 in the numbering sequence. The text is entirely intact and continuous, as confirmed by the catchword `Оу҆слы́шахъ` on p686 linking to `Оу҆слы́ша Прⷪ҇ро́къ` on p687.
[^1918]: Habakkuk 3:2 (LXX): The Great Canon Heirmos of Ode 4 (Tone 6).
[^1919]: Genesis 31:40: Jacob's labor under Laban: "Thus I was: in the day the drought consumed me, and the frost by night; and my sleep departed from mine eyes."
[^1920]: Genesis 29:16-30: Patristic allegory of Leah as *praxis* (the active life of ascetic virtue, fruitful in spiritual children) and Rachel as *theoria* (the contemplative life of divine knowledge, acquired through arduous labors).
[^1921]: Genesis 25:29-34; 27:34-40: Esau selling his birthright to Jacob for a dish of lentils, and losing his father Isaac's blessing.
[^1922]: Genesis 25:30: Edom (*Adōm*, "red"), etymologized here as passionate burning and attachment to carnal sin.
[^1923]: Job 2:8: Job sitting among the ashes on the dunghill, scraping himself with a potsherd.
[^1924]: Job 1:13-22; 2:7-10: Job's sudden deprivation of his children, servants, wealth, and health, yet blessing the Lord without sinning with his lips.
[^1925]: John 19:34: The piercing of the side of Christ on the Cross, whence flowed blood and water.
[^1926]: Psalm 73:12 (LXX): "God is our King before the ages; He has worked salvation in the midst of the earth."
[^1927]: Matthew 22:11-13 (the wedding guest without a wedding garment bound hand and foot and cast out) and Matthew 25:1-12 (the foolish virgins whose lamps were extinguished for lack of oil).
[^1928]: Patristic sacramental typology of the blood and water from Christ's pierced side as the source of the Church's two supreme mysteries (Holy Baptism and the Holy Eucharist), prefigured by the Old and New Covenants.
[^1929]: Luke 18:9-14: The Parable of the Pharisee and the Publican, contrasting self-righteous boastfulness with contrite prayer.

[^1930]: St. Mary of Egypt lived forty-seven years in the desert beyond the Jordan as an ascetic, surpassing human nature through divine grace.
[^1931]: Mary's turning from licentiousness to supreme ascetic perfection, astonishing the bodiless angels.
[^1932]: St. Andrew of Crete, author of the Great Canon, commemorated as archbishop and protector of Crete.
[^1933]: The trisagion hymn in the highest heaven; theological confession of the consubstantial and undivided Trinity.
[^1934]: The paradox of the Virgin birth renewing the laws of nature, quoting the classical liturgical axiom: "Where God wills, the order of nature is overcome" (*Ὅπου γὰρ βούλεται Θεός, νικᾶται φύσεως τάξις*).
[^1935]: Isaiah 26:9 (LXX): "From night my spirit rises early unto Thee, O God, for Thy commandments are light upon the earth." Heirmos of Ode 5 (Tone 6).
[^1936]: 1 Thessalonians 5:5: "Ye are all the children of light, and the children of the day: we are not of the night, nor of darkness."
[^1937]: Genesis 35:22; 49:3-4: Reuben defiled the bed of his father Jacob with Bilhah, forfeiting his birthright.
[^1938]: Genesis 37:26-28: Judah and his brothers selling Joseph to the Ishmaelites for twenty pieces of silver.
[^1939]: Genesis 37:28; Matthew 26:15: Joseph sold into slavery by his brethren, prefiguring Christ betrayed by Judas and delivered up for the salvation of the world.
[^1940]: Genesis 39:7-12: Joseph fleeing the enticements of Potiphar's wife.
[^1941]: Genesis 37:24: Joseph cast into an empty cistern/pit, interpreted patristically as a type of Christ's descent into Hades and three-day burial.
[^1942]: Exodus 2:2-3: Moses placed in an ark of bulrushes smeared with bitumen and pitch, laid among the reeds by the bank of the Nile.
[^1943]: Exodus 1:15-22: Shiphrah and Puah defying Pharaoh's decree to kill the Hebrew male infants; Moses adopted by Pharaoh's daughter and suckled with the wisdom of God.
[^1944]: Exodus 2:11-12: Moses smiting the Egyptian taskmaster and burying him in the sand, interpreted allegorically as striking down sinful passions.
[^1945]: Exodus 3:1-6: Moses beholding the angel of the Lord in a flame of fire out of the midst of the bush on Mount Horeb.
[^1946]: Exodus 14:16, 21-22: Moses stretching forth his rod over the sea to divide the waters, a prefiguration of the Holy Cross.
[^1947]: Leviticus 10:1-2 (unblemished priestly fire, contrasted with strange fire); 1 Samuel (1 Kings LXX) 2:12-17, 22-25 (Hophni and Phinehas, sons of Eli, defiling the offerings of the Lord).
[^1948]: 2 Timothy 3:8; Exodus 7:11: The Egyptian magicians who withstood Moses and hardened Pharaoh's heart.
[^1949]: Psalm 50:9 (LXX): "Purge me with hyssop, and I shall be clean; wash me, and I shall be whiter than snow."
[^1950]: Luke 12:47-48: The greater accountability of him who knew his master's will and prepared not himself.
[^1951]: Job 14:4; 15:14-15: "Who can bring a clean thing out of an unclean? Not one."
[^1952]: Matthew 8:1-4 (healing of the leper); Matthew 9:1-8 (healing of the paralytic); Matthew 9:20-22 (the woman with an issue of blood touching the hem of Christ's garment).
[^1953]: Matthew 9:20-22; Luke 8:43-48: The woman with an issue of blood touching the hem of Christ's garment in faith.
[^1954]: Luke 13:11-13: Christ laying His hands upon the woman bowed down with a spirit of infirmity for eighteen years, immediately making her straight.
[^1955]: John 4:5-15: Christ at Jacob's well offering the living water whereof he that drinks shall never thirst.
[^1956]: John 9:7: Christ sending the man born blind to wash in the pool of Siloam (which is translated "Sent"), receiving spiritual and physical sight.
[^1957]: St. Mary of Egypt prevented by an invisible divine barrier from entering the Church of the Holy Sepulchre in Jerusalem, until she wept in repentance before the icon of the Mother of God and was permitted to venerate the Precious Cross.
[^1958]: St. Mary crossing the Jordan River into the desolate desert, finding peace from worldly attachments.
[^1959]: St. Andrew invoked as pastoral intercessor and archbishop of Crete.
[^1960]: Trisagion hymn: theological confession of the consubstantial and undivided Trinity.
[^1961]: The Mystery of the Incarnation: God the Creator uniting human nature to Himself in the womb of the Virgin Mary.
[^1962]: Jonah 2:2, 6: "I cried by reason of mine affliction unto the Lord, and He heard me; out of the belly of hell cried I... yet hast Thou brought up my life from corruption, O Lord my God." Heirmos of Ode 6 (Tone 6).
[^1963]: Penitential compunction: offering the tears of the eyes and the inward groaning of the heart.
[^1964]: Numbers 16:31-33: The earth opening its mouth and swallowing Dathan and Abiram alive into Sheol on account of their rebellion against Moses and Aaron.
[^1965]: Hosea 4:16 ("For Israel slids back as a backsliding heifer"); Proverbs 6:5 ("Deliver thyself as a gazelle from the hand of the hunter, and as a bird from the snare of the fowler").
[^1966]: Exodus 4:6-7: The Lord commanded Moses to put his hand into his bosom, and it was leprous as snow; and when he put it into his bosom again, it was restored like his other flesh. The 1640 Kyiv print reads `па́съ` for `па́ки` (*paki*, "again" / Greek πάλιν) or `на́съ` (*nasъ*, "us" / Greek ἡμᾶς).
[^1967]: Exodus 14:26-28: The returning waters of the Red Sea overthrowing Pharaoh, his chariots, and his captains (*tristatai*).
[^1968]: Numbers 11:4-6: The Israelites lusting after worldly meat rather than the divine Manna from heaven.
[^1969]: Exodus 16:3; Numbers 11:4-6: The Israelites murmuring in the wilderness and longing for the fleshpots, swine's flesh, and food of Egypt.
[^1970]: Genesis 26:15-22; Exodus 17:6; Numbers 20:11; 1 Corinthians 10:4: The cisterns of Canaanite passions contrasted with the spiritual Rock from which flowed living streams of theology.
[^1971]: Exodus 17:6; John 19:34: Moses striking the rock in Horeb as a prophetic prefiguration of the pierced side of Christ on Golgotha, whence issued blood and water.
[^1972]: Numbers 13:17-25; Joshua 1:1-9: Joshua the son of Nun surveying the land of promise and urging Israel to dwell therein through observance of God's law.
[^1973]: Exodus 17:8-13 (Joshua defeating Amalek while Moses held up his hands); Joshua 9:3-15 (the deceitful Gibeonites who tricked Israel with worn-out garments and dry bread).
[^1974]: Joshua 3:14-17: The priests bearing the Ark of the Covenant into the Jordan, whose waters stood in an heap, permitting Israel to enter the Land of Promise.
[^1975]: Matthew 14:30-31: Peter walking on the waves began to sink and cried out: "Lord, save me!", and Jesus immediately stretched forth His hand and caught him.
[^1976]: Christ the calm haven and deliverer from the depths of despair.
[^1977]: Luke 15:8-10: The parable of the lost drachma. The woman lighting a lamp and sweeping the house until she finds it is interpreted patristically as the divine Word illuminating humanity through His holy Incarnation to restore the lost royal image.
[^1978]: Quenching the burning fires of passions through persistent streams of ascetic tears.
[^1979]: Attainment of holy dispassion (*apatheia*) through forty-seven years of solitude in the wilderness.
[^1980]: St. Andrew of Crete commemorated as archbishop, proedros, and intercessor for the whole universe.
[^1981]: Confession of the undivided Trinity and distinction of the Three Divine Hypostases.
[^1982]: Incarnation of the Word through the Virgin Mary, fashioning human nature anew.
[^1983]: Kontakion of the Great Canon (Tone 6), attributed to St. Romanos the Melodist, chanted after Ode 6 with prostrations.
[^1984]: Ikos of the Great Canon: Christ's open dispensary of healing for Adam's race causing the devil to lament before his demonic hosts.
[^1985]: The Order of the Great Canon of St. Andrew of Crete, chanted in its entirety at Matins on Thursday of the Fifth Week of the Great Fast.
[^1986]: St. Andrew of Crete (*c.* 660–740): Born in Damascus, educated in grammar and the liberal arts, and tonsured at the Monastery of the Holy Sepulchre in Jerusalem.
[^1987]: St. Andrew's patristic homilies and panegyrics (*Panēgyrika*), festal orations delivered on the great feasts of the Church.
[^1988]: The universal historical scope of the Great Canon, encompassing biblical typologies from Adam to the Ascension of Christ.
[^1989]: The pastoral purpose of the Great Canon: awakening the hard heart unto genuine contrition and repentance.
[^1990]: St. Sophronius, Patriarch of Jerusalem (560–638), author of the hagiographical Life of St. Mary of Egypt, appointed to be read at Matins of Thursday of the Fifth Week.
[^1991]: Consolation offered unto penitent sinners, encouraging perseverance as the Forty-Day Fast nears its completion.
[^1992]: The moral pedagogical power of contrasting righteous examples with the warnings of wicked men in the Great Canon.
[^1993]: The Great Canon contains 250 troparia across its nine odes, in contrast to ordinary canons which typically contain thirty or fewer.
[^1994]: The Sixth Ecumenical Council (Constantinople III, 680–681), which condemned Monothelitism. St. Andrew represented Patriarch Theodore of Jerusalem at the council.
[^1995]: St. Andrew appointed deacon and director of the orphanage (*orphanotrophos*) at the Great Church of Hagia Sophia in Constantinople, prior to his consecration as Archbishop of Gortyna in Crete.
[^1996]: St. Andrew's death at Eressos (Hierissos) on the island of Lesbos (Mytilene) while returning from the imperial capital unto his see in Crete (*c.* 740).
[^1997]: Liturgical rubric for the Beatitudes (*Makarismoi*) at Matins of Thursday of the Fifth Week: chanted in Tone 6 with sixteen penitential troparia and accompanying metanias or prostrations.
[^1998]: Luke 23:42-43: The Penitent Thief crying upon the cross: "Lord, remember me when Thou comest into Thy Kingdom."
[^1999]: Judges 13:2-24: Manoah and his barren wife visited by the Angel of the Lord, promising the birth of Samson.
[^2000]: Judges 16:17-19: Samson revealing the secret of his strength to Delilah, who had his seven locks shorn while he slept upon her knees.
[^2001]: Judges 15:15-17; 16: Samson slaying a thousand Philistines with the fresh jawbone of an ass, yet later subdued through carnal lust.
[^2002]: Judges 4:4-16; 11:1-33: The judges and deliverers of Israel celebrated for their faith and fortitude (cf. Hebrews 11:32).
[^2003]: Judges 4:17-22; 5:24-27: Jael driving a tent-pin (*drevo ostro*) into the temple of Sisera, captain of Jabin's host, interpreted as a type of the wood of the Cross vanquishing Satan.
[^2004]: Judges 11:30-40: Jephthah sacrificing his only daughter in fulfillment of his vow, interpreted allegorically as the sacrifice of bodily passions.
[^2005]: Judges 6:36-40 (the dew falling upon the fleece alone); Judges 7:4-7 (the three hundred men chosen by lapping water like dogs).
[^2006]: 1 Samuel (1 Kings LXX) 2:12-25, 29; 3:13: Eli reproached for failing to restrain his corrupt sons Hophni and Phinehas.
[^2007]: Judges 19:29-30; 20: The Levite dividing the body of his violated concubine into twelve pieces and sending them to all the coasts of Israel to demand justice against the men of Gibeah in Benjamin.
[^2008]: 1 Samuel (1 Kings LXX) 1:12-17: Hannah speaking in her heart, only her lips moving while her voice was not heard, deemed drunken by Eli until she explained her sorrow.
[^2009]: 1 Samuel (1 Kings LXX) 1:19-28; 7:15-17: Samuel reared in the house of the Lord in Shiloh, returning to Ramah (*Armathem*) and judging Israel with righteousness.
[^2010]: 1 Samuel (1 Kings LXX) 16:1-13; Psalm 88:21 (LXX): Samuel anointing David with the horn of holy oil in the midst of his brethren.
[^2011]: Penitential supplication: entreating the Creator to spare His handiwork and pardon transgression.
[^2012]: Triadicon of the Beatitudes (Tone 6). The troparion continues on physical leaf p701: "...I hymn the Holy Spirit who shone forth together with the Father and the Son."

[^2013]: Ode 6, completion of the Triadikon begun on p700 (fol. 688). In traditional Byzantine and Slavonic hymnody, the trinitarian theology emphasizes the single procession of the Holy Spirit from the Father, co-shining with the Father and the Son.
[^2014]: Ode 6, Theotokion of the Beatitudes. The text affirms the Chalcedonian dogma: Christ is one hypostasis/person in two distinct, unconfused natures (*сꙋгꙋ́бѡ и҆сповѣ́дꙋетсѧ є҆стествы̀*).
[^2015]: Ode 7, Heirmos. Tone 6. Based on the prayer of the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian furnace (Dan. 3:28–30 / Song of the Three Children 1:5–8).
[^2016]: Ode 7, Troparion 1. St. Andrew applies the confession of the Three Holy Youths and Psalm 50:5 (*"for behold, I was conceived in iniquities"*) to the penitent soul.
[^2017]: Ode 7, Troparion 2. The soul appeals directly to Christ as righteous Judge, citing the internal confession of secrets before the judgment seat.
[^2018]: Ode 7, Troparion 3. Saul sent to seek his father's asses (1 Sam. [1 Kgs LXX] 9:3–10) found a kingdom, but the soul chose bestial lusts over the heavenly kingdom.
[^2019]: Ode 7, Troparion 4. King David's double fall into adultery with Bathsheba and the murder of Uriah (2 Sam. [2 Kgs LXX] 11:2–17) contrasted with the soul's willful and unrepented passions.
[^2020]: Ode 7, Troparion 5. David's profound repentance (Psalm 50) contrasted with the impenitent condition of the soul.
[^2021]: Ode 7, Troparion 6. David's composition of Psalm 50 (*"Have mercy on me, O God"*) painted as on an icon to expose and condemn his sin before God.
[^2022]: Ode 7, Troparion 7. The incident of Uzzah (Oza) touching the Ark of the Covenant when the oxen stumbled (2 Sam. [2 Kgs LXX] 6:6–7; 1 Chron. 13:9–10).
[^2023]: Ode 7, Troparion 8. Absalom's rebellion against his father David and his incestuous defilement of David's concubines (2 Sam. [2 Kgs LXX] 16:21–22).
[^2024]: Ode 7, Troparion 9. Ahithophel's traitorous counsel to Absalom (2 Sam. [2 Kgs LXX] 15:31; 17:1–14, 23), which the Lord turned into foolishness and scattered.
[^2025]: Ode 7, Troparion 10. King Solomon's apostasy and idolatry in his old age under the influence of foreign wives (1 Kgs [3 Kgs LXX] 11:1–8).
[^2026]: Ode 7, Troparion 11. Solomon, once the exemplar of divine wisdom (*рачи́тель премрⷭ҇ти*), enslaved to lust and estranged from God.
[^2027]: Ode 7, Troparion 12. Rehoboam rejecting the counsel of the elders (1 Kgs [3 Kgs LXX] 12:6–15) and Jeroboam the servant who led Israel into apostasy (1 Kgs [3 Kgs LXX] 12:26–33).
[^2028]: Ode 7, Troparion 13. King Ahab's idolatry and covetousness (1 Kgs [3 Kgs LXX] 16:30–33; 21:25–26).
[^2029]: Ode 7, Troparion 14. The Prophet Elijah calling down fire twice upon the captains of fifty sent by Ahaziah (2 Kgs [4 Kgs LXX] 1:9–12) and slaying the false prophets of Baal (1 Kgs [3 Kgs LXX] 18:40).
[^2030]: Ode 7, Troparion 15. The three-and-a-half-year drought during Ahab's reign (1 Kgs [3 Kgs LXX] 17:1) and the widow of Zarephath (*Сара́фѳїа*) who fed Elijah (1 Kgs [3 Kgs LXX] 17:8–16; Luke 4:25–26).
[^2031]: Ode 7, Troparion 16. King Manasseh's idolatrous abominations and subsequent fervent repentance in Babylon (2 Chron. [2 Paralipomenon LXX] 33:1–13; Prayer of Manasseh).
[^2032]: Ode 7, Troparion 17. The penitent sinner crying out with tears exceeding the sinful woman who anointed Christ's feet (Luke 7:37–38).
[^2033]: Ode 7, Troparion 18. An allusion to the buried talent and the darkened image of God, citing David's prayer in Psalm 50:12: *"Restore unto me the joy of Thy salvation."*
[^2034]: Ode 7, Troparion 19. Allusion to David's petition in Psalm 50:1: *"Have mercy on me, O God, according to Thy great mercy."*
[^2035]: Ode 7, Troparion 20. King Hezekiah weeping bitterly on his deathbed and receiving fifteen additional years of life through the Prophet Isaiah (2 Kgs [4 Kgs LXX] 20:1–6; Isaiah 38:1–5).
[^2036]: Ode 7, To St. Mary, Troparion 1. St. Mary of Egypt praying before the icon of the Theotokos in Jerusalem and receiving liberation from carnal desire (Life of St. Mary, ch. 12).
[^2037]: Ode 7, To St. Mary, Troparion 2. St. Mary following Christ into the Jordanian wilderness after her sincere repentance.
[^2038]: Ode 7, To St. Andrew, Troparion. Intercession addressed to St. Andrew of Crete, author of the Great Canon, asking to be established on the rock of faith.
[^2039]: Ode 7, To the Trinity, Troparion. Triadikon affirming the simple, undivided essence and threefold hypostases of the Holy Trinity.
[^2040]: Ode 7, Theotokion. The incarnation of Christ from the undivided Trinity opening the gates of heaven unto mankind on earth.
[^2041]: Ode 8, Heirmos of the First Triodion (to the Holy Apostles). Tone 8. Sung without prostrations (*без̾ Мета́нїй*). Based on the Song of the Three Holy Children (Dan. 3:57–88).
[^2042]: Ode 8, First Triodion, Troparion 1. The Apostles invoked as fiery coals of immaterial fire (*ᲂу҆́глїе невеще́ственнагѡ ѻ҆гнѧ̀*) to burn away passions.
[^2043]: Ode 8, First Triodion, Troparion 2. The Apostles likened to the trumpets of Joshua that brought down the walls of Jericho (Joshua 6:20).
[^2044]: Ode 8, First Triodion, Troparion 3. The Apostles who shattered pagan idols and temples invoked to shatter the idols of soul-destroying passions.
[^2045]: Ode 8, First Triodion, Theotokion. The paradox of the Virgin Mother containing in her womb the uncontainable Creator and nursing the Sustainer of creation.

[^2052]: Ode 8, Troparion 1. The soul cries out for conversion and forgiveness, echoing David's confession: *"Against Thee only have I sinned"* (Psalm 50:4).

[^2056]: Ode 8, Troparion 5. The hospitable Shunammite woman who prepared an upper chamber for Elisha (2 Kgs [4 Kgs LXX] 4:8–10), contrasted with the inhospitable soul.

[^2058]: Ode 8, Troparion 7. King Uzziah stricken with leprosy in the temple for presumptuously burning incense on the altar (2 Chron. [2 Paralipomenon LXX] 26:16–21).

[^2061]: Ode 8, Troparion 10. The Prophet Jonah fleeing to Tarshish, knowing God's abundant compassion towards penitents (Jonah 1:3; 4:2).
[^2062]: Ode 8, Troparion 11. Daniel shutting the mouths of the lions in the pit (Dan. 6:16–22) and the Three Youths quenching the Babylonian furnace (Dan. 3:23–27; Heb. 11:33–34).
[^2063]: Ode 8, Troparion 12. St. Andrew concludes the Old Testament examples of the Great Canon, exhorting the soul to follow the righteous and shun the wicked.
[^2064]: Ode 8, Troparion 13. Supplication to Christ the Righteous Judge for deliverance from eternal fire and unsparing condemnation.
[^2065]: Ode 8, Troparion 14. The penitent combines five New Testament exemplars: the wise thief (Luke 23:42), Peter weeping bitterly (Matt. 26:75), the Publican (Luke 18:13), the sinful woman (Luke 7:38), and the Canaanite woman (Matt. 15:22).
[^2066]: Ode 8, Troparion 15. The Good Samaritan's healing ministrations (oil and wine, Luke 10:34) applied mystically to the festering wounds of the soul through repentance.
[^2067]: Ode 8, Troparion 16. Mingling the petitions of the Canaanite woman (Matt. 15:22), the woman with the issue of blood (Matt. 9:20), and Martha and Mary weeping over Lazarus (John 11:32–35).

[^2069]: Ode 8, Troparion 18. Self-accusation surpassing all sinners, joined to confession of faith in Christ's singular mercy (1 Tim. 1:15; Psalm 50:4). Note the pronoun dittography *ти тебѣ̀* in the 1640 printed text.
[^2070]: Ode 8, Troparion 19. Christ the Good Shepherd seeking the lost sheep and snatching it from the mouth of the noetic wolf (Luke 15:4–6; John 10:11–12).
[^2071]: Ode 8, Troparion 20. The terror of the inexorable judgment seat (*непостоѧ́ннагѡ сꙋди́ща*, translating Greek ἀδέκαστον κριτήριον / ἄстекτον βῆма).
[^2072]: Ode 8, To St. Mary, Troparion 1. St. Mary illuminated by the Theotokos, Mother of the Unwaning Light (*Свѣ́та незаходи́магѡ Мт҃и*).

[^2077]: Ode 9, Heirmos of the First Triodion (to the Holy Apostles). Tone 8. Sung without prostrations (*без̾ Мета́нїй*). The Magnificat / Luke 1:48: *"All generations shall call me blessed."*
[^2078]: Ode 9, First Triodion, Troparion 1. The Apostles addressed as springs of living water to quench the burning thirst of sin (John 4:14; 7:37–38).

[^2081]: Ode 9, First Triodion, Theotokion. The Virgin Mother who bore divine Joy asked to grant purifying tears of repentance.
[^2082]: Ode 9, Heirmos of the Second Triodion. Tone 8. Magnification of the Mother of God as mediatress of heaven and earth and dwelling place of the bodily fullness of the Godhead (Col. 2:9).
[^2083]: Ode 9, Second Triodion, Troparion 1. The apostolic assembly magnified as radiant luminaries enlightening the world (Matt. 5:14).
[^2084]: Ode 9, Second Triodion, Troparion 2. The Apostles as fishers of men (Matt. 4:19) drawing rational fish into the net of the Gospel.
[^2085]: Ode 9, Second Triodion, Troparion 3. Supplication for the apostolic prayers to deliver the faithful from temptation.
[^2086]: Ode 9, Second Triodion, To the Trinity. Triadikon confessing the Holy Trinity: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, one consubstantial God without beginning.
[^2087]: Ode 9, Second Triodion, Theotokion (completed on p710). The Mother of God bearing the Lord and delivering humanity from the ancient curse of Eve.
[^2088]: Ode 9, Heirmos of the Great Canon. Tone 6. Luke 1:48. Affirmation of Christ's virginal and seedless conception, which renewed human nature.
[^2089]: Ode 9, Troparion 1. The total spiritual breakdown of the soul: wounded mind, diseased body, ailing spirit, deadened life, standing before the imminent judgment.
[^2090]: Ode 9, Troparion 2. St. Andrew references the Genesis account of creation (*Мїробытїе*) and the Old Testament figures presented in the previous odes.
[^2091]: Ode 9, Troparion 3. The soul's neglect of the Law, the Gospel, and the Prophets, resulting in unattended festering sores.
[^2092]: Ode 9, Troparion 4. Introduction to the New Testament exemplars of the Great Canon, urging the soul to appease Christ with fasting, prayer, purity, and reverence.
[^2093]: Ode 9, Troparion 5. Christ's call to repentance: the Kingdom of Heaven taken by force by repentant publicans, harlots, and thieves (Matt. 11:12; 21:31).
[^2094]: Ode 9, Troparion 6. The Incarnation of Christ: taking on human flesh and assuming all natural human properties, yet without sin (Heb. 4:15). Text continues onto p711.

[^2095]: Heb. 2:14–17; 4:15. Concluding strophe of the troparion from Ode 9 (fol. 698–699): Christ took upon Himself human flesh and fulfilled all aspects of human nature by His own will, sin alone excepted.
[^2096]: Matt. 2:1–18; Luke 2:8–38. Scriptural references to the adoration of the Magi, the angelic calling of the Bethlehem shepherds, the slaughter of the Holy Innocents, the righteous elder Simeon, and the prophetess Anna in the Temple.
[^2097]: Matt. 4:1–2; Luke 4:1–2. Christ's forty-day fast in the wilderness of Judea, establishing the archetype of Lenten asceticism.
[^2098]: Matt. 4:3–10; Luke 4:3–13. The threefold temptation of Christ in the wilderness, warning the soul against demonic suggestions and worldly dominion.
[^2099]: Matt. 14:3–11; Mark 6:17–28. St. John the Baptist, the desert turtle-dove and prophetic lamp of Christ, crying repentance in the wilderness and rebuking the unlawful union of Herod Antipas with Herodias.
[^2100]: Matt. 3:1–6; Mark 1:4–5. St. John the Forerunner baptizing all Judea and Jerusalem in the Jordan upon their confession of sins.
[^2101]: John 2:1–11; Heb. 13:4. Christ blessing the institution of marriage at Cana of Galilee, turning water into wine as His first public sign.
[^2102]: Matt. 9:1–7; Luke 7:11–17; Luke 7:1–10 (Matt. 8:5–13); John 4:5–26. Christ healing the paralytic, raising the son of the widow of Nain, restoring the centurion's servant, and revealing worship in spirit and truth to the Samaritan woman at Jacob's well.
[^2103]: Luke 8:43–48; Matt. 8:1–4; Matt. 9:27–30; Mark 7:31–37; Luke 13:10–17. Christ healing the woman with an issue of blood, cleansing lepers, giving sight to the blind, healing the lame, deaf, and dumb, and straightening the bowed-down woman.

[^2161]: The Studite Typikon (*Устав Студийский*) rigorously prescribed xerophagy (*сухоядение*) without wine or oil throughout the Fifth Week of the Great Fast due to its penitential character marked by the singing of the Great Canon of St. Andrew of Crete.
[^2162]: The Athonite / Holy Mountain Typikon (*Устав Святой Горы* / *Типик Святогорский*), reflecting the Sabbath-Lord's Day dispensation and vigil mitigations, allowed two cooked dishes with oil (*двѣ вареніи съ елеемъ*) and two measures of wine for the vigils of the Forty Martyrs (March 9), the Finding of the Head of the Forerunner (February 24), Mid-Lent Wednesday, the Great Canon (Thursday of the 5th week), and the Akathist Hymn (Saturday of the 5th week). Metropolitan Petro Mohyla charitably records both customs so that each ascetic may choose according to their strength.

[^2164]: Physical leaf coordinate: Page_0786 (fol. 774). The rubric directs the reader to the confluence when the Annunciation coincides with Friday of the Fifth Week (the Eve of the Akathist Saturday).

[^2209]: Physical leaf p731 (fol. 719). Friday of the Fifth Week of Great Lent (Eve of the Saturday of the Akathist), at Vespers (Presanctified Liturgy). First Prosomoion to the Most Holy Theotokos at "Lord, I have cried" (under the model specified on fol. 718v: *Tone 6, Podoben: "Having laid aside all earthly hope" [Всѐ ѿло́жше]*; Greek: *Ὅλην ἀποθέμενοι*): "Совѣ́тъ превѣ́чный, ѿкрыва́ѧ тебѣ̀ Ѻтрокови́це..." (Greek: *Βουλὴν προαιώνιον, ἀποκαλύπτων σοι Κόρη*). The hymn incorporates the classic Marian typological imagery: the unsown earth (cf. Isa. 45:8), the unburnt bush (*rubus incombustus*, Ex. 3:2), the unfathomable depth, the bridge leading to heaven, Jacob's ladder (Gen. 28:12), the golden urn of manna (Ex. 16:33; Heb. 9:4), the undoing of the ancestral curse, and the restoration of Adam. The numeral "в҃" in the text indicates that each of the three prosomoia is sung twice to complete the six stichera of the Theotokos.

[^2210]: Physical leaf p731 (fol. 719). Second Prosomoion to the Most Holy Theotokos: "Ꙗ҆влѧ́еши ми сѧ ꙗ҆́кѡ чл҃вкъ..." (Greek: *Φαίνῃ μοι ὡς ἄνθρωπος*). A dialogical hymn expressing the Holy Virgin's chaste wonderment before the Archangel Gabriel. The phrase "Село̀ простра́ное" translates the Greek *χωρίον εὐρύχωρον* (spacious dwelling / capacious realm), contrasting the uncontainable divine majesty with the humble womb of the Virgin.

[^2211]: Physical leaf p731 (fol. 719). Third Prosomoion to the Most Holy Theotokos: "Бг҃ъ и҆де́же хо́щетъ, побѣжда́етсѧ є҆стества̀ чи́нъ..." (Greek: *Ὅπου Θεὸς δὲ βούλεται, νικᾶται φύσεως τάξις*). Gabriel's response establishing the miraculous transcendency of divine will over natural law, followed by the Virgin's humble submission: "Бꙋ́ди мнѣ̀ нн҃ѣ по гл҃ꙋ твоемꙋ̀" (cf. Luke 1:38: *Γένοιτό μοι κατὰ τὸ ῥῆμά σου*). The theological term "ра́ди растворе́нїѧ" reflects the patristic *ἀνάκρασις* / *κρᾶσις*, denoting the unconfused commingling and deification of human nature through the divine Incarnation.

[^2212]: Physical leaf p731 (fol. 719). Doxastikon at "Lord, I have cried," Tone 2: "Е҆́же ѿ вѣ́ка та́инство, ѿкрыва́етсѧ днесь..." (Greek: *Τὸ ἀπ᾽ αἰῶνος μυστήριον, ἀνακαλύπτεται σήμερον*). Text spans from the bottom of physical leaf p731 to the head of leaf p732. This majestic hymn articulates the doctrine of the *admirabile commercium* / *theosis*: Christ assumes our fallen, lesser human estate that He may impart unto us the divine and better (cf. 2 Cor. 8:9), reversing the ancient deception whereby Adam foolishly grasped at divinity without God.

[^2213]: Physical leaf p732 (fol. 720). Conclusion of the Doxastikon, Tone 2: "божделѣ́въ бы́ти не бы́сть, чл҃вкъ быва́етъ Бг҃ъ..." (Greek: *θεὸς ἐπιθυμήσας οὐ γέγονεν, ἄνθρωπος γίνεται Θεός, ἵνα θεὸν τὸν Ἀδὰμ ἀπεργάσηται*). The Archangel brings the angelic greeting *Χαῖρε* ("Rejoice"), which is the direct antithesis and antidote to Eve's ancestral sorrow (*печа́ли сопроти́вное*).

[^2214]: Physical leaf p732 (fol. 720). Rubric governing the order of Vespers when the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts is not celebrated (e.g., if Friday falls on an aliturgical dispensation or special circumstance). The stichera of the Theotokos are set to 8, followed by the Aposticha with the daily idiomela, martyrikon, and Doxastikon cross-referenced to the Praises of Matins on Saturday ("Ꙗ҆зы́ка є҆го́же невѣ́даше", fol. 724 / p736).

[^2215]: Physical leaf p732 (fol. 720). Prokeimenon at Vespers, Psalm 102:8, 1 (LXX). Tone 4.

[^2216]: Physical leaf p732 (fol. 720). First Old Testament Reading at Vespers: Genesis 22:1-5 (LXX), the opening narrative of the Binding of Isaac (*Akedah*), the quintessential Old Testament type of the voluntary sacrifice of the Only-Begotten Son of God. The physical leaf contains verses 1 through 5, ending with Abraham instructing his servants to abide with the donkey while he and Isaac go forward to worship.

[^2217]: Physical leaf p733 (fol. 721). Continuation of the First Old Testament Reading: Genesis 22:6-17a (LXX). Depicts Isaac carrying the wood of the whole burnt-offering (a direct type of Christ carrying the Cross), Isaac's inquiry concerning the lamb, Abraham's prophetic response ("God will provide for Himself a sheep..."), the binding of Isaac upon the wood, the halting of the sacrifice by the Angel of the Lord, the substitution of the ram caught in the Sabek bush (*ἐν φυτῷ Σαβέκ*), the naming of the mountain *Kyrios eiden* ("The Lord hath seen"), and the renewal of the divine covenant to multiply Abraham's seed as the stars of heaven.

[^2218]: Physical leaf p734 (fol. 722). Conclusion of the First Old Testament Reading: Genesis 22:17b-18 (LXX), completing the divine oath to Abraham: his seed shall be as the sand upon the seashore, shall inherit the cities of their adversaries, and in his seed shall all nations of the earth be blessed.

[^2219]: Physical leaf p734 (fol. 722). Second Prokeimenon at Vespers, Psalm 103:24, 1 (LXX). Tone 4.

[^2220]: Physical leaf p734 (fol. 722). Second Old Testament Reading at Vespers: Proverbs 17:17-28 (LXX). In the LXX text, verse 17 begins: "εἰς δὲ ἀνάγκας χρήσιμοι ἔστωσαν, τούτου γὰρ χάριν ἀδελφοὶ γίνονται" (rendered into Slavonic as: "Бра́тїе: въ бѣда́хъ бл҃гопотрѣ́бни да быва́ютъ..."). The passage imparts sapiential instruction on friendship, foolhardy surety, contentious speech, discipline of sons, and the restraint of one's words.

[^2221]: Physical leaf p735 (fol. 723). Conclusion of the Proverbs Reading: Proverbs 17:27-28 and 18:1-5 (LXX). Between verses 27 and 28, the 1640 Kyiv text includes an explicit bracketed interpretive gloss: "[Лꙋ́чшїй ѡ неищꙋ́щаго ꙋ҆чи́тисѧ]" ("better than he that seeks not to learn"), reflecting an ancient explanatory marginal scholion incorporated into the printed Ruthenian witness. Chapter 18 continues with warnings against contentious separation from brethren and the integrity of judgment.

[^2222]: Physical leaf p735 (fol. 723). Rubric concluding the Presanctified Liturgy and directing that at Compline the "Great Prayers" (the Penitential Office with prostrations) are chanted.

[^2223]: Physical leaf p735 (fol. 723). Typikon Markova Glava 78 (Chapter 78): *If on this Saturday of the Akathist the memory of the Holy Forty Martyrs of Sebaste should occur*. The rubric details the liturgical harmonization between the Akathist Saturday and the feast of the Forty Martyrs, specifying the distribution of stichera at "Lord, I have cried" (10 stichera: 2 of the day, 5 to the Theotokos, 3 to the martyrs), prostrations at the Presanctified, transferring the Matins Triodion to Compline, and the structural arrangement of Matins with four stases of the Akathist interspersed with the canons and sessional hymns.

[^2224]: Physical leaf p736 (fol. 724). Continuation of Typikon Chapter 78: specifies the Patristic reading at Matins (St. Basil the Great's *Panegyric on the Forty Holy Martyrs*, PG 31:507-526), the hymnody at the Praises, the Great Doxology, the troparia, the dismissal, the distribution of troparia and kontakia at the Little Hours, and the propers at the Divine Liturgy (Beatitudes from Ode 3 and Ode 6, troparia, kontakia, and the sequence of scripture readings).

[^2225]: Physical leaf p736 (fol. 724). Typikon Markova Glava 88 (Chapter 88): *If on this Saturday of the Akathist the feast of the Annunciation of the Most Holy Theotokos (March 25) should occur*. Outlines the festive order: Friday evening Presanctified with 10 stichera (2 of the day, 8 of the feast), 5 Old Testament readings, prostrations, followed at the 3rd hour of the night by the festive all-night vigil beginning with Great Compline, litany in the narthex, bread-blessing, and Matins with the Polyeleos and the Akathist Hymn.

[^2226]: Physical leaf p737 (fol. 725). Conclusion of Typikon Chapter 88: Matins continued from leaf p736. The Four-ode Canon of the Triodion is omitted at Matins (having been chanted in advance at Small Compline on Thursday), the 9th Ode omits the Magnificat ("More honorable"), the festive Exaposteilarion is sung thrice, followed by the Praises with the prosomoion model *Tone 4, Podoben: "As one noble among the martyrs" (Нб҃нымъ чиномъ: "To the heavenly ranks")*, Great Doxology, reading of the Catechesis of St. Theodore the Studite, anointing of the brethren with holy oil, First Hour in the narthex, procession outside the monastery, and the propers of the Divine Liturgy.

[^2227]: Physical leaf p737 (fol. 725). Typikon Markova Glava 86 (Chapter 86): *If the Apodosis of the Annunciation should occur on the Saturday of the Akathist (March 26)*. Details the combination of the Apodosis of the Annunciation with the Saturday of the Akathist: Vespers on Friday with 10 stichera (2 of the day, 8 of the Annunciation), 5 readings, Matins with the four stases of the Akathist, and the Four-ode Canon transferred to Thursday Compline.

[^2228]: Physical leaf p737 (fol. 725). Conclusion of Typikon Chapter 86: Divine Liturgy rubrics for the Apodosis of the Annunciation on Akathist Saturday. The Beatitudes are taken from Ode 3 and Ode 6 of the Akathist. Epistle readings: Hebrews section 322 (Heb. 9:11-14, the High Priesthood of Christ) followed by Hebrews section 320 (Heb. 9:1-7, the Theotokos propers of the Tabernacle). Gospels: Mark section 35 (Mark 8:27-31) and Luke section 4 (Luke 1:39-49, 56, the Visitation). Communion Verse: "I will take the cup of salvation" (Ps. 115:4 [LXX]).

[^2229]: Physical leaf p738 (fol. 726). Full-width narrative woodcut illustration preceding the Matins of the Saturday of the Akathist (fol. 726r). The central panel depicts the Mother of God Hodegetria enthroned in glory, holding the Christ Child Emmanuel, titled: "ПОХВАЛА• ПРЕ: С: БОГОРОДИЦИ •" (*The Praise of the Most Holy Theotokos*). She is surrounded by seven prophetic and typological vignettes: the High Priest Aaron holding the rod that blossomed (Num. 17:8); the Prophet Moses beholding the Burning Bush that was not consumed (Ex. 3:2); the Judge Gideon holding the dew-covered fleece (Judg. 6:37-40); the Patriarch Jacob beholding the heavenly ladder (Gen. 28:12); the Prophet Daniel beholding the stone cut from the mountain without hands (Dan. 2:34-35); the Annunciation to the Virgin Mary by the Archangel Gabriel (Luke 1:26-38); and King Solomon holding the model of the Temple of Holy Wisdom (Prov. 9:1). This composition visually encapsulates the theological typologies of the Akathist Hymn.

[^2230]: Physical leaf p738 (fol. 726). Rubric for Matins on the Saturday of the Fifth Week of Great Lent (The Saturday of the Akathist Hymn / Laudation of the Theotokos). The great bell and semantron are sounded at the 4th hour of the night (approx. 10:00 PM or early morning). The priest censes the entire church during the Six Psalms. After the Six Psalms and Great Litany, "God is the Lord" is sung in Tone 8, followed by the festive Troparion chanted three times slowly.

[^2231]: Physical leaf p738 (fol. 726). Troparion of the Akathist, Tone 8: "Повелѣ́нное та́йнѡ прїи́мъ въ ра́зꙋмѣ..." (Greek: *Τὸ προσταχθὲν μυστικῶς λαβὼν ἐν γνώσει*). Attributed to the 7th-century Byzantine liturgical poet (traditionally Patriarch Sergius or St. Romanos the Melodist). The troparion introduces the mystery of the Archangel's embassy to Nazareth, depicting Gabriel's reverent awe at the condescension of God into the Virgin's womb. Text continues across the folio break onto leaf p739.

[^2232]: Physical leaf p739 (fol. 727). Conclusion of the Akathist Troparion and rubrics for the chanting of the Akathist Hymn. Following the troparion and Kathisma 16 of the Psalter (Psalms 109-117 [LXX]), the choir gathers in the center of the church to chant the preliminary Kontakion ("To thee, the Champion Leader") slowly and with elaborate melody (*ко́снѡ и҆ согла́снѡ*). The deacon censes the altar, holy icons, and the entire congregation at the beginning of each of the four stases of the Akathist.

[^2233]: Physical leaf p739 (fol. 727). The Prooimion / Kontakion 1 of the Akathist, Tone 8: "Взбра́нной Воево́дѣ побѣди́телнаѧ..." (Greek: *Τῇ ὑπερμάχῳ στρατηγῷ τὰ νικητήρια*). Composed as a victory thanksgiving hymn commemorating the miraculous deliverance of Constantinople from the Avar and Persian siege in 626 (or subsequent Saracen sieges), acknowledging the Mother of God as the supreme defender and deliverer of the Christian realm.

[^2234]: Physical leaf p739 (fol. 727). Rubric governing the recitation of the 24 stanzas (Oikoi) of the Akathist Hymn. The 24 stanzas are arranged as an alphabetical acrostic according to the Greek alphabet (from Alpha through Omega: Α through Ω). They are divided into four stases of 6 stanzas each (3 Kontakia and 3 Ikoses per stasis). The priest vests in full sacerdotal vestments (*въ всю̀ сщ҃е́ннꙋю ѡ҆де́ждꙋ*) to recite the stanzas from the Holy Altar or before the icon of the Theotokos, while the faithful remain standing throughout the entire office (whence the name *Akathistos*, "not sitting"). A special candlestick with a lit lamp and fragrant censer is stationed before the Royal Doors.

[^2235]: Physical leaf p739 (fol. 727). First Stasis of the Akathist. Ikos 1 (Acrostic letter Alpha, **Α**): "А҆́гг҃лъ Пе́рвоста́тель съ Нб҃съ посла́нъ бы́сть..." (Greek: *Ἄγγελος πρωτοστάτης, οὐρανόθεν ἐπέμφθη*). Gabriel greets the Virgin with twelve salutations (*chairetismoi*), hailing her as the dawn of joy, the end of the curse, the restoration of Adam, the relief of Eve's tears, and the throne of the King. Text begins on leaf p739 and concludes on leaf p740.

[^2236]: Physical leaf p740 (fol. 728). Conclusion of Ikos 1: completes the final four salutations: the star revealing the Sun, the womb of the divine Incarnation, the renewal of creation, and the worship of the Creator, concluding with the invariant refrain of all twelve Ikoses: "Ра́дуйсѧ невѣ́сто неневѣ́стнаѧ" (Greek: *Χαῖρε, Νύμφη ἀνύμφευτε*; "Rejoice, unwedded Bride!").

[^2237]: Physical leaf p740 (fol. 728). Kontakion 2 (Acrostic letter Beta, **Β**): "Ви́дѧщи ст҃а́ѧ себѐ въ чистотѣ̀..." (Greek: *Βλέπουσα ἡ Ἁγία, ἑαυτὴν ἐν ἁγνείᾳ*). Depicts the Virgin Mary's prudent and bold interrogation of Gabriel, noting that human reason cannot easily grasp a conception without seed, yet concluding with the invariant refrain of all twelve short Kontakia: "А҆ллилꙋ́їа" (*Alleluia*).

[^2238]: Physical leaf p740 (fol. 728). Ikos 2 (Acrostic letter Gamma, **Γ**): "Ра́зꙋмъ небразꙋмѣ́нный, разꙋмѣ́ти Дв҃аѧ и҆́щꙋщи..." (Greek: *Γνῶσιν ἄγνωστον γνῶναι, ἡ Παρθένος ζητοῦσα*). The Virgin seeks to understand the incomprehensible mystery of birth from a chaste womb, and the Archangel replies with twelve salutations praising her as the initiate of ineffable counsel, the ladder by which God descended, the bridge to Heaven, the wonder of angels, the wound of demons, and the illuminator of the minds of the faithful.

[^2239]: Physical leaf p740 (fol. 728). Kontakion 3 (Acrostic letter Delta, **Δ**): "Си́ла вы́шнѧгѡ, ѡ҆сѣнѝ тогда̀..." (Greek: *Δύναμις τοῦ Ὑψίστου, ἐπεσκίασε τότε*). Commemorates the overshadowing of the Holy Spirit upon the Virgin (cf. Luke 1:35), portraying her fruitful womb as a fertile and sweet field yielding the harvest of salvation for all who sing *Alleluia*.

[^2240]: Physical leaf p740 (fol. 728). Ikos 3 (Acrostic letter Epsilon, **Ε**): "И҆мꙋ́щи бг҃опрїѧ́тнꙋю Дв҃аѧ ꙋ҆тро́бꙋ, востечѐ..." (Greek: *Ἔχουσα θεοδόχον, ἡ Παρθένος τὴν μήτραν*). Opening line of the third Ikos depicting the Virgin's visitation to her kinswoman Elizabeth (Luke 1:39-40). The leaf concludes with the catchword "къ" (carrying over to "къ Е҆лисаве́ти" on fol. 729r / physical leaf p741).

[^2241]: Akathist Hymn, Ikos 3 (Epsilon / Ѻ҆́ноѧ / Elizabeth). In the 1640 Kyiv Triodion, the twenty-four stanzas of the Akathist are distributed across the Matins service in four strophes of six stanzas each, interspersed with Kathismata and historical readings.
[^2242]: Akathist Hymn, Kontakion 4 (Zeta / Бꙋ́рю внꙋ́трь / A storm within).
[^2243]: Cross-reference to the prooimion of the Akathist, Kontakion 1 ("To thee, the Champion Leader"), printed on fol. 726 (physical leaf p738).
[^2244]: Rubric prescribing the reading of the historical Synaxarion of the Akathist in 11 sections. In Petro Mohyla's 1640 edition, this narrative is presented in Early Modern Ruthenian (prosta mova), recounting the historical sieges of Constantinople.
[^2245]: Title and opening blessing of the Ruthenian historical narrative recounting the miraculous deliverance of Constantinople from the combined Persian and Avar/Scythian siege of 626 AD.
[^2246]: Historical context: The Byzantine-Sasanian War of 602–628. Khosrow II Parvez dispatched his general Shahrbaraz (rendered in Ruthenian as Сарва́ръ) through Byzantine Anatolia.
[^2247]: Reference to the tyrannical reign of the usurper Phocas (602–610 AD), overthrown by Emperor Heraclius (610–641 AD).
[^2248]: Heraclius' celebrated campaign across the Black Sea (Euxine Pontus) into Transcaucasia and Persia, leaving Constantinople guarded by Patriarch Sergius and Patrician Bonus.
[^2249]: The Slavic and Avar dugouts (monoxyla, μονόξυλα) utilized during the siege of 626 AD to assault the seaward walls and the Golden Horn.
[^2250]: Scriptural citation in margin: Joshua 8:2–9 (the ambush at Ai).
[^2251]: Scriptural citation in margin: Judges 7:16–20 (Gideon arming his three hundred men with trumpets, pitchers, and torches).
[^2252]: The historical relics carried in procession around the land walls: the Mandylion / Acheiropoietos Icon of Christ, the Virgin's Robe (Maphorion) from Blachernae, and the True Cross.
[^2253]: Scriptural prayer: Psalm 67:2–3 (LXX) / Numbers 10:35.
[^2254]: The Church of the Theotokos of the Spring (Pege, Ζωοδόχος Πηγή) outside the Golden Gate / Silivri Gate.
[^2255]: The historical ultimatum of the Avar Chagan, demanding unconditional surrender and mocking Christian trust in God.
[^2256]: Scriptural citation: Proverbs 3:34 (LXX) / James 4:6 / 1 Peter 5:5: "God resists the proud, but gives grace unto the humble."
[^2257]: Scriptural allusions from the prayer of King Hezekiah against Sennacherib (2 Kings 19:15–19 / Isaiah 37:16–20) and Psalm 78:9–10.
[^2258]: The Golden Horn (Chrysokeras), the estuary of Constantinople, referred to here as the Horn (ѻ҆тно́га Кера́тскаѧ / Greek Κέρας).
[^2259]: The Church of the Theotokos of Blachernae at the northern corner of Constantinople adjacent to the Golden Horn, where the Virgin's Robe was dipped into the sea, immediately raising a storm that wrecked the enemy fleet.
[^2260]: Typological reference to the crossing of the Red Sea and destruction of Pharaoh's host (Exodus 14:26–31).
[^2261]: Scriptural quotation: Deuteronomy 32:30 / Joshua 23:10.
[^2262]: Scriptural quotation from the Song of Moses: Exodus 15:6–7.
[^2263]: Historical etymology and establishment of the Akathist (А҆ка́ѳїстосъ, Greek Ἀκάθιστος, "unseated" / "without sitting"), so named because the clergy and people chanted it standing all night in thanksgiving.
[^2264]: Rubric prescribing the recitation of Kathisma 17 (Psalm 118, The Blameless, Непорочны).
[^2265]: Rubric for the repetition of Kontakion 1 ("To thee, the Champion Leader", fol. 726) and the second strophe of the Akathist (Ikoses 4–6 and Kontakia 5–7).
[^2266]: Akathist Hymn, conclusion of Ikos 4 (Zeta / Слы́шаша па́стырїе / Hearing the shepherds).
[^2267]: Akathist Hymn, Kontakion 5 (Eta / Бг҃оте́чнꙋю звѣздꙋ̀ / The God-running star).
[^2268]: Akathist Hymn, Ikos 5 (Theta / Ви́дѣша Ѻ҆́троцы / The sons of the Chaldeans beheld).
[^2269]: Akathist Hymn, Kontakion 6 (Iota / Проповѣ́дницы бг҃оно́снїи / God-bearing heralds).
[^2270]: Akathist Hymn, Ikos 6 (Kappa / Возсїѧ́вый въ Е҆гѵ́птѣ / Having shone forth in Egypt).
[^2271]: Akathist Hymn, Kontakion 7 (Lambda / Хотѧ́щꙋ Сѵмеѡ́нꙋ / When Simeon was about to depart).
[^2272]: Cross-reference rubric repeating Kontakion 1 (fol. 726 / p738).
[^2273]: Heading and opening rubric for Part 2 of the Akathist historical narrative, treating the Second Wonder (the First Arab Siege under Constantine IV, 674–678 AD) and the Third Wonder (the Second Arab Siege under Leo III, 717–718 AD).
[^2274]: The First Arab Siege of Constantinople (674–678 AD) during the reign of Constantine IV Pogonatus, during which the Umayyad fleet wintered at Cyzicus before suffering shipwreck off the Pamphylian coast at Syllaeum.
[^2275]: Title of the section detailing the Third Wonder: The Second Arab Siege of Constantinople (717–718 AD) by the Umayyad Caliphate.
[^2276]: Historical narrative recounting the rapid Arab expansion through Persia, Egypt, North Africa, and Spain (al-Andalus), culminating in the land and sea assault on Constantinople.
[^2277]: The massive Umayyad expedition of 717–718 AD led by Maslama ibn Abd al-Malik, comprising an estimated 1,800 ships and vast land forces.

[^2278]: The supplicatory prayer of the citizens during the procession combines motifs from Ps. 43:24, 27 LXX (*Exsurge, quare obdormis Domine?*), Ps. 82:2-3 LXX (*non taceas Deus... inimici tui extulerunt caput*), and Ps. 78:10 LXX (*ne forte dicant in gentibus: Ubi est Deus eorum?*), concluding with the Christological confession of Phil. 2:11.
[^2279]: Ruthenian *в єденъ рокъ* ('in a single moment' or 'within a year'); the narrative records the sudden divine retribution upon a Saracen horseman who blasphemed the Hagia Sophia.
[^2280]: Slavonic/Ruthenian *Клику́нъ* ('crier'), denoting the Islamic muezzin (*mu'adhdhin*) calling from a high post to prayer.
[^2281]: The intervention of the Bulgarian Khan Tervel against the Umayyad siege forces in 718 AD, resulting in a catastrophic defeat for Maslama's land army with casualties reported in Byzantine chronicles (Theophanes the Confessor) as exceeding 20,000–30,000 (hyperbolically rendered here as two hundred thousand / *подвакроть сто тисячей*).
[^2282]: The defensive boom chain across the Golden Horn between Constantinople and the Tower of Galata (Kastellion), and the naval destruction of the Umayyad fleet at Sosthenes (modern İstinye) by the Byzantine navy employing Greek fire (*Грекове попалили*).
[^2283]: Historical account of the famine suffered by the Umayyad siege army during the severe winter of 717–718 AD, compelling them to consume carrion and prompting numerous desertions to the Byzantine side.
[^2284]: The destruction of the retreating Umayyad fleet in the Aegean Sea by a violent submarine volcanic eruption and pumice fall near Thera (Santorini) in 718 AD, described in Byzantine sources (Theophanes, Chronographia AM 6210) as boiling sea water and burning volcanic hail (*гра́дъ з нб҃а зпа́лъ зна́гла... смо́лꙋ корабле́й розтопи́ло*), from which only five (here recorded as three) vessels escaped to Syria.
[^2285]: Ps. 73:13 LXX (*Σὺ συνέτριψας τὰς κεφαλὰς τῶν δρακόντων ἐπὶ τοῦ ὕδατος* / *Ты̀ сокрꙋши́лъ є҆сѝ главы̑ ѕмі́євъ въ водѣ̀*).
[^2286]: Ex. 15:1 LXX, the First Biblical Ode (*ᾌσωμεν τῷ Κυρίῳ, ἐνδόξως γὰρ δεδόξασται* / *Пои́мъ Го́сподеви, сла́внѡ бо просла́висѧ*).
[^2287]: The homiletic catalogue of Christian virtues reflects Mohyla's pastoral theology in the Kyiv Collegium tradition, emphasizing moral synergy and interior virtue over mere external ritualism. The reference to love as the head of the Law and Prophets cites Matt. 22:37–40.
[^2288]: The philosophical adage that thanksgiving consists in deeds rather than empty words echoes classical and Patristic parenesis (cf. St. Basil the Great, *Homilia in martyrem Julittam* 1; St. John Chrysostom, *In Genesim sermo* 5).
[^2289]: This impassioned prayer references the internecine strife, confessional strife, and Cossack-Polish warfare tearing apart Ruthenian lands in the early 17th century (reign of Władysław IV, Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth), where Orthodox Christians of the same baptism (*тогожъ Дха, єдиного Хрта, тоеижъ Вѣры*) fought one another as in a night battle (*въ ночной войнѣ зранены бываемъ*).
[^2290]: Closing supplication of the Akathist Synaxarion narrative invoking the maternal intercession of the Theotokos for civic peace, relief from social discord, and ecclesiastical unity.
[^2291]: Concluding nautical metaphor and solemn Trinitarian doxology sealing the historical-theological narrative of the Akathist Saturday.
[^2292]: A critical codicological marginal gloss printed in the 1640 Kyiv Triodion explaining that seven synaxaria across the Triodion were translated from Church Slavonic into Early Modern Ruthenian (*prosta mova* / *ру́сскїй дїале́ктъ*) for pastoral comprehension, defending the vernacular idiom against accusations of liturgical impropriety (*аще и не свойствено славенску... разумъ ничтоже есть противно*).
[^2293]: Rubric prescribing Psalm 50 without the usual poetic stichology of the Kathismata (*Не Стїхосло́вимъ*), reflecting the extended festal length of the Akathist Matins.
[^2294]: Rule for combining canons at the Akathist Matins when celebrated in a church dedicated to a saint: 6 troparia for the patron saint and 8 troparia for the Akathist Canon.
[^2295]: Alternative rule when celebrated in a church dedicated to the Mother of God: 4 troparia for the Menaion saint and 10 for the Akathist Canon (Heirmos repeated twice, troparia counted to 8).
[^2296]: The acrostic of the Akathist Canon composed by St. Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886): in Greek, *Χαρᾶς δοχεῖον, σοὶ πρέπει χαίρειν μόνῃ. Ἰωσήφ* ("Receptacle of joy, it befitts thee alone to rejoice. Joseph").
[^2297]: Tone 4, Heirmos of Ode 1 (*Ἀνοίξω τὸ στόμα μου* / *Ѿве́рзꙋ оу҆ста̀ моѧ̀*), derived from Ps. 44:2 LXX.
[^2298]: Gabriel's address to the Virgin as the animate Book (*Кни́гꙋ ѡ҆дꙋшевле́ннꙋю* / *βίβλον ἔμψυχον*), sealed by the Holy Spirit (cf. Isa. 29:11; Rev. 5:1).
[^2299]: Tone 4, Heirmos of Ode 3 (*Τοὺς σοὺς ὑμνολόγους, Θεοτόκε* / *Твоѧ̀ Пѣ́сносло́вца, Бц҃е*).
[^2300]: Images of the Theotokos as the animate Table (*Тра́пеза ѡ҆дꙋшевле́ннаѧ*) bearing the Bread of Life (cf. John 6:35) and the fountain of living water (John 4:14).
[^2301]: Types of the red heifer of Num. 19:2, the Lamb of God of John 1:29, and the propitiatory mercy-seat (*hilasterion* / *ѡ҆чисти́лище*, Ex. 25:17; Rom. 3:25).
[^2302]: Cross-reference to the text of the Kontakion *Возбра́нной Воево́дѣ* on physical leaf 738 (fol. 726/727).
[^2303]: The second station of the Akathist Hymn comprising stanzas 13 through 18 (Nu through Sigma / Ikoses 7–9 and Kontakia 8–10), sung after Ode 3 of the Canon.
[^2304]: Ikos 7 concludes with the refrains praising the Mother of God as the reconciler of sinners and the unwedded Bride (*Νύμφη ἀνύμφευτε*).
[^2305]: Kontakion 8 (Xi / *Ξένον τόκον ἰδόντες* / *Странное Рождество видѣвше*), urging transcendence of worldly concerns through contemplation of the divine condescension.
[^2306]: Ikos 8 (Omicron / *Ὅλος ἦν ἐν τοῖς κάτω* / *Весь бѣ въ нижнихъ*), dogmatic stanza professing the hypostatic union and divine omnipresence: the divine descent involved no displacement of locus (*съхожденїе бо Бжтвенное не прехожденїе же мѣстное бысть*).
[^2307]: Kontakion 9 (Pi / *Πᾶσα φύσις ἀγγέλων* / *Всякое есттво Агглское удивися*), marveling at the mystery of the unapproachable God becoming approachable in the flesh.
[^2308]: Ikos 9 (Rho / *Ῥήτορας πολυφθόγγους* / *Вѣтїя многовѣщанныя*), contrasting Greek philosophy and Athenian disputation with the apostolic gospel of the simple Fishermen (*Рыба́рскїѧ мрє́жа*).
[^2309]: Kontakion 10 (Sigma / *Σῶσαι θέλων τὸν κόσμον* / *Спасти хотя миръ*), highlighting Christ's incarnation as the Good Shepherd calling mankind like to like (*подо́бнымъ бо подо́бное призва́въ*).
[^2310]: Cross-reference rubric directing the repetition of the prooimion *To thee, the Champion Leader* (fol. 726/727 / p738).
[^2311]: Rubrical instruction for the confluence of the Akathist Saturday with an commemorated Menaion saint of rank.
[^2312]: The 1640 Kyiv text designates Tone 4 (`Гла́съ, д҃`), though the automelon *Гро́бъ тво́й* (*Τὸν τάφον σου Σωτήρ*) is canonically Tone 1; in Byzantine and Synodal Triodia, this sessional hymn (*Великій воинъ*) is set in Tone 1.
[^2313]: Rubric permitting a patristic homiletic reading (from St. John Damascene or St. Germanus of Constantinople) if Matins has proceeded rapidly before dawn.
[^2314]: Tone 4, Heirmos of Ode 4 (*Ὁ καθήμενος ἐν δόξῃ* / *Сѣдѧ́й въ сла́вѣ*), based on Hab. 3:2.
[^2315]: Types from the Psalter and Exodus: the curdled and rich mountain of Ps. 67:16–17 LXX (*Гора Божия, гора тучная, гора усыренная*), the golden lampstand (Ex. 25:31), and the jar containing the hidden Manna (Ex. 16:33; Heb. 9:4).
[^2316]: Patristic metaphors of Jacob's Ladder (Gen. 28:12) and the living Bridge transferring mortal men from death to life.
[^2317]: The metaphorical murex shell (*червлени́ца* / *κογχύλη*) dyeing the imperial purple of Christ's humanity from the pure blood of the Virgin.
[^2318]: The Christological doctrine of theosis (*ѡ҆боже́нїе* / *θέωσις*), accomplished through the Incarnation of the true Lawgiver born of the Virgin.
[^2319]: Tone 4, Heirmos of Ode 5 (*Ἐξέστη τὰ σύμπαντα* / *Оу҆диви́шасѧ всѧ́ческаѧ*).
[^2320]: Christ as the Way of Life (John 14:6), and the Virgin as the Ark delivering creation from the deluge of sin (cf. Gen. 7).
[^2321]: Theotokion imagery: the temple of glory (Ps. 25:8 LXX), the destruction of Hades, and the bridal chamber of the divine Bridegroom.
[^2322]: Typological contrast between the Garden of Eden with its Tree of Life (Gen. 2:9) and the Theotokos as the animate Paradise bearing Christ.
[^2323]: Allusions to Ps. 86:3 LXX (*Glorious things are spoken of thee, O city of God*) and Dan. 2:34–35, 45 (the mountain unhewn by hands).
[^2324]: The patristic image of the precious pearl (*margarites* / *бисеръ*) formed within the shell of the Virgin without human seed (cf. Matt. 13:45–46).
[^2325]: Rubric directing that after Ode 5, the canon of the patron saint ceases, referring back to fol. 713 (p725) for subsequent rubrics and kathismata.
[^2326]: Tone 4, Heirmos of Ode 6 (*Τὴν θείαν ταύτην καὶ πάνтиμον* / *Бжтве́нное сїѐ и҆ всечⷭ҇тно́е*).
[^2327]: Troparion pairing the prophetic witness of the Old Testament (*Прр҇кѡмъ ѡ҆глаше́нїе*) with the apostolic proclamation of the New (*А҆́плѡмъ оу҆добре́нїе*).
[^2328]: The dew upon Gideon's fleece (Judg. 6:36–40) as a type of the virginal conception without corruption, extinguishing the fire of idolatry.
[^2329]: Navigational metaphor common to Byzantine hymnography, imploring the Theotokos as a tranquil harbor amidst the stormy billows of temptations.

[^2330]: Saturday of the Fifth Week of Great Lent (Saturday of the Akathist). The physical leaf opens with the conclusion of the final Troparion of Ode 6 of the Akathist Canon (*Ра́дости вина̀, ѡ҆блг҃ти́ нашъ по́мыслъ...*), continuing directly from the bottom of physical leaf 760 (f. 443v). The Marian invocation *Неопали́маѧ Купина̀* references the Burning Bush of Exodus 3:2.
[^2331]: Church Slavonic: *Та́же Четверопѣ́снецъ, Е҆мꙋ́же А҆крости́хисъ: Сїѧ̀ Пѣ́нь Їѡси́фова . Пѣ́нь, ѕ҃. Гласъ, ѕ҃.* The acrostic (*Сїѧ̀ пѣ́нь Їѡси́фова*, "This song is of Joseph") attributes this Four-Ode Canon to St. Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886), a standard component of the Lenten Saturday Matins office in the Studite and Kyivan recensions.
[^2332]: Heirmos of Ode 6, Tone 6: *Житє́йскагѡ мо́рѧ воздвиза́ема* (Greek: *Τοῦ βίου τὴν θάλασσαν*). In accordance with liturgical practice, the physical text cites only the opening incipit, referring the choir to the standard Heirmologion.
[^2333]: Ode 6, First Troparion of the Four-Ode Canon (Martyrikon). The image of the holy martyrs rolling as chosen stones upon the earth draws upon Zechariah 9:16 LXX (*λίθοι ἅγιοι κυλιόμενοι ἐπὶ τῆς γῆς*).
[^2334]: Ode 6, Second Troparion of the Four-Ode Canon, uniting the martyrdom theme with the ascetic stadium of the Great Forty Days (*тече́нїе по́ста*, *cursus jejunii*).
[^2335]: Ode 6, Third Troparion of the Four-Ode Canon (Hymn for the Departed (Поко́инъ)), interceding for the departed faithful through the merits of the holy martyrs.
[^2336]: Ode 6, Theotokion of the Four-Ode Canon.
[^2337]: Ode 6 of the Second Four-Ode Canon (Tone 5), authored by St. Theodore the Studite (759–826). The Heirmos incipit *Ѿ Ки́та Прр҃ка* corresponds to Greek *Ἐκ κήτους τὸν προφήτην* (referencing Jonah 2:1–11).
[^2338]: Ode 6, First Troparion of the Second Four-Ode Canon (Martyrikon).
[^2339]: Ode 6, Second Troparion of the Second Four-Ode Canon (Martyrikon).
[^2340]: Ode 6, Triadikon of the Second Four-Ode Canon, articulating the Nicene-Constantinopolitan dogma of the divine Tri-hypostatic monad (*є҆ди́ници по сꙋществу̀*).
[^2341]: Physical leaf 762 (f. 444v) begins with the final clause of the Theotokion of Ode 6 of the Second Four-Ode Canon (*...є҆сѝ Дв҃о, Хрⷭ҇та̀ сп҃са дꙋша́мъ на́шимъ :~*), completing the stanza initiated on leaf 761.

[^2348]: Ikos 10 of the Akathist Hymn (*Стѣ́на є҆сѝ Дв҃амъ...* / Greek: *Τεῖχος εἶ τῶν παρθένων...*), opening the Fourth and final Station of the Akathist. The Greek acrostic letter is *Τ* (Tau), represented in the Kyivan Triodion by Slavonic *Т*.
[^2349]: Kontakion 11 of the Akathist Hymn (*Пѣ́нїе всѧ́кое побѣжда́етсѧ...* / Greek: *Ὕμνος ἅπας ἡττᾶται...*). The Greek acrostic letter is *Υ* (Upsilon), marked in the Slavonic text as *Y*. The text spans the page turn from leaf 762 to leaf 763.
[^2350]: Physical leaf 763 (f. 445r). Ikos 11 of the Akathist Hymn (*Свѣтопрїе́мную свѣщꙋ̀...* / Greek: *Φωτοδόχον λαμπάδα...*). The Greek acrostic letter is *Φ* (Phi), marked in the text as *Ф*.
[^2351]: Kontakion 12 of the Akathist Hymn (*Бл҃гть да́ти восхотѣ́въ...* / Greek: *Χάριν δοῦναι θελήσας...*). The Greek acrostic letter is *Χ* (Chi), marked in the text as *Х*.
[^2352]: Ikos 12 of the Akathist Hymn (*Пою́ще твоѐ Рождество̀...* / Greek: *Ψάλλοντές σου τὸν τόκον...*). The Greek acrostic letter is *Ψ* (Psi), marked in the text as *Y*. The text spans the page turn from leaf 763 to leaf 764.
[^2353]: Physical leaf 764 (f. 445v). Kontakion 13 of the Akathist Hymn (*Ѡ Всепѣ́таѧ Мт҃и...* / Greek: *Ὦ πανύμνητε Μῆτερ...*), the concluding stanza of the hymn (acrostic letter *Ω* / *Ѡ*). The marginal rubric instructs: *Сїи Ко: гл҃и г҃. Покло́нъ на ко́ждое:* ("This Kontakion is said thrice, with a metania at each").
[^2354]: Rubric directing the repetition of the first Ikos (*А҆́гг҃лъ Предста́тель*, "An Archangel was sent...") and the Kontakion (*Возбра́нной воево́дѣ*, "To Thee, the Champion Leader") back on leaf 427 (physical page 727, f. 427r).
[^2355]: Rubric prescribing the reading of the Synaxarion: first from the Menaion for the day's commemorated saint, followed by the historical Synaxarion of the Akathist Saturday.
[^2356]: Iambic verses preceding the Synaxarion (*Пѣ́ньми невсы́пными бл҃гѡтве́ннѡ / Гра́дъ въ бра́нехъ бо́дрꙋю пое́тъ Предста́телницꙋ* / Greek: *Ἀκοιμήτοις ὕμνοις εὐχαρίστως / Ἡ πόλις μέλπει τὴν ἄγρυπνον ἐν μάχαις Προστάτιν*).
[^2357]: Opening of the historical Synaxarion for the Saturday of the Akathist, composed in Early Modern Ruthenian (*prosta mova*), recounting the three miraculous deliverances of Constantinople through the intercession of the Most Holy Theotokos. Marginal note in cinnabar: *Гре́ческїй* ("Greek [source]").
[^2358]: Physical leaf 765 (f. 446r). The narrative describes the 626 AD siege of Constantinople during the Persian Wars of Emperor Heraclius (reigned 610–641). Chrysopolis (*Хрѵсо́поль*, "Golden City") corresponds to modern Üsküdar on the Asiatic shore of the Bosphorus.
[^2359]: Historical account of Patriarch Sergius I (610–638) and the Patrician Bonus (*Во́носъ*) carrying the sacred relics along the Theodosian and Blachernae walls: the Mandylion (Icon of Christ Not-Made-by-Hands), the True Wood of the Cross, and the Venerable Robe (*Ша́та*, *Maphorion*) of the Theotokos.
[^2360]: Reference to the skirmish at the Church of the Theotokos at the Spring (*Пи́геа*, *Pege* / *Zoodochos Pege*) outside the Golden Gate, and the arrogant ultimatum of the Avar Chagan.
[^2361]: Physical leaf 766 (f. 446v). Account of the destruction of the Slavic/Avar *monoxyla* (dugout boats) by a sudden supernatural tempest in the Golden Horn (*Кера́то*, *to Keras*) off Blachernae on August 7, 626.
[^2362]: Etymological and liturgical explanation of the Akathist (*Несѣда́лное*, Greek: *Ἀκάθιστος*, "unseated"): the grateful people sang the entire office standing through the night.
[^2363]: Account of the Second Siege of Constantinople (674–678 AD) under Emperor Constantine IV Pogonatus ("the Bearded", reigned 668–685) by the Umayyad Arab fleet, which was destroyed by Greek fire and winter storms at Syllaeum (*Силе́й* / *Σύλλαιον*) in Pamphylia.
[^2364]: Account of the Third Siege of Constantinople (717–718 AD) by Maslama ibn Abd al-Malik under Emperor Leo III the Isaurian (reigned 717–741).
[^2365]: Physical leaf 767 (f. 447r). Continuation of the 717–718 siege: the Bulgarian intervention under Khan Tervel, the massive iron boom chain stretched across the Golden Horn from Galata, and the destruction of the Arab fleet by storms at Sosthenion (*Сосоѳе́нїѡнъ* / *Σωσθένιον*), leaving only three ships to return.
[^2366]: Conclusion of the historical exposition, reiterating that the congregation remains standing throughout the singing of the 24 stanzas of the Akathist, unlike other ikoi where sitting is permitted during the kathismata.
[^2367]: Concluding devotional prayer of the Ruthenian Synaxarion (*Твое́ю вале́чною и҆ незви́тѧжоною Мт҃ре мл҃твами...*).
[^2368]: Rubric prescribing the chanting of the Biblical Odes (the Canticles of the Old Testament), referring back to leaf 413 (physical page 713, f. 413r).

[^2370]: Ode 7, First Troparion of the Akathist Canon, hailing the Theotokos as the spiritual Chariot of the Sun and the true Vine yielding the Cluster of immortality.

[^2670]: Genesis 27:33. Septuagint: Ἐξέστη δὲ Ἰσαὰκ ἔκστασιν μεγάλην σφόδρα καὶ εἶπεν Τίς οὖν ὁ θηρεύσας μοι θήραν καὶ εἰσενέγκας μοι, καὶ ἔφαγον ἀπὸ πάντων πρὸ τοῦ ἐλθεῖν σε, καὶ ηὐλόγησα αὐτόν; καὶ εὐλογημένος ἔσται.
[^2671]: Genesis 27:34. Septuagint: Ἐγένετο δὲ ἡνίκα ἤκουσεν Ἡσαῦ τὰ ῥήματα Ἰσαὰκ τοῦ πατρὸς αὐτοῦ, ἀνεβόησεν φωνὴν μεγάλην καὶ πικρὰν σφόδρα καὶ εἶπεν Εὐλόγησον δὴ κἀμέ, πάτερ.
[^2672]: Genesis 27:35. Septuagint: Εἶπεν δὲ αὐτῷ Ἐλθὼν ὁ ἀδελφός σου μετὰ δόλου ἔλαβεν τὴν εὐλογίαν σου.

[^2674]: Genesis 27:37. Septuagint: Ἀποκριθεὶς δὲ Ἰσαὰκ εἶπεν τῷ Ἡσαῦ Εἰ κύριον αὐτὸν ἐποίησά σου καὶ πάντας τοὺς ἀδελφοὺς αὐτοῦ ἐποίησα αὐτοῦ οἰκέτας, σίτῳ καὶ οἴνῳ ἐστήρισα αὐτόν· σοὶ δὲ τί ποιήσω, τέκνον;
[^2675]: Genesis 27:38. Septuagint: Καὶ εἶπεν Ἡσαῦ πρὸς τὸν πατέρα αὐτοῦ Μὴ εὐλογία μία σοί ἐστιν, πάτερ; εὐλόγησον δὴ κἀμέ, πάτερ. κατανυχθέντος δὲ Ἰσαὰκ ἀνεβόησεν φωνῇ Ἡσαῦ καὶ ἔκλαυσεν.
